Dogfooded end-to-end: a plain scan completes in under a second with embeddings left `NULL`; `--semantic` against an unembedded project returns the hint message; `--embed` on a fresh scan populates real embeddings and `--semantic` finds the right function by meaning; a changed function's embedding is confirmed `NULL` after a follow-up plain scan.
- [x] 11. Investigate and fix §13's remaining `drspec scan` slowness — see §13 for the original write-up; both of its leads panned out, plus a serious correctness bug found while chasing the second one.
  - [x] 11.1. **Confirmed §13's `get_artifact` anomaly by tracing every call site, not assumed**: all 7 call sites across `graph_sync.py` only ever read `.code_hash` off the result (or just checked existence) — never any other field. Fixed by (a) a new lightweight `get_artifact_code_hash(conn, function_id)` query (`SELECT code_hash`, not the full row including `body`), and (b) a `code_hash_cache: dict[function_id, code_hash]` threaded through the whole `sync_scan_results` call tree, populated for free by `_sync_nodes` (which already visits every function to hash-diff it) and reused by `_sync_edges`/`_sync_references` instead of re-querying. Verified via profiling: `get_artifact` calls went from 2,216 to zero; the replacement `get_artifact_code_hash` is called exactly 498 times for 498 functions — a 1:1 ratio, confirming the redundancy is fully gone, not just reduced.
  - [x] 11.2. **Re-tested batching against *this* bottleneck specifically, per §13's explicit instruction not to assume Task 9's negative result transfers** — and this time it helps, substantially, verified directly: 500 parameterized inserts to a file-backed DB took 2.15s under DuckDB's default per-statement auto-commit vs. 0.44s wrapped in one explicit transaction (~4.8x). Unlike Task 9's `find_spec` overhead (which scaled with *bound parameter count*, immune to batching), this is per-*transaction-commit* cost (a real fsync on a file-backed DB) — reducing the number of commits is exactly what helps here. `drspec scan`'s CLI command now wraps its whole write path (`sync_scan_results` + the `queue_push` loop) in one `conn.begin()`/`conn.commit()`, with `conn.rollback()` on any failure.
  - [x] 11.3. **Real bug found while implementing 11.2, not a pre-planned task — a second, more serious instance of the same category of discovery as Task 10.3.** Wrapping the whole scan in one explicit transaction broke the existing `_fk_references_detached` workaround *and* `cascade_delete_artifact`'s delete-referencing-rows-then-delete-the-row sequence: both rely on DuckDB's FK checker seeing an *already-committed* DELETE before validating a later UPDATE/DELETE on the row it referenced — confirmed directly that DuckDB does not honor an uncommitted same-transaction DELETE on a referencing row for this check. Previously this was invisible because every statement auto-committed individually (each DELETE was durable by the time the next statement ran); the one-big-transaction change made the deletes and the dependent operation share an uncommitted transaction for the first time, and both call sites failed immediately. Fixed by having `_fk_references_detached` and `cascade_delete_artifact` each call `conn.commit()` internally at the point their deletes must become durable (and `_fk_references_detached` again after its restore) — safe to call unconditionally (confirmed `conn.commit()` is a no-op without an open explicit transaction), so both work correctly whether called standalone (DuckDB's default auto-commit, unchanged from before) or nested inside the CLI's new outer transaction (which gets split into sub-commits at exactly these points, not broken).
  - [x] 11.4. **Side effect of 11.2/11.3, not an additional feature**: `drspec scan` is now atomic with respect to everything it can control — a failure partway through (anywhere after the transaction begins) rolls back fully, leaving the database exactly as it was before the scan started, rather than a partially-synced state. Previously every statement auto-committed as it went, so a mid-scan crash could leave some functions/edges updated and others not.
  - [x] 11.5. Tests: `test_update_with_fk_reference_survives_an_outer_explicit_transaction` and `test_cascade_delete_survives_an_outer_explicit_transaction` in `test_db_queries.py` (11.3's regression, both call sites), `test_scan_is_atomic_rolls_back_fully_on_failure` in `test_scan_command.py` (11.4, a mocked mid-scan failure leaves zero artifacts). Full suite green (1194 passed, same 1 pre-existing unrelated failure).
  - [x] 11.6. **Verified end-to-end on this project's own real `src/` tree (498 functions), not just the synthetic benchmarks**: a fresh full scan went from §13's reported 10.6s to **5.48s** (~1.9x) — short of 11.2's raw 4.8x insert-only benchmark, since real scan time also includes parsing, hashing, and resolution work the transaction change doesn't touch. A full rescan with nothing changed takes ~1.1s (dominated by the now-minimal `get_artifact_code_hash` lookups). A rescan with ~24 genuinely-changed functions (each paying 11.3's per-function commit split) takes ~3.2s — confirms the detach-requiring path isn't regressed, just not further sped up by 11.2's batching, exactly as expected.

  - [x] 12.1. **Chose option 1 of §15's two named directions (the `--force`/`--rehash`-style flag), not option 2 (multi-path `drspec scan` support), and didn't implement both.** Reasoning: `--force` fully closes the demonstrated gap on its own (re-resolving an unchanged caller's calls against the DB's current state, regardless of what order modules were scanned in), while multi-path scanning only helps the *first-time, scanned-together* case — it wouldn't retroactively fix an already-scanned, already-unchanged module's stale-forever edges without *also* needing something like `--force` underneath it anyway. Multi-path remains a real, separate, smaller convenience feature, not implemented here — out of scope since `--force` alone already closes the gap §15 demonstrated.
  - [x] 12.2. New `drspec scan --force` flag (default off). Threaded as a new `force: bool` parameter through `sync_scan_results()`: when set, the functions passed to edge/reference resolution (`_sync_edges`/`_sync_references`) become *every* function in this scan's fresh parse, not just the `changed_or_new` subset `_sync_nodes` identifies. Safe without any other change to those two functions' internals — `code_hash_cache` (Task 11.1) already covers every function `_sync_nodes` touches, changed or not, so the "always a cache hit" guarantee those functions already relied on still holds under `--force`.
  - [x] 12.3. Resolves §15's first open question ("does this interact with `--deep-scan`'s staleness trade-off, or is it separate?"): **confirmed separate, as leaned in §15.** `--force` only widens *which functions* get resolution re-attempted; `--deep-scan` only widens *which resolution steps* run for a given function (adds §3.5 Step 3's fallback). Orthogonal and composable — `drspec scan --force --deep-scan` re-resolves every scanned function using every step, including the fallback.

- [x] 14. Implement §17's live backend-connected graph viewer (`drspec serve`) — see §17 for the full write-up and its five open questions.
  - [x] 14.1. **Resolved the single most load-bearing open question (concurrent-scan safety) by direct empirical testing, not by trusting the "DuckDB is file-based, so a fresh query likely sees new state, for free" hope in §17's own text — which turned out to be incomplete.** Verified with genuinely separate OS processes (not `subprocess.run()` from within a process that already has DuckDB loaded — see 14.9 for why that specific method is misleading): a **read-write** connection (DuckDB's default) held open by one process blocks *every* other process — reader or writer — from opening the same file at all, for as long as it's held. A **read-only** connection held open does **not** block a separate writer from opening and writing. This directly decides open question 3 (read-only vs. writes) as a hard *requirement*, not just the safer preference §17 already leaned toward: a read-write server connection would make `drspec scan` fail outright for the server's entire runtime.
  - [x] 14.2. Resolves open question 1 (server lifecycle): **blocks the terminal, Ctrl-C to stop — no daemonization.** Every other drspec command is a one-shot invocation; a background-daemon model is a materially bigger change to this project's operating model, not something to introduce as a side effect of one feature.
  - [x] 14.3. Resolves open question 2 (port handling): fixed default (`8787`, chosen arbitrarily as an uncommon-but-memorable port with no special meaning) with a `--port` flag to override, `--port 0` to let the OS assign a free one. A bind failure (e.g. port already taken) raises a clear `RuntimeError` surfaced as a normal CLI error response, not a raw traceback.
  - [x] 14.4. Resolves open question 3 (read-only vs. writes): **read-only**, per 14.1 — both the safety reasoning §17 already gave *and* now a hard DuckDB-concurrency requirement. `drspec serve` never writes to the database anywhere in its code path.

  - [x] 14.6. Resolves open question 5 (does a fresh per-request query naturally see a concurrent scan's new state?): **yes, confirmed directly (14.1)** — provided the server's own connection is read-only and, per 14.1's finding, never held open between requests. Implemented exactly that way: every `/graph` request opens a brand-new `duckdb.connect(db_path, read_only=True)`, uses it, and closes it before the response is sent — never a connection held for the server's lifetime. This also shrinks (it cannot fully eliminate) the already-small window where a request's brief connection could coincide with a scan's own brief write-lock; that case is caught and returned as a clean HTTP 503 with a message naming the likely cause, not a crash.

New `drspec serve <function_id> [--depth] [--direction] [--port]` CLI command, registered directly on the main app (not a nested `Typer` sub-app) from the start — same Task 6 finding #2 lesson already applied proactively to `scan`/`search`.

New `render_live_graph_html()` produces the live page: on load it `fetch()`es `/graph` instead of reading embedded data, a Refresh button re-fetches for the current center/depth/direction, and a "Center on this node" button in a node's detail panel re-fetches centered on that node — resolving the literal "select a node then it refreshes in backend" ask from §17's own `## The gap`.
  - [x] 14.9. **Second real, non-obvious DuckDB finding, surfaced while writing the automated test suite, not during the main investigation**: within *one Python process*, opening a second connection to the same database file with a *different* `read_only` setting than an already-open (or even already-closed) connection to that same file raises `ConnectionException: Can't open a connection ... with a different configuration than existing connections` — a same-process limitation, distinct from 14.1's cross-process lock behavior. Also found (and discarded as misleading for this purpose): spawning a writer via `subprocess.run()` from a process that has already used DuckDB in a background thread reproducibly fails with a confusing `CatalogException: Table ... does not exist`, even though genuinely separate, independently-launched processes (the real `drspec serve` + `drspec scan` deployment shape) do not have this problem at all — confirmed both ways directly. Net effect: the cross-process concurrency claim (14.1) is verified by real separate CLI invocations (14.11), not by an in-process or `subprocess.run()`-based automated test, which would have been testing an artifact of this environment's process model, not the real behavior.

Wired into `resolve_name_imports` (bare `from pkg import X`), `resolve_module_imports` (`import pkg as p`, unioning the package's direct children with per-name collision-dropping), and `bind_declared_types` (a type annotation imported the same package way) — the explore-agent research that preceded this fix flagged `bind_declared_types` as a second, unreported call site of the identical blind spot, fixed here too rather than left as a known-but-unaddressed gap.

Documented, accepted residual risk: this is an approximation, not a trace of `__init__.py`'s actual `__all__`/import list — a same-named *private*, never-actually-re-exported function that happens to be unique within the same package directory would be a false positive. Not hit in this project's own real codebase (verified by the dogfood in 16.4), and avoiding it would require on-demand parsing this module deliberately doesn't do.

Fixed with a two-character change — `"venv*"`/`".venv*"` glob patterns (the matching mechanism already supports `fnmatch` globs, e.g. the existing `"*.egg-info"` entry) — covering any non-standard venv directory name, not just the exact two spellings previously hardcoded.

- [x] 17. Implement §21's grep-rewriting hook — see §21 for the full write-up and its four open questions.
  - [x] 17.1. Resolved open question 1 (translation completeness): deliberately narrow, conservative translator (`_translate_grep_to_drspec_search` in `.claude/hooks/prefer_drspec.py`) that returns a rewrite only when confident, `None` (fall back to the existing §18 `ask`) otherwise. Only the classic `grep`/`egrep`/`fgrep` family is attempted (not `rg`/`ag`/`ack` — their flag semantics aren't verified to match closely enough to risk a silent mistranslation). Bails on: piped/chained/redirected commands (checked token-by-token after `shlex.split`, not a raw-string scan — see 17.3), command substitution (`$(...)`/backticks, anywhere, regardless of quoting), any unrecognized flag (including inside a bundled short-flag cluster like `-rn`), more than one path argument, and a contradictory `-c`/`-l` combination.
  - [x] 17.2. Resolved open question 2 (rewrite vs. ask, or both) and open question 4 (scope relative to §18's hook) together: **both, layered — and never `allow`.** A confidently-translatable `Bash` grep command gets `permissionDecision: "ask"` *with* `hookSpecificOutput.updatedInput` swapping in the equivalent `drspec search ...` command, plus a reason spelling out the exact substitution; anything not confidently translatable falls through to §18's original plain-`ask` nudge, unchanged. Deliberately **not** `allow`: `updatedInput` is a real but under-documented harness field (confirmed present, but with no first-party worked example and an open upstream doc-gap issue about this exact feature) — pairing an unverified mechanism with `allow` risks a silent failure mode (the *original*, unrewritten grep running with no prompt at all, if this harness version doesn't actually apply it) that's strictly worse than doing nothing; pairing it with `ask` fails safe in both directions. A `Grep` tool call (not `Bash`) keeps the exact pre-§21 plain-`ask` behavior — found directly while designing this: a hook's `updatedInput` can only replace the *same* tool's own input, so there's no mechanism to rewrite a `Grep` call into a different tool's call at all.
  - [x] 17.3. **Real, live-caught correctness bug, found by dogfooding the finished hook on a genuine grep call during this very task — not a hypothetical edge case**: the first version translated any pattern as a *literal* drspec search whenever `-E` wasn't present, reasoning "no `-E` means not regex." That's wrong — plain `grep` is still POSIX *basic* regex (BRE), just with different escaping (`\|` for alternation, `\(...\)` for groups), not literal matching. Caught live: a real `grep -n "pat1\|pat2" docs/design.md` got silently rewritten into a literal search for the string `pat1\|pat2`, returning a confidently wrong "0 matches" when the real (BRE-interpreted) answer was 1 match — verified both ways directly (`re.compile(..., re.MULTILINE)` reproducing the true match count, and the hook's own mistranslated output showing zero). Fixed by bailing (never translating to literal) whenever the pattern contains any BRE metacharacter (`.`/`*`/`^`/`$`/`[`/`]`/`\`) and `-E` wasn't given — only a pattern with none of those is safely literal-translatable; this also incidentally fixed the shell-pipe-vs.-quoted-pipe ambiguity noted in 17.1 (`-E "a|b"`'s `|` is inside quotes and still correctly handled via the earlier token-level check, independent of this fix).
  - [x] 17.4. **The live Claude Code harness actually applied `updatedInput` for real during this same dogfood** — the strongest possible confirmation available for an under-documented field: a real `Bash` grep tool call in this session came back as genuine `drspec search` JSON output (matching `success_response`'s own shape exactly), proving the rewrite mechanism works in this harness version, not just in theory. (A second, textually-similar grep call in the same session did *not* get rewritten — most likely some harness-side decision caching/dedup behavior across similar-looking commands, not inspectable from inside this sandbox; noted but not chased further, since it doesn't change the hook's own correctness and isn't something this hook's code controls.)
  - [x] 17.5. Tests: new `test_prefer_drspec_hook.py` (33 tests) — the translation function tested directly and exhaustively (bundled short flags, each supported grep flag, the pipe-inside-a-pattern-vs-real-pipe distinction, command substitution, multiple paths, unrecognized flags, the BRE-literal distinction from 17.3, ripgrep/non-grep exclusion), plus `main()`'s stdin/stdout contract via a small fake-stream harness (rewrite applied with `ask` not `allow`, other `tool_input` fields preserved in `updatedInput`, untranslatable commands keep the plain nudge, the `Grep`-tool-can't-be-rewritten case, malformed JSON). Full suite green (1348 passed, 20 deselected, no failures).
  - [x] 17.6. **Second real, live-caught correctness bug, reported directly after this task had already shipped and re-confirmed by reproduction before fixing**: `shlex.split('grep x;ls')` returns `['grep', 'x;ls']` as *one* token — Python's `shlex` module has no concept of shell operators at all, it only splits on whitespace and quotes. 17.1's original check tokenized first and flagged a token only when it was made up *entirely* of operator characters (a bare `;` as its own token) — which silently missed an operator glued directly to adjacent text with no surrounding whitespace, since that glued token is indistinguishable, after the fact, from one that came from a legitimately quoted pattern containing the same characters (`grep -E "a|b"` -> token `a|b`, already correctly treated as safe). `grep x;ls` was silently rewritten into a literal `drspec search` for the string `'x;ls'`, **dropping the `ls` command's execution entirely** — a real, confirmed, silent behavior change, not a security issue (nothing executes anything it shouldn't; a real command just stops running). Fixed by replacing both the token-based operator check *and* the separate raw-string command-substitution regex with one unified function, `_has_unquoted_shell_syntax`, that walks the *raw* command string once, tracking quote state character by character — the only way to find an operator regardless of whether whitespace happens to separate it from its neighbors, and the only way to apply real shell quoting rules correctly (nothing is special inside `'...'`, including `$(...)`/backticks; inside `"..."` the classic operators are literal but `$(...)`/backticks still substitute, matching real bash exactly). 14 new regression cases added directly for this (glued `;`/`&`/`|`/redirect, single- vs. double-quote semantics for both operators and substitution, an escaped-semicolon-outside-quotes literal case, and one correcting the task's own first attempt at a test — a bare unquoted `|` in *plain* `grep` turned out to already be correctly treated as literal, since POSIX BRE only gives `\|` special meaning, not a bare `|`). Full suite green (1399 passed, 20 deselected, no failures — including the two already-passing scan-collision tests from the separate §22 review fix landed just before this, and §20/Task 19's own new tests below).

- [x] 18. Implement §22's background staleness-resolution worker — see §22 for the full write-up and its four open questions.
  - [x] 18.1. **Resolved a real ambiguity in §22's own "Proposed shape" wording before writing any code**, rather than guessing: the text says a plain `drspec scan` "can flag likely-stale ones cheaply... into a queue," which would mean changing `drspec scan`'s own correctness-critical hashing logic to add an untested mtime/size fast-path — a materially bigger, riskier change than the stated gap ("no signal that source changed *since* the last scan") actually calls for. Resolved instead as: `drspec scan` gains exactly one new, purely-additive side effect (`graph_sync._record_scanned_file_snapshots`, upserting each scanned file's current mtime/size into a new `scanned_files` table — free, since the file is already being read to parse it); the worker itself is the *only* thing that ever diffs those snapshots against the real filesystem, on its own schedule, project-wide — not scoped to whatever path a given `drspec scan` invocation happened to target. `drspec scan`'s existing hash-based change detection is completely untouched.
  - [x] 18.2. Resolved open question 1 (launch trigger): **`drspec scan` itself, on completion** — `spawn_worker_if_not_running()`, best-effort and silent (a launch failure never turns a successful scan into a reported one). Not the first query needing freshness info (would turn every read-only `search`/`deps_get` call into a process-spawning side effect) and not `drspec serve` (Task 14.2 already decided against daemonizing that command specifically).
  - [x] 18.3. Resolved open question 2 (queue storage shape): **two new dedicated tables**, not a reuse of `queue`/`config` — `scanned_files` (one row per scanned file: path, mtime, size, last-scanned-at — a snapshot, not a queue) and `staleness_queue` (a file path's mere presence means "possibly stale, not yet re-verified" — no status column at all, since nothing ever needs to distinguish a stuck "PROCESSING" row from a crash). Reusing `queue` (function-level, contract-review-workflow semantics) would have overloaded an unrelated table's meaning, the same reasoning that originally kept `code_references` separate from `dependencies` (§9).
  - [x] 18.4. Resolved open question 3 (staleness-indicator shape): **a plain `possibly_stale: bool` per function**, file-granular (not per-edge) — added to every function entry `search`, `deps get`, and `deps unused` return (plus their MCP `search`/`deps_get`/`unused` equivalents), computed by `stale_file_paths_among()` reading whatever `staleness_queue` already knows — never a live recheck at query time, per §22's own "decided already" note. A richer confidence-tier-style breakdown was considered and not built — file-level presence/absence is the whole signal the worker actually produces; anything richer would be invented, not reported.
  - [x] 18.5. Resolved open question 4 (retry/backoff parameters) **on the worker's own side**: none, deliberately — every DB touch opens a fresh connection and closes it immediately (never held between files or passes, Task 14's lesson applied here too); if a write-capable connect fails (busy), that pass does nothing further, and the worker's own poll loop (default every 10s) *is* the retry. No escalating backoff, no retry counter, no give-up threshold — the simplest mechanism that still satisfies "never blocks an explicit, human-initiated write," matching §22's own leaning against over-engineering this upfront. **This subtask originally overclaimed how short the worker's write-held window actually is** — corrected directly in 18.11, below, after direct review found and confirmed the real gap: the "fresh connection, closed immediately" framing is true but incomplete, since `staged_scan`'s own whole-file-copy publish mechanism (inherited, not introduced here) means the window's *length* scales with total DB file size, not with how much work the worker is actually doing in that pass.
  - [x] 18.6. Singleton enforcement: a PID file (`_drspec/staleness_worker.pid`) created with `O_EXCL` (atomic create-if-absent, closing most of the obvious near-simultaneous-launch race — not literally race-proof against a worker crashing mid-write without cleaning up, but §22 explicitly asked for a straightforward mechanism first, not a pre-emptively bulletproof one). A `SIGTERM` handler (installed only for the worker loop's own duration) ensures `drspec worker stop` always releases the PID file on the way out — found necessary directly: Python's default, uncaught-`SIGTERM` disposition kills the process without running `finally` blocks, which would otherwise leave a stale (but harmless — reclaimed next launch) PID file behind every time.
  - [x] 18.7. New `drspec worker run/status/stop` commands (`cli/commands/worker.py`) — `run` is what `drspec scan` actually launches (not hidden, so someone debugging a missing worker can run it directly); `status`/`stop` exist because an invisible background process with no way to check on or stop it would be a real gap in this project's otherwise-consistent "every command returns structured JSON state" design.
  - [x] 18.8. **Real bug, caught only by dogfooding the auto-spawn through the actual `drspec scan` CLI, not by any unit test**: the first version launched the worker as `drspec worker run --db <path>` — but `--db` is a *global* option on the main `app` callback, not on `worker run` itself, so Click rejected it ("No such option") and the subprocess exited immediately, silently, with nothing surfacing the failure (by design — a launch failure must never turn a successful scan into a reported one, which is exactly what let this hide). Every unit test for the spawn logic passed throughout, because they all called `spawn_worker_if_not_running`/`run_worker` as library functions directly, never actually exec'd the real `python -m drspec ...` command line the way production code does. Fixed by moving `--db` before the subcommand (`drspec --db <path> worker run`); re-verified via the real CLI end-to-end (`drspec scan .` → `drspec worker status` shows it running → editing the scanned file → `drspec deps get` reflects the edit ~10s later with no explicit rescan → `drspec worker stop` → PID file gone) — not just re-running the existing unit suite, which this bug had already passed in full.
  - [x] 18.9. **A second real robustness gap, also caught by dogfooding against this repo's own already-`drspec init`-ed database, not a synthetic fixture**: `search`/`deps get`/`deps unused` are read-only commands that never run DDL, so a project `drspec init`-ed before this task (every project that existed before today, including this repo's own dev database) has no `staleness_queue` table at all — the first version of `stale_file_paths_among()` let that raise a raw `duckdb.CatalogException` straight through `drspec search`, turning an ordinary query into a crash. Fixed by treating "table doesn't exist" the same as "nothing flagged" — an honest answer for a project this table hasn't reached yet, not an error. `drspec scan` itself was separately made to call `init_schema()` defensively (idempotent — `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` only) so a project reaches the new tables the first time it's rescanned, without needing a destructive `--force` rebuild just to pick up a schema addition.
  - [x] 18.10. Tests across every new piece, each exercised against real files on disk and (for every concurrency/process-lifecycle claim) genuinely separate OS processes, not mocks or same-process assumptions (Task 14's methodology, applied consistently here): `test_staleness_worker.py` (17 — drift detection for a real content change/deletion/no-op, full detect-then-remediate passes including a file that stays queued across passes, singleton acquire/release/stale-PID-reclaim, the worker's own idle-timeout exit and its do-nothing-if-already-running case, a real separate-process `SIGTERM` releasing the PID file, and `run_one_pass` correctly reporting `db_busy` against a real separate process holding the write lock); `test_worker_command.py` (4, including one real `drspec worker run`/`status`/`stop` lifecycle across genuinely separate processes); 10 new cases in `test_db_queries.py` for the two new tables plus the `CatalogException`-tolerance fix; `possibly_stale` end-to-end coverage added to `test_scan_graph_sync.py` (through the real CLI) and `test_mcp_server.py` (all three MCP tools). `tests/conftest.py` added (new file) — a session-wide `autouse` fixture disabling the auto-spawn side effect for the whole test run, since every existing scan-exercising test in the suite would otherwise have spawned a real, independent 15-minute background process with nothing to ever reap it. Full suite green (1383 passed, 20 deselected, no failures).
  - [x] 18.11. **Two real, serious issues found by independent review, both confirmed by direct reproduction before fixing — not taken on faith.**
    1. `staged_scan` (the publish mechanism both `drspec scan` and the worker's own remediation use) holds the *original* connection's write lock for its entire checkpoint → whole-file-copy → write-on-the-copy → fsync → atomic-replace cycle — confirmed by reading `db/staging.py`'s own docstring, which already says so directly. The whole-file-copy step's duration scales with **total DB file size**, not with how much actually changed in that pass — a real, inherited characteristic of the existing `staged_scan` mechanism (not something this task introduced), but one the worker's existence makes a routine, recurring collision instead of a rare one (two humans running `drspec scan` at the exact same instant). Redesigning `staged_scan` itself (e.g. publishing via a DuckDB transaction directly against the live file instead of a copy) was considered and **deliberately not done here** — that mechanism exists specifically because a plain transaction was insufficient (Task 11's FK-workaround helpers commit internally, which `staged_scan`'s own docstring says would otherwise corrupt atomicity on the live file); redesigning it safely is a separate, substantial undertaking than this task's own scope, not a small follow-on.
    2. **`drspec scan` had no error handling at all for a locked database — confirmed by direct reproduction** (a genuinely separate process holding a write connection, the same Task 14 methodology used throughout this task's own tests): it crashed with a raw Python traceback instead of this project's normal clean JSON error, for an exception class (`duckdb.IOException`/`duckdb.Error`) that existed, and could in principle have fired, since Task 1 — a real pre-existing gap, just one the worker now makes realistic to actually hit instead of near-impossible.

    **Fixed the second, scoped the first as acknowledged future work, not fixed today** — a deliberate split, not an oversight: `scan_command` now retries acquiring its connection up to 3 times, 1 second apart (`_get_connection_with_retry`), papering over the *common* case (the worker's own publish clearing within a couple of seconds) without pretending to solve the underlying `staged_scan` scaling characteristic; if every retry still fails, a new `DATABASE_BUSY` error code returns a clean, actionable message naming the likely cause (a concurrent scan or the background worker) instead of a traceback. Re-verified both outcomes by direct reproduction after the fix: a brief (1.5s) lock now recovers transparently; a lock outlasting all three retries (8s) now returns the clean error, not a crash. New `TestScanCommandDatabaseBusy` in `test_scan_command.py` (2 tests, both against a genuinely separate OS process holding the lock, timing made deterministic with a marker file rather than a fixed sleep). Full suite green (1385 passed, 20 deselected, no failures).

- [x] 22. Implement §25's worker-routing redesign — see §25 for the full write-up and its ten open questions. Independently re-verified §25's own core claim first (a held-open read-only connection blocking a plain writer), both with a zero-drspec-code repro and at the drspec level, before building anything.
  - [x] 22.1. **Interface shape** (open question 1): a fixed set of operations, not a generic passthrough — matches Task 15's MCP-tools precedent, as the write-up itself leaned. Built as `worker_ipc.WorkerConnection`, a drop-in stand-in for `duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection`'s own `execute`/`begin`/`commit`/`rollback`/`close` (generic enough that most call sites needed a one-line swap, not a rewrite), plus exactly two purpose-built operations beyond that: `sync_scan` (drspec scan's publish step) and `remediate` (the staleness worker's own re-scan-and-publish step) — not one-per-CLI-command, since every read (`search`, `status`, `deps get/plot/unused`, `queue peek/get`) is already just SQL the generic `execute` covers.
  - [x] 22.2. **Where today's command logic lives** (open question 2): stays in `cli/commands/*.py` — the CLI layer keeps doing argument validation, JSON shaping, and error formatting; only the DB-connection part became an IPC call. `scan_command` itself was split: file-walking/tree-sitter parsing (no DB needed) stays client-side; the DB-touching half — the removed-file inventory diff, schema init, and the `staged_scan` publish — moved into `cli/commands/scan.py::publish_scan_results`, which now runs *inside* the worker process, reached via the new `sync_scan` op. Parsed scan results (`ScannedFunction`/`FileImports`, both plain nested dataclasses) cross the IPC boundary as JSON via `dataclasses.asdict`/matching `_function_from_dict`/`_file_imports_from_dict` reconstructors.
  - [x] 22.3. **Concurrent requests to the worker** (open question 3): serialized, via one shared `threading.Lock` around the worker's single real connection — not a request queue or a dedicated DB thread. Each accepted client gets its own handler thread processing its own requests in order; a bare `execute` holds the lock only for that call, an explicit `begin()` holds it until that same session's `commit()`/`rollback()`, and a client that disconnects mid-transaction is caught in the handler's own `finally:` (rollback + release) so one dropped connection can't wedge the worker for everyone else. Deliberately the simplest model that's correct, as the open question itself invited.
  - [x] 22.4. **Error propagation through IPC** (open question 4): a `duckdb.Error` raised inside the worker is serialized by its exact class name and reconstructed as that *same* exception class on the client side (`getattr(duckdb, error_type)`, falling back to `RuntimeError`) — several existing call sites' `except duckdb.Error:` clauses (Task 18.11's retry logic, `mcp_server.py`, `server.py`) depend on catching the real type, not a generic wrapper.
  - [x] 22.5. **IPC mechanism** (open questions 5 and the duplicate "IPC mechanism" bullet): a Unix domain socket, not HTTP — purely local, no port to manage or collide over, stdlib-only. Framing is newline-delimited JSON, the same pattern `mcp_server.py` already uses for its own stdio protocol.
  - [x] 22.6. **Bootstrap case** (open question 6): `drspec init` stays untouched and direct (no worker exists yet, and nothing else could be concurrently touching a file that doesn't exist yet). Every other command is "connect, or spawn-and-wait" — `worker_ipc.get_worker_connection()` tries the socket first, and only spawns (`spawn_worker_if_not_running`, reused as-is) and polls until it comes up if that fails, never assuming readiness the way the old fire-and-forget spawn did.
  - [x] 22.7. **Timeouts** (open question 7): a client waits up to 15s total for the worker's socket to come up after a (re-)spawn (cold start — schema init, first connect — should clear well inside that), and up to 120s for any single request/response round trip (generous for a real scan, short enough that a genuinely hung worker is reported, not waited on forever). Exceeding either raises `WorkerIPCError` with a message naming `drspec worker status`/`run` as the next step — never a silent fallback to a direct connection, per the write-up's own layered-recovery requirement.
  - [x] 22.8. **Scope** (open question 8): every routed command (`scan`, `search`, `status`, `deps get`/`plot`/`unused`, `queue peek`/`get`/`next`/`prioritize`, `drspec serve`, the MCP tools) now goes through `get_worker_connection()`; `drspec init` is the one deliberate carve-out (22.6).
  - [x] 22.9. **Rollout** (open question 9): all at once, not incremental — the write-up itself didn't require incremental rollout, and splitting writes-first/reads-second would have meant two separate connection models live simultaneously mid-migration, a worse state to verify than doing it in one pass.
  - [x] 22.10. **Idle behavior** (one of the three decisions already made in conversation, not an open question): implemented as stated — `IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` is now `float("inf")` by default; the worker blocks on its own poll loop rather than exiting, with the parameter kept only so a test can still force deterministic termination.
  - [x] 22.11. **A new finding the write-up didn't anticipate, discovered during implementation, not assumed away**: `staged_scan`'s copy-then-`os.replace()` publish mechanism (Task 11) was always safe for the *old* architecture (a connection opened fresh per command and closed immediately after, never read from again) but is actively unsafe for the *new* one (the worker's one connection, reused across every request) — confirmed directly, not assumed: after the swap, the connection passed in as `staged_scan`'s `source` keeps its file descriptor pinned to the old, now-unlinked inode. A *read* through that same connection afterward shows stale (pre-publish) data, and a *write* through it lands only in that orphaned, invisible file and is lost the moment it closes — silently, with no error. Fixed by `worker_ipc.WorkerIPCServer._reconnect()`: closes and reopens the worker's one connection immediately after every `sync_scan`/`remediate`, while still holding the exclusivity lock, so nothing ever observes or writes through the stale handle. Verified directly with a minimal repro (checkpoint, copy, replace, then compare a long-held connection's own view against a genuinely separate process's view of the real file) before writing the fix, not after.
  - [x] 22.12. The staleness worker (§22/Task 18) itself restructured to match: `run_worker()` now opens exactly one real connection for its entire run, starts a `WorkerIPCServer` on it, and runs its own autonomous detect-and-remediate loop through a **loopback** `WorkerConnection` (`worker_ipc.connect_loopback`) connected to its own just-started socket — sharing the exact same lock/reconnect discipline as every external client, not a second, separately-reasoned-about path to the same connection. `_detect_drift`/`run_one_pass` were widened to accept either a real connection or a `WorkerConnection` (`ConnectionLike`), so the same code runs standalone in tests and live inside the worker.
  - [x] 22.13. **Test-mode carve-out, not anticipated by the write-up, needed to keep ~1400 existing unit tests from each spawning a real OS process**: `DRSPEC_DISABLE_AUTO_WORKER` (already set session-wide by `tests/conftest.py` for the pre-§25 reason) now also makes `get_worker_connection()` return a `_DirectConnection` — a plain, single-use `duckdb.connect()` per call, exactly the pre-§25 connection lifetime, no socket, no worker. Not a production code path (nothing outside the test session sets this flag) and so not the "silent fallback" the write-up's layered-recovery model rules out — that concern is about masking a real worker being unreachable in a live project, not about same-process, non-concurrent unit tests.

  - [x] 22.17. **Two real, severe issues found by independent review, both confirmed by direct reproduction before fixing — not taken on faith (same discipline as 18.11's own precedent):**
    1. **`contract`, `source`, `verify`, `vision`, and `learn` still called `get_connection()` directly** — explicitly left un-audited by Task 21.2 at the time, a reasonable carve-out *then* (the worker only briefly opened/closed a connection per staleness pass). §25's "dormant, not exited" idle behavior makes the worker hold its connection open for its *entire* run, which starts automatically after any `drspec scan` — turning that old carve-out into a guaranteed crash for ordinary usage. Reproduced live exactly as reported: after one `drspec scan`, `drspec contract list`/`source get`/`learn stats`/`vision list` all crashed with a raw, unhandled `duckdb.IOException` traceback. Two more instances of the identical gap found independently while fixing this, via `drspec search`/dogfooding rather than assumed covered: `drspec serve`'s own pre-flight `get_artifact` lookup (same crash), and `drspec worker status`'s queue-size read (silently degraded to `null` instead of crashing, since it already had a broad `except Exception`, but broken the same way). **Fixed**: all seven files migrated to `get_worker_connection()`, following Task 21.2's own audit discipline (each command's actual body checked for a write, not assumed from its name) — `contract.get`/`list`, `source.get`, `verify.script`, `vision.list_findings` read-only; `contract.save`, `verify.run`, `vision.save`/`update`, all three `learn.py` commands (`init_learning_schema` runs unconditionally, even in the two that read-only by name), and `serve_command`'s pre-check write-capable. `worker.py status` now only goes through the worker when one is confirmed already running (`is_worker_running()` first) — never through `get_worker_connection()` unconditionally, which would otherwise *spawn* a worker just to check whether one exists, backwards from what the command reports. Two smaller, pre-existing gaps fixed as part of touching these exact lines (not scope creep — left broken while already present would have been negligent): `verify.py`'s three connection-leak paths (`run()` leaked on every early-exit except one; `script()` never closed at all) wrapped in `try/finally`; `learn.py`'s three commands silently ignored the global `--db-path` override (now respected, via the same `db_path_override` pattern every other command already uses).
    2. **A real DuckDB semantics mismatch, found while fixing #1, not anticipated by §25's own design**: `WorkerConnection.commit()`/`.rollback()` raised an opaque error whenever called with no explicit transaction open — but a real `duckdb` connection's `.commit()` with nothing open is a confirmed, documented-elsewhere safe no-op (Task 11.3), which `_fk_references_detached` (reached from `contract.save`/`verify.run` via `update_artifact_status`) relies on unconditionally. Fixed: `WorkerIPCServer._op_commit` now matches real `duckdb` exactly — a no-op when nothing's open, not an error; `_op_rollback` keeps erroring in that case (also confirmed to match real `duckdb`'s own behavior directly, not assumed), now with the real `TransactionException` type/message instead of a generic one.
    3. **A second, independent new bug found while dogfooding the now-fixed write commands against a real worker, not caught by any existing test**: `WorkerConnection.execute()` only JSON-encoded row *results* coming back, never the outgoing *params* — crashed with a raw `TypeError` the moment a call site fed a previously-fetched value (a `datetime`) back as a later statement's own bound parameter, exactly what `_fk_references_detached`'s restore step does for every one of its six tables. Fixed: params now go through the same `encode_rows`/`decode_rows` helpers already used for row results, symmetrically on both the client's outgoing request and the server's incoming one.
    4. **A separate, moderate gap in the same review**: the user's own §25 decision was "dormant, **not exited**" — minimal CPU usage while idle, not just never exiting. `run_worker`'s loop only implemented the "never exit" half, polling `_detect_drift` on the same fixed cadence forever regardless of idle time. Fixed: new `dormant_after`/`dormant_poll_interval` parameters (default 120s / 300s) — once a pass finds nothing to detect or remediate for that long, every subsequent sleep uses the dormant interval instead of the fast one, snapping straight back the moment a pass finds real work.
    5. Verified live against a real spawned worker, not just the test suite: `contract list`/`save`, `source get`, `verify script`/`run`, `learn stats`, `vision list`/`save` all succeed cleanly post-fix (confirmed via a scripted reproduction before AND after each fix, matching this project's own "confirm the bug, then confirm the fix" discipline throughout this file) — including `contract save`'s full `_fk_references_detached` path (a fetched `datetime` round-tripped as a parameter, exercising finding 3 directly) and `worker status` correctly reporting a real queue size while the worker is running (not `null`). New tests: `test_staleness_worker.py::TestRunWorkerLoop::test_backs_off_to_the_dormant_interval_while_idle`; `test_worker_ipc.py::TestWorkerConnectionBasics::test_a_fetched_datetime_round_trips_back_as_a_bound_parameter`. Full suite green: 1426 passed, 20 deselected, no failures.
  - [x] 22.18. **A second round of independent review found two more real issues — one a straight continuation of 22.17's own pattern, the other a genuinely new structural gap in §25's own recovery model:**
    1. **`src/drspec/api/__init__.py` — the public programmatic API (`query_contract`, `run_verification`, `list_queue`, `get_dependencies`) — was missed entirely by 22.17's audit.** All four called `get_connection()` directly; reproduced live exactly the same way (a plain Python import + call against a running worker raised the same raw `duckdb.IOException`). **Fixed**: all four migrated to a new `_connect_readonly()` helper (all four are confirmed read-only) wrapping `get_worker_connection(..., read_only=True)`; a new public `DatabaseBusyError(DrSpecError)` exception replaces a leaked internal `WorkerIPCError`, so a caller of this module never needs to import `drspec.worker_ipc` just to catch it.
    2. **§25's layered recovery model named the worker itself as layer 1 — "self-supervising... this is its own job as the server side" — and that layer didn't exist.** `run_worker`'s main loop called `run_one_pass` with no `try/except` around it at all; one uncaught exception from a single pass (a transient IPC hiccup, a file that fails to re-parse mid-remediation) silently killed the *entire* worker process, since a spawned worker's stderr goes to `/dev/null` and nothing else supervises it — confirmed structurally in code, with one real crash actually observed mid-review (not pinned down to an exact trigger, but the structural gap itself was the solid, reproducible claim). **Fixed**: each pass now runs in its own `try/except Exception`, treated identically to "nothing detected/remediated" for the dormant-backoff cadence (22.17.4) if it raises — so a *repeatedly* failing pass still backs off over time instead of busy-looping at the fast cadence forever. Also moved connection/schema-init/`WorkerIPCServer.start()` setup *inside* the same `try/finally` that releases the PID file and socket (previously outside it) — a setup-time failure used to skip cleanup entirely, relying on the PID file's own separate stale-reclaim logic to self-heal on the *next* launch attempt instead of cleaning up immediately.
    3. Verified live: `drspec.api`'s four functions all succeed against a real separate-process worker (a new `TestRoutesThroughTheWorker` test, genuinely separate OS processes, this project's own standard for the claim). The crash-recovery fix verified by *proving the test fails without it* — reverted the fix, confirmed `test_a_single_bad_pass_does_not_kill_the_worker` fails with the exact same `RuntimeError` escaping `run_worker` that was reported, then restored the fix and confirmed it passes — not just "the new test is green," direct evidence the test would have caught the reported bug. New tests: `test_api.py::TestRoutesThroughTheWorker` (1 case) + `DatabaseBusyError` added to the existing exception-hierarchy test; `test_staleness_worker.py::TestRunWorkerLoop::test_a_single_bad_pass_does_not_kill_the_worker`. Full suite green: 1447 passed, 21 deselected, no failures.
  - [x] 22.19. **A third round of independent review found a severe bug in 22.18.2's own fix, and a smaller observability gap in the same fix — both confirmed by direct reproduction before being fixed:**
    1. **`acquire_singleton()`'s PID-file write was two separate syscalls — `os.open(O_CREAT | O_EXCL)` (creates an *empty* file), then a separate `os.write()` (fills it in) — leaving a real window where the file exists but is still empty.** A concurrent `is_worker_running()` landing in that exact window read the empty file, got `ValueError` from `int("")`, and concluded "no PID here" — indistinguishable from a genuinely stale file — so `acquire_singleton` reclaimed it out from under the process still in the middle of legitimately writing it. Both processes then believed they were the sole worker, which is exactly the scenario 22.18.2's own self-supervision fix depends on never happening (two workers racing the same DB file defeats the entire point of §25). Reproduced directly with a synchronous two-step simulation against the old code *before* writing the fix, not assumed. **Fixed**: the PID is now written to a per-call-unique temp file first (`{pid_path.name}.{os.getpid()}.{threading.get_ident()}.tmp` — the thread ident alongside the PID so a deterministic same-process test can simulate two distinct callers without their temp files colliding, a genuine robustness improvement beyond just the test's own needs), then `os.link()`ed into the final path — existence and correct content become atomic together, since hard-link creation is one atomic directory-entry operation on POSIX with the same `EEXIST`-on-collision guarantee the old `O_EXCL` had, but now with the content already correct the instant the path becomes visible at all.
    2. **Smaller, same-review finding**: 22.18.2's own new `except Exception` around each worker pass had zero logging anywhere — a persistently-failing pass degraded to silent, permanent no-ops (same dormant-backoff cadence as "nothing to do"), observationally indistinguishable from a healthy idle worker. **Fixed**: `staleness_worker.py` gained its own `logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)` (mirroring `scanner.py`'s existing pattern); the except block now calls `logger.exception(...)` before falling back to the empty-result cadence — never silent, never raises past that point either.
    3. Verified: a direct manual reproduction against the *old* `acquire_singleton` (two sequential Python calls simulating the empty-file window) showed both returning `True` before the fix, and the fix closes it. A deterministic `threading.Event`-controlled regression test (`test_a_losing_concurrent_acquire_backs_off_instead_of_both_winning`) pins the exact interleaving; a looser, real-multi-process companion (`test_concurrent_acquires_never_produce_two_winners_under_real_load`, 15 subprocesses) checks the same claim under real load. Confirmed the deterministic test is a genuine regression test, not just green-by-construction, by reverting the fix and watching it fail for the expected reason. Also fixed, in the same pass, a pre-existing pre-§25 bug in `learning/diff.py` found while writing unrelated test coverage: `get_modified_functions()` called `parser.extract_functions(content)`, a method `PythonParser` has never had (only `.parse()`) — broke both the CLI's `drspec learn analyze` and the new `learn_analyze` MCP tool (24.1/26 below) identically; fixed to `parser.parse(content).functions`, with new `TestGetModifiedFunctions` coverage (none existed before, which is how this went unnoticed). Full suite green: 1451 passed, 21 deselected, no failures.

- [x] 23. Implement §26's `drspec search --lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C` — see §26 for the full write-up and its six open questions.
  - [x] 23.1. New `--lines` flag: for each match in the default (non-`-c`/`-l`/`--semantic`) listing mode, re-reads that match's file live off disk (`Path.cwd() / file_path`, not the DB) and returns every line the pattern occurs on as `{"lines": [{"line", "text"}, ...]}` on that match — using Python's `re`, not DuckDB's own regex engine the DB-backed `-E` path uses (a different engine, confirmed, not elided — a known, accepted gap for syntax only one of the two supports).
  - [x] 23.2. Every literal occurrence in the *whole file* is reported, not just ones inside the matched function's own body — directly required by this gap's own motivating cases: a module-level import line and a module-level dict constant's own definition are never part of any function's stored body at all, so restricting to "inside the matched function" would have left both unresolved.
  - [x] 23.3. A file deleted or unreadable since the last scan surfaces as `{"live_read_error": "..."}` on that match instead of omitting the `lines`/`context_blocks` key — never silently hidden, matching this project's "never silently stale" discipline elsewhere (confidence tiers, `possibly_stale`). An invalid `-E` regex surfaces the same way (`live_read_error`), rather than crashing the whole command.
  - [x] 23.4. `--lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C` rejected outright (`INVALID_INPUT`) in combination with `-c`/`-l`/`--semantic` — none of those three return per-match function records to attach line/context data to, so the combination has no meaning rather than silently doing nothing.
  - [x] 23.5. New `-A`/`-B`/`-C NUM` context flags, mirroring grep's own meaning exactly (`-C` sets both sides; an explicit `-A`/`-B` overrides just its own side) — same live re-read as `--lines`, returned as `{"context_blocks": [{"start_line", "end_line", "matched_lines": [...], "context": [{"line", "text"}, ...]}, ...]}` instead of the flatter `"lines"` shape (kept additive, not replaced, so existing `--lines`-only callers are unaffected). Overlapping or adjacent windows across multiple occurrences in the same file merge into one block — matching grep's own actual behavior (no duplicate/separate blocks for close matches) and resolving the "how do context lines interact with multiple occurrences" sub-question the same way.
  - [x] 23.6. Re-verified directly against all three of this write-up's own real motivating cases, not just synthetic tests: `drspec search "get_connection" --path src/drspec/cli/commands/worker.py -C 2` correctly shows the module-level import line (outside any function body) merged into one context block alongside the in-body occurrence; `drspec search "_TOOL_HANDLERS" --path src/drspec/mcp_server.py --lines` correctly shows the module-level dict constant's own definition line, previously unreachable by `drspec search` at all. New tests: 8 cases across `TestSearchLines`/`TestSearchContext` in `test_search_command.py` (every-occurrence reporting including outside the function body, regex mode, a deleted file's `live_read_error`, the `-c`/`-l`/`--semantic` rejection, `-A`-only, `-C`-overridden-by-explicit-`-A`, overlapping-occurrences merging). Full suite green (see Task 24.6 for the final combined count).

- [x] 29. Implement §31: line mode as a live search over files — see §31 for the write-up and the recommended direction.
  - [x] 29.1. `--lines` / `-A` / `-B` / `-C` (CLI) and `lines` / `context` / `before_context` / `after_context` (MCP) no longer decorate function matches. They run `core/live_search.py::live_search`, which reads every indexed file in scope (narrowed by `--path` / `--include`) and returns a flat `hits` list (`file_path`, `line`, `text`, `function_id`) or, with context, per-file merged `blocks` (`start_line`, `end_line`, `matched_lines`, `context`, `functions`). The CLI and the MCP tool share it; the old per-match `lines` / `context_blocks` fields and their helpers are gone. Two new DB helpers pick the files and the stored function ranges (`list_indexed_files`, `list_function_ranges`). The text renderer prints hits as `file:line:text` and blocks like `grep -C` (`:` match, `-` context).
  - [x] 29.2. Both problems in §31 are closed. Duplication: a line appears once (a file with three matching functions used to print its hit list three times). Missed module-level lines: `search "HANDLERS = " --lines` finds the constant; the same search without `--lines` finds nothing, because no function body holds it. Each hit names its innermost enclosing function from the stored ranges, but not in a file that is possibly stale; those hits are unlabelled and the files are listed in `possibly_stale_files`.
  - [x] 29.3. Decisions the write-up left open. `-v`, `--status` and `--confidence` filter functions, so they are rejected in line mode (`INVALID_INPUT`) rather than given a guessed meaning. `--limit` caps matching lines across all files, and `truncated` says when it cut the list. A file that was deleted or isn't UTF-8 is listed in `unreadable_files`, never silently dropped. An invalid regex is a clean `INVALID_INPUT` before any file is read. Costs named in §31 stand: each call reads every in-scope indexed file, the response shape changed for anything reading the old `lines` field, and files drspec doesn't scan (docs, config, unsupported languages) are out of reach. (Files added since the last scan, and scanned files with no functions, were first left out as well; both are now searched, see Task 32.3.)
  - [x] 29.4. The agent-facing skill (`.claude/skills/drspec/SKILL.md`) was out of date well beyond this change (it said two tools exist, that scan isn't a tool, and that non-function text needs grep); rewritten to describe the 25-tool surface and line mode. The MCP tool's `lines` description also said "ignored if semantic" while the handler returns an error; fixed.
  - [x] 29.5. Tests: `TestSearchLines` / `TestSearchContext` rewritten for the new shape (including the module-level-only case, once-per-line, limit and truncation, stale files, deleted files, rejected combinations, invalid regex, text output); `test_live_search.py` (nested-range labelling, non-UTF-8, case folding, limit across files, window clamping, the two DB helpers, exact-prefix matching); the MCP `search` tests rewritten.

- [x] 31. Fix the seven bugs in §33.
  - [x] 31.1. **`learn stats` after a learning event** (root cause matched the inference in §33): the worker encoded only `datetime` and sequences, so a `date` reached `json.dumps` in the handler thread, which only caught `OSError`, and that client's session ended. Fixed twice: `worker_ipc` now round-trips `date`, `time`, `timedelta`, `Decimal` and `bytes` with their own types, and a response that still can't be serialized comes back as an error naming the type (`Object of type UUID is not JSON serializable`) with the session intact. Tests drive a real in-thread server (`CAST(... AS DATE)`, and `SELECT uuid()` for the guard); unit tests run through `_DirectConnection`, which never serializes, so they could not see this.
  - [x] 31.2. **`learn analyze` crediting unchanged functions**: confirmed with the old rule on a real commit (a change to `add` also credited `mul`). `get_modified_functions` now derives the lines actually added (new file) and removed (old file) from each hunk's `+` / `-` lines. A function counts if a line was added inside it, or a line was removed from the same-named function in the parent version. Context lines and the one-past-the-end bound no longer matter; deleting a whole function credits no survivor. The old test built a hunk with no lines and only passed because of the bug; replaced with tests on real commits.
  - [x] 31.3. **IDs title-cased as dictionary keys**: `_label` now title-cases only keys shaped like field names (letters, digits, underscores); anything else (an ID, a path) prints verbatim. Status-style keys (`PENDING`) still print as `Pending`.
  - [x] 31.4. **Command right after `worker stop` hangs 15s**: two fixes. `stop_worker` (new, shared by the CLI and the MCP tool) waits up to 10s for the PID file to disappear and reports a worker that outlived the wait as an error. `get_worker_connection` re-attempts the spawn every 2s inside its wait loop, since the first attempt is a no-op while the old worker is still registered. **Found while testing this fix**: a worker registered its PID file before installing its SIGTERM handler (the heavy import in between takes a moment), so a `stop` in that window killed it with the default disposition and skipped all cleanup (exit code -15, PID file left behind). The handler is now installed first and everything after registration runs inside the `try/finally` that releases it. A real-process test runs stop-then-immediately-run-a-command.
  - [x] 31.5. **`init` with a live worker**: the cause is deeper than reported. The reinit probe opened the database directly; against a live worker that raised a lock error, which the surrounding `except` read as "corrupt DB" and fell into a fresh init that failed on the next direct open with the raw lock message. That happens without `--force` too (hit directly earlier today). The CLI now skips the probe when a worker is running (a worker holding the file has already initialized the schema). The MCP `init` had a separate, worse problem: `force` rebuilt the schema and wiped the data; that, and the stop-the-worker step this entry first added for it, are superseded by Task 32.1. Tests cover the CLI side, and the original failure was reproduced with the worker check disabled.
  - [x] 31.6. **Test isolation and timing**: `test_cli_commands.py` now runs every test in a fresh initialized temp project instead of the checkout (with a live worker on the real database its tests went red; killing the worker turned them green with no code change). The fixed `sleep(1.0)` waits and the `< 2.0` second bounds in four worker tests are replaced by a `wait_until` fixture (they failed under load, which showed up as a 21-minute suite run). `tests/conftest.py` still cited the design doc; rewritten.
  - [x] 31.7. **`worker status` as an MCP tool**: `worker_status` added (25 tools), backed by a shared `get_worker_status` that the CLI command now also uses; it queries through the worker only when one is confirmed running, as before.

- [x] 32. Fix the five problems in §34.
  - [x] 32.1. **MCP `init` wiping data**: reproduced the mechanism directly (`init_schema(conn, rebuild=True)` takes a one-row database to zero rows, and that was the exact call the tool made when `force` was set). The CLI never rebuilds a usable database; on an initialized project its `--force` only refreshes the agent templates. The MCP tool now follows the CLI, sharing one validity check (`is_initialized_database`, also used by the CLI): a usable project is never opened or rebuilt, `force` overwrites templates only, and `confidence_threshold` is ignored there (the tool description says so). A database that lacks drspec's schema is still initialized fresh. Because a usable project is never opened directly, the stop-the-worker step from Task 31.5 is no longer needed and was removed. The test that asserted the rebuild was replaced by tests that data survives `force`, templates are refreshed, the threshold is untouched, and a worker-held database is never opened.
  - [x] 32.2. **Wrong labels before the worker polls**: a file counted as stale only once the worker's poll had queued it, so until then hits were labelled from the old line ranges. Line mode now also compares each hit file's current size and mtime with its scan snapshot (`scanned_file_snapshots`), the same test the worker uses. A drifted file's hits are unlabelled and the file is listed in `possibly_stale_files`. The staleness flag on plain function matches (`search`, `deps_get`, `unused`) still depends on the worker's poll; that is unchanged.
  - [x] 32.3. **Unscanned files**: line mode now searches the supported source files on disk under the requested scope (the scanner's own ignore rules and language map), plus any file the database still lists, rather than only files that have functions in the database. A file added since the last scan is searched and reported under `unscanned_files` (count, up to 10 examples, a hint to run `drspec scan`); its hits are unlabelled. This also reaches scanned files that hold no functions (a constants-only module), which §31 had named as out of reach. A search no longer returns zero hits for text that is literally in a file. Cost: each call walks the scope on disk as well as reading the files.
  - [x] 32.4. **`learn` printing twice**: `history`, `stats` and `analyze` each hand-printed a human block under `if not json_output` and then also called the shared renderer, which since §30 prints the same data. The hand-printed blocks are removed. The one thing only the hand-printed text carried, which commit a result came from, now lives in the data: each `learn analyze` result has `commit` and `commit_message` (CLI and MCP). The tests count occurrences case-insensitively, since the old text differed from the renderer's only in capitalization.
  - [x] 32.5. **Unbounded `scan` output**: the per-function `resolution_reasons` and `contracts_invalidated` maps are now `{total, by_reason, examples}` with at most 20 examples, and the parse-error list is capped at 20 with `error_count` and `errors_omitted`. Both the CLI and the MCP tool get this from `publish_scan_results`. A scan of this project's own `src/` (668 functions) prints 51 lines instead of thousands. The three existing assertions that indexed into the old maps now read `examples`.

A worker at high lifetime-average CPU turned out to be an idle worker after a large catch-up pass, not a runaway; the slow test run was machine load (load average above 30 from other projects).

- [x] 33. Fix the findings in §35.
  - [x] 33.1. **Line numbers**: reproduced (`grep -n` says line 3 for `needle` in a form-feed file and a U+2028 file; line mode said 4). `live_search` now splits on `\n` only, drops the final empty element, and strips a trailing `\r`, so numbers match `grep -n` and editors, and match the function ranges used for labelling (the parser counts newlines only, so the old splitting could also mislabel). Covered for form feed, vertical tab, the C0 separators, next-line, U+2028/U+2029, CRLF, no final newline, an empty file, and trailing blank lines. An end-to-end test puts a form feed before a function and checks both the line and its label.
  - [x] 33.2. **Long lines**: reproduced (a 150,000-element minified file gave a 300,013-character hit). A line over 300 characters is now clipped to a 300-character window placed around the first match, with an ellipsis at each cut end, and the hit carries `line_length` (absent for short lines). Context lines are clipped the same way (a non-matching long line shows its start). The same file now yields a response under 1 KB with the match visible.
  - [x] 33.3. **`init.py`**: `check_existing_files` and `prompt_overwrite_confirmation` deleted: no references anywhere (`init_command` does its own inline check). The report that the overwrite confirmation no longer exists is mistaken: driving the real CLI through a pseudo-terminal in an initialized project shows "Overwrite existing agent files? (y/N)" and honors the answer; the deleted function printed a similar message, which is likely the source of the impression. What was actually inaccurate was "Force re-initialization": `--force` never touches the database of a usable project (Task 32.1). The help now reads "On an already-initialized project, overwrite the agent templates without asking. Never clears the database." A CLI test checks that `--force` keeps the data, matching the MCP one.
  - [x] 33.4. **Flaky timing bounds**: the reported timeout was the two-commands-at-once test in `test_readonly_connections.py` (`communicate(timeout=10)`); the same file's other test asserted `elapsed < 2.0` after a fixed 4 s holder sleep. Neither bound is the behavior under test (the return codes are), and a lock conflict fails immediately rather than blocking, so the holder now waits on a marker file instead of sleeping, and the wall-clock assertion is dropped. Every other 5-20 s bound on "did it hang" in the tests (subprocess waits, thread joins, localhost requests, the 15-process singleton test) was raised to 30-90 s. Parameters that are the thing being tested (`idle_timeout`, `stop_worker(timeout=0.2)`, the short verification timeout) were left alone.

  - [x] 33.6. **Leaked staging folders** (reviewer finding after §35): a publish killed part-way left a `.drspec-scan-*` folder (a full copy of the database) that nothing deleted; this project's `_drspec` held four. Staging folders are now named `.drspec-scan-<pid>-<random>`, and `sweep_orphaned_staging` removes those whose owner process is gone, plus old-style folders (no PID in the name) older than an hour. It runs at the start of every publish and when the worker starts. A folder whose owner is still running is never touched (one of the four here was a live publish). Covered by `tests/unit/test_staging.py`. The four existing folders in this repo's `_drspec` are left for the next publish or worker start by the new code to remove; they were not deleted by hand.

- [x] 34. Build D2–D5 of §36: a bounded background maintenance worker, new-file discovery, search-driven re-scan queueing, and background embedding.
  - [x] 34.1. **Settings**: `core/settings.py` registry (validated, stored in `config` under `setting:`), read by the worker on every pass so a change applies without a restart. `drspec config get|set|reset` and MCP `config_get`/`config_set` (27 tools before D1 removed four). Keys: `worker.poll_interval` (60 s), `worker.rescan_budget` (10), `worker.discovery_budget` (2000), `worker.wake_min_gap` (5 s), `embedding.enabled` (on), `embedding.top_n` (2000), `embedding.half_life_days` (7), `embedding.cpu_percent` (25), `embedding.threads` (1), `embedding.max_load_per_core` (1.0), `embedding.min_available_memory_mb` (1500), `usage.search_weight` (1), `usage.lookup_weight` (5).
  - [x] 34.2. **Bounded pass (D2)**: a stat sweep queues drifted files; at most `worker.rescan_budget` files are re-scanned per pass, ordered search-queued, then drift, then new files (reason classes in the queue). A file that fails to parse goes into `rescan_backoff` (1, 2, 4 … up to 15 minutes) and does not block others; editing it clears the backoff. The worker sleeps on an event and any external client request wakes it (at least `worker.wake_min_gap` between passes); the worker's own connection and the embedding process are marked internal so they do not wake it.
  - [x] 34.3. **Stall finding**: the cause was `refresh_contexts` rewriting every context fingerprint on every publish, not parsing (see "Resolved while building" in §36). Fixed by writing only changed rows in batches, and by a shared `set_config_many` also used for the per-file scan snapshots (adding a function on a 2,400-file project went from 5.5 s to 3.5 s; the rest is the existing re-resolution of all callers, documented as a known limit).
  - [x] 34.4. **New-file discovery (D3)**: `core/discovery.py` walks the project root (the parent of a `_drspec` folder; off for a `--db` elsewhere) with the scanner's own rules, in sorted order with a saved cursor, at most `worker.discovery_budget` files per pass, queueing unscanned supported files with reason `new_file`.
  - [x] 34.5. **Search queues what it finds (D4)**: function-mode search checks the files of its own hits (marking `possibly_stale` in the same result); line mode queues its drifted and unscanned files (reasons `search_drift`, `search_unscanned`). The write is one best-effort batch of at most 20 files, errors swallowed, skipped on a read-only connection. `source_get` (CLI and MCP) gained `possibly_stale` and the same check; `deps_get`, `unused` already had it.
  - [x] 34.6. **Usage (D5)**: search hits, `source_get` and `deps_get` (the function, its neighbours and the direct edges) add to `usage_stats.pending` in the same best-effort style; the worker folds pending into a score decayed with a 7-day half-life. `status` and `worker status` show a `backlog` section: files waiting for re-scan, files waiting for discovery, files in retry wait, and embedding progress (embedded/total, how many are owed, state, one-line message).
  - [x] 34.7. **Background embedding (D5)**: `core/embedding_backlog.py` decides what is owed (previously-embedded items that are not up to date for the current model and source hash, then the top `embedding.top_n` by decayed usage); `core/embedding_runner.py` is the throttled process (`drspec worker embed`: nice 19, one thread via fastembed's `threads=`, a duty cycle for the CPU cap, pauses for load and low memory, model loaded once, 20 items a chunk, exits when nothing is owed); `embedding_supervisor.py` runs in the worker, folds usage, starts at most one process, and retries after a growing wait when the model cannot be loaded or the process fails or makes no progress. First use downloads the model inside the process; `status` says so while it happens and reports a failed download. Output goes to `_drspec/embedding.log`. `embedding.enabled false` stops all of it, including the download.
  - [x] 34.8. **Acceptance tests** (all run for real, against a project with a real worker): 1 `test_no_request_stalls_while_hundreds_of_files_are_rewritten` (300 rewritten files, worst wait about 1 s, bound 1.5 s; the test's completion check was also fixed, it previously could never finish); 2 new-file discovery; 3 a search queues an edited file and the worker fixes it within seconds; 4 `TestBackgroundEmbeddingEndToEnd` (`scan --embed`, edit an embedded function, no manual scan, the function is re-embedded and `search --semantic` ranks it first; usage ranking and decay in `test_embedding_backlog.py`); 5 an unparseable file does not block others and is retried on a backoff; 6 a dormant worker re-scans a search-queued file within seconds; 7 with an empty backlog no embedding process exists and an idle worker used under 1 s of CPU in 6 s. 35 unit tests cover the backlog rules, the usage decay, the status messages, the runner (chunks, pauses, duty cycle, failed model load) and the supervisor (backoff, no respawn without progress).
