- [x] 36. Optional trace of what drspec does while it runs (§37).
  - [x] 36.1. **Core**: `core/tracing.py`. JSON lines in `_drspec/trace.log`; levels `off`/`slow`/`all`; a module-level level checked per event (off costs 0.19 µs per event call and creates no file); one open-append-close per event (so a `trace clear` or a rotation by another process is never written around); rotation to `trace.log.1` when the next line would pass `trace.max_mb`; the first failed write stops further attempts in that process. A correlation id (`cid`) is thread-local with a per-process default.
  - [x] 36.2. **Settings** `trace.level` (a new string kind with a fixed set of words), `trace.slow_ms` (500), `trace.max_mb` (20). The worker and the embedding process apply them on every pass and every chunk; both ignore `DRSPEC_TRACE`, because they keep the environment of whichever command started them.
  - [x] 36.3. **`command` events** (CLI and MCP): command path, option names only, status, output size, `connect_ms`, whether the call started the worker, and the client's total round-trip time (`ipc_ms`, `ipc_calls`). The search pattern, cut to 80 characters, only at `all`. A command follows the worker's level: the worker attaches a small `trace` report to its replies while tracing is on, and a client with no flag or variable adopts it (an older worker sends none, so the client stays off).
  - [x] 36.4. **Worker events**: `request` (operation, SQL prefix with quoted values masked, `lock_ms`, `run_ms`, rows), `pass` (detect, discover, re-scan ms, queue and batch sizes, settings in force), `rescan` (`parse` per file; `publish` split into checkpoint, copy, sync, commit, fsync, replace; `reconnect`), `embed` (per chunk and per pause: round, items, embed/store ms, sleep, items still owed, the first three item keys), `supervisor` (start, exit with code, backoff with its reason). Every client IPC request carries the command's `cid`; the worker uses it for the events of that request, and gives each of its own passes and each embedding chunk a fresh one.
  - [x] 36.5. **Reading**: `drspec trace summary [--since] [--slowest] [--cid]`, `trace tail`, `trace clear`, and the MCP tool `trace_summary`; both call one function, so the numbers are the same. Reading never opens the database or starts the worker.
  - [x] 36.6. **Differences from §37 as written**: (a) `--trace` is a plain flag meaning `all`, with `--trace-level off|slow|all` for the other levels, not `--trace[=slow|all]`: Click gives an option with an optional value the next word on the line, so `drspec --trace search x` took `search` as the level (checked on the installed Click 8.1.8). (b) The `supervisor` events that have no duration (start, backoff) have `ms` 0, so they are not written at `slow`, which keeps the rule "only events at or above `trace.slow_ms`" without exceptions. (c) The command name is read from `sys.argv`: Click empties the context's own argument list before a group callback runs.
  - [x] 36.7. **Acceptance** (1–2 and 6 on real processes in a scratch project of 300 files; the rest in `tests/unit/test_tracing.py`, 65 tests): 1 off by default: no file after a scan, a search and a worker pass on real processes (the unit test also runs `status`). 2 with `all`, one `status` during a re-scan took 2754 ms; its `ipc_ms` was 2685 (1824 requests), the worker's request times added to 2387 (1942 lock wait, 445 run), the rest being socket and JSON time. 3 a fake whose store reports success while the item stays owed shows the same item in five consecutive `embed` chunk events (simulated: the real DuckDB array-column bug was still in the tree when this was written, and §39 / Task 38 is its fix; with that guard in place the runner now stops after the first such round, and the test asserts that instead). 4 `slow` writes only events at or above the threshold. 5 with `trace.max_mb` 0.01, the file never passed 10 KB and only one older file is kept. 6 `trace summary` and `trace_summary` return equal data; `--cid` of a real `search` returned its `command` event and every worker request of it. 7 a missing directory, a directory in place of the file and a full disk (simulated by a failing write) raise nothing and stop retrying; a command still succeeds. 8 a client talking to a strict old-protocol server that ignores `cid` and sends no report works and stays off. No older build of the worker exists to run (the worker IPC is not in git history), so this one is covered by that stand-in only.
  - [x] 36.8. **Overhead**: 21.6 µs per event written, 0.19 µs per call off. The most event-heavy command measured, `status` (1824 requests), would add about 40 ms to 2.7 s, about 1.5%; a re-scan of the 300 rewritten files emits a few dozen events. Wall time of the scan itself (three runs each, off then `all`: 5.1/3.6/3.9 s against 3.4/3.9/3.0 s) varied more than any difference between them, so the bound comes from the per-event cost, not from that comparison.
  - [x] 36.9. **What the trace showed on its first real use** (not fixed here): (a) `drspec status` sends one `SELECT value FROM config` per function for the verification evidence (1800 requests for 1800 functions), which is most of its 1.5–4 s (fixed in Task 39). (b) In every publish of 50 body-only edits, `sync_ms` was 3.2–4.6 s while parsing was 0.5 ms per file and checkpoint, copy, fsync and replace together were about 0.3 s: the cost is in `sync_scan_results` inside the lock, the "known limit" already noted in §36, now measured per publish.

The scan publish calls it in the same transaction as the sync for every file it scanned except the ones it could not read (`protected_file_paths`, which stay queued), and the worker's own re-scan now calls the same function instead of its own three loops. Sampled every 100 ms over a real run (100 functions, 34 files rewritten during it): at most 2 threads (12 to 39 before) and a peak of 100% of one core during model load and 110% after it, mean 14%. I did not size the CPU cap by sampling peak: with every pool capped, the peak is bounded by `embedding.threads` cores by construction, and the sample above agrees.

- [x] 38. Embeddings stored as lists, verified, existing databases rebuilt (§39).
  - [x] 38.1. **Schema and search**: `embedding FLOAT[]` in the three tables; the semantic search uses `list_cosine_similarity(embedding, ?::FLOAT[])`. `_fk_references_detached` is kept (lists are rewritten as delete plus insert too). The corruption regression runs the §39 scenario on `FLOAT[]` (passes) and on `FLOAT[384]` (marked as an expected failure, so a DuckDB release that fixes it does not break the suite and the test documents why the column is a list), and once through the real store path across four sessions.
  - [x] 38.2. **Length**: `check_embedding_length` (one `EMBEDDING_DIM`, from `embedder.py`) is called by every embedding writer in `queries.py` (artifact, dependency and reference upserts and hash updates), by `store_embeddings` (before any write: a bad vector stores nothing from its batch), by the `scan --embed` backfill, and for the query vector of the semantic search. A wrong length is a `ValueError`.
  - [x] 38.3. **Read-back**: after every store the same keys are read with the full-scan query `find_backlog` uses, and any that do not read back raise `VectorNotPersisted` (over IPC, the reply names them). The embedding process then writes the state `error: vector did not persist`, prints a line to `embedding.log`, emits an `embed` trace event of kind `not_persisted`, and exits with code 4; the supervisor treats that like a failed run (growing wait) without overwriting the message. The runner also stops when a round stored vectors but none of the items it embedded left the backlog, which is what looped for a minute before; the earlier trace test that staged that loop now asserts it is cut short after one round.
  - [x] 38.4. **Rebuild** (`db/migration.py`): at worker start (before the file is opened) and in `drspec init` on an existing project (skipped with `--force`, and when a worker is running), `ensure_embedding_layout` looks at the `schema:embedding_layout` marker, then at the column types; an old layout is rebuilt into a file in a `.drspec-scan-<pid>-` folder from a copy of the old one: current schema, then every table of the old database (tables created on demand, such as the learning history, from their old definition, sequences and indexes with them) except the vectors, in foreign-key order, row counts compared per table, sequences moved to the value the old database would return next (a loaded database reports its next value as `last_value` and a live one its last, so the value is read with `nextval` on the disposable copy), `config` copied whole (so the `embedding:` rows make the backlog owe exactly the items that had a vector), marker written, file synced, old file copied to `contracts.db.before-list-embeddings`, atomic rename. The backup is removed by the next successful start. Any failure leaves the old file as it was; the worker then does not start, records the reason in `_drspec/worker_startup_error.txt` (shown by `worker status` and by the connection error of every command), and `worker run` prints it and exits 1. Tried on a copy of this repository's database (2,614 functions): 1.2 s, row counts identical except the one marker row, sequences identical, 67 `embedding:` rows kept, vectors empty, backup identical to the original.

The two `FLOAT[384]` assertions in `test_db_connection.py` now read `FLOAT[]`.

- [x] 39. Review of the trace and what it found (§40).
  - [x] 39.1. **Rotation (finding 1)**: `_write` set the descriptor to `None` after the first `close`, so the `finally` can no longer close it twice (and so can no longer close another thread's descriptor that reused the number). Rotation is serialised across processes with `fcntl.flock` on `trace.log.lock`, the size is checked again once the lock is held, and a `FileNotFoundError` from the rename counts as "someone else rotated". One failed write no longer ends tracing in that process for good: it pauses writes for 5 s, doubling to 300 s on repeated failures, and the first success resets it. Six real processes appending to one 50 KB log (started together so they contend): all six end with no failure, every line whole, each file at most the limit plus one line per writer (a writer that read a size just under the limit can still append one more line, so with six writers a file can pass it by up to six lines; the single-process bound is still one line).
  - [x] 39.2. **Publish cost (finding 2)**. The profile named three separate causes, none of them what §40 guessed (per-edge work inside `sync_scan_results` was one of three, and not the biggest on real code):
    1. *Per-edge `UPDATE`s*: one statement per incoming edge to refresh its hash (3.4–15 ms each). Now one `UPDATE … FROM (VALUES …)` per 500 edges (`set_edge_hashes`), with the callers' hashes read in one query: 120 edges 1,757 ms → 25 ms.
    2. *The foreign-key workaround on every changed function*: it moved every referencing row through Python and committed twice. An `artifacts` UPDATE needs it only when it writes an indexed column (`file_path`, `function_name`, `status`) or the vector, because DuckDB rewrites those as a delete and an insert; a changed function usually writes none of them (measured: the plain UPDATE on a row with 120 incoming edges works). `insert_artifact` now writes only the columns that changed and uses the workaround only when one of those four is among them; the workaround itself parks the rows in temporary tables and puts them back with one `INSERT … SELECT` per table (825 ms → 40 ms for a 120-edge hub, every row back exactly, also when the block raises).
    3. *Import bindings recomputed per function* (the big one on real code): `bind_declared_types` looked up the file's imports (a `LIKE '%/stem.%'` scan of every artifact row per import) once per function, and did so for functions with no annotated parameters, whose answer is always empty. A symbol-set change re-resolves every function in the project, so this was 93,910 queries, **100 of 112 seconds**. Now it returns at once without annotations, and a file's bindings and each module guess are worked out once per sync.
    Then the re-resolution itself: after a symbol-set change, functions in files the scan did not read are re-resolved only if they could resolve differently — they call, read, annotate or import a name that was added, removed, moved or changed its class or property status, or import from a stem of a file that appeared or disappeared (an ambiguity). `--force`, a resolver change, and the first scan still re-resolve everything. 14 equivalence tests (`tests/unit/test_incremental_resolution.py`) run each kind of edit twice from the same state, an ordinary sync and a forced one, and require identical `dependencies`, `code_references` and `artifacts`; with the filter deliberately broken, 7 of them fail.
    Measured: synthetic project (610 functions, 1,200 edges), 10 hubs edited: 9.7 s → 0.55 s (0.25 s without the one-time pandas import of a fresh process); a caller with 10 edges 193 ms; a leaf 158 ms; 10 hubs that have vectors (cleared through the workaround) 789 ms with every edge intact. **This repository's own database, five hub-heavy files edited with new functions added: 112 s → 2.6–3.6 s, and the resulting edge tables (3,224 calls, 9 references) are identical to a forced full re-resolve.** Statement-count tests (`test_publish_cost.py`) keep each mechanism from coming back.
    *Not done*: serving reads while a publish runs (§40 suggested fix 3). The remaining 2.6–3.6 s of the real case is spread thin (copying and checkpointing the 50 MB database about 0.6 s, refreshing contexts 0.6 s, snapshot rewrite 0.4 s, edges 0.9 s), and the live connection cannot take writes while the private copy is being built (they would be lost at the swap), so letting readers through needs a read-only/write distinction in the worker that this did not justify. A `status` call during that publish still waits for it.
  - [x] 39.3. **`status` (finding 3)**: the evidence is read with one query (`starts_with(key, 'verification:')`) and a contract is looked up only for a function that has evidence. A test counts the requests (under 40 for 80 functions) and another pins the evidence counts.
  - [x] 39.4. **`trace summary` (finding 4)**: the reader streams. Per event name only the durations are kept (a compact array); of the events, a bounded heap of the slowest and, for `--cid`, the matching ones; `tail` keeps a deque. A synthetic 125 MB log of 497,476 events: 3.8 s, 56 MB peak for the whole process (966 MB before; most of the 56 is the interpreter and imports). Tests check the peak with `tracemalloc` and the numbers against a plain computation.
  - [x] 39.5. **Memory and CPU (finding 5)**: `command` events carry `max_rss_mb` and `cpu_s` (so waiting can be told from computing); `pass`, `rescan publish` and `embed` chunk/start events carry `rss_mb`, `db_mb`, `wal_mb`, `load1` and `mem_avail_mb`; the embedding process's `exit` event carries its peak memory and CPU seconds. A field that cannot be read is left out, not written as 0.
  - [x] 39.6. **A `trace.*` setting applies at once (finding 6)**: when a write to `setting:trace.*` goes through the worker it applies the new level immediately, and the reply to that very request already carries it, so the `config set` command itself is traced. Other settings are untouched.
  - [x] 39.7. **The wrong note (finding 7)**: corrected in 36.7 above.
  - [x] 39.8. **Found on the way, caused by this round's own startup check, fixed**: the full suite caught `worker stop` failing when it arrives while the worker is starting. The worker's first parameterised query runs `import pandas` inside DuckDB (when pandas is installed), and DuckDB drops every exception raised during that import, including the `SystemExit` the `SIGTERM` handler raised, so the stop was lost and the worker carried on for good. The handler now also sets a flag the worker loop checks at every step, so a swallowed exception no longer matters; a test simulates the swallow.
  - [x] 39.9. **Full suite**: 1905 passed, 20 deselected, 1 expected failure.

- [x] 40. The grep-rewriting hook and the notice for a search of nothing (§41).
  - [x] 40.1. **The hook rewrites only what drspec can answer** (`.claude/hooks/prefer_drspec.py`): the path must exist inside the project and be a file with an indexed extension (the scanner's list, kept in step by a test) or a directory searched with `-r`; a file of any other type, a missing or outside path, a directory without `-r` and a bare `grep pattern` (which reads standard input) get the plain nudge. An absolute path inside the project is made relative; a directory becomes `dir/` so a sibling with the same prefix is not matched.
  - [x] 40.2. **The rewrite is line-oriented**: every translation is `drspec search PATTERN --lines …`; `-A`, `-B`, `-C` (separate, attached, `--after-context=N`) and `-NUM` become `--after-context`, `--before-context`, `--context`; a non-numeric value is not translated. `-c`, `-l`, `-v` (and `-L`, `-o`, `-w`) are no longer translated at all: drspec's counts and file lists are per function (so they miss module-level lines) and its inversion is per function, so a translation would answer a different question. This removes two translations §21 had.
  - [x] 40.3. **`drspec search` says when it searched nothing**: function, semantic, count and file modes (CLI and MCP) now report `files_searched`, and when it is 0 a `note` says that no indexed file matches the path (or language filter, or project) and that docs, config and data files are out of reach. `--lines` mode already did.
  - [x] 40.4. **Tested against real output** (`TestAgainstDrspecsRealOutput`): in a scratch project (scanned for real) each rewritten command is run through the CLI and its matched lines compared with the lines real `grep -n` prints for the same command: a module-level match, a directory search, `-i`, `-E`, and `-C 1` (including the context lines). A `grep -c` on a `pyproject.toml` that real grep answers with 1 is shown not to be rewritten.
  - [x] 40.5. **Acceptance** (through the real hook script with this repository as the working directory): `grep -n pandas src/drspec/db/connection.py` → `drspec search pandas --lines --path src/drspec/db/connection.py`; the reviewer's `-n -i -E` grep on `pyproject.toml` (absolute path) and `grep -c dependencies pyproject.toml` → not rewritten; `drspec search pandas --path notes.toml` → `Files Searched: 0` and "Nothing was searched".

- [x] 41. Review of Tasks 38-40 (§42).
  - [x] 41.1. **A worker from before an upgrade is noticed and replaced** (`core/build_id.py`, `worker_ipc.py`): a build identifier (package version, a layout number, and a fingerprint of the package's source files, so an editable install with an unchanged version is told apart) is reported by the worker in the `hello` reply. A client that connects compares it with its own and, when they differ, stops the worker and starts the current one before sending its request; once per process and database, so two installs sharing a project do not keep replacing each other's worker. A worker too old to report a build counts as outdated. `drspec worker status` (and the MCP tool) reports `build`, `current_build`, `outdated` and a note, and never acts on it. `drspec init` no longer relies on "a worker is running": an outdated one is stopped, then the rebuilds run. Not fixable from here: an `mcp-serve` process started before the upgrade runs old code (its semantic search binds `FLOAT[384]`) until the editor reloads it.
  - [x] 41.2. **Memory and CPU are shown**: `trace tail` and `trace summary --slowest` print `max_rss_mb`, `cpu_s`, `rss_mb`, `db_mb`, `wal_mb`, `load1` and `mem_avail_mb`; `trace summary` adds `peak_rss_mb`, `median_rss_mb` and `total_cpu_s` to every event name that has them (and the MCP `trace_summary` equally).
  - [x] 41.3. **The hook puts a dash-leading pattern after `--`**: `grep -n -- -x f.py` becomes `drspec search --lines --path f.py -- -x`, which runs and prints the same line as grep (compared against real grep).
  - [x] 41.4. **A killed rebuild no longer leaves a copy of the database until later**: the worker sweeps orphaned staging folders again after `ensure_*_layout`.
  - [x] 41.5. **Side observation**: a project path too long for a Unix socket (about 100 bytes) puts the socket in the temporary directory under `drspec-<hash of the database path>.sock` (every process derives the same name); if that is too long as well, the error says so and names the way out.
  - [x] 41.6. **Tests**: `tests/unit/test_worker_build.py` (19: the identifier changes with the source; real separate worker processes, one reporting an older build, are recognised, reported, replaced by a command, left alone by `status`, replaced only once per process; `init` stops an outdated worker and a current one is left alone; the sweep fails without its second call; the report fields; deeply nested projects with a real worker).
- [x] 42. Carry a contract and its history over when a function is renamed or its signature changes inside the same file (§43).
  - [x] 42.1. **Pairing** (`core/carry_over.py`, called from `sync_scan_results` before the removal and after the new nodes are written, inside the publish): per scanned file, the stored functions missing from the fresh parse are paired with the fresh ones not stored yet. Same qualified name with other parameters: one-to-one when exactly one on each side, otherwise by the closest body when strictly best for both sides. Otherwise a rename: Dice overlap of the body tokens (the function's own name made anonymous) of at least 0.6 and better than every other candidate of either function by 0.1. A tie or an unclear choice pairs nothing. Different files are never paired and nothing is remembered between scans.
  - [x] 42.2. **What moves**: the contract (with its source, context and verification records), reasoning traces, vision findings, learning history and the usage score are written under the new identifier; the new function is `STALE` with reason `SIGNATURE_CHANGED` or `RENAMED`; the contract's confidence becomes `min(old, threshold - 1)`. The record (`carryover:<new id>`: from, reason, original and lowered confidence, similarity, time) is shown by `contract get` / `contract_get` as `carried_over`, appended to the traces as an `agent = scan` row, and the scan reports `carried_over`. Edges and vectors are not moved; the normal sync rebuilds them.
  - [x] 42.2a. **Review fixes**: the overlap now counts only the meaningful words of the body (identifiers, names, numbers), not the signature, the keywords and the punctuation every function has, and a rename needs at least three such words on both sides (a rewrite from arithmetic to string joining that scored 0.609 now scores 0); a function carried a second time before anyone re-checked it keeps the confidence it had before the first move (`original_confidence`, with `earlier` listing the previous identifiers), and saving a contract again clears the record.
  - [x] 42.3. **Tests** (`tests/unit/test_carry_over.py`, 19): pure rename, rename with edited lines, rewrite (not carried), a signature change, defaults changing nothing, move to another file in one pass and in two (not carried), delete-then-add later, two equally good candidates and two replaced twins (not paired), an untouched twin not blocking a rename, the thresholds, a failure in the middle of a publish leaving the database as it was.

The tokens are read from the syntax tree by `parsers/parameters.py` and exposed as `ExtractedFunction.parameter_tokens`.

Four of the eight definitions in the reviewer's C++ sample were not indexed before; all are now.

A call that can reach several definitions of one name gets one `AMBIGUOUS` edge to each (same-file free functions only when they share a qualified name; different qualified names stay unresolved); one definition is `EXTRACTED` as before. A plain scan now also diffs the `AMBIGUOUS` edges of such overloads (others from `--deep-scan` are still left alone). Not done: choosing by argument count, which the call-site record does not carry.

The seven places that took the last `::` segment now use `display_name`; `learning/diff.py` builds identifiers with `function_identity` and credits by qualified name.

Vectors are copied. Row counts, references and the fingerprint are checked; a failure leaves the old file untouched (backup kept to the next start) and the worker refuses to start with the reason. A database that is half old and half new works; two rows that would get one identifier abort with both named. On a copy of this repository's database (2,859 functions, 3,388 edges) it took 2.5–4.2 s, every table's count was equal, and all but five identifiers equalled the fresh parse of the current files; those five are in files edited since the database was made, and none of them has a body identical to a fresh function of the same name.

- [x] 46. Drain the re-scan queue (§47).
  - [x] 46.1. **Two rhythms**: detection (the stat sweep and the walk for new files) runs every `worker.poll_interval` (dormant interval after 120 s idle, and right after a client request woke an idle worker, as before); the queue is worked off back to back, with the pause the limits ask for: the machine busy or short of memory (5 s), the worker's CPU over wall time and cores above `worker.cpu_percent` (20), otherwise `worker.yield_ms` (50). A batch ends after `worker.rescan_budget` files (default 200) or `worker.pass_seconds` (1 s) of parsing, at least one file. The `pass` event gains `queued_after`, `gap_ms`, `pause_reason`. 150 new files after `init` were all indexed within a minute by real processes with the default 60 s interval (before: ten files a minute).
  - [x] 46.2. **Order**: what a search or a lookup asked for, then drift, then new files modified within `worker.recent_days` (14), then the other new files, then new documents; within a tier by path (not newest first). `source get`, `deps get` and the other function commands queue the file of a function that is not indexed yet, first.

Not done: `nice` 19 for the drain (the worker's request threads share its process, so it would slow answers under load); `contract` commands do not carry the note.
- [x] 47. Index what parsed, keep what is stored (§48): a file read only in part has its cleanly parsed functions indexed, nothing stored for it is removed, it is settled (not retried), and `scan` and `status` say how many files and functions (`files_partially_parsed`, `functions_skipped`, examples with the first error line). Harmless constructs (`template<> … = default;` and `= delete;`) are blanked out **before** parsing, so what follows them is no longer folded into the error, and counted as `skipped_constructs`. Decorator macros (export macros by a default pattern, plus the names listed one per line in `_drspec/cpp_macros.txt`) are blanked the same way, so `EXPORT int f()` and `class FOO_API Widget {…}` are indexed; what an error still swallows is reported as skipped definitions by line (`suspected_losses`).
- [x] 48. One embedding-model cache per user (§49): `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/drspec/models` (`~/.cache` otherwise; `~/Library/Caches` on macOS; `%LOCALAPPDATA%` on Windows), `DRSPEC_MODEL_DIR` overrides. A project-local model is adopted once (hard links, whole-or-nothing rename) and `worker status` says the old folder can be deleted; an unusable shared folder falls back to the project's own and says so; one download at a time per folder (`flock`); a snapshot counts as cached only when every file of it resolves.

Anything a crash leaves is finished by the next call. `status` shows `removals_pending`.

- [x] 51. Documents (§52): Markdown files are read by a deterministic parser (`core/doc_parser.py`) into sections (tree with paths and neighbours, front matter, fenced blocks, tables, links, keywords by TF-IDF against the stored sections, important lines by a fixed rule list) in `doc_sections`, `doc_links`, `doc_file_links` (no foreign keys). They go through the same detector and queue (after the code files) and the same transaction; `drspec scan` indexes them too. Sections are linked to code in the tiers of the proposal (`id`, `qualified`, `path+name`, `name`, `fence-call`, `fuzzy`, ambiguous, file links); links store the hash and signature at link time and become `drifted` (`body_changed` / `signature_changed`), follow a rename, or are kept as `removed`; editing a section clears its drifted links and records it; a new function re-links only the sections that name it. Commands `docs stale|for|search|links|ack|undocumented`, MCP tools `docs_stale`, `docs_for`, `docs_search`, `search --docs`, `documented_in` in `source get` and `deps get`, a note after `scan`, document counts in `status`; ten settings under `docs.*`. Not done: the embeddings tier, `near` links, `.rst`/`.txt`; keywords use the stored sections for document frequency rather than a separate table.

Bindings: named, default, namespace and aliased imports, `import type`, `require` (plain, destructured, `.name`), `await import("literal")`. On the fixture project (8 files) the call edges went from 5 to 25; `run` reaches `add`, `mul`, `main`, `plus`→`add`, `Calc` (constructor), `Calc.twice`, `Calc.of` and `legacy`; the unresolved share is below 25%.

Not nodes: interface and abstract member signatures (so a call through an interface lands on the implementers, not on `Shape.area`), enum members, class static blocks (their calls are not attributed to anything). A change in a file's facts changes the symbol fingerprint (a per-file digest), so callers in files that were not scanned are re-resolved. Files read by an older parser are queued once at the next worker start (`core/facts_version.py`). The contracts of JavaScript and TypeScript functions whose calls now resolve are marked stale once, as always when a function's dependencies change.

- [x] 54. One pipeline for code and documents (§55). Delivered in three stages.
  - [x] 54.1. **Stage 1, the unchanged scan.** `core/project_files.py` lists the project's files once (git-aware, see 55.3); `core/changes.py` (`detect_changes`) compares them with the stored size and modification time and returns added, changed and removed files for code and documents alike; the manual scan and the worker's discovery use the same list and the same stat rule. `ingest_document` takes a keyword table (`DocumentFrequency`) built once per publish and updated as documents are replaced, so it no longer costs documents × sections; `publish_documents` is the one function that ingests and removes documents, called by the worker's batch. A test shows the stored sections after a publish of six documents equal those of six separate ingests. Measured on a 223-file copy of this repository (3,618 functions, 14 documents): the unchanged scan takes 1.7 s, reads nothing and ingests no document (it took 72-88 s on the repository itself before).
  - [x] 54.2. **Stage 2, `scan` asks the worker and waits.** `core/scan_service.py`: `start_scan` finds what changed (a target is a path, a function ID with or without its hash, or a section ID; a directory means everything under it, `--no-recursive` only its children), queues the files in a new top tier (`scan`, above what a search or lookup queued; a file queued lower is moved up, not queued twice) with the scan's options (`deep`, `embed`, `full`) in a new `scan_requests` table, and records the scan in `config` (`scan:run:<id>`). The worker's ordinary batches (`worker.rescan_budget` files or `worker.pass_seconds` of parsing) read them; the batch publishes by option and adds its results to the scan's record (counts add up, per-reason tables combine). While a scan waits, the pause between batches is only `worker.yield_ms`: neither `worker.cpu_percent` nor the busy-machine pause applies. The command polls `scan_progress` (one updating line on a terminal, nothing when piped) and prints the same summary as before: every old field keeps its name and meaning, and `files_queued`, `files_done`, `files_failed`, `files_cancelled`, `files_unchanged`, `files_skipped_by_gitignore`, `files_removed_as_ignored`, `neighbours_re_resolved`, `up_to_date`, `state` are added. Unchanged targets say "Up to date, nothing to do". `--full` (and `--force`, kept) re-reads everything and re-resolves every call link; `--deep-scan` and `--embed` apply to the files the scan reads (a hint says so when nothing was read); `--no-wait` queues at background priority and returns; Ctrl-C cancels (exit 130): batches already published stay, the rest leave the queue; `--code-only` and `--docs-only` narrow it. A file that cannot be read leaves the scan (reported in `errors`) and goes back to the queue with its retry wait; a batch that cannot be published fails the scan with the reason (exit 1) instead of making it wait. With no worker the command fails with a clear message. The CLI no longer parses files: `publish_scan_results`, `sync_scan` and the client-side parse path are gone; the MCP `scan` tool shares `run_scan` and waits up to 60 s, then answers with the progress and leaves the scan running. Neighbours: after a target is published, callers in files that were not read are re-resolved by the existing symbol invalidation when the symbols they call changed, and document links and contract staleness are updated in the same publish; `neighbours_re_resolved` counts them. In the in-process test mode (`DRSPEC_DISABLE_AUTO_WORKER`) the scan's files are read right away, in the same batches.
  - [x] 54.3. **Stage 3, reads during a publish.** While the worker builds a publish it answers a plain read (a `SELECT` that writes nothing, recognised by words, not substrings) on a cursor of its own from the last committed state, and holds back usage counters (`INSERT INTO usage_stats`) to write them right after; every other write waits as before. Measured while a full re-scan of the 223-file copy was publishing: `search`, `status`, `deps get` and `source get` took 1.8-2.9 s, the same as with the worker idle (they took 6-17 s before; the process start dominates on this machine at load 17-23, so the 1.5 s target is not reachable here and the claim is "as fast as idle"). A test shows a read during a held publish returns the pre-publish rows within 2 s and the new rows afterwards.
  - [x] 54.4. **Tests**: `test_scan_pipeline.py` (37), `test_project_files.py` (18), `test_scan_with_worker.py` (4, real processes: scan waits, `--no-wait`, SIGINT cancel, reads during a publish), the read-during-publish class in `test_worker_ipc.py`. The tests that scanned an unchanged file and expected it to be read again now say `--full`.
  - [x] 54.5. **Parallel parsing (decision 19)** (`core/parse_pool.py`). Measured on 213 Python files at load average 19 on 12 cores: one process 4.9 s, **four threads 4.9 s** (the tree-sitter parser keeps the interpreter lock), four processes 1.8 s plus 0.3 s of start-up; so the pool is **processes**, started with `spawn` (the worker holds a database, sockets and threads that a forked child would inherit half-way). The pool only turns paths into parse results, returned in the order given; the worker still publishes alone, so the stored result is the same with one parser or several (a test compares the artifacts and edges of the same 40 files scanned with `scan.workers` 1 and 4). A waiting scan uses all cores but one (`scan.workers`, 0 = automatic); the background drain uses `round(cores × worker.cpu_percent / 100)` parsers (2 of 12 at the default 20%) and one while the machine is busy; both are held down by free memory (about 250 MB per parser above `embedding.min_available_memory_mb`). The parser processes' CPU time is counted in the cap, so the pauses of the drain are computed over the worker and its parsers together. A batch of fewer than 6 files, a single core, and the in-process test mode parse in the worker itself; a parser process that dies makes the batch fall back to parsing in the worker. The pool is dropped after 60 s without work and when the worker stops (the processes are terminated, and also leave by themselves if the worker is killed outright; checked with `ps` after `worker stop`). The frozen executable calls `multiprocessing.freeze_support()`. **Measured effect: small.** On a scan of 139 files the parse rounds took 2.1 s in total, but the publishes took about 7.5 s per batch (median): parsing was never the large part, publishing is, so the acceptance "a first scan at least 3 times faster than one parser" is **not met** (the first scan took 66 s against 60–93 s before). Parsing itself is about 2.5 times faster with four processes in the benchmark; a faster scan needs the publish to be faster (the §50 target, still not met).
  - **Not done / not verified**: the worker's periodic sweep is still two bounded steps (the stat sweep of known files and the incremental discovery walk) that share the file list and the stat rule with `detect_changes`; it is not literally one function. The crash tests of §50 passed in the suite but were not repeated against a kill in the middle of a multi-batch scan. A scan of a very large repository (tens of thousands of files) was not measured. The publish itself is as slow as before (about 10 s for 40 files on the loaded machine: the 1-second publish of §50 is still not met), so a first scan of the 223-file copy took 60-93 s.
- [x] 55. Review of Tasks 44-53 (§56): the six findings.
  - [x] 55.1. **Documents use the shared commands.** The `docs` group and the `docs_stale`, `docs_for`, `docs_search` tools are gone. `search` returns functions and sections marked `kind` (`--code-only` is the old result: no `kind`, same fields; `--docs-only`; `--status STALE --docs-only` lists the sections whose code changed with their findings; `--undocumented`), `deps get` and `source get` take a section ID (the `#hash` may be left out) or a document path, `scan` has `--code-only`/`--docs-only`, and `drspec ack <section> [function]` (MCP `ack`) replaces `docs ack`. Help text, README, the CLI helper and the skill are updated. `docs stale --min-score` has no shared equivalent and was dropped.
  - [x] 55.2. **A signature change is not a removal.** The pairs carry-over finds (every pair, not only those with a contract to move) are given to `update_link_states`: a function that kept its name and changed its parameters is a `signature_changed` finding with the old and the new signature, a rename is `renamed`; deleting is still `removed`, a body edit still `body_changed`.
  - [x] 55.3. **`.gitignore` is respected** for code and documents: the list comes from `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`, narrowed by the built-in ignores and by the settings `scan.ignore` (folders to skip although tracked) and `scan.include` (folders to read although ignored); without git the built-in list applies. The scan summary says how many files were skipped, a file that became ignored leaves the index at the next scan (`files_removed_as_ignored`), and a folder that cannot be read stops the listing instead of making its files look removed. A file named on the command line is read even if the list leaves it out. On this repository the list is 233 files (code and documents) and 333 ignored ones (the `_bmad` folders among them) are skipped; the document count in the index of the repository itself was not re-measured.
  - [x] 55.4. **Partial parse (C++)**: the first error line is where the first stretch that failed to parse starts (the file-wide error node no longer says line 1), and a stretch that looks like a function head and body is counted as a skipped function (`broken(` on line 3 gives `first_error_line: 3`, `functions_skipped: 1`).
  - [x] 55.5. **After `init`**: while nothing is indexed and files are waiting (or the worker has not yet looked through the project) the note is "Index being built: 0 of N files scanned; try again in a moment."; "use grep" is only for a project with nothing waiting.
  - [x] 55.6. **`new X().m()` (JavaScript, TypeScript)**: the receiver is typed as the class constructed (also parenthesised), the method resolves as an EXTRACTED edge; `new X()` of a class with no constructor is no longer counted unresolved (`implicit constructor`); a class from a package stays unresolved with its reason. `FACTS_VERSION["javascript"]` is 2, so existing files are read once more at the next worker start.
  - [x] 55.8. **Documents are off by default** (`docs.enabled` is `false`; the user's decision after the review). While off, a scan and the worker neither index nor re-link documents (a queued document is taken off the queue and left alone), and `search`, `status`, `deps get`, `source get` and `ack` show no document results or counts and no `kind` marker, as before documents existed; rows stored earlier stay. `search --docs-only`, `--undocumented` and `scan --docs-only` answer with a note saying how to turn them on (`drspec config set docs.enabled true`, then `drspec scan --docs-only`). Existing projects that had documents indexed under the old default see them disappear from the commands until the setting is turned on.
  - [x] 55.9. **The combined search is grouped by file again** (regression of §45): a section list was treated as "ranked" because sections carry a score, and a list with a section was not recognised as records with a file. Functions and sections are now listed under one `File:` header per file with `ID:` as the part after the file (`helper#hash`, `/Guide/Helper/#hash`), the rule line `Function ID: <File>::<ID>` once, `Kind` on every result, and for a section the title, line range, keywords, `Linked To`, `Status` (`CURRENT` or `STALE`) and `Possibly Stale`. JSON stays one flat list.
  - [x] 55.7. **Tests**: `test_shared_documents.py` (43); `test_docs.py`, `test_mcp_server.py`, `test_grouped_output.py` and the prompt-command tests were moved to the shared commands.

- [x] 56. Review of Tasks 54-55 (§57): the publish cost and the test suite.
  - [x] 56.1. **The publish asks the database a number of questions that does not grow with the number of functions.** `_EdgeRows` (`core/graph_sync.py`) reads the `dependencies` and `code_references` rows of a publish's callers and callees in a few queries and is kept in step with every write the publish makes (only the publish writes them while it runs); `_sync_nodes` reads the stored hash and details of all its functions in a few queries and writes a function only when something differs; the import resolution (`_guess_module_file_path`, the per-file and per-package function lookups, the removed-function list) is cached for the length of one publish through the cache `bind_declared_types` already used; facts are turned into dictionaries without `dataclasses.asdict` (a test shows the result equal for 562 functions of Python, TypeScript, C++ and Rust); the per-file snapshot reads and the scanned-file, digest and snapshot writes are one statement per 300-500 files instead of one each. Statements in a forced re-publish: 64 for 10 files (20 functions), 154 for 40 files (80 functions), 3 per file and none per function (a test fails above 2.5 per function and when any question is asked more than twice per file). A first publish still costs about 9 statements per file, mostly one insert per new function. Profile of a forced re-publish of a 121-file copy: `sync_scan_results` 7.0 s → 1.7 s, `_sync_edges` 3.9 s → 0.5 s, the whole `scan --full` 9.0 s → 4.0 s. **Caveat:** the "before" run was at load average 19 and the "after" run at 2.7; parsing, which is one core and not affected by the load, took 1.5 s and 1.6 s, so the comparison is fair for the publish but not exact. The stored result is the same: a test publishes 15 files, edits two functions, publishes again and forces a third publish, once with `_EdgeRows` and once with the per-row queries kept as fallbacks, and compares every table and every count. Not done: the 142 MB database file (the per-file JSON facts in `config`) is a separate question, and the real project's `scan --full` was not re-timed at the same load as the 23.4 s of the report.

- [x] 59. C++ recovery (§59 and §60): a member comparison, silent losses, partial functions.
  - [x] 59.1. **A same-length rewrite for parsing** (`parsers/cpp_parser.py`, `_rewrite_for_parsing`, tried only when the first parse of a file has an error, and kept only when it has fewer errors): the `<` of `x.m < y`, `x->m < y`, `a.b.c < 1` (a `<` after a member name that is not the start of a template argument list: no matching `>` followed by `(` or `::` before the end of the statement) becomes `-`; the type of `va_arg(list, const char*)` becomes `0`; a macro between string literals (`"%" PRId64 "x"`) is blanked. A third attempt reads only the first branch of each `#if`/`#else` (a conditional whose branches open and close braces differently cannot be read otherwise; the directive lines are blanked, the dropped branches are not indexed). Every attempt keeps every position; the stored body, signature and call text come from the prepared source, never from the rewritten copy (`_get_node_text`). Files without an error are never touched. tree-sitter-cpp 0.22.x was **not** tried (installing packages was refused in this environment), so whether the newer grammar reads the comparison is unknown; the rewrite stays either way.
  - [x] 59.2. **Silent losses** (unconditional, in `_prepare`/`_blank_macros`, same length): a macro invocation after the parameter list (`) IM_FMTARGS(2);` and `) IM_FMTARGS(2) {`, not after `if`/`for`/`while`/`switch`/`catch`), a calling-convention or inline macro (`IMGUI_CDECL`, `__forceinline`), an all-caps macro with an underscore in front of `constexpr`/`inline`/`static`/`virtual`/`explicit`/`friend`/`extern` (`IM_NODEBUGSTEP constexpr ImVec2()`), a macro alone on its line (`IM_MSVC_RUNTIME_CHECKS_OFF`), and compiler probes in `#if`/`#elif` (`__has_warning`, `__has_include`, `__has_feature`, `__has_builtin`, `__has_attribute`: the condition becomes `1`, counted as a skipped construct, not an error). **Conversion operators** (`operator int() const`, `operator float*()`) are functions named `operator int`, with `const` as the token that tells overloads apart. **A cross-check that does not depend on the grammar**: a scan of the braces (comments, strings and preprocessor lines blanked) finds every `{` that follows the parameter list of something named outside another function body; a definition that no extracted function covers is reported as "possibly missed" (a `ParseError` of that name, the line in `suspected_losses`), so a file is not `successful` while a function is missing; it is run against the text that was actually read, so a branch dropped on purpose is not reported.
  - [x] 59.3. **Partial functions** (§59 finding 2): a function whose head was read but whose body has an error is kept (name, signature, location, body, hash, the calls that were read) instead of dropped, and listed in `partial:<file>` with its function id. `search` (CLI and MCP) shows `Partial: Yes` on it, `source get` too; the scan summary counts `functions_partial` separately from `functions_skipped` (a stretch that could not even be recognised as a function). Python, JavaScript, TypeScript and Rust use the same rule. A contract on a partial function is allowed; `contract get` does **not** yet say the body was read in part.
  - [x] 59.4. **The files that lose the most** (§59 finding 3): the summary and `status` list the five files with the most skipped or partial functions, worst first, each with its counts and first error line; the JSON also has the whole list (100 files at most, `partial_files_omitted` says how many more); the totals equal the sums.
  - [x] 59.5. **Smaller items of §60**: `.mm` is read as C++ (Objective-C message sends make such a file partial); the summary says "found 4499 function(s) (2953 definitions, 1546 declarations)"; the `cpp` block of `status` counts `macro_invocations` (all-caps names) and `library_calls` (the C and C++ library, `std::`) apart from the calls to the project, gives `unresolved_reasons` (the reasons add up to `unresolved_calls`), and no longer counts macros and library calls as unresolved.
  - [x] 59.6. **Measured on Dear ImGui** (the compiler's list of the functions each core file defines, `g++ -g -c` and `nm -C -l`, names compared by their last component; the compiler's implicit constructors, destructors and lambda call operators are left out, as they have no source): `imgui.cpp` 17% → 100% (648 of 648), `imgui_draw.cpp` 100%, `imgui_tables.cpp` 100%, `imgui_widgets.cpp` 99% (the two misses are template names in `nm`'s output), `imgui_demo.cpp` 100% of the explicitly defined; all five 61% → 99% by the lenient count of the review. A scratch copy scanned end to end: 4,499 functions found (2,030 before), 9,781 edges (2,141 before), unresolved share of the calls to the project 78.6% → 23.9%; 64 of 108 files are still "partially parsed" (mostly C headers: `glfw3.h`, `imstb_*.h`, the OpenGL loader, `.mm`), with 78 functions skipped and 527 kept as partial. Parsing a file that needs the rewrite takes about two to three times as long (`imgui.cpp` 3 s → 8 s at load 15). Tests: `test_cpp_recovery.py` (43; one per file against the compiler is marked `slow` and skipped without the project or `g++`). The project was only read.

- [x] 60. Review of Task 59 (§61): a defined macro, the cost of a publish, the label "partial".
  - [x] 60.1. **A macro is a decoration because of its definition** (`parsers/cpp_defined_macros.py`, used by `CppParser.parse`): the `#define`s of the file and of the headers it includes (quoted or angle, found next to the file or under the project root, three levels deep, 40 headers at most, read once per process and again when a header changes) are classified. A name whose every definition is empty (object-like only), or made only of attributes (`__attribute__((...))`, `__declspec(...)`, `[[...]]`, `alignas`), calling conventions, inline spellings or other such names is blanked, with the arguments of a function-like one, wherever it is used outside strings, comments and directives. Only a macro that expands to code, a value, a type or a string is left alone, and an empty function-like macro (`LOG(x)`) too, as a statement in disguise. The text is blanked **for the tree only**: the stored signature and body keep the macro. Two faults of the old handling are gone: the decorator patterns no longer rewrite a `#define` line (blanking the name being defined left a bare `#define` and the grammar read the next line as its value, which is how `class EXPORT Widget` had stopped working), and an unknown all-caps name keeps the old rules unchanged. Every row of the table of §61 finds its method and the file is `successful`.
  - [x] 60.2. **A waiting scan is cut by the number of files, not by seconds**: a batch of a scan someone waits for no longer ends after `worker.pass_seconds` of parsing (that cut stays for the background drain), so a 108-file project is one publish (the trace shows one `remediate` request and one `publish` of 3.7 s), not a hundred. Also found with the trace: after a wake the worker slept out `worker.wake_min_gap` (5 s) even when the wake was a scan someone waited for; it now ends the gap as soon as a scan has files queued (checked every 0.1 s, since the files are queued just after the wake). That was about 3.7 s of every small scan.
  - [x] 60.3. **Publish work that grew with the project**, found with `cProfile` of one publish of the 107 files (the review's table, and a first publish into an empty database, which it did not cover):
    - rows are written in a few multi-row statements, not one each (`insert_rows`, `insert_artifacts`, `upsert_edges` in `db/queries.py`; `_sync_nodes` collects the new functions, `_EdgeRows` the new edges and references and writes them at the end of `_sync_edges` / `_sync_references`, and drops, rewrites or keeps in step a buffered row when its pair is forgotten or re-hashed; the declarations are deleted and inserted in a chunk): the statements of a first publish are 3 per file for Python and 0.22 per function for C++, the same for 10 or 40 files, instead of two to four per function;
    - `find_definition` asked the database for each declaration (2.9 ms each, 1,766 times): `definition_index` reads the candidates once, under each `::...` ending of their ids (what `ends_with` matched), and `lookup_definition` is a dictionary lookup; `match_waiting_declarations` uses it too, and `_become_alias` is asked only for a declaration that had an entry before;
    - `_has_overloads` ran a self-join for each ambiguous edge: the functions are grouped once per publish;
    - `compute_contexts` serialised every node again for every root (5.7 s for 3,136 functions): each node's JSON is written once and a root's payload is those pieces joined in the same bytes, so **every fingerprint is unchanged** (checked against the old algorithm on this project and in a test on a ring of calls and with non-ASCII text); and `refresh_contexts` keeps a digest of what the fingerprints are made of (`provenance:inputs`) and recomputes nothing when it is equal, as in a forced rescan of unchanged files;
    - parsing: the lines of a file were split once per function (33,000 splits), the line of a definition counted from the start of the file, and `_collect_errors` entered every subtree; the first two are cached / indexed, the third enters only subtrees with an error. A duplicate copy of four methods of `cpp_parser.py` left by an earlier edit is removed.
  - [x] 60.4. **"Read with warnings"**: a file with a syntax error and no function skipped or kept in part is no longer counted in `files_partially_parsed` (scan summary, `status` `partially_parsed` and the `cpp` block); `files_read_with_warnings` counts them, in the summary, in `status` and in the language block, and the lists (`partial_examples`, `partial_files`, `all_files`) hold only files that lost something. What is stored for such a file is still kept when it is rescanned (the error may hide a function), so the file record is unchanged; only the label and the counts are. On the ImGui copy 31 files are partial and 4 are read with warnings (the review: 64 partial).

Full suite at the end: quick lane 2,567 passed, 1 xfailed (14 min at load 20); slow lane 27 passed (6 min 37 s), no failures.

- [x] 61. Review on Django and Bevy (§62): the per-file record, Rust call resolution, definitions that were lost, and the cost of a publish.
  - [x] 61.1. **The per-file record loses its second copy of the text** (§62 finding 5, item 0; `core/graph_sync.py`). `scan:file:<path>` keeps the imports, the language once, and for each function its id, name, the hash of its body, and what a re-resolution reads (calls, property and symbol reads, owner, flags); a value that is the default is left out. The body, signature, lines, file, language and qualified name are not copied (they are in `artifacts`, in the id or in the record around it); `symbols_hash` is no longer written into every record, and a symbol change no longer rewrites the records of the files it did not scan (one added function writes the record of its own file only; tested). A function is restored from its record without a body, and the check that the record still matches the database compares the stored body hash with `artifacts.code_hash`. **Existing databases** are converted once, inside the first publish after the upgrade (`convert_records`; the form is recorded in `scan:records:form`, a record of either form is read, a database with the key missing is converted again, and a second publish changes no record). `provenance` and `language_health` read only the `imports` / `functions` part of a record (the database cuts it out). On this repository the records are **6.53 MB** for 4,002 functions (the review: 10.35 MB for 4,024; acceptance: under 7 MB). `artifacts.body` is not touched. The identity migration no longer adds an empty `parameter_tokens` or a `symbols_hash` to records.
  - [x] 61.2. **Rust call resolution** (finding 1; `core/rust_resolver.py`). `lookup` and `resolve_path` no longer walk every route of a cycle of globs (`use super::*` in modules that glob each other, which a crate's prelude is) and cut at the first repeat, which cost time that grew with the square of the modules and gave a lookup in a cycle a different answer for each entry. The lookups that reach each other are worked out together, each again from what the others have until none changes (at most 64 rounds), and kept; the depth limit of 8 is gone with the walk. Per-module tables replace the scans of a file's types and `use` lists, and the enclosing impl of a function, the implementers of a trait, the associated functions of a type and the files of a crate are looked up, not searched, per call. **Measured** (bevy, a scratch copy of the sources, one forced publish in-process under `cProfile`, load 19-22): 450 files **200 s → 26 s, the resolver 177 s (89%) → 1.3 s (5%)**, the 450-file edges (6,597) and the stored reasons (3,466 records) **identical** to the walk; all **2,014 files**, which the review could not index in 67 minutes: sync **92.6 s**, the resolver 7.3 s (8%), 27,183 functions, 34,629 edges; time per file 0.059 s at 450 files, 0.047 s at 1,000 and 0.046 s at 2,014 (acceptance: within a factor of 1.5). Equality of edges against the original walk was checked at 450 files only; at 1,000 and 2,014 files the edge counts equal those of an intermediate version that cached the walk (20,173 and 34,629), which was itself identical at 450. **Semantics changed knowingly** in two places: a lookup inside a cycle now gives what every route contributes (it used to depend on where the cycle was entered), and a chain of re-exports longer than 8 is followed to its end. `test_rust_cycles.py` (4) checks the edges of a project of modules that glob each other, entered from two places and in two orders. Not done: item (c), resolving only the callers a change can affect (`_may_resolve_differently` still answers "yes" for Rust and JavaScript).
  - [x] 61.3. **Definitions that were lost** (findings 2, 3, 4). (a) A function written inside a **decorated method** is indexed like one inside a plain method (the decorated branch of `_extract_class_methods` did not look inside the method). (b) **The same definition twice in one scope** (the two branches of an `if` or `try`, or a name defined again) is two functions: the Python parser no longer keeps only the later one, and the scanner gives the last the identifier a single definition has and each earlier one its position in the parameter tokens, so no existing identifier changes and none moves when unrelated code is added (`_distinct_ids`). (c) **The same method in several `impl` blocks of one type** (`impl IntoIterator for &A / &mut A / A`, `AsRef<[u32]>` / `AsRef<Vec<u32>>`, `Tr for W<Vec<T>>` / `Tr for W<u32>`) is one function per block: the parser records the block's header (`IntoIterator for &mut A`) as the function's `discriminator`, the first block keeps the identifier it had and the others get the header mixed into their parameter tokens, so a block moving in the file keeps its identifier. The readable part of the identifier is unchanged (no migration is needed, and the resolver, which matches on it, is untouched). `#[cfg]` variants of one method in one block or one module are still one entry (the existing rule). (d) An `impl` or `trait` **written inside a function** (examples and tests) is indexed; the function is part of its discriminator, since two functions may each define a local `S`. **Measured against `rustc` 1.74** (`-Z unpretty=ast-tree`, every `Fn` with a body, names per file as a multiset) on all 2,014 bevy files: **99.94%** (25,963 of 25,978; 14 files miss something); the three cases checked by hand among the rest (`norm` for `f64`, `type_id_is`, `debug_ensure_aligned`) are `#[cfg]` variants kept as one entry by design; **the other misses were not looked at**. The acceptance of 100% on the 473 files the review indexed is therefore **not met** as stated. (e) **Methods defined under an `if`, `try`, `with` or loop in a class body** (the two branches of `if is_psycopg3: def last_executed_query ...`) belong to the class; the class body was only read at its top level. **Measured against `ast`** (Python 3.8; every `FunctionDef` and `AsyncFunctionDef`, nested ones included, names per file as a multiset) on the Django copy: **100.00%** (30,379 of 30,379; the review: 99.1%), and drspec has no name `ast` lacks; 44 files are unreadable by `ast` (syntax newer than 3.8) and are not in the figure (acceptance: at least 99.8%). `test_definition_identity.py` (19); an older test that expected only the last of two identical Python definitions was changed.
  - [x] 61.4. **The cost of a publish** (finding 5; item numbers are the finding's). (1) **Contexts** (`core/provenance.py`): a short digest of each function's record (its hash, its file's imports, its edges) is kept, and only the functions that can reach one whose digest changed have their fingerprint worked out again; none when nothing changed. The fingerprints are the same bytes as before (checked against the old algorithm in a test and on a 3,136-function database). (2) **Facts**: not stored in a table (see below); the cost of reading them is lower because only the needed part of a record is decoded. (4) **Batch reads**: `IN (SELECT unnest(?::VARCHAR[]))` with one list instead of `IN (?, ?, ... 500)` in chunks (12 times faster for 32,720 ids: 0.19 s against 2.23 s), one query for the stored functions of all scanned files instead of one per file (1.55 s → 0.06 s), the same in `_EdgeRows`, `_load_artifact_details`, `_read_config_values` and the declarations. (b) **Compaction** (`db/compaction.py`, run when the worker starts, after the layout checks): when more than 30% of the blocks of a file of 32 MB or more are free, the file is rebuilt with the machinery of the layout migrations (a new file, checked, one rename; any failure leaves the old file); the 30% is given back, not more. (d) **`status` reports the database** (`database`: file, used and free MB, free percent, the three largest parts by stored text, and a note when more than 30% is free); the documents that are kept while `docs.enabled` is off appear there as "documents" (they are not deleted). A pass that serves a scan someone waits for no longer repeats the sweep for new and changed files (a second on a large project), and the worker no longer sleeps the wake gap for it (60.2 of Task 60 on the gap).
  - [x] 61.5. **The Python block of `status`** (finding 6): `python` has `files`, `calls`, `unresolved_calls`, `unresolved_share`, `unresolved_reasons` (`unknown receiver`: a method of an object whose type is not known; `not found`: a bare name no scanned module defines) that add up to the unresolved calls, and `library_calls` (the language's own names, `len`, `print`, counted apart, not as unresolved), `files_partially_parsed` and `files_read_with_warnings`.

The database file is **237 MB with 45.5% of it free** (129 MB used; the review: 327 MB; acceptance: under 150 MB, which the used part meets and the file does not until the worker starts again and compacts it, **which I did not run on it**). The per-file facts are 47.8 MB of the text (functions 40.5 MB); `status` `python` block on Django: 122,622 calls, 90.2% unresolved (`unknown receiver` 82,905, `not found` 27,751), 16,649 library calls.

Full suite at the end: quick lane 2,614 passed, 1 xfailed (11 min at load 19); slow lane 27 passed (6 min), no failures. The measurements above were taken on scratch copies of Django and bevy and of this repository's own database; the scratch folders were lost to a reboot of the machine and are not kept, so the figures cannot be repeated from them without copying the projects again.

- [x] 62. Review of Task 60 (§63): skipped C++ functions, the stored text, two fragile tests.
  - [x] 62.1. **Skipped functions in C++ files: 22 to 0** (ImGui copy: 55 skipped to 31, all 31 in the Objective-C++ `.mm` files, which a C++ grammar cannot read). The causes were not the one the review suspected (only the first branch of an `#if` being read): (a) **`_extract_class_methods` did not enter `#if`/`#ifdef`/`#else` blocks inside a class body** (the top level did), so every member under a preprocessor block of `imgui.h` was dropped silently and then reported by the brace check; the grammar already holds every branch of an `#if`/`#else`, so walking into the blocks gives **the union of the branches with no extra parse** (the access of the members before the block carries into it); (b) **the same function defined in both branches of an `#if`/`#else`** (`ImRsqrt` for SSE and without) was reduced to one by the C++ parser and the brace check reported the other: both are kept now and the scanner gives them identifiers (the last keeps the identifier a single definition has, as in Task 61.3); (c) **raw string literals** (`R"(...)"`, `R"sql(...)sql"`) are blanked before the brace scan, so the WGSL shader in `imgui_impl_wgpu.cpp` is no longer a function; (d) **a macro defined as `static` or `extern`** (`STBTT_DEF`) is a decoration like the attribute macros. `status` says what remains: `cpp.objective_c_functions_skipped`.
  - [x] 62.2. **Per-file recall against the compiler, with a cause for each miss.** With the comparison of Task 60 (`g++ -g -c`, `nm -C -l`, names by their last word): `imgui.cpp`, `imgui_draw.cpp`, `imgui_tables.cpp`, `imgui_widgets.cpp` and `imgui_demo.cpp` 100% each; `imgui.h` 97.7% (the two misses are `operator new` and `operator delete`, which the comparison names `new` and `delete`: the functions are indexed); `imgui_internal.h` 99.5% (`ImStableVector`, not examined). **I could not reproduce the review's 79% / 85% / 90%**: the review counts "the inline and template functions of the headers that the core files used", which I did not reconstruct; on the metric above the 95% acceptance is met, on the review's it is **not confirmed**.
  - [x] 62.3. **The stored C++ signature and body are the text the user wrote** (`parsers/cpp_parser.py`): the tree is made from a copy in which some macros are blanked (same length), and the stored text now comes from the original, including a macro in front of the function (`API int B(int a) FMT(1) { ... }`, `void A(const char* fmt, ...) FMT(2);`): equal to the source character for character (tested). The hash is over the stored text, so **the hash of every C++ function that has a decorator macro changes on the next scan** and a contract on one becomes stale; nothing else moves.
  - [x] 62.4. **Two fragile tests**: `DRSPEC_BUILD_ID` fixes the build id of the test and of the worker that replaces the old one (the id includes a fingerprint of the source tree, which changes while a file is edited); both tests are marked `slow`. - [x] 63. Full support for C on the existing parser (§64: C1, C2, C3, C4, C6).
  - [x] 63.1. **C1, the label.** `.c` is stored as `c` (a capital `.C` is C++); a `.h` is `c` when the nearest folder that has C or C++ sources (its own, then up to four parents, looking one level into subfolders when a folder has none) has C and no C++, and `cpp` otherwise, decided when the header is scanned (`Scanner.label_of`). One parser serves both (`parser.parse_file(path, language=...)`; `c` also turns on the C-only rewrites below). `core/languages.py` has one family, `C_FAMILY`, and `languages_matching`: `search --include cpp` returns C and C++, `--include c` only C (also in the line search of `live_search`); `status` keeps one `cpp` block with `c_files` and `c_functions` in it. The places that named `cpp` are changed (the resolver query, `graph_sync`, the identity migration, `hasher`, `hints`; the review counted 26, there were about 15 outside the parsers). **Decision 4 (no migration) is not followed to the letter**: C is hashed as C++ (`compute_hash("c")` is the hash of `cpp`), so no hash, identifier or contract changes, and nothing needs to be dropped: the functions of `.c` files are relabelled by one statement when the worker first starts on a database written before (`db/c_label.py`, marker `schema:c_label`), and a header (and any function whose label changed) is relabelled when its file is scanned (the sync now compares the language). The layout key is not bumped.
  - [x] 63.2. **C2, the C-only constructs.** `tests/fixtures/c/constructs.c` holds 25 constructs (the 18 of the review and `_Generic`, `typeof`, variable-length arrays, flexible array members, `_Bool`, X-macros, a function defined by a macro, array parameters with `static`, anonymous unions, function-pointer typedefs), each between two sentinel functions, 78 functions in all: **all found, the file `successful`, no error** (before: 76 found, 14 errors). The two that failed: a **K&R definition** (the parameter declarations are blanked in the text the tree is made from) and a **function returning a function pointer** (`int (*pick(void))(int)`: the name is in the inner declarator, whose parameters are the function's own; also the C++ parameter tokens). **C++ keywords used as names** (`new`, `delete`, `class`, `this`, `template`, `namespace`, `typename`, `operator`, `private`, `protected`, `public`, `virtual`, `friend`, `explicit`, `export`, `mutable`, `using`, `try`, `catch`, `throw`, `typeid`) are respelled with an underscore in the second letter for the parse, outside strings, comments and directives, and put back in the names of functions, parameters and calls. Also GNU **statement expressions** `({ ... })` and **`__asm__`** statements (replaced by a `0`; **calls inside them are not read**), and `[static N]` in array parameters. From the definitions in the file or its headers: **X-macro lists** (`#define COLORS X(RED) X(GREEN)`), **macros that define a function** (`DEFINE_GETTER(w, w)` at the top of a file), **identity macros** (`RAYGUI_CLITERAL(Rectangle){...}`: the macro name goes, the compound literal stays) and **macros that end in `enum`** (`RGFW_ENUM(u8, key) {`). Directive lines inside an `enum` are blanked for every file (the grammar cannot read them there; C++ headers have them too).
  - [x] 63.3. **C3, vendored libraries.** raylib (a scratch copy: 291 `.c`, 78 `.h`): **skipped functions 8,892 (review) to 8,607 with the C work alone, and to 135 once the count was corrected**: 7,015 of `miniaudio.h`'s skipped functions were `if (...) {` lines inside code that the tree had folded into an error region (and code shown in comments), which the check for lost definitions counted as functions; control statements and comment text are no longer counted, and raw strings and the macros above removed the true losses (`RGFW.h` 678 to 0, `raygui.h` 62 to 0 in each of its six copies). What is left: `miniaudio.h` 60, `m3d.h` 54, `jar_xm.h` 9, a few others (not examined). **Against `gcc -DMINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION`** the 1,990 functions the compiler defines in `miniaudio.h` are found at **99.3%** (1,977; the 13 missed are `static` functions in `#else` branches of one platform block, not examined: the same block parsed alone is read correctly), and the 60 skipped are 3% of the compiler's list (acceptance: under 5%). Treating `*_IMPLEMENTATION` as defined was not needed: the grammar holds the `#ifdef` regions, which are walked. Raylib's functions: 11,757, all `c`.

  - [x] 63.5. **C6, tests.** `test_c_support.py` (55): the label rules (a header beside C sources, beside C++, in an `include` folder, alone), the hash and identifier not carrying the label, the family and the filters, `status`, the relabelling and a rescan changing a stored label, the conformance file (the whole file and each construct on its own), each C-only construct, the vendored-library cases, and the **optional slow check against `gcc`** for raylib (skipped without the project or `gcc`): `src/` and `examples/` at **at least 99%** (the files that need a platform's own headers do not compile and are left out). `test_cpp_conditionals.py` (14) holds the §63 cases.

- [x] 64. Review of Task 61 (§65): free space while running, one-file scans, Rust macros, the list of bevy's misses, Python reasons.
  - [x] 64.1. **Free space is given back by the running worker** (finding 1; `worker_ipc.WorkerIPCServer.compact_if_needed`, `db/compaction.py`). After `worker.compact_idle_seconds` (600; 0 turns it off) with nothing to do, and more than 30% of a file of 32 MB or more free, the worker rebuilds the file **in its own process**: it holds the write lock for the whole rebuild (a write waits), plain reads are answered from the last committed state on cursors of their own (as during a publish), the new file is made from a checkpointed copy with the machinery of the layout migrations, and the swap (close, rename, reopen) waits for the reads in flight behind a small gate. Any failure leaves the file and the connection as they were. **A start no longer rebuilds**, so the first command after a start does not wait for it: **3.6 s on django's 250 MB file including the start of two Python processes (11-17 s before)**. Measured on django with the idle time set to 5 s: **237.8 MB to 127.9 MB, 2.7% free, within 30 s, no restart**; the status note says when it will happen. Tests (`test_database_size.py`): reads answered during the rebuild with the right counts, writes after it, a forced failure leaves everything as it was. **Not verified**: the default 10 minutes, the latency of a read during the rebuild against an idle read (the test checks that every read is answered correctly, not how fast), a real full disk or a killed process (a failure is injected).

(a) A scan of named paths lists those paths, not the project (`git ls-files -- <paths>`; `list_project_files(only=...)`); (b) each file's imports are an entry of their own (`scan:imports:<path>`, record form 3, converted once like form 2), so the contexts and the check for changed imports no longer decode the records (a test shows no statement reads all the records in a forced publish of one file); (c) the settings are read with `starts_with` (190 ms to 9 ms on a table of 34,000 keys), and the ignore patterns are one compiled expression (216,000 `fnmatch` calls to none).   - [x] 65.1. **The idle worker checkpoints before it asks** (finding 1; `WorkerIPCServer.compact_if_needed`). The tokens of a macro, which the file's own parse already made, are read directly when they are a plain expression: names, literals, paths, fields and methods, the usual operators, calls, indexes, nested macros and struct literals.   - [x] 67.2. **§69 (init starts the indexing)**, as decided: **(1)** `drspec init` starts the worker after the setup (a fresh `init`, a re-`init`, and the "already initialized, no changes made" answer), waits up to 5 s to see it, and reports `indexing: {started, message}`; the message names what happens ("indexing started in the background (about 20% of the machine); 'drspec scan' finishes it at full speed"). A worker that cannot be started is a **warning in the result and not a failed `init`** (exit 0, `warning`). `--no-index` starts nothing; `DRSPEC_DISABLE_AUTO_WORKER` (set for the whole test run) also starts nothing and says so. **(2)** `drspec init --scan` runs the whole-project scan of `drspec scan` (`core/indexing.scan_whole_project` calls `run_scan`, no new worker code) with the live progress line, ends with the result (`scan` holds the summary, `indexing.message` is "scan finished: N files, M functions"), exits **130** on Ctrl-C (the setup stays), and **1** with the scan's error in the result when the scan cannot run (the setup stays). **(3)** `--scan --no-wait` queues the files and returns at once (`scan queued: N files, read in the background`); `--no-wait` without `--scan` changes nothing; `--scan --no-index` is refused before anything is set up. **(4)** `scan` is unchanged. The MCP `init` tool has `scan`, `no_wait` and `no_index` (a scan waits up to 60 s and then answers, as the scan tool does). `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md` and `init --help` say it (the help text is that of the `init` group: the text of the command function is not shown, so it is in the group's help); I did not find the sentence of §55 that names the throttled index, so §55 is left as it is.

  - [x] 68.1. **§72 finding 1 (the first scan publishes in batches of 200)**: a new setting **`worker.scan_budget`** (default **5,000**, 1 to 100,000) is the most files one batch holds while a scan is being waited for (`drspec scan`, `init --scan`); `worker.rescan_budget` (200) stays for the background drain. `scan_service.batch_budget(conn, settings)` picks one or the other (a waiting scan is one with files queued as `scan`), and the worker pass and `drain` use it. **Measured** (scratch copies, `drspec scan` after `init --no-index`, load 3 to 4): **django 106.7 s to 28.3 s** (acceptance under 35 s), **bevy 133 s to 31.0 s** (acceptance under 45 s). **Same result**: the digest (md5 over the sorted rows, without times) of `artifacts`, `dependencies`, `code_references`, `scanned_files` and of `config` (per-file records, imports, mentions, contexts, hashes, with the run and worker keys left out) is **equal** between batches of 200 and the one batch on django (33,512 functions, 9,674 edges, 443 references). **Peak memory** (the worker and its parser processes together, sampled every 0.5 s): **1,150 MB** with one batch and **1,185 MB** with batches of 200 on django, 1,032 MB on bevy; the batch is held in memory until published, so a project with far more functions than these needs the setting lowered (its description says so). **Two things get worse and are not hidden**: the progress line of a scan does not move while a batch is being read and published (a first scan of django is one batch, so it shows nothing until it ends, about 25 s); and **Ctrl-C ends the scan at the batch in flight**, which is published whole (up to 5,000 files), the rest leaves the queue as before. Three existing tests that set `rescan_budget` to get small scan batches now set `scan_budget`.
