A function without a stored fingerprint now counts as changed, and only what reaches it is worked out (0.5 s). **(b)** After a change of symbols every stored function of every unscanned file was decoded and checked by `_may_resolve_differently`. Each file now keeps **what it mentions** (`scan:mentions:<file>`, written with its record: the names its functions call, read or annotate with, the names and module stems it imports; `{"all": true}` for JavaScript, TypeScript and Rust, whose calls resolve through other files' exports), and a change reads only the files that mention a changed name or stem. A database from before this has no such entry: its files are all read once, and the entries are written then. **Measured on django** (in process like the review's, under `cProfile`, which adds a good part: the same publish took 3.9 s without it and 5.8 s with it before the symbols filter existed; the same starting database for each; one new function with the filter on and off gives **identical** databases by the digest above): one new function **5.1 s (filter off) to 1.2 s**, a new file 1.2 s, ten files with a new function each 1.9 s (**1.5 times** one file), deleting a one-function file 1.8 s, and a comment 1.75 s, **which is not better than the review's 1.27 s** (nothing changes for either; `start_scan` alone took 1.0 s in my run against 0.4 s, so it is the machine); the review's figures for the others were 6.3 (function), 6.6 (file), 7.0 (ten files) and 5.4 s (delete) (acceptance: one new function, new file and delete under 2 s, ten files no more than twice one: **met on django**). Deleting `django/utils/text.py`, a module most of the project uses, takes 6.6 s, which is expected. **Not met on bevy**: a Rust file is always re-resolved (its calls resolve through `pub use` and module paths that the names do not cover), so one new Rust function takes **10.2 s** (the review's 43.6 s, of which the contexts were 15.6 s; acceptance under 6 s) and `resolve_rust_calls` of all 27,352 functions is 4.9 s of it; a comment edit costs 0.84 s because nothing changed. A prefilter for Rust needs a rule for what a re-export can change, which I did not write.

The query is kept as it was, and **is still used for any name with `_`, `%` or an empty directory name**, because in `LIKE` the `_` matches any character, `/` and `.` included, and an index cannot copy that; the directory rule is also not a plain "directory ends with the name": the query drops a file whose path holds the name earlier than at its last directory (`x/utils/y/utils/f.py` is not in `utils`), which the index copies. **Checked against the query** on every module path the stored imports of django name plus every stem and directory of the project (3,439 paths, six classes each): **0 differences**, with 2,223 module, 1,852 package and 6,455 class lookups answered by the index and the rest by the query. Now, **while a batch is running**, a cancel is answered at once (`{"cancelled": N, "deferred": true}`, N the files of the scan still queued) and is **known to the batch** (`scan_service.mark_cancelling`): the batch does not read that scan's files any more, and **if every file of the batch is of cancelled scans nothing is published**; the queue is cleaned up (`scan_service.cancel`) when the batch ends, and a detach asked meanwhile is carried out then too. The client's "Cancelled: X of N done, Y not read" uses the count at the cancel; if the batch was already publishing, those files are published and are counted in both. The progress line still does not move while a batch runs (the review's option (c), progress of the parse phase, is **not done**).

It needs a rule for what a change of the module facts can reach and a differential check against a fresh index on bevy (§73's 42 steps are the model); I did not write either. - [x] 71. Task T-52 (drtasks): after a parser upgrade, the files an older parser read in part are read again (artos: `update()` skipped, callers of `isDoAvoidance` missing).
  - [x] 71.1. **The cause** (from the review): `uniform_grid_obstacle_avoidance.cpp` had a `partial:` record from the scan of 2026-11-12 that skipped 10 functions; the current parser reads the file whole, but the file was never read again because its hash and time did not change, and nothing said the index was out of date.
  - [x] 71.2. **The version** (`core/parser_version.py`): `current()` is a hash (12 hex characters) of the sources that decide what a parse yields (`parsers/*.py` and `core/scanner.py`, where the skipped and partial functions are found) and of the versions of the tree-sitter packages, so a release that changes none of them reads nothing again, and no constant has to be raised by hand. A build with no sources uses the release number. `record_partial_files` writes it into every `partial:` record (`"parser"`); a record with none counts as older than any.
  - [x] 71.3. **The re-read** (`requeue_outdated_partial_files`): the files known to the index (`scanned_files`) whose partial record has another version are queued with the reason `parser_upgrade` (priority of a changed file: after what a scan or a search asked for, before new files; not the `scan` reason, so a cancelled scan leaves them alone; a file already queued keeps its reason). Done **when the worker starts** (`run_worker`, beside `requeue_for_new_facts`; a failure is a warning, never stops the worker) and **when a scan starts** (`start_scan`). The version looked at is remembered (`parser:requeued`), so the next scan and the next start cost one lookup and queue nothing; a record the new parser stamps is not older. Files with **no record are not touched** (a file that parsed whole), and nothing else changes for the queued files except what the new parse finds: their hashes, times and edges are rewritten only by the normal publish of those files. **Warnings-only records are queued too** (the review's 208 files include them, and the old parser may have hidden a loss: see 71.5); a first version of this change queued only records that had lost something and gave 47 files that still lose against the review's 55.
  - [x] 71.4. **`status`** (`parser_upgrade`): "N file(s) that an older parser read in part were queued for a re-read: A now parse whole, B still lose functions, C are read with warnings only, D are waiting" (`parser:reread` keeps the version and the paths, up to 20,000; the counts come from the records now).
  - [x] 71.5. **Measured on a copy of the artos index** (the reviewer's `contracts.db` and the 2,305 files it knows, in a scratch project; a plain `drspec status` started the worker and nothing else was asked): **218 files queued** (the 577 records include 359 of files the index no longer holds, see below), all read within about a minute; the review's 208 files: **78 have no record any more, 75 are read with warnings only (= the review's 153 clean), 55 still lose functions, 103 skipped and 377 partial, the review's numbers exactly**. Functions in the index: 11,922 to 12,352. `UniformGridObstacleAvoidance::update#a7e0893f` is indexed (it was not) and `isDoAvoidance` has a caller edge (it had none).
  - [x] 71.6. **Tests** (`tests/unit/test_parser_version.py`, 12 + 1 slow): the record carries the version; the version changes when a parser source changes; no version is older than any; only older partial records are queued, at the right priority, and nothing else is touched (scanned files and edges equal); a scan and a worker start queue them; the files are read with the current parser and `status` gives the numbers; a second scan, a second start and a lost marker queue nothing and an edit reads only that file; a new version queues the files that still lose; a real worker started by a plain command re-reads them (slow). - [x] 72. Task T-44 (drtasks): an empty callers list says when unresolved calls name the function, and `status` splits "not found".
  - [x] 72.1. **Reproduced** on the reviewer's C++ project: `deps get Base::baseWork --direction callers` printed an empty `Callers:` although `Derived::run` calls `baseWork()` and `this->baseWork()`; `status` said `Unresolved Reasons: not found: 9`.
  - [x] 72.2. **The note** (`core/unresolved.py`, `unresolved_callers`; used by `deps get` and the MCP `deps_get` through `add_unresolved_callers`). When a function has no resolved caller (and callers were asked for), the stored calls are searched by the called name, in the files of the same language family: the files whose stored mentions include the name (a language whose calls resolve through other files' exports mentions everything, so all of its files are read), each file's stored calls (`scan:file:`), minus the calls that resolved to some function (so a same-named method of another class does not count) and the function's own recursive calls. The result is a field `unresolved_callers` right after `callers`: `{name, call_sites, functions, message, sites: [{function_id, file_path, line, call, reason}], sites_omitted}` (50 sites at most; the counts are whole). The line comes from the caller's stored body (the k-th line holding the call text, so two identical calls get two lines). The text prints it under `Callers:`: "(no resolved callers; 2 call sites in 1 function name baseWork but could not be resolved)" and one line per site (`src/more.cpp:17  in game::Derived::run#...: baseWork()  [defined in the project, receiver unknown]`). A function that nothing names has no field and no note.
  - [x] 72.3. **The split** (`status`, every language): a call that was "not found" is now one of **`no definition in the project`** (a library or system call, or a name made at run time), **`defined in the project, receiver unknown`** (one place defines the name: our own function, but the receiver's type or the base class is not known to the resolver) or **`defined in several places`**. A place is a class or namespace, or the file without its suffix for a free function, so a declaration in `a.h` and its definition in `a.cpp` are one place and an override is another (`definition_counts`: one query). The other reasons (`unknown receiver`, `external package`, `macro`, `dynamic`, ...) are as they were. C++ and Python are classified when `status` reads the per-file records; JavaScript, TypeScript and Rust store counts per reason, so the resolver's result now also stores the names of the calls that were "not found" (`names`, in the same `js:unresolved:` / `rust:unresolved:` value): a function re-resolved since this change has them, an older value is shown as "not found" until the function is re-resolved (a `scan --full` does it).
  - [x] 72.4. **Measured**: on the reviewer's project `deps get ...::Base::baseWork --direction callers` prints the note with the two sites (lines 17 and 18 of `src/more.cpp`); `status` gives `defined in the project, receiver unknown: 7` and `no definition in the project: 2`. **The acceptance expected none in "no definition"**: the two are `bodies.size()` (a method of `std::vector`) and `fn(bodies[0])` (a local lambda), neither of which the project defines, so (a) is the honest answer; the other seven are `ctx.stop()`, `helper()`, `this->isDoAvoidance(nullptr)`, `s->wantsAvoid()`, `baseWork()`, `this->baseWork()`, `b.tick()`. On the artos index copy the lookup takes 0.2 to 0.8 s (`isDoAvoidance` 0.54 s: 1 call site; `update` 108 sites in 54 functions).
  - [x] 72.5. **Tests**: `tests/unit/test_unresolved_callers.py` (new, 14): the reproduction (field, message, sites and lines, reason, order of the keys, text output, a resolved caller or an entry point has no note, only for the direction that asks for callers, the MCP tool returns the same field, the status split), one test each for Python, JavaScript and Rust (field, line, reason, no note on `main`, split), the helpers (called name, reason by places, a declaration and its definition are one place), the cap on listed sites; two existing tests that pinned `not found` now pin `no definition in the project`. Full suite: **quick lane 2,870 passed, 1 failed, 1 xfailed (32 min 49 s, load 9 to 12): the one failure is `test_readonly_connections.py::test_two_readonly_cli_commands_run_concurrently_without_blocking`, a 60 s subprocess timeout under load, which passes in 3 s when the file is run alone (twice)**.
  - **Not done / not verified**: `search` (the title says "deps_get/search") is unchanged: the acceptance names only `deps_get`; `unused` lists functions with no callers and does not mention unresolved sites; JavaScript and Rust files are all read for the note (their stored mentions say "all"), which costs more on a very large project (not measured on bevy); the note looks only at calls whose last name equals the function's name, so a call written through an alias or a function pointer is not found; an older `js:unresolved:`/`rust:unresolved:` value keeps "not found" until the function is re-resolved.

- [x] 74. Task T-35 (drtasks): the progress of a first scan moves while the batch reads and publishes.
  - [x] 74.1. **The batch reports itself** (`core/scan_service.py`: `begin_batch`, `note_read`, `batch_progress`, a phase set by `begin_publish`, cleared by `end_batch` and when the worker's publishing state ends): while a batch is running in the worker, `scan_progress` for a scan with files in it carries `batch: {phase: "reading" | "publishing", files_read, files_total, seconds}`. `files_read` counts the files the batch has come to (read, or left alone), so it moves as the parser pool hands results back; in `publishing` it is all of them. It is state of the worker process, written by the batch only, read by the progress request that the worker already answers while the batch holds its lock (T-34/Task 69), so the batch size and the work are unchanged. A batch of files no scan asked for (the background lane) reports nothing.

  - [x] 75.1. **What the parser records now** (`parsers/cpp_parser.py`, `parsers/cpp_types.py`): (1) **receivers** are short chains (`getSteering()`, `bodies[]`, `h.get()`, `this->ctx`, `(*this)` is `this`; arguments and indices left out, 160 characters at most) where only a name or `this` was kept and every other call was dropped; C files keep the old rule (nothing to find through a chain in C); (2) **calls inside a lambda** belong to the enclosing function (a lambda has no identity of its own); (3) **declared types** keep template arguments and arrays (`std::vector<Body>`, `Body[]`), and say what is written for the rest: `auto x = <expr>` is `=<expr>`, `for (auto& x : <expr>)` is `@<expr>`, a lambda's parameters and locals count when the name is not already declared; (4) **type facts** (`facts` of the file's imports, as the JavaScript module facts): `classes` (qualified name, bases, fields, template parameters) and `returns` (qualified function name to its declared return type; overloads that disagree say nothing).
  - [x] 75.2. **The resolver** (`core/cpp_resolver.py`, step 5, only when steps 1 to 4 found nothing, so nothing that resolved before can change): a receiver expression is typed from declared types alone (`TypeRef`: a class and its template arguments): a variable's declared type, a field of the class or of a base, the declared return type of a bare or member call (`getSteering()`, `h.get()`), a constructor call, the element of a container (`std::vector`, `array`, `deque`, `list`, `set`, `span`, ...) or an array, with a class's template parameters bound to the receiver's arguments (`Holder<Body>::get()` returns `T*`: a `Body`). The method is then looked up in that class and, failing that, in its bases in order: **a virtual method resolves to the class that declares it, never to the overrides**; an overload set stays `AMBIGUOUS` (one edge each). A bare call in a derived class finds the base's method only when no scope has a function of that name. Nothing else is guessed: an unknown type, a name two scopes declare differently, `a[0]` on a class that is not a container, a typedef or `using` alias stay unresolved.

- [x] 76. Task T-34 returned: the cancel result was still false when Ctrl-C landed inside the publish.
  - [x] 76.1. **Reproduced** as the review did (django copy, `drspec scan` on a pty, not `--json`): Ctrl-C at 18 s (the line said "Publishing 3054 files read (3 s)") exit 130 after 0.12 s and the result "Cancelled: 0 of 3054 file(s) were done, 3054 not read", `Files Cancelled: 3054`, while the project then held all 33,512 functions. The tests of Task 70 and 74 and my own runs (`--json`) did not see it.
  - [x] 76.2. **Cause** (found by printing the worker's answer to the cancel): it was **not the answer to the cancel**. It was the reply to a `scan_progress`. During a publish the worker is busy, so the client spends most of its time inside a `scan_progress` request waiting for its reply; a Ctrl-C there raised in `WorkerConnection._request` after the request was sent and before its reply was read. The reply stayed on the socket, so the next request (the cancel) read the progress record as its answer, a record that has no `deferred` and no `publishing`, so the cancel was taken as "in time", and the cancel's own reply was then read as the answer to the final `scan_progress` (`cancelled: 3054`, `done: 0`). In a `--json` run the client sleeps 0.2 s between polls and a Ctrl-C usually lands there, which is why my earlier runs saw the right text (18 s, 24 s and 26 s in the Task 74 runs were lucky).
  - [x] 76.3. **Fix** (`worker_ipc.WorkerConnection`): the connection counts the requests whose reply it has not read (`_unanswered`, counted before the request is sent); before it sends the next request it reads and drops those replies (a second Ctrl-C while it waits keeps the count; a lost connection is an error and resets it). This is general: any request interrupted by Ctrl-C used to desynchronise its connection.
  - [x] 76.4. **Measured again** (django copy on a pty, the Ctrl-C sent as a signal to the client, and once as the terminal's own `^C`; the last progress line before it is what says where the batch was): **16 s** (line: "Scanning 1453/3054 files: reading (5 s)", the batch is discarded as designed) exit 130 after **0.11 s**, "Cancelled: 0 of 3054 file(s) were done, 3054 not read", nothing queued; **18 s** (line: "Publishing 3054 files read (4 s)") exit 130 after **0.72 s**, "Cancelled: 0 of 3054 file(s) were done when you cancelled. The batch of 3054 file(s) the worker was publishing is still being finished, not undone: its files are indexed, the counts here do not include them yet", `Files Cancelled: 0`, `Files In Flight: 3054`; **21 s** (line: "Publishing 3054 files read (3 s)") **0.73 s**, the same text; **18 s as the terminal's `^C`** **0.74 s**, the same text; 45 s later `status`: **33,512 functions, nothing pending**.
  - [x] 76.5. **Tests** (`tests/unit/test_interrupted_request.py`, 4 + 1 slow): with a socket pair and a server that answers late, an interrupt (a `SIGALRM` that raises `KeyboardInterrupt` in the main thread) while a request waits leaves the next request its own reply (before: it got the first one), a second interrupt while the reply is being dropped keeps the count, answered requests leave nothing to drop, a lost connection is an error; **slow, the real path**: a real worker runs the 1,500-file batch as `drspec scan` does, the client's read raises `KeyboardInterrupt` once the progress says "publishing" (so it lands in a progress request, as in the scenario), and the summary says `batch_in_flight` with `files_in_flight` 1,500 and no file cancelled, and the project then holds all 12,000 functions; without the fix the unit test gets reply 1 for request 2 and the slow test fails (`KeyError: 'batch_in_flight'`).
  - [x] 76.6. **Suites**: the full quick lane, run once with no other session editing `parsers/` or `core/` (the modification times of all files there were the same before and after), **2,951 passed, 1 xfailed, 0 failed (5 min 13 s)**; the slow tests of the touched areas (interrupted request, scan cancel, scan progress, worker IPC, scan with a worker, init indexing, parser version) **21 passed**; the full slow lane was not run. The root volume was nearly full, so the temporary files of the runs were on the data volume.
  - **Not done / not verified**: an interrupt that comes while a request is being *sent* (a partial message) is not repaired: the next request would wait for a reply that is not coming (the message is a few hundred bytes and one `sendall`); the 16 s run of the review landed in the read phase on this machine (the publish ran from 13 s to 24 s on theirs), so the phases were told from the progress line, not from the clock.

- [x] 79. Task T-68 (drtasks): a method removed from its header and its definition in one scan leaves no ghost declaration and no stale call edge.
  - [x] 79.1. **Reproduced** (`m.h` declares `work` and `keep`, `m.cpp` defines both, `u.cpp` calls both; `init` and `scan`, then `work` removed from `m.h` and `m.cpp` in one scan): the index kept an artifact `m.h::M::work`, its `artifact:context:` row and the EXTRACTED call from `use` to it, until the header changed once more.
  - [x] 79.2. **Cause**: the publish removes the definition (`_sync_removed_nodes`), then `revert_aliases_of` turns the declaration rows that were aliases of it back into entries (an artifact each: with no definition left, the declaration is the only thing that says the method exists), and only then does `forget_declarations_not_in` drop the declaration rows of the header that no longer declares them. The entry it had just made was never removed: the header's own removals had been decided before it existed. The same ordering made the **reverse case** wrong: when only the declaration leaves the header, the definition becomes invisible to the files that saw it through that declaration, but nothing re-resolved their calls, so `use` kept its edge to a definition a fresh scan cannot reach.
  - [x] 79.3. **Fix**: (1) `forget_declarations_not_in` also schedules the removal of the artifact of every declaration it forgets (the pending removals take its edges, contexts and records with it; it returns how many, counted in `functions_removed`); (2) the declarations (their ids and the definitions they alias) are a digest of their own (`_declarations_digest`) in the symbols fingerprint of a publish, and `_symbol_facts` carries them (`declaration:<id>`), so a declaration that comes, goes or changes its definition makes the calls that name the method resolve again (a project with no declaration has the fingerprint it always had; `symbols_hash` itself is unchanged: the identifier migration predicts it).
  - [x] 79.4. **Tests** (`tests/unit/test_declaration_removal.py`, 4, differential: the index of the edited tree is compared with a fresh scan of a copy: artifacts, calls, references, declarations, the `artifact:context:` keys, the keys of the per-file records, every other config row that is not about a run, and the pending removals): both removed in one scan (no `m.h::M::work`, no context, `use` calls only `keep`); the definition removed first (the prototype stays as an unmatched declaration with its edge) and the declaration removed in a later scan; the declaration removed first (the definition stays, `use` no longer reaches it, as in a fresh scan) and the definition removed in a later scan. **Without the fix the first and the third fail** (the ghost artifact; the stale edge); the second was already right. Also changed: `test_cpp_support.py::test_an_auto_variable_is_not_guessed` (T-46) says the only edge an `auto` of an unknown initialiser can get is an INFERRED one, never EXTRACTED or AMBIGUOUS; T-46's own lane had failed on it.
  - [x] 79.5. **Suite**: quick lane (default addopts, other sessions' pytest runs overlapping): 2,996 passed, 66 deselected, 1 xfailed, 1 failed in 56 min; the failure (`test_review_followups::TestAStopSurvivesASwallowedSignal::test_the_worker_stops_even_when_the_exception_is_swallowed`) is load-sensitive and passes alone (2 passed in 1.7 s). `cli/commands/scan.py` was changed by someone else while the lane ran (16:06, after the lane started at 14:51; not part of T-68).
  - **Not done / not verified**: the differential check covers C++ classes with methods declared in a header and defined in a source file (the three orders); free functions and templates were not repeated; a declaration moved from one header to another in one scan was not tested.

# 20. Feedback: `drspec status` Doesn't Report Edge Counts (Caught By the Grep-Fallback-Check Rule, Not a Grep Fallback) (Implemented — see Task 19)

**Status: implemented.** Both open questions resolved (Task 19.1/19.2).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

While dogfooding §19 Finding 1's fix, checking how many `dependencies`/`code_references` rows actually exist (sanity-checking the DB wasn't corrupted/bloated after an earlier killed scan) required a raw `duckdb.connect(...).execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM dependencies")` — there's no `drspec` command that reports this. `drspec status` already reports `artifacts.total` (and a `by_status` breakdown, plus `queue`/`contracts` summaries) but nothing about the edge tables at all — the graph's own size/density (how many `calls` edges, how many `references` edges) isn't visible anywhere in the CLI/MCP surface.

Checked against `feedback_grep_fallback_check.md`'s own two-question test before writing this up, per the user's standing instruction to apply that check and log it when it passes both:
1. Could `drspec` be improved to cover this? Yes — `drspec status` is exactly the right existing place; adding an `edges: {calls: N, references: M}` section (or similar) is a small, natural extension, not a new command.
2. Would it be more efficient than a raw query? Yes — no need to know the schema, open a direct connection, or write SQL; a single existing command already in the normal workflow.

Both yes, so this is written up rather than quietly left as a one-off raw query.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~Exact shape: flat count, or richer confidence-tier breakdown?~~ — **resolved, see Task 19.1: richer**, `{total, by_confidence: {EXTRACTED, INFERRED, AMBIGUOUS}}` per relation type, mirroring `artifacts.by_status`'s own always-padded style.
- ~~Whether this belongs on `drspec status` itself or a new command~~ — **resolved, see Task 19.2: appended to `drspec status`** — directly matches the gap's own motivating observation.

# 21. Feedback: Hook Rewrites Grep Into `drspec search`, Instead of Only Asking (Implemented — see Task 17)

**Status: implemented.** All four open questions resolved (Task 17.1/17.2); a real correctness bug caught live mid-implementation, not hypothetical (Task 17.3); the under-documented `updatedInput` mechanism confirmed actually working in this harness by catching that exact bug in the act (Task 17.4); a second real correctness bug, in the same class but a different root cause, reported directly after shipping and fixed (Task 17.6 — an unquoted shell operator glued to adjacent text with no whitespace slipped past the first fix's token-based check).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

§18/Task 15's `prefer_drspec.py` hook only ever `ask`s before a grep-family command runs — it relies on someone noticing the prompt and choosing to redirect. That doesn't help the actual failure mode already demonstrated twice in this same session: reaching for grep out of pure habit, without even considering drspec as an option, with no decision point for the hook's prompt to meaningfully interrupt (the habit happens *before* any deliberation the hook could catch).

Researched directly (not guessed): Graphify's own equivalent hook reportedly goes further — it "rewrites grep/find/rg to graphmind search" rather than asking. That removes the reliance on the agent noticing and choosing correctly; the substitution happens structurally, before execution.

## Confirmed technically feasible, not just a Graphify-specific trick

Checked against this harness's own hook schema: a `PreToolUse` hook's response supports `hookSpecificOutput.updatedInput`, documented as "Modified tool input (PreToolUse only)." So `prefer_drspec.py` could rewrite a `Bash` tool call's `command` field directly — turning `grep -rn "pattern" src/` into the equivalent `drspec search "pattern" --path src/` before it ever runs — rather than (or in addition to) asking.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~**Translation completeness**: ... needs a reliable way to detect "this grep call is actually outside drspec's function-only scope" and fall back to `ask` rather than rewrite into something broken or silently wrong.~~ — **resolved, see Task 17.1/17.3.** A deliberately narrow translator: only `grep`/`egrep`/`fgrep`, only a closed set of recognized flags (unrecognized flag -> bail), only when the pattern is confirmed safely literal (the BRE-metacharacter check added after 17.3's live-caught bug). Everything else falls back to the plain `ask`.
- ~~**Rewrite vs. ask, or both?**~~ — **resolved, see Task 17.2: both, and never silent.** A confident rewrite still goes through `ask` (never `allow`) — `updatedInput` is under-documented enough that pairing it with `allow` risked a silent unsafe passthrough if unsupported; `ask` fails safe either way, and the reason text always shows the exact substitute command.
- ~~**Partial matches**: pipes/chains — partial rewrite, refuse, or ignore?~~ — **resolved, see Task 17.1: refuse (fall back to plain `ask`).** Detected by checking `shlex.split` tokens for one made up entirely of shell-operator characters — correctly distinguishes a real unquoted pipe from one safely quoted inside the pattern itself (e.g. `-E "a|b"`).
- ~~**Scope relative to §18's existing hook**: replace, or layer on top?~~ — **resolved, see Task 17.2: layer on top** — and found a hard boundary while implementing it: a hook's `updatedInput` can only replace the *same* tool's input, so the rewrite only ever applies to `Bash`; the dedicated `Grep` tool keeps the original §18 behavior unchanged, since there's no mechanism to redirect it to a different tool at all.

# 22. Feedback: Background Staleness-Resolution Worker (Implemented — see Task 18)

**Status: implemented.** All four open questions resolved (Task 18.2-18.5), plus one real ambiguity in this write-up's own "Proposed shape" wording resolved before any code was written (Task 18.1), and two real robustness bugs caught only by dogfooding through the actual CLI rather than trusting the unit suite alone (Task 18.8/18.9).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

Query results (`search`, `deps_get`, and their MCP counterparts) reflect whatever the last `drspec scan` captured — there's no signal in a result telling you whether the underlying source has actually changed since. Researched directly, not guessed: Graphify's own attempt at automatic freshness (their `--update` incremental path) has a documented correctness failure — "already clean" reported while stale nodes silently persisted, and destructive merge regressions degraded an already-current graph (see §21's research). A naive "auto-rescan inside the query path" (an earlier, rejected shape of this same idea) would risk the same trap, and also directly reintroduces the write-lock contention Task 14 found the hard way: only one process can hold a write connection at a time.

## Decided already, in conversation, not re-litigated here

- **Detection and remediation are decoupled**, matching the "queue-based re-index scheduling" pattern research turned up as the actual sound alternative to Graphify's approach: a `drspec scan` already walks files and can flag likely-stale ones cheaply (mtime/size proxies, not a full re-hash inline, so scan speed isn't affected) into a queue; a separate background worker drains that queue asynchronously, off the query path entirely.
- **Write-lock contention**: the background worker defers to explicit, human-initiated writes — if it can't acquire a short-lived write transaction because `drspec scan` (or anything else) currently holds one, it waits/retries rather than the human-initiated operation ever blocking on the invisible background worker. The worker is the opportunistic, lower-priority actor; an explicit `drspec scan` is never held up by it.
- **This is accepted as a genuinely bigger architectural step**, not something to slip in quietly. Task 14.2 explicitly chose no daemonization for `drspec serve` ("a background-daemon model is a materially bigger change to this project's operating model"). This proposal reverses that precedent, deliberately, for a different feature — a conscious choice, not an implicit one.
- **Singleton enforcement**: start with a straightforward mechanism (not over-engineered upfront — a PID file or a DB heartbeat row are the obvious candidates), implement it, and let real dogfooding surface whatever problems it has (stale locks from a crashed process, races on near-simultaneous launch, etc.) rather than trying to design away every edge case before writing any code — consistent with how this project has handled every other piece of genuinely hard concurrency work so far (Task 9, Task 11, Task 14 were all fixed from real, observed problems, not pre-emptively).

## Proposed shape

- Background worker process, singleton (a second launch attempt detects the first and exits immediately), auto-shuts down after 15 minutes idle.
- A staleness queue (new table, or an extension of the existing `queue`/`config` mechanism — not pre-decided) populated by `drspec scan` as a side effect of its own normal file-walking, using cheap proxies (file size, mtime, possibly a quick content-size check) to decide what's worth flagging, not a full hash comparison inline.
- The worker drains the queue: for each flagged node/file, do the real check (recompute `compute_hash`, compare against the stored one) and, if genuinely changed, run the same incremental-scan machinery `drspec scan` already uses (`sync_scan_results` et al.) scoped to just that file/node — not a new scanning mechanism, just a new, async trigger for the existing one.
- Query results (`search`, `deps_get`, `unused`, and their MCP equivalents) gain a staleness indicator per node (and possibly per edge, for more specificity) reflecting whatever the queue/worker currently knows — read from already-computed state, not a live recheck at query time.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~**Launch trigger**: who starts the worker?~~ — **resolved, see Task 18.2: `drspec scan` itself, on completion.** Best-effort, silent, never turns a successful scan into a reported failure.
- ~~**Queue storage shape**: new table, or reuse `queue`/`config`?~~ — **resolved, see Task 18.3: two new dedicated tables** (`scanned_files` — a snapshot; `staleness_queue` — presence-only, no status column) — reusing `queue` would have overloaded its unrelated contract-review-workflow semantics.
- ~~**Staleness-indicator shape**: boolean, timestamp, or edge-level richness?~~ — **resolved, see Task 18.4: a plain `possibly_stale: bool` per function, file-granular.** Richer than that would be invented, not reported — file-level presence/absence is the whole signal the worker actually produces.
- ~~**Worker's write-access retry/backoff parameters**?~~ — **resolved, see Task 18.5: none, deliberately.** A busy DB just means this pass does nothing further; the poll loop's own cadence is the retry.

# 23. Feedback: `deps unused` Doesn't Distinguish "Called Only by Its Own Tests" from "Really Used" (Implemented — see Task 20)

**Status: implemented.** All three open questions resolved (Task 20.1-20.4); re-verified directly against the write-up's own real `normalize_code` example (Task 20.5).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

Found while reviewing Task 16's `deps unused`/`unused` MCP tool (design.md §19 Finding 2): `hasher.py::normalize_code` no longer appears in `drspec deps unused`'s output — not because anything in the real application calls it, but because its own dedicated unit tests (`test_hasher.py::TestNormalizeCode`, `test_feedback_regressions.py::test_legacy_hash_is_invalidated_once`) now resolve as real callers, now that a scan includes `tests/`.

This is *correct* per `_UNUSED_WHERE`'s documented semantics — it checks every resolved caller project-wide, and those test calls are real, resolved edges, not false positives. But it's a real practical gap for the tool's actual purpose (a dead-code-candidate list, per its own docstring): a function whose *only* callers are its own dedicated tests is about as strong a "nothing depends on this" signal as exists — deleting it would also mean deleting those now-pointless tests, and nothing else breaks. Confirmed concretely with `normalize_code` itself: separately verified (via `deps_get`, `search`, and reading `core/__init__.py`) that it has zero callers anywhere in `src/`, is exported in `__all__` but not consumed by anything, and exists only because `test_hasher.py` tests its own behavior — exactly the case this gap hides from `deps unused`.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~Should `_UNUSED_WHERE` exclude edges whose caller is under a test path — by default, by flag, or not at all?~~ — **resolved, see Task 20.1: not excluded at all — *widened* instead.** A function is included if it has no caller/reference anywhere, or if every one it has is itself a test — never hidden behind an opt-in flag.
- ~~Should a test-only-caller function get a distinct label rather than being silently collapsed into the same bucket as zero-callers?~~ — **resolved, see Task 20.2: yes — a new `"only_test_callers": bool` field** on every result, both CLI and MCP.
- ~~Worth a schema/query change now, or defer for a second example?~~ — **resolved: done now**, directly against this write-up's own real example (Task 20.5).

# 24. Feedback: Read-Only Commands Open Write-Capable Connections, Widening §22's Own Collision Window (Implemented — see Task 21)

**Status: implemented.** All three open questions resolved (Task 21.1-21.3); the concurrency improvement verified directly, not assumed from Task 14's earlier finding alone (Task 21.4).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

Found while independently reviewing §22/Task 18's concurrency fix (the `DATABASE_BUSY` retry/clean-error work, Task 18.11): `db/connection.py::get_connection()` has no `read_only` parameter at all — every caller, including `drspec search`, `drspec deps get`, and `drspec deps unused` (none of which ever write anything), opens a full read-write connection. Per Task 14.1's own directly-verified finding ("a read-write connection held open by one process blocks every other process — reader or writer — from opening the file at all"), this means every one of these read-only commands can itself **be** the thing a concurrent `drspec scan` or the background staleness worker collides with, and can itself be blocked by *them* — neither of which should be necessary, since a genuine read-only connection would never be blocked by another reader, and holding one never blocks a writer either (confirmed directly in Task 14's own investigation).

This doesn't change anything about §22/Task 18.11's two already-fixed/documented findings (`staged_scan`'s write-hold duration, `drspec scan`'s missing error handling) — those are both specifically about *write* paths. This is a different, additive lever on the same underlying problem: reducing how often a *read* command even competes for the lock in the first place, rather than shortening how long a *writer* holds it.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~Extend `get_connection()`, or a separate helper?~~ — **resolved, see Task 21.1: extended `get_connection()`** with `read_only: bool = False` — reuses its existing validation/error-handling rather than duplicating it.
- ~~Audit scope: confirm `search`/`deps` never write; `status`/`queue get` may have the identical gap and weren't checked~~ — **resolved, see Task 21.2: audited each command's actual body, not assumed from its name.** `search`, `deps get`/`plot`/`unused`, `status`, and `queue`'s `peek`/`get` (but not `next`/`prioritize`, confirmed to write) switched; `contract`/`source`/`verify`/`vision`/`learn` explicitly left un-audited, not assumed clean.
- ~~Ordering relative to §22's `staged_scan` scaling concern?~~ — **resolved, see Task 21.3: moot**, implemented independently — doesn't touch any write path at all.

# 25. Feedback: Route All DB Access Through the Worker — the Real Fix for DuckDB's Reader-Blocks-Writer Problem (Implemented — see Task 22)

**Status: implemented.** All ten open questions resolved (Task 22.1-22.9, 22.14); one new finding the write-up itself didn't anticipate, discovered during implementation and fixed before being considered done (Task 22.11).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet. A redesign, not a quick fix — written up on its own, separate from any smaller mitigation.**

## The gap

A core assumption this project has treated as bedrock since Task 14.1 — "a held-open read-only connection never blocks a writer" — is false for this project's actual DuckDB version (1.2.2). Verified with an isolated, synchronized, two-process repro: zero drspec code involved, `duckdb.connect(path, read_only=True)` held open in one process, a second process's plain `duckdb.connect(path)` (write-capable) fails immediately with a lock-conflict `IOException`, every run. Reproduced again at the drspec level: a held-open read-only connection made a real `drspec scan` exhaust Task 18.11's 3 retries and return `DATABASE_BUSY`.

This directly undermines the stated purpose of every read-only-connection change made so far — `drspec serve`/the MCP server (Tasks 14/15) and Task 21's `search`/`status`/`deps get`/`plot`/`unused`/`queue peek`/`get` — all of which were built specifically to *stop* colliding with a concurrent `drspec scan`, and none of which actually deliver that guarantee.

## Why DuckDB's own recommended fix doesn't fit this project

Checked DuckDB's own concurrency documentation directly (not assumed): genuine concurrent cross-process read+write against DuckDB's native file format isn't actually supported by the embedded single-file model this project is built on at all. DuckDB's own recommended paths are either "Quack" (a client-server protocol — literally "turns DuckDB into a client-server database," currently in beta as of DuckDB 1.5.2 and not expected to mature until DuckDB 2.0, i.e. not even usable with the 1.2.2 this project pins), or DuckLake (a format requiring a separate PostgreSQL database as its catalog). Both reintroduce exactly the multi-service operational burden this project has deliberately avoided since §1/§5 (the same reasoning that kept it off a Neo4j+Qdrant-style setup). Neither is a fit.

## Decided already, in conversation, not re-litigated here

The real fix: make the staleness worker (§22/Task 18) the **single process that ever opens the actual DuckDB file**, for both reads and writes. If only one process ever touches the file directly, DuckDB's documented single-process concurrency model (multiple threads, MVCC) applies cleanly — the multi-process file-locking problem this whole investigation uncovered disappears structurally, not through a retry/timing mitigation. Every other command (`drspec scan`, `search`, `deps get`/`plot`/`unused`, `status`, `queue`, `drspec serve`, the MCP tools) stops opening `get_connection()` directly and instead sends its request to the worker, waits for a response, and gets its answer back.

This reuses real, already-built infrastructure — the worker's singleton PID-file enforcement, `SIGTERM` handling, idle-timeout, and `drspec worker run/status/stop` lifecycle commands all stay relevant — but is a genuinely larger architectural step than anything decided so far, bigger even than §22 itself. §22/Task 18 was already flagged as reversing Task 14.2's "no daemonization" precedent for one narrow feature (staleness detection); this proposal makes **every single drspec command** permanently depend on that daemon for its actual work, turning drspec from "a CLI tool with an optional background helper" into "a thin client talking to a local server" — effectively building a small, purpose-specific version of what DuckDB's own (unusable-here) Quack feature does. That shift deserves to be made consciously, which is the entire reason this is its own proposal and not folded into a bug fix.

Three further decisions, made explicitly rather than left for the dev to guess at:

- **No stopgap.** The smaller retry-based mitigation (stretching `drspec scan`'s existing 3-retry window) is explicitly **not** being shipped as an interim fix — go straight to this redesign rather than spending effort on something that gets thrown away once it lands.
- **Recovery model, layered, not a single fallback-or-fail choice**: (1) the worker is self-supervising — it should restart/recover itself so it's reliably available, this is its own job as "the server side"; (2) a caller that finds the worker unresponsive makes its own attempt to wake/start it (reusing `spawn_worker_if_not_running()`'s "launch if not already running" logic) rather than assuming it's permanently gone; (3) only if *both* the worker's own self-recovery and the caller's wake attempt fail does a command surface a clear, explicit error. Never a silent fallback to a direct connection (that would quietly reintroduce the exact race this redesign exists to close) and never an immediate hard-fail on the first sign of trouble (that would make one transient hiccup needlessly user-facing).
- **Idle behavior: dormant, not exited.** The worker does **not** fully shut down after 15 minutes idle anymore — it stays alive as a process (so there's no cold-start/wake latency for the next request) but drops to minimal CPU usage while idle (e.g. blocking on an incoming request instead of actively polling on a timer). The distinction that matters: "nothing to do right now" vs. "not running at all" — only the latter should ever require a caller's wake-attempt step.

## What doesn't exist yet and has to be built

- **A request/response interface on the worker.** Today the worker only runs its own autonomous loop (detect drift, remediate, sleep, repeat) — nothing external can ask it to do a specific thing on demand. It needs to accept a request ("run this query," "do this scan," "fetch this result"), do the work using its own already-open connection, and send a response back. This is the bulk of the real work, not a detail — see the "interface shape" and "where today's command logic lives" open questions below, both still unresolved.
- **An actual IPC mechanism.** Not pre-decided: a Unix domain socket (lower overhead, purely local, no port to manage) vs. reusing the HTTP pattern `drspec serve` already establishes (simpler, consistent precedent already in the codebase, but carries HTTP overhead and the same port-collision questions `drspec serve` already had to solve).
- **Launch-then-wait-until-ready, not launch-and-assume-ready.** `spawn_worker_if_not_running()` already does "launch it, no-op if one's already running" — that part reuses directly. What's new: a caller now has to actually wait for the worker's request interface to come up, not just confirm the process exists (today's `spawn_worker_if_not_running` is deliberately fire-and-forget, since nothing in the *same* invocation currently depends on the worker being immediately ready).

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~**Interface shape**: one generic "run this" endpoint..., or a fixed set of operations mirroring each existing command one-to-one...?~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.1: neither literally** — a generic `execute`/`begin`/`commit`/`rollback` surface (mirroring `duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection` itself, not arbitrary SQL passthrough with no shape) covers every read command via a one-line call-site swap, plus exactly two purpose-built operations (`sync_scan`, `remediate`) for the two things that must run inside the worker process itself.
- ~~**Where today's per-command logic lives.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.2: stays in `cli/commands/*.py`.** Only the DB-connection part became an IPC call; `scan_command` was split so the DB-touching half (`publish_scan_results`) runs inside the worker, reached via `sync_scan`.
- ~~**Concurrent requests to the worker itself.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.3: serialized**, via one shared lock around the worker's one connection — deliberately the simplest model that's correct, as this question itself invited.
- ~~**Error propagation through the IPC layer.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.4: serialized by exception class name, reconstructed as the same class client-side** — not a generic wrapper, so existing `except duckdb.Error:` call sites keep working unchanged.
- ~~IPC mechanism: Unix domain socket, HTTP..., or something else?~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.5: Unix domain socket**, newline-delimited JSON, mirroring `mcp_server.py`'s own stdio protocol.
- ~~**Bootstrap case**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.6: `drspec init` stays direct** (no worker, no file yet); every other command connects-or-spawns-and-waits.
- ~~**Timeouts and error handling on the IPC layer itself**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.7: 15s to reach the worker's socket after a spawn, 120s per request round trip** — exceeding either is a clear `WorkerIPCError`, never a silent fallback.
- ~~**Scope**: does literally every command route through the worker..., or is there a deliberate carve-out...?~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.8: every routed command does; `drspec init` is the one carve-out.**
- ~~**Rollout**: migrate all commands at once, or incrementally...?~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.9: all at once** — incremental would have meant two live connection models mid-migration, a worse state to verify.
- ~~**Testing discipline**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 22.14: followed exactly as pre-answered** — genuinely separate OS processes for the actual concurrency claim (the original bug reproduced end-to-end against a real worker), in-thread IPC-mechanics tests for protocol-level correctness that isn't itself a cross-process claim.

# 26. Feedback: `drspec search` Can't Give Live, Grep-Shaped Line Numbers — the One Real Remaining Reason to Reach for `grep` (Implemented — see Task 23)

**Status: implemented.** All six open questions resolved (Task 23.1-23.5); re-verified directly against all three of this write-up's own real motivating cases (Task 23.6).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.**

## The gap

Caught live while reviewing Task 22/§25: needed the exact line number of `get_connection(` inside a handful of named files (`contract.py`, `learn.py`, `source.py`, `vision.py`, ...). Had just used `drspec search "get_connection(" --files-with-matches` successfully one call earlier for the file-level version of the same question, then reached for `grep -n "get_connection(" file1.py file2.py ...` instead of extending the same `drspec search` call — caught by the user, which led to this write-up rather than a silent one-off workaround (per the standing grep-fallback-check rule).

Pressed on *why* grep felt like the safer choice, two real (not imagined) reasons surfaced, not just habit:

1. **Freshness.** `drspec search` answers from the DB built by the *last* `drspec scan` — exactly the kind of staleness §22's own worker exists to catch. `grep` reads the file as it is on disk right now, with zero dependency on anything else being up to date.
2. **Format.** Checked directly, not assumed: a normal `drspec search` match (e.g. for `socket_path`) returns `function_id`, `function_name`, `file_path`, `signature`, `possibly_stale` — function-granularity, no raw line number field at all. "Which line, literally, does this substring occur on" isn't a shape `drspec search` produces today.

Both are real, structural gaps, not a trust problem on the reviewing side — grep wins this specific case for a legitimate reason, which is exactly why it's worth closing rather than shrugging off as "just use grep for this one thing."

**Second, independent occurrence, caught by the dev reviewing separately**: needed to see a module-level `from drspec.db import get_artifact, get_connection` import line together with the function body that uses it, in one view — reached for `grep -A` for the same reason (logged as feedback rather than silently worked around, per the same standing rule). This is a sharper version of the same root cause: `drspec search` only stores function-granular records (signature + body *per function*). An import statement, or any line outside a matched function's own stored body, has no home in that data model at all — not just "the wrong line number," but literally nothing to return, regardless of format. Confirms the fix below needs to cover more than single-line matches.

**Third occurrence**: `grep -n "^_TOOL_HANDLERS" src/drspec/mcp_server.py` (locating a module-level dict constant's own definition, line 538, anchored at the start of the line) has no `drspec search` equivalent either — `drspec search "_TOOL_HANDLERS" --path src/drspec/mcp_server.py` matched `handle_message` instead, the one function whose *body* happens to reference the name, with no line number and nothing distinguishing "this is the definition" from "this function merely uses it." Confirms the gap isn't specific to import statements — it's any module-level declaration (constant, dict, class attribute default, etc.) that sits outside every function's own stored body.

## Proposed fix

A flag on `drspec search` (name TBD — something like `--lines`) that, for each file a match falls in, re-reads that file live off disk at request time (not from the DB) and locates the literal line(s) where the search pattern occurs, returning `{line, text}` pairs alongside the existing structured match data. This closes both of the first gap's objections in one shot: live file content removes the staleness objection, and grep-shaped `{line, text}` output removes the format objection. Because this reads the real file directly rather than a stored per-function body, it is no longer bounded by function granularity either — which is what makes grep's own `-A`/`-B`/`-C` context flags (print N lines after/before/around each match, independent of any function boundary) worth adding alongside it, closing the dev's import-line case too instead of leaving it as a second, separate gap.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Scope of "live."**~~ — **resolved, see Task 23.1: opt-in only, via `--lines`** (or `-A`/`-B`/`-C` implying it) — matches `--semantic`/`--embed`'s own precedent of keeping the default query cheap.
- ~~**Multiple occurrences per file.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 23.2: every literal occurrence in the whole file, not just inside the matched function's body** — directly required by this gap's own motivating cases (an import line, a module-level dict constant) that aren't inside any function's body at all.
- ~~**A file that no longer parses/exists.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 23.3: surfaced explicitly as `"live_read_error"`**, never silently omitted — consistent with this project's "never silently stale" discipline (confidence tiers, `possibly_stale`).
- ~~**Relationship to `-c`/`-l`/`-v` grep-flag emulation.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 23.4: its own documented carve-out** — rejected outright (`INVALID_INPUT`) in combination with `-c`/`-l`/`--semantic`, since none of those return per-match records to attach line data to.
- ~~**Whether this subsumes `--files-with-matches` entirely**, or stays additive~~ — **resolved: stays additive**, as leaned — `-l` is untouched.
- ~~**Context flags (`-A`/`-B`/`-C` NUM...).**~~ — **resolved, see Task 23.5: implemented, mirroring grep's own meaning exactly** (`-C` sets both sides, `-A`/`-B` override their own side) — overlapping/adjacent windows across multiple occurrences in the same file merge into one block, matching grep's own behavior, resolving the "how context lines interact with multiple occurrences" sub-question the same way.

# 27. Feedback: Only 3 of 19 CLI Commands Are Exposed as Agent (MCP) Tools (Implemented — see Task 26)

**Status: implemented.** The user's own direct decision (below) resolved "which commands" outright; the remaining open questions resolved as Task 26.1-26.5. Two real bugs found and fixed along the way: Task 26.5 (pre-existing, `learning/diff.py`) and Task 26.7 (newly introduced by this task, `mcp_server.py`'s missing `insert_learning_event` import).

## The gap

Raised directly, in conversation: §18/Task 15 built an MCP server (`mcp_server.py`) so an agent can call `drspec` as a structured tool instead of shelling out — but it only wraps three read-only operations (`search`, `deps_get`, `unused`). Every other command, including `scan` itself, is still only reachable by an agent running the CLI through `Bash` (seen directly: the dev running `python3 -m drspec scan src/`). Initial instinct was that this is fine as-is — reads are safe to hand an agent broadly, writes are inherently more awkward over a request/response MCP call. Pressed on that reasoning directly, it didn't hold up: a write like `scan` blocks the calling turn for the same duration whether it goes through `Bash` or through an MCP tool call, so "awkward over MCP" wasn't a real difference. The stronger comparison is this very project's own tool design — Claude Code itself prefers dedicated tools (`Edit`/`Write`) over `Bash`+`sed` for *mutations*, specifically for structured parameters, a consistent error shape, and a cleaner permission surface — reasoning that applies to drspec's own mutating commands too, not just its reads. (Checked honestly, not asserted: whether Graphify's own MCP surface — the precedent §18 was modeled on — covers mutations too isn't confirmed either way in anything established so far in this project; not claimed here.)

## Current surface, audited directly (`drspec --help` plus each subcommand's `--help`, not assumed from memory)

| Command | Subcommand | Read/write | Currently an MCP tool? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `scan` | — | write | no |
| `search` | — | read | **yes** (`mcp__drspec__search`) |
| `deps` | `get` | read | **yes** (`mcp__drspec__deps_get`) |
| `deps` | `unused` | read | **yes** (`mcp__drspec__unused`) |
| `deps` | `plot` | read (writes an HTML file as a side effect) | no |
| `status` | — | read | no |
| `queue` | `peek`, `get` | read | no |
| `queue` | `next`, `prioritize` | write | no |
| `contract` | `get`, `list` | read | no |
| `contract` | `save` | write | no |
| `source` | `get` | read | no |
| `verify` | `script` | read | no |
| `verify` | `run` | write + **executes a stored verification script** | no |
| `learn` | `history`, `stats` | read | no |
| `learn` | `analyze` | write (runs git log analysis) | no |
| `vision` | `list` | read | no |
| `vision` | `save`, `update` | write | no |
| `worker` | `status` | read | no |
| `worker` | `run`, `stop` | long-running / control, not request-response shaped | no (`run` especially — it blocks until idle-out/`SIGTERM`, not a fit for a synchronous tool call at all) |
| `serve`, `mcp-serve`, `init` | — | long-running server / meta / one-time bootstrap carve-out (§25 already carves `init` out of the worker-routing requirement for the same reason) | no |

## Decided, directly by the user, superseding this section's own framing below

**Every drspec command gets an MCP tool. Not having one yet is a gap in implementation, not an open design question.** Stated plainly, not as one option among several: the "which of these should become MCP tools at all" question below is resolved — all of them, including the plain mutations and `verify run`. This also reopens two decisions this project had previously treated as settled the other way:

- **Task 15.7's original choice to keep `-c`/`-l`/`-v` CLI-only** ("out of scope for an agent-facing data tool") — superseded. Full flag parity is now the bar, not a deliberately trimmed subset.
- **§29's "should `--lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C` reach `mcp__drspec__search`" open question** — same answer: yes, not a case-by-case call.

The only things this doesn't flatten are the handful of commands that structurally can't be a request/response tool call at all (`worker run` blocks until idle-out/`SIGTERM`; `serve`/`mcp-serve` are long-running servers) — those stay CLI-only because the shape doesn't fit, not because of a scope decision. `init`'s carve-out (§25) is a bootstrap ordering constraint (no worker/DB yet), not a "should it have a tool" question either — it can still get one for the one-time setup case itself.

## Open for the dev — now about *how*, not *whether*

- ~~**Full parameter parity per command**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 26.1: yes, full parity** — `search` gained `invert_match`/`count`/`files_with_matches` (Task 15.7's old carve-out superseded) on top of §29's `lines`/context flags; every other new tool's schema mirrors its CLI command's flags one-to-one.
- ~~**Maintenance surface.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 26.2: accepted as an ongoing cost**, as the write-up itself framed it — not a blocker, same answer Task 24.2 already gave for Phase 1.
- ~~**Permission/confirmation semantics for MCP-exposed writes.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 26.3: not re-litigated — deferred, not newly solved.** Checked directly: an MCP tool call gets whatever permission handling the hosting client (e.g. Claude Code's own MCP integration) already applies to tool calls in general; this project's own `PreToolUse` hook only gates `Bash`, and nothing here adds a parallel gate for MCP writes. Accepted as out of scope for this expansion specifically — the user's own decision was about tool *coverage*, not a new permission layer, and inventing one unasked would be scope creep this project's own conventions reject.
- ~~**Rollout.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 26.4: all 11 remaining commands at once**, mirroring Task 24.4/22.9's own precedent — each is additive (a new tool, not a cutover), so the "two live models mid-migration" risk that justified "all at once" for §25 doesn't even apply here; it's simply the consistent choice.
- ~~**Where tool logic lives.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 26.5: unchanged from Task 24.5's own pattern** — each new handler calls the same `cli/commands/*.py`/`db`/`core` functions its CLI command already calls and builds its own response dict, never the Typer-bound CLI function itself.
- ~~**`verify run` specifically**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 26's own docstring note (`mcp_server.py`): unchanged risk, not a new one** — it reuses the exact same timeout-bounded `execute_verification`/`verify_at_runtime` mechanism `drspec verify run` already uses over `Bash`; the tool call carries the same risk profile as the CLI command, not a new exposure.

# 28. Feedback: Give Module-Level Symbols (Not Just Functions) a Node, Gated by Kind

**Status: proposal, not a task yet.**

## The gap

Surfaced directly while discussing §26's third occurrence: `_TOOL_HANDLERS` (a plain module-level dict in `mcp_server.py`) has no node in the graph at all — the scanner only ever extracts *functions*, so a constant, dict, or any other module-level assignment is structurally invisible to everything the graph can answer (`deps get`, `unused`, call-edge traversal). The one `drspec search` hit for it earlier was a coincidence (the name happened to also appear inside a function's own body), not a real lookup. There's no way to ask drspec "where is `_TOOL_HANDLERS` defined" or "what uses it" today, structurally — only a live text scan (§26) could ever answer "where," and nothing answers "what uses it" at all.

Raised directly in conversation, the instinct to reject this (a new node kind would need its own answer at every place that currently assumes "node = function" — `contract`, `verify`, `queue`, `status`'s completion counts, `unused`'s "no caller" definition) turned out to be solvable, not a wall: gate every function-specific feature on a `kind` field, and let each one answer plainly instead of trying to force function semantics onto a non-function node.

**Second occurrence, confirmed directly (§31's CLI-output migration):** auditing `tests/unit/*.py` for every `runner.invoke(app, [...])` call site needing a mechanical `--json` fix turned up the same gap from a different angle. `mcp__drspec__search`/`drspec search -l` for `CliRunner` returned only 5 files; a plain-text `grep -rl` found far more (module-level `runner = CliRunner()` fixture/instance lines sit outside any function body, so they're structurally invisible to the function-only index — the same root cause as `_TOOL_HANDLERS` above, not a new one). The sharper point this occurrence adds: neither the CLI's `-l` output nor the MCP tool's JSON response gives any caveat that module-level matches are excluded — a result silently looks complete. Worth folding into this proposal's own fix (or, at minimum, adding a "module-level matches are not indexed" note to `search`'s own `--help`/tool description) rather than leaving the omission unstated.

## Proposed fix

Give module-level symbols (starting scope: Python module-level assignments — the concrete case found, same "start with exactly the case found" discipline as §9's own `@property`-only start) a minimal node: an ID (`file_path::NAME`), file, line, and the assigned value's text — not the full function treatment (no signature, no call-resolution body). Add a `kind` column (`function` | `constant`, extensible later) to distinguish them. Every existing feature that assumed "every node is a function" gets an explicit, individually-decided answer instead of an implicit one:

- **`contract get`/`save`, `verify run`** — check `kind` first; for anything not `function`, return a clean "contracts only apply to functions" response, not an attempt to run verification machinery that has nothing to execute.
- **`queue`** — never auto-enqueue a non-`function` node for contract-generation work (checked at the point a scan would otherwise queue it as new/changed).
- **`status`'s completion-rate numbers** (`items_needing_attention`, `completion_rate`) — filtered to `kind = function` only, so a project full of constants doesn't dilute "how much of this codebase has contracts."
- **`deps unused`** — this one isn't a clean guard-clause skip, it needs an actual definition change: "no caller" has to become "no caller *and* no reader" to mean anything for a constant, which depends on the next point.
- **A new `reads`-style edge** (same family as §9's `references`, not reusing `calls`) from a function to a module-level symbol it reads by name — this is what makes "what uses `_TOOL_HANDLERS`" answerable at all, and what `unused`'s widened definition above would actually check against.

## Open for the dev

- **Scope for this first pass.** Module-level assignments only, or also class-body attribute defaults (same "invisible outside any function" problem, one level deeper)? Explicitly bounded, not assumed — same reasoning §9's own write-up used for staying narrow.
- **Schema shape.** A `kind` column on the existing `artifacts` table (simplest, but a function-shaped table gains columns that mean nothing for a constant — `signature`, `confidence`, etc.), or a separate, smaller table for non-function symbols that `kind`-aware queries union against? Different cost/complexity trade-off than it first looks, since `deps get`/`unused` would need to query across both either way.
- **The `reads` edge's resolution rule.** A bare name reference inside a function body is ambiguous the same way a bare call is (§3.5's existing call-resolution steps) — does this reuse that same step-by-step resolution logic, or need its own simpler version given there's no method/class-membership question for a module-level name?
- **Does `--embed` apply to this node kind at all** — semantic search over a constant's value has a real use case (`drspec search --semantic "color palette"` finding `Color = {...}`), but it's a separate decision, not assumed bundled in.
- **Per-language rollout.** Python only first (consistent with how this project has staged other scanner features — e.g. declared-type extraction landed for Python/C++ before JS/TS, and JS/TS was explicitly skipped where the grammar didn't support it), or decided per-language from the start?

# 29. Feedback: §26's New `search` Flags (`--lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C`) Never Reached the MCP Tool (Implemented — see Task 25)

**Status: implemented.** All three open questions resolved (Task 25.1-25.4); re-verified directly against this write-up's own real motivating example (Task 25.5).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.**

## The gap

Caught directly while reviewing §26's implementation: `drspec search` now supports `--lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C` (live, grep-shaped line numbers and context — confirmed working, e.g. `drspec search "_TOOL_HANDLERS" --path src/drspec/mcp_server.py --lines -C 2` correctly returns both the real module-level definition and its usage, with surrounding context). But `mcp__drspec__search` — the MCP tool an agent actually calls instead of shelling out — was checked directly (its live schema, not assumed): it only has `pattern`, `ignore_case`, `regex`, `semantic`, `language`, `path_prefix`, `status`, `limit`. None of §26's new flags made it in.

This isn't a technical limitation — an MCP tool is just a JSON-schema function, and there's nothing stopping `mcp_server.py`'s `search` handler from accepting a few more parameters and passing them through to the same search code the CLI already calls, the same way `regex`/`semantic`/`ignore_case` already are. It's that §26's write-up never raised "should this reach the MCP tool too" as a question at all, so nobody made an explicit call either way — it just didn't happen. There is real precedent for leaving something CLI-only on purpose (Task 15.7 kept `-c`/`-l`/`-v` out of the MCP tool deliberately, "out of scope for an agent-facing data tool"), but that was an explicit decision at the time, not something this gap can claim by default.

The practical effect: an agent using only the MCP tool (the thing §18's whole three-layer system exists to make the default, over `Bash`) still can't get a line number or surrounding context for anything — only an agent willing to shell out via `Bash` can use §26 at all, which cuts against the stated point of having the MCP tool in the first place.

**How this gap was actually caught, which is itself worth recording**: an MCP tool exposes a fixed, already-known parameter schema — there's no "show me this tool's help text" action an agent can call on it, the way `drspec search --help` works for the CLI. So the only way this discrepancy got noticed was a reviewer with `Bash` access directly diffing the CLI's `--help` output against the MCP tool's loaded schema by hand. An agent restricted to MCP tools only — exactly the agent §18 is meant to produce — has no mechanism to ever notice this gap itself: it only knows whatever parameters its schema says it has, with nothing to cross-check that against. That's a sharper version of the "nothing prompts this check" process gap below — it's not just that nobody checked, it's that the MCP-only agent has no way to check even if it wanted to.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Should `--lines`/`-A`/`-B`/`-C` reach `mcp__drspec__search` at all**~~ — **resolved, see Task 25.1: yes**, explicitly, not a default — the gap directly undercuts §18's own stated purpose for having the tool at all.
- ~~**If yes, parameter shape.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 25.2: mirrors the CLI flags directly** (`lines`, `before_context`/`after_context`/`context`), not a simplified variant — keeps one shared implementation.
- ~~**General process gap this exposes**...~~ — **resolved, see Task 25.4: noted in writing, no new standing-checklist mechanism built** — consistent with how this project has handled other identified-but-not-structurally-fixed process gaps so far.

# 9. Feedback: `references` Relation Type (Implemented — see Task 7)

**Status: implemented.** The write-up below is kept as the original proposal/rationale; see Task 7 above for what was actually built and how each open question was resolved.

## The gap

`dependencies` only ever records **calls** — a `call`/`call_expression` AST node, something with trailing `(...)`. Anything else that makes one piece of code depend on another — reading a `@property`, a type annotation, a base class, a decorator, a function passed as a value without being called (`handler = my_function`) — produces **zero edge**, even though a real dependency exists.

Found concretely while dogfooding this project's own code: `RootCauseReport.all_candidates` (`src/drspec/debugging/root_cause.py:91`) is a `@property`, actively used in production code (`root_cause.py:469`) and seven call sites in `tests/unit/test_root_cause.py` — every one written as `report.all_candidates`, no parentheses. `drspec deps get` reports **zero callers**. The method isn't unused; it's invisible to the graph because property access has no `()`, so it's a different AST node shape (`attribute`/`member_expression`) than a `call` node, and nothing currently extracts that shape at all.

This matters because the stated goal of this whole feature (§1) is tracing real dependencies for semantic search and debugging — not specifically "which functions call which other functions with parens." A widely-used property that looks completely unused in the graph is exactly the kind of blind spot that undermines that goal. "Zero callers" being usable as a signal for "safe to remove" is actively wrong for any `@property`.

## Precedent: Graphify hit this exact bug

[Graphify issue #4336](https://github.com/Graphify-Labs/graphify/issues/4336): "Python: reading a `@property` (`response.text`) gets no edge to its getter" — the identical case, different example. Their fix ([PR #4337](https://github.com/Graphify-Labs/graphify/pull/4337)):

- **Did not fold property reads into `calls`.** Added a separate relation type, `references`, tagged with a `context: "property_read"` field — `calls` stays strictly call sites only, never conflated.
- The edge is only added when the receiver's class is **statically known** — same "never guess" discipline this project already holds for `calls` (§3.5). Never points at a plain field, a regular method, or a differently-spelled name.
- Tagged `EXTRACTED`/`INFERRED` depending on how confidently the receiver's type was resolved — the same confidence-tiering concept §3.5 already has.

## Proposed direction

Add `references` as a **second, separate relation type alongside `calls`** — don't broaden or touch the existing `calls`/`dependencies` resolver, which is built, tested, and proven. This is additive, not a rewrite.

Reuses machinery §3.5 already built: the same `classes_by_name`/declared-type/same-file lookups that resolve `self.method()` can resolve `self.property_name` — same lookup, different trigger condition (an `attribute`/`member_expression` node whose name matches a known `@property`, instead of a `call` node). Same confidence tiers (`EXTRACTED`/`INFERRED`/`AMBIGUOUS`) should apply unchanged.

**Explicitly bounded starting scope, not "handle everything":** per the earlier discussion that made this proposal (`references` is a fuzzier, larger category than `calls` — it could otherwise expand to cover type annotations, base classes, decorators, value-passing, string-based dynamic references, and more), start with exactly the case found and the case Graphify already validated: **Python `@property` reads only.** Everything else in the "reference" category is explicitly out of scope for a first pass and should be its own later decision, not assumed bundled in.

## Open for the dev to decide, not pre-decided here

- Schema: a `relation`/`kind` column on the existing `dependencies` table (`calls` vs `references`), or a wholly separate table? `PRIMARY KEY (caller_id, callee_id)` currently assumes one row per pair regardless of relation type — a property read and a call could coexist between the same two functions and would collide under the current key.
- Does the §3.2 "merge to highest confidence" rule apply across relation types, or only within each one separately?
- Does `--deep-scan`'s scoped-diff logic (§4 step 3e, §7) need mirroring for `references`, or does it only ever produce `EXTRACTED` (since Graphify's version never guesses the receiver type) and so has no confidence-tier staleness problem to begin with?
- Should `drspec deps get`/`deps plot` show `references` and `calls` together by default, or opt-in separately — given the whole reason this gap was found is that "zero callers" silently meant something different than expected?

# 10. Feedback: `drspec search` — Mirror grep's Own Flags, Not New Ones (Implemented — see Task 8)

**Status: implemented.** The write-up below is kept as the original proposal/rationale; see Task 8 above for what was actually built and how each open question was resolved.

## Design principle

Agents (and people) reach for `grep` constantly because its flags are already muscle memory. The fastest way to make a new tool's learning curve near-zero is to **not invent a different vocabulary** — reuse grep's actual flag letters and names wherever the concept maps directly (`-i`, `-v`, `-c`, `-l`, `-E`), so existing grep habits transfer immediately. Deviate only where drspec has something grep structurally cannot (semantic/embedding search, confidence-tier filtering) — and even then, make those additions, not replacements.

## Current gap (confirmed against the actual code, not assumed)

There is **no unified search command today.** What exists is fragmented and mostly not CLI-exposed:

| grep flag | drspec today |
|---|---|
| `-i` (case-insensitive) | **missing entirely** — every comparison in `queries.py` is case-sensitive |
| `-E`/`-P` (regex) | **missing entirely** — every filter is exact-match or SQL `LIKE` substring/prefix; DuckDB has native `regexp_matches`, so this is addable without new infra |
| searching file **content** | **missing entirely** — zero queries filter on `body`/`signature`; `source get` returns body but only by exact `function_id`. Likely the single biggest gap, since searching code content is the main reason anyone reaches for grep |
| `-c` (count) | DB-layer functions exist (`count_artifacts`, `count_contracts`, `queries.py`) but **not exposed in any CLI command** |
| `-l` (list matching filenames) | **missing** — no path-only projection anywhere |
| `-v` (invert match) | **missing** |
| `--include=*.py` (file type) | **exists** (`language` column/filter) but not exposed as general search — only used internally for fuzzy-match suggestions |
| path filtering | **exists** (`contract list --path`, prefix-only) but scoped to contracts, not general |
| `-A/-B/-C` (context lines) | **already better than grep** — returns the full function body, not just a line of context; not a gap |

Also confirmed already built but entirely unwired to the CLI: `search_contracts`/`query_contract`/`query_contracts` (`db/queries.py`) are never imported or called from anywhere in `cli/commands/`. `deps get`/`deps plot` don't expose the `confidences` filter that `get_dependencies_by_caller` already supports.

## Proposed flag set for `drspec search <pattern>`

Reusing grep's own names directly:
