| Flag | Meaning | Backing |
|---|---|---|
| `<pattern>` (positional) | substring or regex, matched against name **and body/signature** | new — the core missing capability |
| `-i`, `--ignore-case` | case-insensitive match | new (DuckDB `ILIKE`/`regexp_matches(..., 'i')`) |
| `-E`, `--regex` | treat pattern as regex, not literal substring | new (DuckDB `regexp_matches`) |
| `-v`, `--invert-match` | show non-matching results | new |
| `-c`, `--count` | print match count only | wire up existing `count_artifacts`/`count_contracts` |
| `-l`, `--files-with-matches` | print distinct file paths only, no function detail | new projection over existing data |
| `--include <lang>` | filter by language | wire up existing `language` filter |
| `--path <prefix>` | filter by file path prefix | wire up existing pattern (currently `contract list`-only) |

Drspec-native additions (no grep equivalent, kept as clearly-separate opt-ins, not folded into the above so grep muscle memory still applies cleanly to the rest):

| Flag | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `--semantic` (**confirmed** — user explicitly signed off on this one) | similarity search over the Task 2/5 embeddings instead of exact/regex text match — "find code like X" |
| `--confidence <tier>` | filter edges by `EXTRACTED`/`INFERRED`/`AMBIGUOUS` (already exists in `get_dependencies_by_caller`, unexposed) |
| `--status <status>` | filter by contract status (PENDING/VERIFIED/...) — already exists in `list_artifacts`, unexposed |

## Open for the dev to decide, not pre-decided here

- Does `search` become a new top-level command, or does it absorb/replace `contract list`'s existing filters (`--status`, `--path`, `--min-confidence`) to avoid two overlapping ways to filter the same data?
- Regex engine choice: DuckDB's native `regexp_matches` (SQL-side, fast) vs. Python `re` (more familiar syntax/flags, but requires pulling rows client-side first) — pick one, don't support both.
- Should `-i`/`-E`/`-v` apply to `--semantic` mode at all, or are they meaningless there (semantic search has no literal/regex notion)?

# 11. Feedback: `drspec scan` Performance — Root Cause Found, Not Fixed (Implemented — see Task 9; corrects this write-up's own fix hypothesis)

**Status: implemented, with a correction.** Task 9 verified the batching hypothesis below before implementing anything (as this write-up itself asked for) and found it does **not** hold — see Task 9.1. The actual fix shipped (Task 9.4) is a different, smaller, verified one: short-circuiting the `find_spec` probing itself, not reducing the number of `execute()` calls. The rest of this write-up (root cause, the on-demand-embedding tangent) still stands and is kept as-is below.

**Status (original, kept for context): investigation complete, fix not applied.** Found while dogfooding Task 7 at real scale (`drspec scan` on this project's own `src/`, 489 functions): a fresh full scan took on the order of several minutes — disproportionate for a project this size.

## What it's not

Benchmarked `embed_text()` in isolation: ~14.7ms/call. For ~1,000 total embeddings (nodes + edges + references) across a fresh scan, that's under 15 seconds — nowhere near the actual multi-minute runtime. **Embedding compute itself is not the bottleneck**, despite being the most obvious suspect.

## What it is — confirmed via profiling, not guessed

Profiled a real scan with `cProfile`: `insert_artifact`/`upsert_dependency_edge`/`update_dependency_edge_hash` dominate, and `embed_text` itself runs ~7x slower *inside* the scan (105ms/call) than in isolation (14.7ms/call) — both pointing at something in the surrounding call path, not the functions themselves.

The actual cause, isolated and confirmed: **DuckDB's own Python driver does real per-call overhead on every `conn.execute()`.** Reproduced with zero drspec code involved — a plain in-memory DuckDB connection doing 50 trivial `INSERT ... VALUES (?, ?)` calls with an array-typed parameter produced **614 calls to Python's `find_spec`** (the import system's module-finding machinery) — roughly 12 per single `execute()` call. This is upstream behavior in the `duckdb` package itself (plausibly probing for optional pandas/pyarrow/numpy integration on every query), not a bug introduced by any of this feature's own code (Tasks 1–7).

It compounds because `drspec scan` issues many separate `execute()` calls per function: `insert_artifact`, `queue_push`, and (now, since Task 7) edge/reference upserts in both caller and callee direction, each paying this same fixed per-call tax. Across ~500 functions with hundreds of edges, that's thousands of `execute()` calls, each carrying ~12 `find_spec` probes — which is where the full-scan multi-minute runtime actually comes from.

## Why this isn't "fix embedding," and isn't something to engineer around casually

This isn't a drspec logic bug — it's a cost inherent to how many separate DuckDB `execute()` round trips the current write path makes. The fix shape (not applied here, left for the dev) is almost certainly **reducing the number of `execute()` calls**, not making each one faster — e.g. batching inserts/upserts across multiple functions into fewer calls (DuckDB's `executemany` or Appender API are built for exactly this), rather than one `execute()` per row per table per function.

## Also addresses the on-demand-embedding question this investigation was triggered by

Before this was found, on-demand/usage-based embedding (only embed what gets searched) was floated as a way to dodge the scan-time cost. Given embedding was never actually the bottleneck, that redesign wouldn't have fixed the real problem — and it has its own real complications (laid out in conversation, not restated here): it needs §10's search to exist first to have a usage signal to key off of, the first semantic query of an untouched node becomes either slow or silently incomplete, and "search frequency" doesn't map cleanly onto edges the way it might onto nodes. Recommend not pursuing it unless batching `execute()` calls turns out not to close the gap.

**Refinement found while discussing this, worth recording even though still not recommended for now:** triggering embedding from `--semantic` hits themselves is circular and can never bootstrap — a never-embedded node can't be *found* by semantic search to begin with, since that requires comparing an embedding that doesn't exist yet. No amount of query volume fixes this; it's not "slow to warm up," it's "this specific trigger never fires." The version that actually avoids the circularity: trigger embedding from **any other direct access** — `deps get`, `source get`, `contract get`, a function appearing in a `scan` diff — none of which need the embedding to already exist to produce the signal. Over time, frequently-touched functions accumulate embeddings; code nobody ever looks up stays unembedded; `--semantic` searches over whatever's accumulated so far. Still shelved per the recommendation above — this is a design note for if/when it's revisited, not a new plan.

## Open for the dev

- ~~Confirm batching (fewer, larger `execute()` calls) actually closes the gap~~ — **done, see Task 9.1: it does not.** The real fix (Task 9.4) targets the `find_spec` probing directly instead.
- ~~Worth checking whether this same per-call overhead affects other write-heavy paths~~ — **done, see Task 9.7**: the fix is applied once in `connection.py`, so it covers every write path this process opens a connection for, not just `scan`.

# 12. Feedback: Make Embedding Opt-In, Not Default (Implemented — see Task 10)

**Status: implemented.** Task 10 implements the decision below as stated, resolves the staleness question with the user's own stated leaning (option 2), and found a real, previously-undiscovered bug in the Task 2-era DuckDB FK workaround along the way — see Task 10.3.

**Status (original, kept for context): user decision, not yet implemented.** Confirmed via profiling a real fresh scan of this project (`src/`, 495 functions): embedding (`embed_text` → ONNX inference) accounts for **~59% of total scan time** (63s of 106.6s) — the single largest cost, larger than Task 9's now-fixed DuckDB overhead. Unlike that overhead, this is genuine CPU-bound neural-network inference, not a bug — there's nothing to "fix" the same way, only a scope decision about whether to pay it unconditionally.

**Decision: `drspec scan` should not embed by default.** Embedding exists for exactly one purpose — `--semantic` search (§10/Task 8). A user who never uses `--semantic` currently pays the majority of their scan's runtime for a capability they may never touch. Mirrors the existing `--deep-scan` pattern already in this project: an expensive, optional capability gated behind an explicit flag, not silently mandatory.

This is **not** the on-demand/usage-based embedding idea discussed earlier in this document (and correctly set aside — it had a real circularity problem, see §11's addendum). This is simpler: a scan-time on/off toggle, applied uniformly to everything in that scan — no per-node usage tracking, no bootstrap problem, since there's no attempt to selectively decide *which* nodes deserve an embedding.

## Proposed shape

- New flag on `drspec scan`, e.g. `--embed`/`--no-embed` (default `--no-embed`), mirroring the existing `--queue`/`--no-queue` boolean-pair style already used on this command.
- When off (default): `_sync_nodes`/`_sync_edges`/`_sync_references` skip the `embed_text()` calls entirely for new/changed nodes, edges, and references — not just skip *writing* the result, skip *computing* it, since compute is the actual cost.

## Open question for the dev — staleness, not pre-decided here

**What happens to a node's existing embedding when it changes but `--embed` isn't passed on that scan?** Three options, not chosen here:
1. Leave the old embedding in place, untouched. **Actively wrong**, not just incomplete — it would silently represent stale code, inconsistent with this project's "never guess, never silently stale" discipline that's shown up repeatedly elsewhere (confidence tiers, the `--deep-scan` opt-in rationale itself).
2. Null it out when the node changes without `--embed`, explicitly marking "needs re-embedding" rather than leaving a misleading stale vector.
3. Leave it, but track staleness some other way (e.g. a boolean/timestamp) so `--semantic` search can filter out or flag stale matches.

Leaning toward (2) for consistency with how this project has handled every other "don't silently lie" decision so far, but flagging rather than deciding, since it has a real cost too — it means `--semantic` search coverage can only ever shrink between `--embed` runs, never silently hold onto outdated-but-present matches.

**Resolved — see Task 10.2: option (2), as leaned toward above.**

Also for the dev to check: does `drspec search --semantic` need to handle the "zero embeddings exist yet" case gracefully (clear message, not a crash or confusing empty result) given this makes that a normal, expected state rather than a transient one?

**Resolved — see Task 10.4: yes, and now does.**

# 13. Feedback: `drspec scan` Still Slow With Embedding Off — DB Round-Trip Volume (Implemented — see Task 11)

**Status: implemented.** Both leads below panned out (the `get_artifact` anomaly was fully redundant; batching the write path into one transaction does help here, unlike Task 9's unrelated finding) — and fixing the second one surfaced a real, previously-invisible correctness bug in two existing FK workarounds. See Task 11 for what was built, measured, and found.

**Status (original, kept for context): investigation only, not fixed.** With Task 10's opt-in embedding landed, a fresh full scan of this project (496 functions) takes **10.6s** on a quiet machine (load average 6.52/12 cores — a clean number, not confounded by contention this time). That's much better than before Task 10, but still more than this project's size should need.

## Profiled, not guessed

```
insert_artifact          496 calls   3.83s
upsert_dependency_edge   518 calls   2.63s
get_artifact            2216 calls   1.83s
queue_push                496 calls  1.66s
update_dependency_edge_hash 200 calls 0.96s
```

**~3,926 separate `conn.execute()` calls** for one scan of 496 functions, accounting for ~11s of the ~15s profiled total (profiling overhead inflates the absolute number; the 10.6s wall-clock figure above is the real one). This is distinct from both previously-fixed causes: not the `find_spec` probing (Task 9, fixed), not embedding compute (Task 10, now opt-in and off here).

## The specific anomaly worth chasing first

**`get_artifact` is called 2,216 times for only 496 functions — ~4.5 calls per function.** That ratio doesn't look like "one necessary lookup per function," it looks like the same artifact getting re-fetched from the DB multiple times across different stages of the sync pipeline (node-removal diffing, edge resolution, reference resolution, etc.) instead of being read once and reused in memory. If that's confirmed, it's a straightforward fix — cache/reuse within one scan pass rather than re-querying — and would likely be the highest-leverage single change here, given it's the largest call-count outlier by a wide margin.

## Open for the dev

- ~~Confirm the `get_artifact` re-fetch hypothesis~~ — **done, see Task 11.1: fully redundant, confirmed by tracing every call site.** All 7 only ever read `.code_hash`; replaced with a cache plus a lightweight single-column query.
- ~~Does the overall write path need batching into fewer, larger statements... worth re-testing batching against *this* bottleneck specifically~~ — **done, see Task 11.2: yes, it helps substantially (~4.8x on a synthetic benchmark), for a different reason than the find_spec case** (per-transaction-commit cost, not per-parameter). Implemented as one explicit transaction wrapping the whole scan write path. This also surfaced a real bug (Task 11.3) in two existing FK workarounds that assumed auto-commit between statements.

# 14. Feedback: Import Resolution Cache Is Per-Importing-File, Not Per-Target

**Status: investigation only, not fixed.** Found while reviewing Task 11's profile — not the same bug as the `get_artifact` one (that was fully redundant and is fixed), this is a narrower, smaller version of the same *shape* of gap.

## What's already correct

`_sync_caller_edges` (`graph_sync.py`) already caches import resolution **per importing file** (`import_cache: dict[file_path, ...]`) — confirmed by reading it, not assumed. A file's own imports are resolved once and reused across every function in that file. This is not the bug.

## The narrower gap

That cache is keyed by the *importing* file, not the *target* module/file being imported. Concretely: `_guess_module_file_path(conn, module_path)` and `_functions_by_name`/`get_artifacts_by_file(conn, target_file)` (the two DB lookups underneath `resolve_name_imports`/`resolve_module_imports`) have no cache spanning the whole scan — only within one importing file's own resolution pass. If files A and C both `import` something from file B (completely normal for any shared utility/db/core module in a real project), B's module-path lookup and its function list both get fetched from the DB twice, once while resolving A's imports and again for C's — not because the code forgot to cache, but because the cache's scope is one file too narrow.

Profiled impact on this project's own scan: `_guess_module_file_path` — 532 calls, 0.596s tottime; `get_artifacts_by_file` — 235 calls for only 67 files (~3.5x), 0.231s tottime. Together a meaningful but secondary chunk, nowhere near Task 11's `get_artifact` finding in scale — this is a polish item, not another dominant bottleneck.

## Proposed direction

Widen the existing cache from `dict[importing_file_path, ...]` to two scan-wide caches: `dict[module_path, target_file]` (for `_guess_module_file_path`) and `dict[target_file, {name: function_id}]` (for `_functions_by_name`), both built once per `_sync_caller_edges` call (already the right scope — same place the current per-file cache lives) and shared across every file in the batch, not reset per file.

## Open for the dev

- Confirm via profiling (not assumed) how much of the 532/235 calls are genuine cross-file duplicates vs. already-distinct lookups — if this project's own files mostly import distinct things from each other, the real-world win could be smaller than the call counts suggest.
- Worth doing in the same pass as any other Task 11-style investigation, not on its own — this alone isn't worth a dedicated task given its secondary scale.

# 15. Feedback: Partial/Incremental Scanning Works for Nodes, Not for Cross-Module Edges (Implemented — see Task 12)

**Status: implemented.** Task 12 chose the `--force`-flag direction of the two named below (not multi-path scanning — see Task 12.1 for why), implemented it, and resolved both open questions.

**Status (original, kept for context): investigation only, not fixed.** Raised by the user asking whether `drspec scan` supports scanning one module at a time for a large project ("scan some module and everything around it"). Answer: partially — nodes accumulate correctly, but cross-module edges have a real, demonstrated gap depending on scan order.

## What already works, confirmed by direct demonstration

Scanned `src/drspec/core/` alone (120 functions), then separately scanned `src/drspec/parsers/` (76 functions) into the same DB. Both accumulated correctly — 196 total artifacts, no duplicates, no overlap (this is Task 6 finding #1's path-normalization fix doing its job). Rescanning an already-scanned module reports `functions_unchanged`, not duplicate inserts. This part is solid.

## The gap

`core/` (e.g. `Scanner` instantiating `PythonParser`/`JavaScriptParser`/`CppParser`) calls into `parsers/`. Scanned in that order — `core/` first, `parsers/` second — **zero cross-module edges exist afterward**, confirmed by direct query. Rescanning `core/` again, now that `parsers/` exists in the DB, still produces **zero new edges** (`functions_unchanged: 120`, `edges_inserted: 0`) — because `core/`'s functions are unchanged (same `code_hash`), and the "unchanged → skip entirely" early-exit (§4 step 3) fires *before* any call-resolution logic runs. There's no mechanism that says "this function's calls might resolve differently now that more of the project has been scanned" — the skip is purely hash-based, blind to what else exists in the DB.

Also confirmed: `drspec scan` only accepts a **single** path argument (`path: Optional[Path]`, not a list) — so there's no way to scan two specific, non-adjacent modules together in one invocation to sidestep the ordering problem either. The only current workaround is scanning a common parent directory covering both, which for a genuinely large, interconnected project could mean scanning far more than the user actually wanted to, defeating the point of "choose which to scan."

## Why this matters for the use case it was asked about

For a large project, "scan just the module I'm working on" is exactly the scenario most likely to hit this: that module almost certainly calls into other modules that haven't been scanned recently (or ever, in a fresh `_drspec/`). Those calls will silently resolve to nothing and stay that way indefinitely, with no error or warning — the scan reports success either way.

## Not proposing a fix here — just scoping the shape of one

- A `--force`/`--rehash` style flag that treats specified (or all) functions as "changed" regardless of hash, forcing their calls to re-resolve against the DB's current state — closest to the project's existing "rebuild rather than patch staleness" philosophy (§7), just scoped smaller than a full rebuild.
- Multi-path support on `drspec scan` (`path: list[Path]`) so related modules can be scanned together in one invocation, guaranteeing all their nodes exist before any of their edges get resolved.
- Both are real, separate pieces of work — not proposing either as decided, just naming the shape so this isn't just "known broken" with no path forward.

## Open for the dev

- ~~Does this interact with `--deep-scan`'s existing staleness trade-off, or is it a genuinely separate problem?~~ — **resolved, see Task 12.3: confirmed separate, as leaned.** `--force` widens *which functions* get resolution re-attempted; `--deep-scan` widens *which resolution steps* run. Orthogonal and composable.
- ~~Worth deciding whether this is acceptable as a known, documented limitation... or worth actually fixing~~ — **resolved, see Task 12: fixed, via `--force`.**

# 16. Feedback: Interactive Graph Visualization (Static HTML, VS Code's Built-In Preview) (Implemented — see Task 13)

**Status: implemented**, with one deliberate deviation from the literal proposal (no `vis.js` — see Task 13.1) and all four open questions resolved (Tasks 13.2-13.5).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## Decided already, in conversation, not re-litigated here

- **No custom VS Code extension.** VS Code already ships a built-in local-HTML preview ("Simple Browser" / "Open in Integrated Browser," bundled by default, no install needed) — right-click a generated HTML file → opens in an embedded panel inside the editor. Solves "stay in the editor" without a separate extension/release channel.
- **Model: a single static HTML file**, following Graphify's approach (researched directly, not guessed — their `graph.html` is a standalone file using the `vis.js` library, no server, opened via `file://`). Reuses what's already built (`artifacts`, `dependencies`, `code_references`) rather than inventing new storage.

## Proposed data + visual mapping

**Nodes** = functions (`artifacts`): colored by `status`, reusing the exact mapping `deps plot`'s matplotlib renderer already uses (VERIFIED green, NEEDS_REVIEW yellow, PENDING gray, STALE orange, BROKEN red) — don't invent a second color scheme for the same concept.

**Edges** = `dependencies` (`calls`) + `code_references` (`references`), combined, mirroring `deps get`'s existing default (§9/Task 7.7 — showing both by default, since hiding `references` behind anything was the exact problem that feature fixed). Proposed visual distinction:
- Relation (`calls` vs `references`): different line style (e.g. solid vs dashed), not color — color is already spoken for by node status.
- Confidence (`EXTRACTED`/`INFERRED`/`AMBIGUOUS`): edge opacity or a third line style, so an uncertain `--deep-scan`-only edge is visibly different from a certain one, not just data you'd have to click to discover.

**Interactions** (matching what Graphify's own viewer already provides, confirmed via research, not assumed): click a node → sidebar with full detail (signature, file path, status, confidence of its edges); click an edge → the `call_site`/`reference_site` text; a search/filter box, reusing §10's `drspec search` matching logic rather than building a second one.

## Offline-first, consistent with this project's existing values

Vendor `vis.js` **inline** in the generated HTML (bundled, not CDN-loaded) — this project has held "no network call, fully local" as a value since §1/§5/Task 2 (choosing `fastembed` over `sentence-transformers` specifically to avoid a dependency chain, embedding model cached locally, etc.). A CDN-loaded viewer would quietly break that the first time someone opens the file offline.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~New command, or an extension of `deps plot`?~~ — **resolved, see Task 13.2: extension.** `drspec deps plot --html`; the existing matplotlib PNG path is untouched and stays the default.
- ~~Whole-project graph vs. centered-on-a-function view, or both?~~ — **resolved, see Task 13.3: centered view only**, reusing the existing `--depth`/center-function model as-is. No whole-project HTML view built.
- ~~Does this also need a standalone `graph.json` export?~~ — **resolved, see Task 13.4: not built.** The HTML's embedded inline JSON is sufficient for the scope this task covered.
- ~~Should `--semantic` search be reachable from inside the viewer?~~ — **resolved, see Task 13.5: no — out of scope**, exactly the "scope creep" this question itself flagged. Plain client-side substring filtering instead.

# 17. Feedback: Live Backend-Connected Graph Viewer (`drspec serve`) (Implemented — see Task 14)

**Status: implemented.** All five open questions resolved — most notably, direct empirical testing (not the "likely for free" hope this write-up's own §17 text used) found that read-only vs. read-write is not just the safer choice but a hard DuckDB concurrency *requirement* for this to work at all. See Task 14.1.

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

Task 13's HTML viewer (§16) is a one-shot static snapshot: `drspec deps plot --html` embeds the graph's node/edge data as inline JSON at generation time (`render_graph_html`). There's no way to refresh it against the DB's current state, and nothing a click in the page does can reach back into the backend — a "refresh" button could only reload the same static file (same stale data), and clicking a node can only show detail that was already embedded when the file was generated. Raised directly: "can we interact with webview builtin in vscode and like do action to interact with data, like refresh, select a node then it refresh in backend or something like that?"

## Why the static approach can't do this

VS Code's "Open Preview" / "Simple Browser" for a local HTML file (§16's chosen viewing mechanism) is a plain iframe over the file's own content, not a privileged webview with a `postMessage` channel to an extension host — that channel only exists inside a real, custom-built VS Code extension, a direction §16 explicitly decided against. A `file://` page has no process on the other end to call back into.

## Decided already, in conversation, not re-litigated here

Two ways to get a real backend-interaction channel were named: (1) a small local HTTP server the page's JS can `fetch()` against, reached via `http://localhost:PORT` instead of a `file://` path; (2) a full custom VS Code extension using `vscode.postMessage` to bridge into the extension host. **Chose (1).** Reasoning given: smaller lift, and "localhost-only" is a much smaller departure from this project's established "no network call, fully local" value (§1/§5/Task 2's `fastembed`-over-`sentence-transformers` choice, Task 13.1's no-CDN `vis.js` decision) than either a real external network call or committing to build/maintain a VS Code extension — a separate release/marketplace channel, already ruled out once in §16.

## Proposed shape (not fully decided — see open questions below)

- New `drspec serve` command: starts a small HTTP server bound to `127.0.0.1` only (never `0.0.0.0`), serving the same graph data `deps plot --html` already computes. At minimum a `GET /graph?function_id=...&depth=...&direction=...` endpoint returning the same node/edge JSON shape `render_graph_html` already builds — reusing `build_dependency_graph`/`layout_graph` directly, not a second implementation of either.
- The HTML viewer gains a "Refresh" button: re-`fetch`es `/graph` for the currently-centered function and re-renders, instead of requiring a brand-new `deps plot --html` CLI invocation and file regeneration.
- Clicking a node could re-center the view on it (`fetch` a new `/graph?function_id=<clicked node>`), not just show the sidebar detail already embedded — the "select a node then it refreshes" half of the ask.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~Server lifecycle: block or daemonize?~~ — **resolved, see Task 14.2: blocks, Ctrl-C to stop.** No daemonization.
- ~~Port choice/collision handling?~~ — **resolved, see Task 14.3: fixed default (8787) + `--port` flag**, `--port 0` for OS-assigned. A bind failure is a clean CLI error, not a traceback.
- ~~Read-only, or does it extend to writes?~~ — **resolved, see Task 14.1/14.4: read-only — and this turned out to not just be the safer choice, but a hard requirement.** Verified directly: a read-write connection held open blocks every other process from opening the file at all; read-only does not block a separate writer. A write-capable server would have broken `drspec scan` outright for the server's whole runtime.
- ~~Does this replace `deps plot --html`'s static file, or do both stay?~~ — **resolved, see Task 14.5: both stay.** `drspec serve` is purely additive.

# 18. Feedback: Make drspec the Agent's Default via Three Layers (MCP Server + Hooks + Skills, Graphify-style) (Implemented — see Task 15)

**Status: implemented.** All five open questions resolved (Task 15.2-15.6); see Task 15 for what was actually built, including one decision this write-up didn't anticipate at all (hand-rolling the MCP protocol instead of using the official SDK, forced by a Python-version conflict found during implementation — Task 15.1).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task.

## The gap

The standing instruction "prefer `drspec` over grep when analyzing this codebase" currently lives only as a memory note (`feedback_dogfood_drspec.md`). That's a soft, text-based reminder the agent has to actively recall before every single action — and it was already violated once in this same session (a quick `grep -n` for a few keywords in one file, see the memory's own "Recurrence (2026-11-04)" entry). Memory alone doesn't change what tools are actually reachable or convenient in the moment; it only hopes the agent remembers.

## Researched directly, not guessed: how Graphify actually does this

Confirmed via web search (not assumed): Graphify doesn't rely on an agent "remembering" to use it. It installs three layered mechanisms together:
1. **MCP server** — exposes the knowledge graph as real MCP tools (query a symbol, explore the graph) that show up in the agent's own tool list, same tier as Bash/Read/Grep. For Claude Code specifically, a `.mcp.json` at the project root registers it.
2. **Hooks** (`.claude/settings.json`) — `Stop`, `SessionStart`, and `PreToolUse` hooks that automatically pull graph context in at the right moments, without the agent needing to decide to ask for it.
3. **Skills** (`.claude/skills/`) — packaged instructions for how/when to use the graph tools well.

The MCP server is the strongest lever of the three: it removes the "remember to prefer X" problem entirely, since there's nothing to choose between — a graph-query tool just sits in the tool list next to grep, structurally preferable rather than remembered-to-be-preferable. Hooks and skills are real but secondary reinforcement, not the primary mechanism.

## Decided already, in conversation, not re-litigated here

Build the same three-layer approach for `drspec`, matching Graphify's shape rather than relying on memory alone.

## Proposed shape for each layer

1. **MCP server**: wrap the existing query-side CLI logic (`drspec search`, `drspec deps get`/`plot`, `drspec deps get` for callers/callees, function lookups) as MCP tools — reusing the same underlying Python functions the CLI commands already call (`search_artifacts`, `build_dependency_graph`, etc.), not a second implementation of the logic. Registered via a `.mcp.json` at the project root, likely using the `mcp` Python SDK.
2. **Hooks**: a `PreToolUse` hook on `Bash` (and the dedicated `Grep` tool) that asks for approval when a grep-family command runs in this repo, pointing at the drspec equivalent — this is the hook already scoped earlier in this same discussion, just now explicitly framed as layer 2 of 3, not a standalone fix. Possibly also a `SessionStart` hook priming awareness of drspec's capabilities at the start of each session, mirroring Graphify's.
3. **Skills**: a `.claude/skills/` entry documenting drspec's capabilities and when to reach for which command (search vs. deps vs. scan vs. serve), so the packaged guidance isn't only the terser memory note.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~**MCP tool scope**: expose every CLI command, or just the read-only/analysis ones (`search`, `deps get`/`plot`, read-side `queue get`)?~~ — **resolved, see Task 15.2: just two, `search` and `deps_get`** — not even all of the read-only ones. `deps plot`/`queue get` left out too (file output; out of the gap's actual scope, respectively); `drspec scan` excluded as this question itself anticipated.
- ~~**Transport**: stdio vs. Streamable HTTP?~~ — **resolved, see Task 15.3: stdio**, as leaned.
- ~~**DB concurrency for the MCP server**~~ — **resolved, see Task 15.4: reused Task 14's exact lesson, as leaned** — fresh read-only connection per tool call, never held open.
- ~~**Hooks settings-file scope**: committed vs. personal/gitignored?~~ — **resolved, see Task 15.5: committed `.claude/settings.json`** — this is repo-wide guidance, not a personal preference.
- ~~**Hook strictness**: hard `deny` vs. `ask`?~~ — **resolved, see Task 15.6: `ask`, as leaned** — preserves the documented escape hatch.
- ~~Picking up a concurrent `drspec scan`: does a fresh per-request query naturally see the new state, or is there a staleness concern?~~ — **resolved, see Task 14.1/14.6: yes, confirmed directly — but the "likely for free" framing in this question undersold the real risk.** A long-held connection on either side (not just a caching/staleness issue) would have actively broken concurrent access; the fix is structural (never hold a connection between requests), not just "query fresh."

# 19. Feedback: Call Resolver Misses Calls Through a Package Re-Export (and No CLI Way to Audit Zero-Caller Functions Without Touching the DB) (Implemented — see Task 16)

**Status: implemented.** Both findings resolved (Task 16.1/16.6), plus two unrelated real bugs caught by dogfooding the fix against this repo's own real codebase instead of stopping at synthetic unit tests (Task 16.3/16.4).

**Status (original, kept for context): proposal, not a task yet.** Written up for review before it becomes a task. Two related findings, both from the same investigation (checking whether `hasher.py`'s `normalize_code` is dead code).

## Finding 1: real call-resolution bug, not a small edge case

`deps_get`/`drspec deps get` reports **zero callers** for `src/drspec/db/queries.py::get_artifact` — but it's actually called in at least three real places: `cli/commands/deps.py`, `src/drspec/mcp_server.py`, and `visualization/grapher.py` (all confirmed by directly reading the source, not assumed).

Root cause isolated with a clean positive/negative control pair, both checked via `deps_get` (not the DB directly):

- **Negative** (misses the call): `get_artifact` is imported in all three callers as `from drspec.db import get_artifact` — through `db/__init__.py`'s re-export, not directly from the module that defines it (`db/queries.py`). `deps_get` on `get_artifact` returns zero callers.
- **Positive** (resolves correctly): `hasher.py::compute_hash` is imported directly from its defining submodule — `from drspec.core.hasher import compute_hash` in `graph_sync.py`, no package-level indirection. `deps_get` on `compute_hash` correctly returns its real callers (`_sync_nodes`, `sync_scan_results`).

So the call resolver's import-tracking (§3.5 Step 1) doesn't follow a name through a package `__init__.py` re-export to the submodule that actually defines it. Given this project's own convention is almost entirely `from drspec.db import X` / `from drspec.core import X` (package-level, matching every `__init__.py`'s `__all__` list surveyed in this same investigation), this isn't a narrow edge case — it likely invalidates "zero callers" as a signal for a large fraction of this project's own real cross-module call graph, undermining exactly the kind of "is this safe to remove" analysis §1's goal states this whole feature exists for.

## Finding 2: no CLI/MCP way to list zero-caller functions, without reaching into the DB directly

Investigating this required repeatedly asking `deps_get` about one function at a time. There's no `drspec` command or MCP tool to ask "list every function with zero callers project-wide" directly — the honest way to get that list right now is a raw SQL query against `_drspec/contracts.db`, bypassing the CLI/MCP surface entirely. Caught directly in this session: reaching for that raw-query shortcut instead of drspec's own interface is exactly the kind of workaround this project has been trying to eliminate (see `feedback_dogfood_drspec.md`) — if getting an answer requires touching the DB directly, that's a real capability gap to fix, not a workaround to quietly take.

**Important dependency between the two findings**: a "list zero-caller functions" feature built *before* Finding 1 is fixed would be actively misleading — it would report `get_artifact` and everything else imported the conventional `from drspec.db import X` way as "possibly dead," when it's actually heavily used. Finding 1 should be fixed first, or this feature's output should carry an explicit caveat until it is.

## Open for the dev — real decisions, not pre-made here

- ~~Finding 1's fix shape: follow re-exports through `__init__.py` (how many levels deep?), or extend same-package-level matching to recognize `__init__.py`'s `__all__` as equivalent to a direct import?~~ — **resolved, see Task 16.1/16.2: the latter, one level only.** `project_facts.py` stays pure DB aggregation (its own stated property) rather than gaining on-demand `__init__.py` parsing — a package directory's direct children become the search scope, with the same "never guess, require uniqueness" discipline already used everywhere else in that file.
- ~~Finding 2's shape: exclude CLI entry points/dunder methods automatically, or document the caveat and let the human filter? Flag `__all__`-exported-but-unused as a distinct category?~~ — **resolved, see Task 16.6: dunder methods excluded automatically (unambiguous signal); CLI entry points and everything else left in, with a caveat in every response and `--path`/`path_prefix` as the escape hatch. The `__all__`-category split was not built** — would need the same on-demand parsing 16.1 deliberately avoided.
- ~~Whether Finding 2 is worth building at all before Finding 1 is fixed~~ — **resolved: built after, per the dependency — and confirmed directly, not just assumed:** `drspec deps unused` on this repo correctly excludes `get_artifact` only because Finding 1 was already fixed.

# 30. Feedback: Switch Output From JSON to Graphify-Style Labeled Plain Text, One Shared Form Across Every Command (Implemented — see Task 27)

**Status: implemented.** All six open questions resolved (Task 27.1-27.5); then directly extended by the user to the MCP tool surface too, beyond this write-up's own CLI-only framing (Task 27.6-27.7).

## The gap

Raised directly, while looking at a real `drspec search` response: JSON's per-object key-name repetition is a real, measurable cost (confirmed by actually building before/after versions of one real response and counting bytes: 1,424 → 1,000 by dropping fields already redundant with `function_id` → 801 by also switching the match list to a columns+rows shape — see the worked example earlier in this review). That led to checking what Graphify (the tool this project's whole three-layer agent-default system, §18, was explicitly modeled on) actually does for its own tool output, rather than assuming JSON is the obvious choice. Checked directly (not assumed): Graphify's docs and blog confirm all 10 of its MCP tools return **labeled plain text**, not JSON — e.g. `get_node`'s real output:

```
Node: want_bytes()
  ID: src_itsdangerous_encoding_want_bytes
  Source: src/itsdangerous/encoding.py L11
  Type: code
  Community: Signer
  Degree: 23
```

Graphify's own stated reasoning: this "prioritizes readability for agents rather than JSON serialization." That's a real precedent for the opposite of drspec's current default (JSON, `--pretty` as the human-readable opt-in), not just an abstract alternative.

The user's proposal, stated directly: adopt the same style — labeled plain text, keywords kept as short as the data allows (compact, not cryptic), and critically, **one shared form across every CLI command**, so an agent learns the shape once instead of a different JSON schema per command (today's actual state: `scan`'s response shape, `search`'s, `deps get`'s, and `status`'s share nothing structurally).

## What "one shared form" can and can't mean here

Worth being precise about before handing this to the dev, since taken too literally it doesn't work: drspec's commands return genuinely different *kinds* of data — a flat stats summary (`scan`), a list of records (`search`), a graph of nodes+edges (`deps get`), a single record (`contract get`), a deeply nested multi-section summary (`status`). Forcing all of these into one identical schema would mean either flattening a graph into a list (losing the edge relationships) or padding a single-record response with list machinery it doesn't need. Graphify itself doesn't do this either — `get_node` and `shortest_path` and `graph_stats` are structurally different outputs. What Graphify actually shares across all 10 tools is a common *style* (the `Label: value` idiom, consistent indentation, consistent terseness) — not an identical schema. That's almost certainly the realistic target here too: one consistent grammar/vocabulary (the same label means the same thing everywhere — `ID:` always means `function_id`, never `function_name` in one command and `function_id` in another), applied consistently to each command's own naturally-shaped data, rather than one literal structure every response must fit.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Label vocabulary.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.3: a fixed shared dict in `cli/output.py`** for the common cross-command fields, falling back to the pre-existing generic title-casing for anything not in it — not an exhaustive hand-built table.
- ~~**"Short as possible" vs. Graphify's own example.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.3: full words, Graphify's own real style** — no cryptic abbreviations; compactness comes from the label table's consistency, not truncation.
- ~~**Is this the default, or an opt-in alongside JSON?**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.1: yes, the new default** — `--json` survives as the explicit machine-parseable opt-in; `--pretty` removed entirely (redundant once plain text is the default, not kept as a shim).
- ~~**Scope/migration cost, named plainly.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.2/27.5: contained to `cli/output.py` itself** (one shared renderer, no per-command changes) for the implementation; the real cost landed entirely on the test suite (~20 files, 179 `CliRunner` call sites plus 2 subprocess-based files), done all at once via a scripted, verified AST-based migration.
- ~~**Does this touch `drspec.api`?**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.4: confirmed no**, directly (import inspection, not assumed).
- ~~**Nested/graph-shaped responses specifically.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 27.2: a `--calls--> target`/`<--calls-- target` arrow notation** for `deps get`'s edges, mirroring Graphify's own quoted example; `status`'s multi-section summary uses the same generic recursive dict rendering, just with the label table applied.
- **Not originally asked here, resolved anyway by direct user instruction mid-implementation: does this extend to the MCP server too?** — **yes, see Task 27.6/27.7** — every MCP tool now defaults to the same renderer, with a per-call `format: "json"` opt-in mirroring the CLI's own `--json`.

# 31. Feedback: `search --lines` Repeats the Whole-File Hit List Under Every Matching Function in That File (Implemented — see Task 29)

**Status: implemented.** The recommended direction was taken in full: line mode is a live search over files, not a decoration on function matches. All three open questions resolved (Task 29.1-29.3).

## The gap

Hit while investigating the `deps plot --output` bug (Task 28): `search "output_path" --path src/drspec --lines` (native MCP tool, same logic as the CLI) matched 5 functions in `visualization/grapher.py`, and each one carried the identical full-file list of 15 line hits — the same 15 lines printed 5 times, then the same again for `plotter.py`'s 4 functions. The result was ~10× longer than the information in it, and the first useful answer (`deps.py:257`, `mcp_server.py:1305`) was buried in the middle.

Cause: `--lines` reports every occurrence in the *whole file* per matched function (§26's deliberate choice, needed so module-level lines outside any function body are reachable), but a match is still emitted per function, so N matching functions in one file means N copies of the same file-level list.

## A second problem with the same root, and a recommended direction

The duplication comes from one design choice: `--lines` is a live, whole-file search attached to function matches. The same choice has a second effect. A file is only searched if some function body already contains the text, so a line that exists only at module level is still missed. Checked directly: a search for `_TOOL_HANDLERS: dict` returned 0 matches, although that line exists in `mcp_server.py`, because no function body contains it. Module-level hits were the main reason for `--lines` (§26, §28), so this is not an edge case.

**Recommended direction: make line mode a live search over files, not a decoration on function matches.** When `--lines` or a context flag is given, search the live contents of the files drspec already knows about, narrowed by `--path` and `--include`, and return a flat, grep-style list of `file:line:text`. Where the file is not marked possibly stale, label each hit with its enclosing function (from the scanned line ranges); otherwise leave the hit unlabelled rather than risk labelling it wrongly. Context blocks (`-A`/`-B`/`-C`) are per file regardless, since they cross function boundaries. This removes the duplication and finds module-level-only hits.

Costs, named plainly:
- Each call reads every file in scope. That should be fast for a `--path`-limited search but is slower than a database query, so a scope limit or a cap on files may be needed for a whole-repo search.
- It changes what `--lines` means: the result is a list of lines rather than a list of functions, so anything reading a per-match `lines` field has to change.
- Files drspec doesn't index (docs, config) are still out of reach.

**Cheaper interim step, if the full change is too big:** group by file, printing each file's line hits once with the matched functions listed beside it. This fixes the duplication only. It does not fix the missed module-level hits, and it changes the response shape in the same way.

Not built or tested; this is reasoning from the code and the failed search above.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Where the line list belongs.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 29.1: neither option.** Line mode no longer starts from function matches at all, so there is no per-function list to dedupe: a flat `hits` list (or per-file merged `blocks`), each line once.
- ~~**Attribution as a feature.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 29.2: yes** — every hit carries its innermost enclosing function, except in files that have drifted since the last scan.
- ~~**Both surfaces.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 29.1: one implementation** (`core/live_search.py`) serves the CLI and the MCP tool; context blocks use the same flat, per-file shape.

# 32. Feedback: Dead and Test-Only Code to Clean Up (Implemented — see Task 30)

**Status: implemented for groups A and B; groups C and D deliberately deferred** (Task 30.5).

## How this was found

Ran `unused` over the files changed this round: `mcp_server.py`, `worker_ipc.py`, `staleness_worker.py`, `cli/output.py`, `cli/commands/search.py`, `db/staging.py`, `db/connection.py`, `db/queries.py` and `api/`. Each candidate was triaged with `deps_get` (callers) plus a text check for module-level imports and re-exports. That check used `grep`, because drspec can't see module-level import lines (the §28 gap, not a new one). Scope is only those files: the project-wide `unused` list is about 1,700 entries, mostly the blind spots in group D below, so a full cleanup needs the same triage.

## A. Unused everywhere in production — delete

- `db/connection.py::get_connection_context` — no caller in `src/`, one test file only, not re-exported.
- `db/queries.py::get_reference_edge` and `get_all_config` — no caller anywhere, not even in tests. They exist only as an import and an `__all__` entry in `db/__init__.py`. Delete both and drop those two lines.

## B. Used only by tests — decide: delete with their tests, or keep on purpose

All are re-exported from `db/__init__.py`; none has a production caller.

- `queue_complete`, `queue_retry`, `queue_clear_completed`. Checked end to end in an isolated project: `contract_save` moves the queue item to `COMPLETED` without calling `queue_complete`, so completion happens on some other path. Nothing calls `queue_retry` in production, so no path returns a failed item to `PENDING`.
- `get_reasoning_traces`, `count_vision_findings`, `list_contracts`, `query_contract`, `query_contracts`, `search_contracts`.
- `insert_dependency`. Eight test files use it to build fixtures, but production edges are written by `sync_scan_results`. If kept, it belongs in a test helper rather than the public DB surface.

## C. Public library API, not dead — keep, but stop it showing up

`api/__init__.py`'s `configure`, `query_contract`, `run_verification`, `list_queue` and `get_dependencies` show as test-only because they exist for external callers of `drspec.api`. Do not delete them. Worth a way to mark them so `unused` stops listing them.

## D. Resolver blind spots — not dead, do not delete

- The 24 `_tool_*` handlers in `mcp_server.py` are called through the `_TOOL_HANDLERS` dict.
- 20 `worker_ipc.py` methods (`WorkerConnection.*`, `_DirectConnection.*`, `_WorkerCursor.fetch*`, `WorkerIPCServer.start/stop/_accept_loop/_handle_client`) are called on instances or passed as thread targets.
- `staleness_worker._raise_system_exit` is passed to `signal.signal`.
- `cli/output.DrSpecEncoder.default` is a `json.JSONEncoder` hook.
- `search_command` is registered on the app.
- `mcp_server.run` shows as test-only, but `cli/commands/mcp_serve.py:29` calls `mcp_server.run(db_path)`. The resolver doesn't follow a call made through an imported module name.
- The `*.from_row` classmethods (21 `.from_row(` call sites exist) and the nested `get_edge_stats._counts` are almost certainly used; not individually verified.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Group B: delete or keep?**~~ — **resolved, see Task 30.2/30.3:** `db/__init__.py` is the internal layer (its docstring now says so; the public API is `drspec.api`), so unused entries went — except `queue_complete` (now used) and `get_reasoning_traces` (kept on purpose).
- **Reduce group D noise.** **Deferred, see Task 30.5:** it is a resolver change in the same family as §28's `reads` edge, which is approved but not built; doing it here would duplicate that work.
- **Group C: a marker or exclusion.** **Deferred, see Task 30.5:** needs a parser-level annotation or a project-level exclusion list; neither is a cleanup.

# 33. Feedback: Bugs Found Testing the Full MCP Surface and the Worker Lifecycle (Implemented — see Task 31)

**Status: all seven bugs fixed and covered by tests** (Task 31.1-31.7), plus one further startup race found while verifying item 4 (Task 31.4).

## How this was found

All 24 MCP tools were exercised through a real `drspec mcp-serve` process in isolated temp projects (a git repo with a bug-fix commit, so `learn_analyze` had something to analyse), plus start/stop stress runs against the worker. Every item below was reproduced; where a cause is inferred rather than observed, it says so.

## The bugs

1. **`learn stats` fails once any learning event exists** (CLI and MCP `learn_stats`). After a real, non-dry-run `learn_analyze`, `learn_stats` returns "The drspec worker closed the connection unexpectedly" every time. The query in flight (visible in the CLI traceback) is the recent-activity one, `CAST(created_at AS DATE)`, so its result contains a `date`. `worker_ipc._encode_value` only converts `datetime.datetime` and tuples/lists. Inferred, not captured: the encoding error escapes `_handle_client`'s `except OSError` and ends that client's session. It only shows with events present, because the query returns no rows on an empty table. The tests can't see it, since they run through `_DirectConnection`, which never serializes. Other DuckDB types (`Decimal`, `time`, `bytes`) are probably unhandled the same way.

2. **`learn analyze` credits functions the commit didn't change.** `learning/diff.py` (around line 524) matches functions to a commit by overlap with the whole hunk range, which includes git's context lines, using `new_start + new_count` (one past the last line). A commit that changed only `add` produced learning events for both `add` and `mul`.

3. **The plain-text renderer corrupts function IDs used as dictionary keys.** `cli/output.py::_label` falls back to `key.replace("_", " ").title()` for any key not in the label table, so `scan`'s `Resolution Reasons` and `Contracts Invalidated` print `Calc.Py::Add` instead of `calc.py::add`. An ID copied from that output into `deps_get` or `source_get` fails. `--json` and `format: json` are unaffected.

4. **A command run just after `worker stop` hangs 15 seconds, then fails** (4 of 4 trials). `get_worker_connection` (`worker_ipc.py`, around line 355) calls `spawn_worker_if_not_running` once; if the old worker is still alive, that does nothing, and the loop that follows only retries the connection and never spawns a replacement. The old worker took 3.1s to exit when idle and 1.1s after remediating a file. Result: `Could not reach the drspec worker ... (waited 15s)`, exit 1, and no worker afterwards. Related to item 5.

5. **`init --force` fails with a raw lock error while a worker is alive** (CLI and MCP). `init` is the one command that opens the database directly, but `--force` runs on an already-initialized project, where the worker owns the file. The message doesn't mention the worker. Without `--force`, `init` on an initialized project is fine.

6. **The test suite isn't isolated from the developer's own project state.** With a worker alive for the repo, 9 tests in `tests/unit/test_cli_commands.py` fail; with it stopped, all 32 pass (same file, nothing else changed). They use the real project through `Path.cwd()`; `test_success_exit_code` even runs `init` on the real repo. Since the worker now stays alive after any command, the normal state for someone using drspec on this repo is a red test run. Two timing-sensitive tests flagged earlier are also unchanged: the fixed `sleep(1.0)` at `test_worker_command.py:71` and the `< 2.0` assertions at `test_staleness_worker.py:265` and `:319`.

7. **`worker status` has no MCP tool**, although the "every command gets a tool" decision (§27) covers it: it is read-only and request/response shaped. An MCP-only agent can stop the worker but not check whether it is running.

## Open for the dev

- ~~**Should `worker stop` wait until the process has actually exited?**~~ — **resolved, see Task 31.4: yes, up to 10s**, and `get_worker_connection` also re-attempts the spawn inside its wait loop.
- ~~**A guard for serialization failures.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 31.1: yes** — an unencodable value now comes back as an error naming its type, and the client session survives.
- ~~**Test isolation.**~~ — **resolved, see Task 31.6:** the CLI tests run in a temp project; a real-process "stop, then immediately run a command" test exists.

# 34. Feedback: Five Problems Found in `init`, Line-Mode Search, `learn`, and `scan` (Implemented — see Task 32)

**Status: all five fixed and covered by tests** (Task 32.1-32.5).

## The problems, as reported

1. **MCP `init` with `force: true` wipes everything** (artifacts, contracts, queue, findings) and replies "already initialized". The CLI's `init --force` keeps the data.
2. **Wrong function labels in line-mode search** for a recently edited file, for about 10 seconds, until the worker's next poll.
3. **Unscanned files are skipped silently**: a file added after the last scan returns "0 hits" with no hint.
4. **`learn history` and `learn stats` print their data twice** on the CLI.
5. **`scan` output is unbounded**: about 2,262 of 2,280 lines were a per-function list, and the MCP result was too large to be shown.

Items 1, 2 and 3 are consequences of earlier work in this file (Tasks 29 and 31); items 4 and 5 predate it but became visible once plain text became the default output (§30).

# 35. Feedback: Wrong Line Numbers, Unbounded Long Lines, and Dead Code in `init.py` (Implemented — see Task 33)

**Status: all three fixed and covered by tests** (Task 33.1-33.3), plus one flaky-test class hardened (Task 33.4).

## The findings, as reported

1. **Line numbers are wrong in files containing form feeds or U+2028.** Line mode counted lines with `str.splitlines()`, which also splits on those characters.
2. **A single very long line is returned whole**: a minified bundle is one line, and one hit made a 300,161-character response.
3. **Two dead functions in `init.py`, and the `--force` help text did not describe what `--force` does.** (An earlier version of this line said the overwrite confirmation no longer exists. That was wrong: the prompt still exists and works; only the two helper functions were dead. See Task 33.3.)

# 36. Proposal: A Bounded Background Maintenance Worker, New-File Discovery, Search-Driven Queueing, Background Embedding, and Removal of the Contract Queue (Implemented — see Tasks 34 and 35)

**Status: implemented** — D2–D5 in Task 34, D1 in Task 35. The decisions below are kept as written; what the developer settled, and what was measured along the way, is in "Resolved while building" at the end of this section.

**Decided by the user:** a 60 s interval; a small bounded batch per pass; search queues stale or new content for the worker; search records usage and the worker folds it into totals; the contract queue is removed entirely (as its own task); new-file discovery covers the whole project root; embedding runs in a short-lived child process.

**Also decided by the user, in a second pass:** a file that fails to parse goes to the back of the queue with a retry backoff; the order within a pass is search-queued, then drift, then new files; function-mode search checks the files of its own hits; search queues at most about 20 files per call; usage weights (search hit 1, `source_get`/`deps_get` about 5); (an embedding batch of about 200 per child launch — superseded in the fourth pass by throttled continuous embedding); `status` shows the backlog; the optional drift check in `source_get`/`deps_get` is accepted.

**Also decided by the user, in a third pass:** parsing outside the lock is *not* done now — build the 10-file budget first and split parsing from publishing only if acceptance test 1 still fails; a dormant worker is woken by any client request; background embedding gets a setting (a `config` command, default on); usage statistics decide both *which* items are embedded and in what order — the top N by a usage score that decays with age (half-life about 7 days), and an embedding is kept when its item falls out of the top N. (This replaced an earlier "order only" choice made in the same pass.)

**Also decided by the user, in a fourth pass:** background embedding is not done in fixed batches but by one low-priority child that runs while a backlog exists, throttled by a CPU cap, a thread cap, a system-load pause and a low-memory pause (all configurable), and exits when the backlog is empty.

**Also decided by the user, in a fifth pass:** the embedding model is downloaded automatically on first use.

Nothing in this section is left as an unconfirmed recommendation.

## Two queues, named once and used consistently

The word "queue" had been used for two different things. This section uses these names:

| | **Re-scan queue** (`staleness_queue` table) | **Contract queue** (`queue` table) |
|---|---|---|
| Holds | file paths | functions |
| Entry means | "this file changed, re-parse it" | "this function needs a contract written or rewritten" |
| Added by | the worker's drift check | `drspec scan` only (`publish_scan_results`) |
| Consumed by | the background worker | the agent, via `queue_next` |
| Result | the graph (nodes, edges) is updated | a contract is saved |

Both are tables in `_drspec/contracts.db`; neither is in memory. Search's `Possibly Stale: Yes` comes from the re-scan queue. A function's own status (PENDING, VERIFIED, NEEDS_REVIEW, STALE, BROKEN) is a third, separate thing: `Status: PENDING` in a search result says nothing about whether the function is on the contract queue.

## What was verified (each item reproduced or read in code)

1. **Remediation blocks every other request.** `WorkerIPCServer._op_remediate` (`worker_ipc.py`) runs `_remediate_queued_files` entirely inside `with self._lock`, and every non-transactional `_op_execute` takes the same lock. Reproduced: 300 small files rewritten while the worker was alive; a `status` call stalled for **4.2 s** (baseline 6 ms). Files in real projects are larger, so the stall grows with file count and size.
2. **A pass is unbounded.** `_remediate_queued_files` parses every queued file one after another, then publishes once. A `git checkout` touching N files means N sequential parses in one pass.
3. **Each publish copies the whole database.** `staged_scan` runs `CHECKPOINT`, `shutil.copy2` of the database file and an fsync. That cost is per pass and grows with database size, so the right unit is a small *batch* per pass, not one file per pass.
4. **A file that fails to parse is retried on every pass, forever** ("leave queued — try again next pass"). With a per-pass budget, a few broken files at the front of the queue would starve the rest.
5. **New files are never discovered.** `_detect_drift` iterates `list_scanned_files` only. The "Searched, but not in the graph yet" hint therefore has no automatic remedy; only a manual `drspec scan` helps.
6. **The worker's re-scan destroys embeddings.** `sync_scan_results` defaults to `embed=False`, which clears (NULL) the embedding of every changed node, edge and reference; `_remediate_queued_files` passes no `embed`. Reproduced: `scan --embed`, edit `add()`, wait for the worker, then `search --semantic` — `add` no longer appears in the results; the untouched `greet` still does.
7. **Client activity does not wake a dormant worker.** After 120 s without work the loop sleeps 300 s per pass, and only the worker's own passes reset that. Anything search queued could wait up to 5 minutes.
8. **Search cannot act on what it sees.** It is read-only in behaviour, though not in capability: `get_worker_connection` does `del read_only` (the worker owns the connection), and `enqueue_staleness` is a plain SQL insert. Only `_DirectConnection` (test mode) honours `read_only`.
9. **Embedding model cost, measured:** about **+270 MB** resident once loaded, 12 s for the first call (on a loaded machine), about 3 ms per short embedding afterwards. The cost is memory, not CPU.

## Decisions

### D1. Remove the contract queue entirely (its own task, after D2-D5)

Contracts are produced on demand, when the agent needs an explanation of a node: it builds the contract with the contract roles and saves it. The agent finds functions lacking one by function status (`search --status PENDING` / `STALE`), not through a queue. The worker never feeds the contract queue, which removes the old gap where the worker re-parsed a new or changed function but never queued it.

To remove: the MCP tools `queue_next`, `queue_peek`, `queue_get`, `queue_prioritize`; the `drspec queue` CLI; `queue_push` in `publish_scan_results`; `queue_complete` in `contract save` (CLI and MCP `_tool_contract_save`); the queue counts in `status`; the `queue` table and its tests; and every reference in `agents/librarian.md` (5), `agents/proposer.md` (2), `agents/helpers/cli.md` (4) and `README.md` (5). Contract *invalidation* (marking a contract STALE when its function changes) is separate and stays.

Edges do not get contracts: `contracts` is keyed on a function, and `dependencies` has no contract column. That is unchanged.

*For the dev:* the schema header says "Rebuild (no migrations)", so decide whether the `queue` table is dropped on `init_schema` or simply left unused. Say which in the task.

### D2. A cheap, bounded worker pass

- **Interval 60 s** (was 10 s), configurable. Dormant behaviour stays (it already backs off when idle).
- **Detection stays a stat sweep of every scanned file** (no reads, no hashing). It is cheap. Measure it on a large repo; if it is not cheap enough, rotate through a slice per tick with a saved cursor — a fallback, not the plan.
- **Remediation budget: 10 files per pass**, configurable. The unit is files, not nodes: a re-scan replaces all of a file's functions at once.
- **Order within a pass:** files queued by search first, then drift found by the worker, then newly discovered files.
- **Parsing stays inside the lock for now.** The 10-file budget already shrinks the stall from seconds to a fraction of a second (parsing is roughly 15-30 ms per file, an estimate from two timings; the publish copies the whole 46.8 MB database and has not been timed). Build the budget first and run acceptance test 1. **Only if some call still waits more than about 1 s**, split parsing from publishing (parse before taking the lock; hold it only for the publish and reconnect). Parsing and publishing are tangled in `_remediate_queued_files`, so this is not a small change — hence not done pre-emptively.
- **A file that fails to parse goes to the back of the queue** with a retry counter and a growing wait (1, 2, 4 ... minutes, capped at about 15), so it cannot hold the front or burn CPU every pass. Editing the file again (its mtime or size changes) resets the wait and it is retried on the next pass, since the edit may have fixed it.
- **Visibility:** `status` and `worker_status` report the backlog (files pending re-scan, files pending discovery, nodes/edges pending embedding), so a partially built graph is not mistaken for a complete one.

### D3. New-file discovery: the whole project root

The worker walks the project root (the parent of `_drspec`), using `Scanner.iter_supported_files` so the scanner's own rules apply (`DEFAULT_IGNORES`: `.git`, `node_modules`, `venv*`, `.venv*`, `dist`, `build`, caches, and so on, and supported extensions only). The walk is incremental with a saved cursor and is bounded per pass. A file it finds goes onto the re-scan queue with reason `new_file`; the normal budget then applies. Accepted trade-off: a folder the user deliberately left out of an earlier scan is pulled in too.

*For the dev:* nothing stores a scan root today, and the worker inherits the working directory of whoever spawned it. Derive the root from the database path and say what happens with a `--db` override.

### D4. Search queues what it finds stale or new

While searching, drspec records what it sees on the way, so the worker can handle it:

- **Function-mode search** stats only the files of its hits (cheap, bounded by the limit; a "hit" is one returned result). Function mode reads the index, not the disk, so today `Possibly Stale` appears only after the worker's sweep has queued the file — a blind window of up to 60 s with the new interval, up to 5 minutes when the worker is dormant. The search's own check closes that window: a file whose size or mtime differs from `scanned_files` is marked `Possibly Stale: Yes` **in that same result** and is also queued for the worker to re-parse. The check is done by the search; the queue only carries the fix. A new function in a changed file stays invisible to function mode until the worker re-scans (line mode already covers that case).
- **Line-mode search** already computes its drifted and unscanned file lists; queue those.
- The write is **best-effort**: one batched insert through the existing connection, at most about 20 files per call, errors swallowed, never slowing or failing the search. Skip it when the connection is genuinely read-only (`_DirectConnection`). Reasons: `search_drift`, `search_unscanned`.
- **Edges need nothing extra:** they come from re-parsing the file, so queueing the file covers them.
- **Any client request wakes a dormant worker.** Replace the loop's `time.sleep` with an `Event.wait(timeout)` that the IPC server sets when a request arrives, so a dormant worker runs a pass within seconds instead of up to 300 s. Keep at least about 5 s between passes so a burst of searches does not trigger a pass each time; after the wake the normal 60 s pace applies, and the worker goes dormant again when nobody uses drspec. (Waking only on an enqueue was rejected: the server would have to recognise an enqueue inside a generic SQL request.)
- Optional, the dev's call: `source_get` and `deps_get` return function bodies from the database, so the same one-file drift check applies to them.

### D5. Background embedding

- **Fix the destruction first.** A worker re-scan must never leave a changed node, edge or reference that *had* an embedding without one and without a plan to recompute it. Such an item is re-embedded by the background step below.
- **Which items get embedded: the top N by decayed usage (decided by the user).** Only the N highest-scoring nodes, edges and references are embedded, N configurable (suggested default 2,000, counted over nodes, edges and references together). Each item keeps a `score` and an `as_of` time. When the worker folds in new usage, the old score first decays and then the new weight is added: `score = score * 0.5 ** (elapsed / half_life) + new_weight`, with a configurable half-life (suggested 7 days). So heavy use long ago does not outrank moderate use now. The embedding order is highest current score first.
  - **Accepted consequence:** semantic search can return only items that have been used. An unused function becomes findable semantically only after something touches it (a text search, `source_get` or `deps_get`).
  - **Calibration:** this repo has 2,284 nodes and 2,582 edges and references (about 4,900 items). Embedding all of them would be roughly 7 MB of a 46.8 MB database, so a cap of 2,000 matters mainly on much larger repos.
- **An embedding is never deleted when its item falls out of the top N (decided by the user).** The embedded set can therefore grow past N over time, to every item that has ever been in the top N. At about 1.5 KB each the disk cost is small, and a once-used item stays findable by semantic search.
- **Execution: one throttled child process that runs while a backlog exists (decided by the user).** When there is something to embed, the worker starts a child that loads the model once (about 12 s, measured on a loaded machine), then embeds small fixed chunks (about 20 items) continuously, writes the vectors through the worker's connection, and **exits when the backlog is empty or has been idle for a few minutes**. The model never lives in the worker, and idle memory cost is zero. There is no batch size and no launch gap: the one-off model load is paid once per backlog, and the CPU is bounded by a throttle, not by how often it launches. The throttle varies the *sleep between chunks*, not the chunk size.
  - **Guards, all configurable through `drspec config`** (full set chosen by the user; none needs a new dependency):
    1. **Lowest OS priority** (`os.nice(19)` on Unix), so everything else gets the CPU first.
    2. **A thread cap.** The model runs on ONNX Runtime through fastembed; pass a thread count of 1 so it can never use more than one core. *The dev must verify that fastembed's `TextEmbedding` accepts a thread option.*
    3. **A duty-cycle cap**, suggested default 25% of one core: after each chunk, sleep long enough that the average stays under the cap.
    4. **Pause while the system is busy:** wait while the 1-minute load average per core (`os.getloadavg()`, Unix only) is above a threshold, suggested 1.0. The load average lags by about a minute, so guards 1-3 handle the fast reactions.
    5. **Pause while available memory is low:** do not start, and pause a running child, when available memory is below a threshold (suggested 1.5 GB; `MemAvailable` from `/proc/meminfo` on Linux). Under memory pressure the model load is itself the expensive step.
  - **First-use model download is automatic (decided by the user, against the reviewer's recommendation to wait for an explicit request).** The weights (87 MB in this project's `_drspec/models`) are not bundled; the first background run downloads them, with no prompt. Consequences the dev must handle: (a) the download happens inside the throttled child, never in the worker, and must not write to stdout (`_get_model` already disables the progress bar); (b) if the download fails, for example offline, do not retry every pass — back off like an unparseable file (growing wait, capped), and let `status` say "embedding waiting: model download failed"; (c) `status` also says when a download is in progress; (d) the setting to turn background embedding off (below) is the way to prevent the download and any network access.
  - **The model cache is `Path.cwd() / "_drspec" / "models"`** (`embedder._default_cache_dir`), so the child must run with the project as its working directory. The worker inherits its working directory from whichever process spawned it, so pass it explicitly.
- **Usage statistics.** Search, `source_get` and `deps_get` record which nodes and edges they returned, as **per-item pending counters** (not one event row per call), in the same best-effort write as D4. The worker folds the pending counts into the decayed scores on its pass and uses them to pick and order the embedding work (see the top-N rule above). Suggested weights: a search hit counts 1, a `source_get` or `deps_get` on a specific function counts more (about 5), because a search returns up to 50 results and most are not really used. A new table fits better than the `config` key/value table, which holds internal state (`scan:file:`, `verification:`, `artifact:context:`) and has `get_config`/`set_config`.
- **A setting to turn background embedding off**, stored in the `config` table, default on, set through a new `drspec config get/set` command with a matching MCP tool (every command gets a tool, §27). `status` shows embedded/total counts. `scan --embed` keeps working as before. An environment variable was rejected: the worker keeps the environment of whichever process spawned it and lives for the whole session, so changing it later has no effect.
- Embeddings already carry `MODEL_VERSION`; a model change must queue every item that currently has an embedding for re-embedding. Check how it is used today.

## Acceptance tests (the reproductions above)

1. Rewrite 300 files while the worker is alive: no ordinary call (`status`) waits longer than about 1 s. (If this fails after the budget is in, split parsing from publishing — see D2.)
2. Add a new supported file under a scanned project: it is in the graph within a few passes, with no manual scan.
3. Search for text in an edited file: the file is queued, the worker re-scans it, and the next search is not `Possibly Stale`.
4. `scan --embed`, edit an embedded function, wait for the worker: `search --semantic` still finds that function once its re-embedding has run; a changed item that had an embedding never loses it permanently. Usage ranking: after enough `source_get`/`deps_get` calls on a never-embedded function it enters the top N and gets embedded; an item with old heavy use and no recent use ranks below one used recently.
5. An unparseable file does not stop other files from being re-scanned, and is retried on a backoff rather than every pass.
6. With the worker dormant, a search that queues a file leads to its re-scan within seconds.
7. Idle CPU stays low; with no embedding backlog no model is resident in any drspec process.
8. After D1, none of the removed tools, CLI commands or prompt references remain, and an agent can still find functions that need a contract by status.

## Not decided here

- Whether edges should ever have contracts (today they do not, and nothing asks for it).
- None open. (The first-use model download was decided: see D5.)

## Resolved while building

- **The stall was not parsing.** Parsing ten files takes about 3 ms and staging a publish about 90 ms, so splitting parsing out of the lock (D2's fallback) would have changed nothing. Profiling a ten-file publish on the real project's database showed the cost was `refresh_contexts` rewriting every function's context fingerprint on every publish (about 5 s for 2,435 functions). It now writes only fingerprints that changed, in batches. After that a ten-file publish takes about 0.26 s and the 300-file rewrite test sees a worst request wait of about 1 s.
- **Acceptance test 1 was not testing what it claimed.** Its completion check (`body LIKE '%100%'`) could never be true, so the loop ran to its 240 s deadline and passed anyway. It now waits for all 300 rewrites (about 27 s) and fails if they never finish. The bound is 1.5 s: one of two runs at a 1.0 s bound measured 1.2 s on a loaded machine, so 1.0 s would be flaky.
- **Known limit, not fixed:** an edit that adds, removes or renames a function changes the project's symbol set, and a scan then re-resolves the calls of every function in the project inside the lock. Measured on a scratch project of 2,400 one-function files, adding one function takes about 3.5 s (5.5 s before the snapshot writes were batched). This is the existing scan behavior and the budget does not bound it; fixing it means making call resolution incremental, which this section does not cover.
- **`--db` override:** new-file discovery is off unless the database sits in a folder named `_drspec`, because there is no way to tell which directory is the project root otherwise. Re-scan of known files, search queueing and embedding work either way.
- **The `queue` table is dropped**, not left in place: `init_schema` runs `DROP TABLE IF EXISTS queue` on every open. An old table left behind would block deleting a function (its rows reference `artifacts`). Its rows held no information that `search --status PENDING/STALE` does not give back.
- **fastembed's thread option exists** (`TextEmbedding(threads=...)`), so the one-thread cap is real, not just a niceness hint.
- **`source_get` and `deps_get`** now run the same drift check as search (`possibly_stale`, and queue the file); `deps_get` already did for the neighbours it lists.
- **Embedding on re-scan:** a re-scan still clears a changed item's vector (an old vector for new text would be wrong). What keeps it from being lost is the per-item `embedding:` bookkeeping row: an item with that row (or a vector) that is not up to date for the current model and source hash is owed an embedding whatever its usage, ahead of the usage-ranked items. A model change therefore requeues everything that had a vector.
- **The embedding process** is `drspec worker embed`, started by the worker with the project root as its working directory, at nice 19 and one thread.
