It writes through a dedicated `store_embeddings` request that runs under the worker's lock, because storing a function's vector briefly detaches the rows that reference it and no other request may see that half-done. Its output goes to `_drspec/embedding.log`. A process that exits with an error, can't get the model, or finishes leaving as much owed as before is retried after 1, 2, 4 … 15 minutes. `status` reports `embedding off`, `waiting: model download failed`, `downloading the model`, `paused: machine busy|low memory`, `failed: …`, `N waiting` or `up to date`.
- **Not explained:** in one hand run on a busy machine (load average about 5) a single owed item stayed "running" through 32 status polls (each poll took several seconds) before finishing; the same step took seconds in the end-to-end tests and in later hand runs, and the child's output file did not exist yet so nothing was captured. The log file and the `error` state were added for the next occurrence.

# 37. Proposal: An Optional Trace of What drspec Does While It Runs (Implemented — see Task 36)

**Status: implemented** — Task 36. The text below is kept as proposed; where the build differs (the flag spelling, `slow` and events without a duration, how the command name is found) Task 36.6 says so, and 36.7–36.9 hold the measurements.

## Why this is needed (cases from the Task 34/35 review)

1. A first `search` call took about 78 seconds once and was never reproduced. Nothing recorded whether it waited for the worker's lock, for a worker start, or did real work.
2. The embedding process re-embedded the same changed function over and over for about a minute (see the review finding on the DuckDB array-column bug). It was found only after a stack-dump trick injected through `sitecustomize`. The dev's own "Not explained" note in §36 describes the same symptom (one owed item stuck "running"), with no record of what the process did.
3. Every stall question so far ("is it parsing, the lock, the publish, the checkpoint?") needed a hand-written timing script. §36's own stall diagnosis was wrong at first for that reason (it was `refresh_contexts`, not parsing).

A trace must answer "where did the time go" from a file, after the fact, without reproducing the problem.

## What to record

JSON lines, one object per event, in `_drspec/trace.log` (already inside the git-ignored folder). Every line has `t` (ISO time), `pid`, `proc` (`cli`, `mcp`, `worker`, `embed`), `event`, `ms` and event-specific fields.

| Event | Fields | Answers |
|---|---|---|
| `command` | CLI command or MCP tool name, argument *names* and short safe values, exit status, output size, time spent connecting to or starting the worker | which commands are slow, and whether the wait was the worker |
| `request` | worker operation, first 120 characters of the SQL with parameter values removed, **lock wait ms**, **run ms**, rows returned | lock contention versus real query cost |
| `pass` | worker pass: detect ms, discover ms, files queued, rescan batch size, backlog sizes, settings in force | what a pass costs and why it ran |
| `rescan` | per file parse ms; publish ms split into checkpoint, copy, sync, replace, fsync, reconnect | whether parsing or publishing dominates |
| `embed` | per chunk: items, embed ms, store ms, pause reason, sleep, round number, items still owed | a loop on the same item shows up at once |
| `supervisor` | embedding process started, exited with code, backoff chosen and why | retry and backoff behaviour |

Each CLI or MCP call gets a correlation id (`cid`) that is sent to the worker as an optional field of every IPC request (an old worker ignores it). The worker's `request` events carry the same `cid`, so one command's whole path can be put back together: connect, lock wait, run, publish.

## How to turn it on

- **Setting** `trace.level`: `off` (default), `slow` or `all`, through `drspec config set` and the existing `config_set` MCP tool. The worker already re-reads settings every pass, so it applies without a restart.
- **`trace.slow_ms`** (default 500): at `slow`, only events at or above it are written.
- **`trace.max_mb`** (default 20): at this size the file is renamed to `trace.log.1` (one previous file kept, replaced on the next rotation).
- **For one command**: a global flag `drspec --trace[=slow|all] <command>` and the environment variable `DRSPEC_TRACE`, for processes that must be traced without touching the database, such as a failing `init`. The environment variable cannot control the worker (it keeps the environment it was spawned with, the same reason as for the embedding setting in §36), so the worker uses the setting only.

## Reading it

`drspec trace summary [--since 1h] [--slowest 20]` prints, per event name, the count, total, median, 95th percentile and maximum, then the slowest individual events with their `cid`. `drspec trace tail`, `drspec trace clear`, and a matching MCP tool `trace_summary` (every command gets a tool, §27). A `--cid` option prints one command's whole path.

## Safety, privacy and cost

- **No file contents, function bodies or SQL parameter values** are written. File paths are project-relative. Search patterns are cut to 80 characters and written only at `all`.
- **Off must cost nothing**: one setting read when a process starts and a module-level boolean checked per event; no file is created while it is off.
- **Never affects a command**: every write is wrapped, errors are swallowed, a full disk or an unwritable file only stops tracing. Lines are written with a single append and flushed per event, so a killed worker still leaves its last events.
- **Overhead on**: measure it on the 300-file rewrite scenario and record the number in the task. The suggested bound is under 5% added wall time at `all`.

## Acceptance tests

1. Off by default: no `trace.log` appears after a scan, a search and a worker pass.
2. At `all`, a 300-file rewrite produces `pass`, `rescan` and `request` events whose lock wait and run times add up to the observed `status` latency.
3. The embedding loop from the review finding, reproduced with the DuckDB bug, shows the same item stored round after round in `embed` events.
4. At `slow`, only events at or above `trace.slow_ms` are written.
5. Rotation: the file never grows past `trace.max_mb` plus one line.
6. `drspec trace summary` and the `trace_summary` tool give the same numbers; `--cid` reassembles a search from `command` to the worker's `request` lines.
7. An unwritable trace file, or a full disk, does not fail or slow any command.
8. A `cid` sent to an older worker is ignored without error.

# 38. Feedback: Review of Tasks 34-35 — Queue Rows Left Behind by a Manual Scan, Broken Commands in the Prompts, and the Embedding Thread Cap (Implemented — see Task 37)

**Status: implemented** — Task 37. This section holds three of the four findings from the review of Tasks 34 and 35. The fourth (re-embedding a changed function loses vectors, a DuckDB 1.3.2 bug) is deliberately **not** written here: the fix depends on the storage design the user is still deciding, so it has its own section, §39.

## What was verified and works

Full suite 1590 passed (7 minutes, repository untouched). In real-worker scenarios: 300 rewritten files drain in bounded passes with the worst `status` wait at 1.06 s (bound 1.5 s) and every sampled file refreshed; a new file reaches the graph in 2 s once the queue is clean; the first search after an edit reports `Possibly Stale: Yes` and the graph is fresh in 1 to 3 s; healthy files are re-scanned past a broken one, and fixing it is picked up in 1 s; a worker idle for 140 s wakes on the first search (fresh in 1 s); background embedding embeds only used functions, downloads the model on first use, exits when done, and survived 34 rewrites of embedded files during a run; `config get|set|reset` rejects bad values, unknown keys and returns right exit codes; the removed queue commands and tools are gone; `sweep_orphaned_staging` removes dead-owner folders, keeps live and fresh ones. The overwrite-prompt and §35 fixes were verified in the previous round.

## Findings

### 1. A manual `scan` leaves the worker's queue rows behind

**Reproduction (3 runs of the same shape).** Worker running, `worker.rescan_budget` 1, poll 2 s. Create 20 new files; the worker discovers 16 of them (`Files Pending Discovery: 16`). Run `drspec scan .`, which scans all 20. Straight after, `status` still shows 14 pending; 6 s later 10. The worker re-scans already-scanned files, one per pass.

**Cause.** The worker's own re-scan (`_remediate_queued_files`) calls `dequeue_staleness` and `clear_rescan_backoff` for every file it publishes. The scan publish path does not, so rows queued before or during a scan survive it.

**Effects seen.**
- Redundant work: each leftover row costs a parse and a publish, and a publish copies the whole database. A project scanned by hand after the worker discovered its files can spend hundreds of passes on files that are already current.
- Wrong numbers in `status`: a 300-file project showed 260 "pending discovery" files right after its scan (the `config set` calls had started the worker before the scan finished, so its first discovery pass queued every file as new while the scan was still publishing).
- A real new file waited 43 s behind them, against 2 s with a clean queue. Drift rows are affected the same way (edit, worker queues it, manual scan, the row stays).

**Suggested fix.** In the scan publish, dequeue and clear the backoff for every path in `scanned_file_paths`, in the same publish (and for paths the scan removed). One shared helper used by both the worker and the scan avoids the two paths drifting apart again.

**Acceptance.** Worker alive and 20 files queued with a budget of 1; run `drspec scan .`; `status` shows no pending rescan or discovery and no further re-scan or publish happens in the next passes.

### 2. The commands the prompts give for finding work do not run

Task 35.3 replaced the contract queue with `drspec search --status PENDING` and `--status STALE`, written without a pattern in `agents/librarian.md` (lines 32, 33, 102, 103, 168), `agents/proposer.md` (29), `agents/helpers/cli.md` (43, 44) and `README.md` (46, 93). Every one of them fails:

- `drspec search --status PENDING`, `... --path <dir>`, `... --limit 5`: exit 2, `Missing argument 'PATTERN'`.
- The MCP `search` tool requires `pattern` too (`Missing required argument: pattern`).
- What works: `drspec search "" --status PENDING` (an empty pattern matches everything). Verified.
- `agents/helpers/cli.md` lines 18-19 still show `drspec --pretty search --status PENDING  # Correct`; `--pretty` was removed in §30 (`No such option: --pretty`).

So an agent following the prompts has no working way to find the functions that need a contract. Acceptance 8 of §36 searched for removed *names*, which these commands are not, so nothing caught it.

**Suggested fix.** Preferred: make the pattern optional when `--status` is given, in the CLI and the MCP schema, so the documented commands work and "functions that need a contract" is one obvious call. Otherwise write the empty pattern in every prompt. Either way remove `--pretty` from `helpers/cli.md`.

**Acceptance and a guard so it cannot drift again.** A test extracts every `drspec ...` command line from `agents/**/*.md`, `README.md` and the project skill and checks each parses (for example by running it with `--help`, or by checking the subcommand and options exist). A quick script doing this found only the `--pretty` lines and prose false positives, so it is cheap to keep.

### 3. `embedding.threads` does not bound the embedding process (low; plausible, not proven)

`run_embedding_process` passes `embedding.threads` to `embedder.load_model`, which sets fastembed's thread count. Sampling `/proc/<pid>/stat` of the running process with `embedding.threads` at 1 and `embedding.cpu_percent` at 2 showed `num_threads` of 12, 17, 18, 34 and 39 on a 12-core machine, and a burst of 62% of one core in the first half second (model load). The other threads come from pools the setting does not reach (probably the tokenizer and OpenMP or BLAS). Average CPU stayed near zero, so the duty-cycle cap works; what does not hold is the design's statement that the process "can never use more than one core". Also `drspec config set embedding.threads 64` is accepted.

**Suggested fix.** Set `OMP_NUM_THREADS`, `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS`, `MKL_NUM_THREADS`, `RAYON_NUM_THREADS` and `TOKENIZERS_PARALLELISM=false` in the environment `spawn_embedding_process` builds for the child, from `embedding.threads`; give the setting an upper bound (at most the number of cores); and size the CPU cap by sampling peak, not average.

**Acceptance.** With `embedding.threads` 1, a run sampled every 100 ms shows a thread count in the low teens at most and no sample above about 110% of one core outside model load.

# 39. Fix: Store Embeddings as Variable-Length Lists, Verify Every Store, and Rebuild Existing Databases (Implemented — see Task 38; upstream report not filed)

**Status: implemented** — Task 38 (item 7, the upstream report, is left to the user). Decided by the user: switch the embedding columns to the variable-length list type, and rebuilding **existing** databases is acceptable. This is the fourth finding of the Task 34/35 review that §38 left out. Everything below was measured on DuckDB 1.3.2 with Python 3.8.10 (the newest DuckDB available for that Python).

## The problem

When a function changes, the worker's re-scan clears its embedding (`embedding = NULL`, `graph_sync.py` and the upsert in `queries.py`), and the background embedding process later writes the new vector (`store_embeddings`, an `UPDATE`). On the fixed-size array type `FLOAT[384]` this sequence corrupts the column's validity information:

- **Reproduced with plain DuckDB, no drspec code.** The 12-line script below reports `(100, 98)`: two rows of 100 are read as NULL by scans.
- **Which rows.** The row that was written, and **the last row of the table** (when the written row is itself the last, only one row is affected). It is not a neighbour.
- **It accumulates.** The two-row figure is for one clear-and-write cycle. Repeating the cycle on a 100-row table (300 cycles, each in its own session with a checkpoint, as the worker and the embedding process do) left scans seeing **1 of 100** rows as embedded, against 100 of 100 with the list type. The file did **not** grow (780 KB at the start and after 1, 50, 100, 200 and 300 cycles, for both types), so nothing piles up in storage; what degrades is what scans can see, with every edit of an embedded function.
- **The data is not deleted.** Reading either row by key returns the right vector (`f0` = 9.0, `f99` = 100.0). What is wrong is how scans read it: a projection, `COUNT(embedding)`, `SUM(CASE ... IS NOT NULL)` and `array_cosine_similarity` all treat the two rows as NULL, while `WHERE embedding IS NOT NULL` still counts all 100. The table gives different answers depending on the access path, and the answer survives closing and reopening the file.
- **Silent.** Nothing is raised; `store_embeddings` returns 1. `embedding.log` stayed empty.
- **Conditions** (all measured): the column must be a fixed `FLOAT[n]` array with n of 128 or more (16, 64 and 3 were fine); the table must be small (lost at 120 to 350 rows with n = 384, fine at 400 and above; lost at 120 rows and fine at 200 with n = 128); some rows of the column must be NULL (a table with no NULLs and in-place vector-to-vector updates never lost anything); a primary key is not needed (it reproduces without one). The documented DuckDB limitation about updating nested or indexed columns (turned into delete plus insert, which can raise a spurious constraint error) is a different thing: that one raises an error, this one does not.
- **In drspec.** The semantic search is a full scan, so the two rows disappear from `search --semantic`. `find_backlog` is a full scan too, so the changed function is still "owed" after being stored, and the embedding process embeds and stores it again, round after round: about 60 s of CPU for one edited function in a 6-function project, and an edited function that never ranks first within 120 s in a 100-function one. The dev's "Not explained" note in §36 (one owed item stuck "running") is this.
- **Who is affected.** Projects whose tables stay under a few hundred rows: small repositories, and any early stage of a new one. This repository (2,473 functions) is above the threshold for `artifacts`, so it did not show the loss. The edge tables (`dependencies`, `code_references`) have the same column and the same exposure.

Minimal reproduction, for the dev and for an upstream report (duckdb 1.3.2):

```python
import duckdb, os, tempfile
p = os.path.join(tempfile.mkdtemp(), "t.db"); DIM = 384
c = duckdb.connect(p)
c.execute(f"CREATE TABLE a (id TEXT PRIMARY KEY, embedding FLOAT[{DIM}])")
c.executemany("INSERT INTO a VALUES (?, ?)", [[f"f{i}", [float(i + 1)] * DIM] for i in range(100)])
c.execute("CHECKPOINT"); c.close()
c = duckdb.connect(p); c.execute("UPDATE a SET embedding = NULL WHERE id = 'f0'"); c.close()
c = duckdb.connect(p); c.execute("UPDATE a SET embedding = ? WHERE id = 'f0'", [[9.0] * DIM]); c.execute("CHECKPOINT"); c.close()
c = duckdb.connect(p)
print(c.execute("SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(embedding) FROM a").fetchone())                  # expected (100, 100); 1.3.2 prints (100, 98)
print([r[0] for r in c.execute("SELECT id, embedding IS NOT NULL FROM a").fetchall() if not r[1]])  # ['f0', 'f99']
print(c.execute("SELECT (embedding)[1] FROM a WHERE id = 'f99'").fetchone())              # (100.0,): the value is still there
```

## What was tested as a fix

| Storage | Result |
|---|---|
| Fixed array `FLOAT[384]` (today) | loses rows, as above |
| Fixed array, but never clear it (overwrite vector with vector in place) | fine **only if the column has no NULLs at all**; with never-embedded rows (always the case here: embedding is opt-in and top-N) updates still lose rows |
| Fixed array, `UPDATE` with an explicit cast, inside an explicit transaction, or after a `CHECKPOINT` | no change, still loses rows |
| **Variable-length list `FLOAT[]`** | no loss in 3 NULL-heavy patterns at 10, 50, 120, 350, 400 and 1000 rows, with values checked after checkpoint and reopen |
| Separate table with a `NOT NULL` vector column (`DELETE` then `INSERT`) | no loss at 10 to 1000 rows |
| Same table with a zero vector instead of NULL | no loss at 10 to 1000 rows |

**The list type was then run through the real code, not only plain DuckDB.** In a scratch copy of the package (not the repository) with exactly four lines changed (three schema lines and the search query), the scenario that looped for 64 s finished in 3 s, and the 100-function scenario (34 files rewritten while the embedding process ran) embedded all 100 vectors, left `embedding.log` empty, and ranked the edited function first for its new text after 6 s (it did not within 120 s before). The embedding, search, schema and usage unit tests (333) passed except two that assert the literal type string `FLOAT[384]` and two that fail identically on the unpatched tree because of in-progress work (`test_the_duty_cycle_sleeps_in_proportion_to_work`, and the tool-list test that does not yet know `trace_summary`).

**Chosen: the list type**, the smallest change. The cause inside DuckDB is not known, so the evidence is strong but not a proof; that is why the read-back check below is part of the fix and not optional.

## Changes

1. **Schema.** `embedding FLOAT[]` for `artifacts`, `dependencies` and `code_references` (`db/schema.sql` lines 18, 40, 58); update the comments and the docstrings that name `FLOAT[384]` (`queries.py` around line 146, `worker_ipc.py` around line 99).
2. **Search.** `list_cosine_similarity(embedding, ?::FLOAT[])` in the semantic search (`queries.py` around line 775). It exists in 1.3.2 and gave the right score in the check. The only other place that scores vectors was found by search to be this one.
3. **Keep the dimension.** The list type no longer enforces 384. Validate the length (one `EMBEDDING_DIM` constant, already 384 in `embedder.py`) in `store_embeddings`, in the `scan --embed` path of `graph_sync`, and for the query vector; reject a wrong length with an error instead of storing it.
4. **Keep `_fk_references_detached`.** The DuckDB documentation says nested-type updates, lists included, are rewritten as delete plus insert and hit the same foreign-key limitation; the patched runs worked with it. Note for the dev: `COALESCE(excluded.embedding, a.embedding)` is **not** supported on fixed arrays in 1.3.2 (`Unimplemented type for case expression: FLOAT[128]`), so do not try that route to keep an old vector.
5. **Read-back check after every store** (applies whatever the storage is). After `store_embeddings` writes a batch, read the same keys back with the **full-scan** query that `find_backlog` and `embedding_counts` use, and compare. If a stored vector does not read back, write the embedding state `error: "vector did not persist"`, log it (and emit a trace event when `trace.level` is on, §37), and put the item into the existing failure backoff. Also stop the runner's `while True` loop when a round leaves the owed count unchanged. Today the supervisor's no-progress logic only works between process runs, which is why one item looped for a minute.
6. **Existing databases (rebuild; the user accepted this).** `ALTER TABLE ... ALTER embedding TYPE FLOAT[]` fails ("Cannot alter entry ... because there are entries that depend on it"), and so does `ADD COLUMN` on `artifacts` and `dependencies` (the foreign keys and indexes depend on them), and copying through an `UPDATE` hits the foreign-key limitation. So an old database has to be rebuilt:
   - **When.** At worker start (the worker is the sole owner of the file) and in `drspec init` on an existing project: if the `embedding` column of any of the three tables has type `FLOAT[384]`.
   - **How (proposed, not tested).** Create a new database file beside the old one with the new schema, using the staging prefix (`.drspec-scan-<pid>-...`, so `sweep_orphaned_staging` cleans leftovers); attach the old one read-only; copy **every table except the vectors** in foreign-key order (`artifacts`, then the tables that reference it); carry over the sequence values (`reasoning_traces`, `vision_findings`) and the whole `config` table; compare row counts per table; fsync and swap with `os.replace`, as `staged_scan` does.
   - **What is kept.** Everything that is not derived: contracts, reasoning traces, vision findings, learning history, settings, usage scores, scanned-file snapshots. The user accepted a rebuild; there is no reason to throw away contracts, which are the product, and the vectors are derived data.
   - **Vectors.** Left NULL in the new file. The `embedding:` meta keys in `config` are copied, so the backlog already treats every previously embedded item as owed (a stored hash and no vector counts as a repair) and the background process re-embeds them with no extra code. Items never embedded stay as they are.
   - **Failure.** Any error leaves the old file untouched; the worker refuses to start with a message that says why and names the backup, and does not run on the old layout. Keep the old file as `contracts.db.before-list-embeddings` until the next successful start. **Never use `init_schema(conn, rebuild=True)` for this**: that path drops the data, and is the one MCP `init --force` once used (§34).
   - **A schema marker** in `config` (for example `schema:embedding_layout = list`) so detection is one lookup, not a metadata scan, on every start.
7. **Upstream.** File the reproduction above with the DuckDB project (version 1.3.2, Python 3.8) and link it here; revisit the choice if a later release fixes it, but the list type is not harmful to keep.

## Acceptance tests

1. **Regression for the corruption.** A 100-row table with NULLs, clear one row, write it back in a later session, checkpoint, reopen: every row reads back as embedded with the right value, by a full scan. This fails on `FLOAT[384]` and passes on `FLOAT[]`.
2. The real flow: `scan --embed`, edit one embedded function in a 6-function project, no manual scan: the function is re-embedded and ranks first for its new text within a few seconds, and the embedding process runs one round, not repeated rounds.
3. The 100-function scenario with files rewritten while the embedding process runs ends with all 100 embedded and an empty `embedding.log`.
4. Wrong-length vectors are rejected on store and on query.
5. Read-back detection: with a connection that drops the write, the state becomes `error: vector did not persist`, the item enters backoff, and the runner stops instead of looping.
6. Migration: an old-layout database with rows in every table and some embedded items becomes a new-layout database with identical row counts in every table, NULL vectors, the same settings and contracts, sequences continuing from the same values, and a backlog that owes exactly the previously embedded items; the backup file exists.
7. An interrupted or failing migration leaves the old database byte-for-byte unchanged and the worker refuses to start with a clear message.
8. The two tests that assert the literal `FLOAT[384]` (`test_db_connection.py`, lines 172 and 182) are updated to `FLOAT[]`.

## Related, not part of this task

There is no backend-neutral database interface: 23 of 86 source files mention DuckDB, SQL is written directly in modules such as `status.py`, `graph_sync.py` and `settings.py`, and callers catch `duckdb.Error` (about 19 places in `mcp_server.py`). The "connection" the code shares copies DuckDB's own API. Moving to another engine (SQLite was considered) would be a project of its own; recorded here so it is a decision, not a surprise.

# 40. Feedback: Review of Task 36 (the Trace) and What It Found on the First Real Use (Implemented — see Task 39; fix 3 of finding 2 not done)

**Status: implemented** — Task 39. Written after the dev's Task 36 and while the dev had started §39. The trace was switched on for this project (`trace.level` = `all`, `trace.slow_ms` and `trace.max_mb` at their defaults) so that performance data accumulates from now on.

## What was verified and works

Full suite 1655 passed (5 minutes, repository untouched by the run). On real processes in scratch projects: nothing is written by default (init, scan, search, status, source and eight seconds of worker passes); `trace.level` rejects a bad word and accepts `all`; at `all`, events arrive from `cli`, `worker` and `embed` with the kinds `command`, `request`, `pass`, `rescan`, `embed` and `supervisor`; **privacy**: a planted marker that exists only inside function bodies never appears in the log, a 115-character search pattern is recorded cut to 80, option *values* (a `--path` value) are not recorded (names only), and no SQL field keeps a quoted literal; `--cid` of a real `search` returns its `command` event and the worker's requests; `trace summary` and `trace tail` change nothing in the database and do not start the worker; `trace clear` empties the file and the summary still works; `drspec --trace`, `--trace-level slow|all` and `DRSPEC_TRACE` behave as documented, and a bad level is a clean usage error. The overhead figure was not re-measured. §38 findings 2 (the prompt commands: the pattern is now optional in the CLI and in the MCP schema) and 3 (the thread-limit environment variables are set in `embedding_supervisor.py`) look fixed; finding 1 (queue rows left by a manual scan) was not re-checked in this round.

## Findings

### 1. Rotation closes a file descriptor twice, and one collision turns tracing off for that process

`tracing._write` closes `fd`, calls `os.replace`, opens a new `fd`, and its `finally` closes `fd` again. If `os.replace` or the second `os.open` raises (for example another process already moved the file), the `finally` closes the already-closed descriptor: `OSError: [Errno 9] Bad file descriptor`, which hides the real error. `emit` then sets `_failed`, and **that process never traces again** (only a change of level resets it).

**Reproduced.** Six processes appending to one log with a 50 KB limit: in all three runs, 5 of the 6 ended with tracing disabled within 0.2 s. The raw `_write` errors were all `Bad file descriptor`.

**Why it matters beyond tracing.** The worker is multithreaded. Between the two closes another thread can open a file or a socket and receive the same descriptor number; the second `close` then shuts that thread's descriptor. That is a rare but real way to break an unrelated request.

**Suggested fix.** Set `fd = None` after the first close and close only if not `None`; treat `FileNotFoundError` from `os.replace` as "someone else rotated" and carry on; serialise rotation with an `fcntl.flock` on a small lock file (or `O_EXCL` rename), and re-check the size after taking the lock; and do not disable tracing for good on one failure in a process that lives for days (retry after a growing wait). **Acceptance.** The six-process script above ends with all six processes still tracing and no line corrupted.

### 2. A re-scan publish costs time proportional to the call edges of the changed functions, and the worker holds its lock for all of it

**First real use of the trace.** On this repository (2,473 functions) one worker pass re-scanned 5 changed files (about 80 functions): parsing took about 56 ms, checkpoint plus copy 83 ms, and **`sync_scan_results` took 97,143 ms** inside the `remediate` request, which holds the worker's lock. For those 97 seconds every other request waits: `drspec worker status` hung past 60 s, and the first `status` call of the review session ("Lost contact with the drspec worker: timed out" after the 120 s request timeout) is very likely the same thing. The unexplained 78 s first call from earlier is a candidate for the same cause, not proven.

**Controlled measurement** (a scratch project of 640 functions and 1,200 call edges; `sync_ms` per publish, trace at `all`):

| Edit | Edges involved | `sync_ms` |
|---|---|---|
| 1 leaf function nobody calls | 0 | 81 |
| 5 leaf functions | 0 | 216 |
| 1 caller file, 3 functions, 6 call edges | 6 | 1,178 |
| 10 hub functions, each called by about 120 | about 1,200 | 11,717 |

So the cost follows the edges attached to the changed functions, roughly 10 ms per edge here and worse on the real repository, whose hub files (`queries.py`, `mcp_server.py`) have far more. The acceptance test of §36 (no request waits more than 1.5 s with 300 rewritten files) passes because that project has no hub functions; real code does.

**Cause (hypothesis, not yet profiled).** Per-edge work in `sync_scan_results` (re-resolving and rewriting each edge of a changed function, with the foreign-key detach and restore that array-column updates need) instead of one batched statement per table. The trace has no breakdown inside `sync_ms`, so this is the first thing to add.

**Suggested fixes, in order.**
1. Add `span`s inside `sync_scan_results` (nodes, caller edges, callee edges, references, contexts, snapshots) so the next trace names the expensive step.
2. Batch the edge and reference writes: one `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` or `UPDATE ... FROM` per table per publish, and only for edges whose hash really changed.
3. Do not block readers during the sync. The sync works on a private copy (`staged_scan`) and the live connection is untouched until the swap, so `status`, `search` and `worker status` could be served from the live connection while it runs; only writes need to wait. If one connection cannot serve two threads, use a cursor per thread.

**Acceptance.** The scratch project above with the hub edit publishes in well under a second per hundred edges, and a `status` call issued during a hub-file publish returns in under 1.5 s.

### 3. `status` makes one request per function (medium)

Measured with the trace: one `status` over 61 functions made 85 worker requests, 61 of them `SELECT value FROM config WHERE key=?` (the dev noted this in 36.9 a and left it). On this repository that is about 2,500 requests per `status`, and each one can wait behind a publish, which is how a 2.7 s `status` turned out to be 1.9 s of lock wait. **Fix.** One query for all keys (`WHERE key IN (...)` or `starts_with(key, 'verification:')`), done once per call.

### 4. `trace summary` reads everything into memory (medium for the limits chosen)

`read_events` parses every event of both files into a list. A synthetic 100 MB log (497,476 events) took 3.9 s and **966 MB** of memory for one file; with the older file also at the limit that is about 2 GB. At the default 20 MB it is about 190 MB per file. For this reason the project's `trace.max_mb` was returned to the 20 MB default. **Fix.** Aggregate while streaming: keep per-name lists of `ms` only (or a quantile sketch), keep a bounded heap for the slowest events, and for `--cid` and `--since` stream and filter.

### 5. The trace records time but not memory or CPU time

Every event has `ms`, and `command` has `connect_ms`, `ipc_ms` and `ipc_calls`; nothing records memory or CPU. The only memory reading in the code is `MemAvailable` inside the embedding process's pause check, and it is not written to the trace. For the performance work this is meant for, and for hypotheses such as "the slow call was the machine swapping", these are missing:
- on each `command` event: peak resident memory of the command (`resource.getrusage(RUSAGE_SELF).ru_maxrss`) and CPU seconds (user + system), so waiting can be told from computing;
- on each worker `pass`, each `embed` chunk and each `rescan publish`: the process's current resident memory (`/proc/self/statm`), the database and WAL file sizes, the 1-minute load average and `MemAvailable`;
- on the `embed` process start and exit: peak resident memory (the model is about 270 MB).
All are a syscall or a small file read, far below the 21.6 µs per event already measured, and none needs a new dependency.

### 6. A new `trace.*` setting reaches the worker on its next pass (low)

Right after `drspec config set trace.level all`, the first commands were not traced: the worker applies settings on its next pass, and a command follows the worker's level. Document it, or let `config set trace.*` wake the worker so it applies at once.

### 7. A note in Task 36 does not match the tree

Task 36.7 item 3 says "the real DuckDB array-column bug is fixed, so it is simulated". At the time of the review the tree still had `embedding FLOAT[384]` and `array_cosine_similarity`, and the bug was reproducible. §39 is the fix and the dev has since started it (`db/migration.py` appeared while the review ran; for a few minutes `init.py` imported it before it existed and every `drspec` command failed on import).

## What this review is now useful for

With tracing on in this project, the next performance question can be answered from `drspec trace summary --since 1h --slowest 20` instead of a new script. The first answer it gave is finding 2.


# 41. Feedback: The `prefer_drspec` Hook Rewrites Grep Into a Search That Cannot Find the Lines (Implemented — see Task 40)

**Status: implemented** — Task 40. Found while answering "does the project need pandas?". It is a bug in the hook `.claude/hooks/prefer_drspec.py` (§18 layer 2, §21), not in `drspec search`. **Removed (2026-11-23):** the hook was removed by the person's decision (no ask-before-grep hook); the script and its tests are gone, copy in `/home/thanh/drspec_backups/hook_cleanup_2026-11-23/`.

## What happened

The hook intercepts a `Bash` grep in a project with `_drspec/` and rewrites it into a `drspec search` call (permission decision `ask`, with `updatedInput`). The command

```
grep -n -i -E "pandas|numpy|dependencies|duckdb" /media/thanh/data/Projects/drspec/pyproject.toml
```

was rewritten to

```
drspec search 'pandas|numpy|dependencies|duckdb' --ignore-case --regex --path /media/thanh/data/Projects/drspec/pyproject.toml
```

and printed `Total Matches: 0 / Matches: (none)`. Real `grep -c` on the same file finds 3 matching lines. A false "0 matches" reads as "not used anywhere"; the answer to the pandas question was nearly given from it.

## Reproduced (both cases, on the current tree)

| Case | Command run | Result |
|---|---|---|
| A. file drspec does not index | the rewrite above (`pyproject.toml`) | 0 matches; real grep: 3 lines |
| B. indexed file, text outside a function body | `drspec search pandas --path src/drspec/db/connection.py` (what `grep -n pandas <that file>` becomes) | 0 matches |
| C. the same with `--lines` | `drspec search pandas --lines --path src/drspec/db/connection.py` | 2 hits (lines 12 and 23: the module-level guard) |

Case B is the more general one: function-body search cannot see imports, constants, comments or decorators, and a line-oriented tool like grep is asked for exactly those. The hook was written before `--lines` and the context options existed, so it drops `-n` as "ignorable" and has no mapping for `-A`, `-B`, `-C`. §21.3 records the same kind of false zero (basic-regex patterns taken as literals) and fixed only that one.

## Suggested changes

1. **Rewrite only what drspec can answer.** Translate only when the path argument is a file with an indexed extension (`.py`, `.js`, `.ts`, `.tsx`, `.cpp` and the other extensions the scanner handles) or a directory; for `.toml`, `.md`, `.sql`, `.yml`, `.json` and the like, fall back to the plain `ask` nudge (or allow the grep outright, since drspec cannot cover it).
2. **Make the translation line-oriented.** Add `--lines` to every rewrite, map `-A`, `-B`, `-C` (and `-NUM`) to `--after-context`, `--before-context`, `--context`, and stop treating `-n` as ignorable only if the output would then lose it (`--lines` already prints `file:line:text`). The no-path form (`grep -r pattern`) needs the same.
3. **Make the zero honest in `drspec search`.** When `--path` matches no indexed file, print `Files Searched: 0` (or "no indexed files under this path") in function mode too. Today function mode prints no `Files Searched:` line, so a path that matched nothing looks the same as a path with no hits; `--lines` mode already prints it.
4. **Test the hook against drspec's real output.** The unit tests check only the produced command string. Add a test that runs each translated command in a scratch project and compares the matched lines with real `grep` on a file that has a module-level match and a file type that is not indexed.

## Acceptance

- `grep -n pandas src/drspec/db/connection.py` (through the hook) returns lines 12 and 23.
- `grep -c dependencies pyproject.toml` is not rewritten to a search that returns 0.
- `drspec search x --path <unindexed file>` says that nothing was searched.

# 42. Feedback: Review of Tasks 38-40 (Embeddings as Lists, the Trace Follow-ups, the Hook) — One Upgrade Gap and Three Smaller Findings (Implemented — see Task 41)

**Status: implemented** — Task 41. Written after the dev's Tasks 38, 39 and 40. At the user's request the real project's database was rebuilt during this review (see "The real project" below).

## What was verified and works

Full suite: 1947 passed, 20 deselected, 1 expected failure (the `FLOAT[384]` regression, kept on purpose). Everything below was run on real processes in scratch projects, or on a replica of this repository with a copy of its database.

- **Task 38 (§39).** The DuckDB scenario with 300 clear-and-write cycles reads 1 of 100 rows as embedded on `FLOAT[384]` and 100 of 100 on `FLOAT[]`. A 6-function project, one function edited with no manual scan: it ranked first for its new text after 6.6 s, with one embedding run, one chunk, one store and an empty `embedding.log`. A 100-function project with all 34 files rewritten three times while the embedding ran: 100 of 100 embedded, nothing owed, log empty, the edited function found first. Wrong-length vectors are rejected. The rebuild on a copy of the real database (2,728 functions): 1.3 s, every table identical except the marker row in `config`, sequences identical, backup byte-identical to the original, a second call reports `current`. A failure injected after the build (the rename raises) and a `kill -9` at the rename both leave the original byte-identical; a backup path that cannot be written makes the worker refuse to start with the reason in `worker status`, in every command's error and in `worker run` (exit 1). `drspec init` on an old project rebuilds it. The backup is removed at the next successful worker start.
- **Task 39 (§40).** Six processes appending to one 50 KB log (three runs): all six still tracing, no corrupt line (before: 5 of 6 lost tracing). A hub-heavy edit of five files, which held the lock for 97 s on the real project, now publishes in 2.2 s (`sync_ms` 1.7 s) on a replica holding the real data, and the edge tables equal those of a forced full re-resolve (3,324 calls, 9 references, and the forced scan changes nothing). `status` makes 24 requests (about 1,800 before). The reader takes 3 s and 113 MB for a 100 MB log (about 1 GB before). `config set trace.level` is traced at once.
- **Task 40 (§41).** The reviewer's `-n -i -E` grep on `pyproject.toml` and `grep -c dependencies pyproject.toml` are no longer rewritten; `grep -n pandas src/drspec/db/connection.py` becomes a `--lines` search and prints the same lines as real grep, `-C 1` included. `drspec search pandas --path pyproject.toml` says that nothing was searched.

**Not reproduced here:** the read-back check and the runner's stop condition (§39 items 5 and the loop guard). They need a database that drops writes, which no longer exists once the type is fixed; they rest on the dev's unit tests.

## Findings

### 1. `drspec init` skips the rebuild whenever a worker is running, and nothing makes an old worker restart (medium)

`init.py` runs `ensure_embedding_layout` only `if is_reinit and not force and is_worker_running(db_path) is None`, with the comment "A running worker has already done this at its own start". After an upgrade that is false: the package is reinstalled, the worker from before the upgrade keeps running on old code, and the new CLI talks to it without any version check.

**Reproduced on the real project.** The code was reinstalled, the worker (pid 219568) had run since 11:23 on pre-fix code, and `_drspec/contracts.db` was still `FLOAT[384]` with no `schema:embedding_layout` marker. `drspec init` would have skipped the rebuild silently, and the old worker keeps the array bug (rows hidden from scans after every edit of an embedded function) until someone runs `worker stop`. The two `mcp-serve` processes the editor started earlier also run old code, and its semantic search (`array_cosine_similarity(..., ?::FLOAT[384])`) raises `Binder Error` on a list column (reproduced), so semantic search through them fails after the rebuild until the editor is reloaded.

**Suggested fix.** Give the worker a build identifier (the package version plus a schema-layout number) that it publishes in `worker status` and in the IPC handshake; a client that sees a different identifier asks the worker to restart itself (stop, then start the current code) before it sends the request, or at least `init` and `worker status` say "the worker is older than the installed code; run `drspec worker stop`". `init` should not rely on "a worker is running".

### 2. The memory and CPU fields of Task 39.5 are written but never shown (medium)

`trace.log` has `max_rss_mb` and `cpu_s` on `command` events and `rss_mb`, `db_mb`, `wal_mb`, `load1`, `mem_avail_mb` on `pass` events (read from the raw file). `trace tail`, `trace summary --slowest` and the MCP `trace_summary` print none of them: `trace_report._DETAIL_FIELDS` (line 26) does not list them and the summary aggregates only `ms`. The only way to see memory today is to open the JSON by hand.

**Suggested fix.** Add the seven names to `_DETAIL_FIELDS`; add to `trace summary` a peak and a median of `max_rss_mb` and total `cpu_s` per event name where present. **Acceptance.** `drspec trace tail` shows `max_rss_mb` and `cpu_s` on a `command` event and `rss_mb`, `load1` on a `pass`; the summary of a log containing them reports a peak memory per command.

### 3. The hook rewrites a dash-leading pattern into an option (low)

`grep -n -- -x src/drspec/db/connection.py` is rewritten to `drspec search -x --lines --path ...`, which fails with `No such option: -x`. `--` is how grep is told that the next word is a pattern. In `prefer_drspec.py` (`shlex.join(rewritten)`, line 325) put the pattern after `--`, or do not translate a pattern that starts with `-`. **Acceptance.** The rewritten command for that input runs and prints the matching lines.

### 4. A killed migration leaves a full copy of the database until later (low)

`run_worker` calls `sweep_orphaned_staging` before `ensure_embedding_layout` (staleness_worker.py:472-474). After a `kill -9` during the swap the second run migrates correctly, but the `.drspec-scan-<pid>-...` folder (a complete copy of the database, about 57 MB here) is still on disk until the next worker start or scan publish. Call the sweep again after a successful migration, or sweep once after it.

## The real project

At the user's request the real database was rebuilt: the old worker was stopped (it left a defunct entry under one `mcp-serve` process, which holds nothing and disappears when the editor is reloaded), a new worker started and rebuilt `_drspec/contracts.db` from `FLOAT[384]` to `FLOAT[]` (57 MB to 37 MB, marker set). Compared on copies of the old and new files, the contracts, reasoning traces, vision findings, learning history, usage statistics and scanned-file tables are identical; artifacts, dependencies and the re-scan queue differ only because the new worker was already working through the project's 172-file queue. All 234 vectors were re-embedded in about a minute (`embedding.log` empty) and a semantic search returns the same functions with the same scores as before. At the user's instruction the backup file was deleted.

## Side observation, not a finding

A project path long enough to make `<project>/_drspec/worker.sock` exceed about 108 characters makes the worker die with a raw `OSError: AF_UNIX path too long` traceback and every command fail with "Could not reach the drspec worker". Found by accident in a scratch directory (126 characters). A clear error, or a socket in a shorter place derived from the database path, would help anyone who keeps a project in a deeply nested folder.

# 43. Proposal: Carry a Function's Contract and History Over When It Is Renamed or Its Signature Changes Inside the Same File (Implemented — see Task 42)

**Status: implemented** — Task 42. Proposed by the user ("we can detect it somehow like git diff, so move the data to that node and mark it stale"). It must come **before §44**, which depends on it. **Order of work: §42 (independent, can go any time), then §43, then §44, then §45** (§45 prints the IDs of §44).

## Why

When a stored function's ID is no longer in its file's fresh parse, the scan hard-deletes it together with everything attached: `_sync_removed_nodes` calls `cascade_delete_artifact` (graph_sync.py:382-396; `delete_artifact` in `queries.py` documents that callers delete the referencing rows first). Nothing recognises a rename or a move. So today renaming a function, or moving it to another file, destroys its contract, reasoning traces, vision findings and learning history. Contracts are the product.

Measured on this repository, last commit against the working tree (47 files, months of work): of 136 modified functions, 13 (9%) changed their parameters, 123 changed only their body, 25 were removed and 137 added. One repository, mostly one author and well typed, comparing two snapshots rather than each edit, so it is a rough figure; it says that signature changes are common, not rare, and that §44 turns each of them into a "removed plus added" pair.

## The idea

A scan already holds both sides: the stored functions that disappeared and the new ones that appeared. Pair them, move the data, and mark the new function STALE (the status that already exists for changed code), so the agent re-checks it.

## Matching rules (proposed; thresholds to be tuned on real code)

Done in the same publish (one `staged_scan`), after the parse and before the delete:

1. **Same file, same qualified name, different parameters.** If exactly one stored function of that qualified name disappeared and exactly one with that qualified name appeared, they are the same function with a changed signature. With several on each side, pair by the best body similarity, one to one; leave unpaired what is left.
2. **Rename inside the file.** Pair a disappeared function with an appeared one **of the same file** whose normalised body is identical (a pure rename), or whose token overlap is high (a starting point: 60%, as git's rename detection does at 50%) and clearly better than any other candidate.
3. **No unique best match, or a tie: no pairing.** The function is deleted and the new one is new, as today. A wrong carry-over costs a re-verification, but a silent wrong one would be worse than none, so ambiguity means no.

**Same file only (decided by the user).** A function moved to another file is treated as an independent removal from the old file and an addition to the new one: its contract is deleted as today and the new function starts without one. That keeps the rule simple and avoids any dependence on which files the worker happens to re-scan in the same pass.

## What is carried

For a pair, re-point to the new ID: the contract, reasoning traces, vision findings, learning-history rows and the usage score; set the new function's status to STALE and **lower the contract's confidence** (decided by the user): to just below the project's confidence threshold (`min(old, threshold - 1)`, so below 70 by default), so that it cannot count as verified until it is re-verified; the original value goes into the record of the carry-over so it is visible and can be compared after the re-check; record where it came from ("signature changed", "renamed from X") so it is visible in `contract get` and in the history. Edges and vectors are derived and are rebuilt by the normal sync.

## Limits to be honest about

- **A move to another file is not carried over (decided by the user).** The removal and the addition are seen as two unrelated changes, in the same pass or in different ones. No record of removed functions ("tombstones") is kept, so there is no extra state. A contract on a function that is moved to another file is lost, as it is today.
- **Re-pointing rows is awkward in DuckDB.** A primary key that other tables reference cannot be updated in place; it needs a copy and delete, in one transaction, like the foreign-key workaround already in `queries.py` (`_fk_references_detached`).
- **Heuristic.** It will sometimes decline a true pair and, rarely, accept a false one; hence STALE on every carry-over.

## Acceptance tests

1. Add a parameter to a function that has a contract: after the sync it has a new ID, the contract and traces are on the new ID, the status is STALE, the confidence is below the threshold with the original value recorded, and the source of the carry-over is recorded.
2. Rename a function without changing its body: carried over. Rename it and change a few lines: carried over. Rewrite it completely: not carried (deleted and new).
3. Move a function to another file (in the same publish or in two separate ones): not carried over; the old function and its contract are deleted and the new one is new. This is the documented, intended behaviour.
4. Two near-identical functions, one removed and one added: not paired when the choice is not clear.
5. Delete a function and, in a later unrelated scan, add a different one: not paired.
6. A failure in the middle leaves the database as it was (the publish is already atomic; the test shows the carry-over is inside it).

## Not decided here

The similarity measure and thresholds. (Decided by the user: same file only, no tombstones. Decided by the user: the carried contract's confidence is lowered, not kept. The exact rule above, `min(old, threshold - 1)`, is a proposal the dev may refine, for example a smaller reduction for a pure rename whose body is identical.)

# 44. Proposal: One Identity Rule for Every Language — a Readable Name Plus a Hash of the Parameters (Implemented — see Task 43)

**Status: implemented** — Task 43. Decided by the user: the function ID is built from the signature for every language (not only C++), stored as the readable name plus a short hash. **Depends on §43** (a parameter edit now changes the ID).

## Why

Today the ID is `file::qualified_name`, built in one place (`scanner.py:129`) from the name each parser chooses. Two same-name definitions in a file collapse to one: the Python parser keeps the last (python_parser.py:72-88), the C++ parser the first definition (cpp_parser.py:72-85). Checked with small samples: the second and third C++ overloads of `add`, the second `Calc::mul`, the second `Calc2::div` are not indexed at all (8 definitions, 4 indexed); a Python property and its `@x.setter` collapse to one entry (the setter's); TypeScript overload signatures collapse into the implementation. Not indexed means: no search for the body, no contract, a change to it probably not noticed as stale (only the kept function is hashed), and calls to it resolve to the kept one. Python's "last definition wins" is right at run time, but the others are real, separate functions.

## The rule

`ID = <file>::<qualified_name>#<8 hex>`, for example `src/drspec/db/queries.py::insert_artifact#7c1e4a90`.

- **The hash** is the first 8 hex characters of the SHA-1 of a canonical text of the parameters. Use a stable hash, not Python's `hash()`. Eight characters are plenty: a collision only matters between functions with the same qualified name in the same file.
- **Canonical text.** A version tag and, in order, one token per parameter: the declared type (whitespace normalised; C++ `const`, `&`, `*` kept; a method's `const` or reference qualifier appended after the list), or the parameter's name when it has no declared type. `*args` and `**kwargs` as such. **Not included:** default values, the return type, decorators, and `self`/`cls` (Python) or the implicit `this` (C++): changing any of them must not give the function a new ID. Example: Python `def _sync_caller_edges(conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, caller_id: str, caller_hash: str, ...)` has tokens `duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, str, str, ...`; untyped `def f(a, b)` has `a, b`, so renaming an untyped parameter changes the ID (§43 carries the contract over).
- **A function with no parameters** still gets a suffix (the hash of the empty list), so the rule is the same everywhere.
- **One function builds it** (`function_identity`), used by `scanner.py`; parsers expose the parameter tokens in `ExtractedFunction`. The canonical text carries a version number so that improving the normaliser later is a deliberate migration.
- **Constants and classes** keep `file::name`. Checked in the code: `ScannedConstant` builds its own `file::name` ID (`scanner.py:170`) and has no parameters; `project_facts.class_identity` is only an internal dictionary key used to resolve classes (called from `graph_sync._sync_edges`, `_sync_references` and four helpers in `project_facts.py`), not a stored function ID, and stays as it is. Not checked: whether constants and functions share one table, which matters only for the plain `file::name` lookup below (a plain name must also find a constant).

## Parsers stop collapsing same-name definitions

De-duplicate by (qualified name, canonical parameters), not by qualified name. Python: two defs with identical parameters still keep the last (run-time behaviour); a property and its setter are two functions. C++/TypeScript: a bare declaration is dropped only when a definition of the same qualified name exists in the same file (this keeps TypeScript overload signatures out and keeps header declarations in); overloads with bodies are all kept.

## Call resolution must change with it

Found while checking this section against `core/call_resolver.py`. The resolver does not build callee IDs from names; it looks them up in maps built from the artifacts. Several of those maps hold **one** ID per name: `classes_by_name` (`{class: {method_name: function_id}}`), `imported_names` (bare name to one ID) and `imported_modules` (`{alias: {attribute: function_id}}`). With overloads two functions share a (class, method) key, so these maps would silently keep one of them. And in `_resolve_bare` the same-file lookup resolves only `if candidates and len(candidates) == 1`; otherwise the call is **unresolved** (unless `deep_scan`). Today a C++ call `add(1, 2)` resolves to the one indexed `add`; once the three overloads are indexed it would resolve to nothing, losing the edge. That would be a regression caused by this change.

Required: make those maps hold a list of IDs per key; when a call can reach more than one function with the same qualified name, emit **one edge per candidate with the existing AMBIGUOUS confidence** (the tier `_resolve_fallback` already uses for several candidates) instead of none. Optional improvement, for the dev to judge: if the call site's argument count matches the parameter count of exactly one candidate, resolve to that one as EXTRACTED (the call-site record would need the argument count; it keeps only the text today).

## Lookups keep working

A function ID given as `file::name` without a hash resolves when exactly one function has that qualified name in that file; when several do, the command fails with the candidates (IDs and signatures). Applies to the CLI and the MCP tools (`validators.validate_function_id`, `find_similar_functions`). Search output shows the ID value with its hash (§45), so agents copy it.

## Code that takes the ID apart

- Seven places derive a display name with `function_id.split("::")[-1]` (the last segment): `core/dependency_info.py` lines 84, 120, 158; `db/graph.py` lines 173, 395; `debugging/missing.py` line 213; `visualization/grapher.py` lines 311, 435. For `calc.cpp::add#3fa9` or any C++ name the result is wrong or carries the hash. Use the stored `function_name` of the artifact (and the part of the ID after the file and before `#` as the fallback).
- `learning/diff.py:565` builds an ID itself, `f"{file_diff.path}::{func.name}"`, from the short name, where the index uses the qualified name. Read in the code, not run: a modified method is credited to `file::method`, an ID the index does not have (`file::Class.method`, as the Python parser's `A.x` shows). Only `analyze_commit_range` calls it (Python files only). Replace it by the shared builder, which with this rule also needs the function's parameters (the parse result has them).
- Splitting on the first `::` to separate the file (validators, contract and source commands) stays correct.

## Migration (every ID changes once)

All 2,748 functions of this project get a new ID, in every table and key that holds one: `artifacts`, both ends of `dependencies` and `code_references`, `contracts`, `reasoning_traces`, `vision_findings`, `learning_history`, `usage_stats`, `scanned_files`, and the `config` keys that embed an ID (`embedding:...`, `verification:...`). The dev must list every column and key that holds an ID (search for `function_id`, `caller_id`, `callee_id` and the key prefixes) and test each.

Same pattern as `db/migration.py` (§39): at worker start and in `init`, a marker `schema:identity = v1`; build a new file beside the old one from a copy, compute each new ID, copy every table in foreign-key order with the IDs replaced, compare row counts, move the sequences, write the marker, sync, back up the old file, atomic rename; any failure leaves the old file untouched and the worker refuses to start with the reason. The new IDs should be computed from the **stored signature text** (no file access, deterministic); verify on this repository that they equal the IDs a fresh parse produces for all 2,748 functions, and fall back to a re-scan for any that do not. The embedding vectors are copied (they depend on the function text, not on the ID). Prompts, README and agent documents that show example IDs are updated.

## Acceptance tests

1. A C++ file with three `add` overloads, two `Calc::mul` and two `Calc2::div`: all seven are indexed with different IDs, each has its own body, hash and callers' edges; editing one marks only it stale.
2. A Python property and its setter are two functions.
3. Changing a default value, the return type or a decorator does not change the ID; changing a parameter's type, adding a parameter or renaming an untyped parameter does (and §43 carries the contract over).
4. The same source parsed twice, and on two machines, gives the same IDs.
5. `file::name` resolves when unique and lists candidates when not; with a hash it is exact.
6. The seven display-name places show the right name for a C++ method with `::` in its type.
7. Migration of a copy of this repository's database: every table's row count equal, every contract, trace and finding on the new ID, the edge tables equal to those of a fresh full scan, an interrupted migration leaves the old file byte-identical.
8. A C++ call `add(1, 2)` in a file with three `add` overloads produces an edge to each of them with AMBIGUOUS confidence (not no edge, and not an edge to one only); a call to a name with a single definition is EXTRACTED as before; the `{method: id}` maps keep every overload.

# 45. Proposal: Group Search Results by File and Keep the Labels (Implemented — see Task 44)

**Status: implemented** — Task 44. Asked for by the user ("`src/drspec/db/queries.py` duplicates a lot"); the labelled text format was kept at their request.

## The problem

Every match repeats `ID`, `File`, `Language` and, nearly always, `Status: PENDING` and `Possibly Stale: No`; the path appears three times per match (in `ID`, in `File`, and in the next block of the same file).

## The format

The header block (`Pattern`, `Semantic`, `Files Searched`, `Total Matches`, `Matches:`) is unchanged, plus one line stating the rule `ID = File::<ID value>` (for example `Function ID: <File>::<ID>`). Matches are grouped under one `File:` line that carries the language and the count. Every field the user wants to see is kept:

```
File: src/drspec/core/graph_sync.py   (python, 3 matches)
  ID: _record_scanned_file_snapshots#3fa91c0e
    Status: PENDING
    Possibly Stale: No
    Signature: def _record_scanned_file_snapshots(conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, scanned_file_paths: Iterable[str]) -> None:
  ID: _sync_caller_edges#7c1e4a90
    Status: PENDING
    Possibly Stale: No
    Signature: def _sync_caller_edges( conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, caller_id: str, caller_hash: str, ... ) -> None:
```

- `File` and `Language` once per file. `Language` belongs to the file, not to the function.
- **There is no `Name` line.** The client does not need a name as a field; it needs something to pass to the next command (`source get`, `deps get`, `contract get`), which is the ID. The `ID:` line prints the part of the ID after the file, the qualified name **with its `#hash`** (§44); the full ID is `File` + `::` + that value, and the header says so in one line. (The user confirmed this over printing the full ID on every match, which would repeat the path; the saving is the same either way, since the line replaces `Name`.) Until §44 lands, the value is the qualified name only (`Class.method`), which also fixes today's `Name` dropping the class. The name still appears in `Signature`, which stays untouched, so it is shown twice by design. `--json` keeps `name`, `id` and every other field.
- `Status` and `Possibly Stale` on every match: nothing is implied by absence.
- **`Signature` is unchanged** (full parameter names and types, no cap, no reformatting). Decided by the user after seeing that types alone save only about 19% and lose which argument is which and all information in untyped code.

## Measured

The same five results of `drspec search upsert`: 2,145 characters now, 1,661 in this format (23% less). With signatures capped at 160 characters and joined onto one line it was 1,015 compact or 1,387 labelled (36% less), but the user kept the signature as it is. The saving grows with the number of matches in one file (real searches: `conn` 24,450 characters, `upsert` 6,350, `test_` 15,625 in the current format).

## Scope

The same grouping where a result lists several functions of one file: `search` (all modes), `deps_get`, `unused`, `contract_list`; the dev proposes the layout for the last three in the same style. `--json` / `format: json` output is unchanged. Anything that parses the text format (tests, prompts) is updated; nothing in the product should parse it.

## Acceptance tests

1. A search with matches in three files prints three `File:` groups, each path once, each with its count and language.
2. Rebuilding `File` + `::` + the `ID:` value for any printed match gives an ID that `source get` accepts; the header line states that rule.
3. A project with Python and TypeScript files shows the right language on each group.
4. Output of a search with one match per file is not longer than today's by more than the group line.
5. The JSON output is byte-identical to before.

# 46. Proposal: Keep the Trace Log Outside the Project, One File Per Project (Implemented — see Task 45)

**Status: proposal, not yet a task.** Asked for by the user: "store the log somewhere like /tmp so it won't accumulate over time in the project; consider several drspec runs in several projects, so the log would be appended from many projects."

## Why

Today the log is `_drspec/trace.log` beside the database (`tracing.bind`: `Path(db_path).parent / "trace.log"`), plus `trace.log.1` after a rotation and `trace.log.lock`. It lives in the project folder, so it grows there (up to about 40 MB with the older file), is easy to commit by mistake, and is written with mode 0644 although it contains SQL text prefixes, file names and timings. The real project's log was 1.2 MB with about 4,900 events after one day of use with `trace.level = all`.

## The change

1. **Where.** A per-user folder in the temporary directory: `<tempdir>/drspec-trace-<uid>/` (`tempfile.gettempdir()`, so it follows `TMPDIR`). Created with mode 0700. If it exists it must be a real directory (not a symbolic link) owned by the current user, otherwise tracing is disabled for that process with one line on standard error. Files inside are written with mode 0600. The operating system clears the temporary directory sooner or later, which is what the user wants.
2. **One file per project, never mixed.** The file name is derived from the database path, the same way `worker_ipc.socket_path` already derives `drspec-<hash>.sock`: `<first 16 hex of SHA-1 of the resolved database path>.log` (and `.log.1`, `.log.lock`). All processes of one project (CLI, worker, embedding process) still append to the same file, with the existing rotation lock; different projects write to different files. So two projects never share a log, whatever runs at the same time.
3. **No project data in the log, not even to name the project** (decided by the user: "just log enough information for improvement, don't log project data, which is dangerous"). The file is identified by its hash only; no header, no sidecar file and no event holds a project path. A new `drspec trace path` prints the log location of the project it is run in (the hash is derived from the current database path), so the owner can find it; see "What the log may contain" below for the rest.
4. **Reading.** `trace summary`, `trace tail` and the MCP `trace_summary` find the log of the current project by the same hash (they already take the database path). New: `drspec trace list` prints every log in the folder (hash, size, last event time; no path, because none is stored), and `trace clear --all` empties them all. Nothing in the reader reads another project's file unless asked.
5. **Keeping it small.** Per-file rotation stays (`trace.max_mb`, 20 MB, one older file). New: on worker start and on `trace clear`, delete log files in the folder that were not written for N days (proposed: 7) and, if the folder is larger than a total cap (proposed: 200 MB), the oldest files first. Both numbers as settings next to `trace.max_mb` (`trace.keep_days`, `trace.max_total_mb`).
6. **Override.** The environment variable `DRSPEC_TRACE_DIR` (and, if the settings table can hold a text value, a `trace.dir` setting) points to another folder, for example one inside a project for a one-off investigation. The same ownership and mode rules apply.
7. **The old place.** Nothing is written to `_drspec/` any more. If `_drspec/trace.log` (or `.1`) exists, the reader still shows it, marked "legacy", until `trace clear` removes it; nothing is deleted automatically. `.gitignore` entries for the old file can go.

## What the log may contain (tightened)

Only what is needed to find out where time and memory go: event kind, process kind, durations, counts, sizes, memory, CPU, load, exit status, option **names** (not values), a request's operation name, the **shape** of a SQL statement (drspec's own text with `?` in place of every value: table and column names of drspec's schema are not project data), and error **class names**.

It must never contain project data: no file or directory path, no file name, no function, class or constant name or identifier, no search pattern or query text, no contract or finding text, no SQL value, no environment value, and no exception message (messages often quote a path or a value). Checked against the real project's log (5,182 events from one day with `trace.level = all`), these are the offenders today:

- the `file` field, on the re-scan `parse` events (289 events), holds the real path of each scanned file (for example `src/drspec/staleness_worker.py`): keep the duration and the number of functions, drop the name (if a per-file figure is still wanted, a short hash of the path is not enough, because paths can be guessed; use a running index within the batch);
- the `pattern` field of search commands holds what the user typed (`conn`, `upsert`, `test_` were found): record its length and the mode (literal, regex, semantic) only;
- the `reason` text and any other free text: an enumerated code, not a sentence that may quote data (the sample seen, "no progress: 106 still owed", is only a count and is fine as a code plus a number).

The writer should enforce this rather than rely on each call site: `tracing.emit` accepts fields only from a whitelist of names with number, boolean or short enumerated-string values; anything else is dropped (and counted in a `dropped_fields` figure so the omission is visible).

## Not changed

The levels (`off`, `slow`, `all`), `trace.slow_ms`, the rotation lock, the worker applying a changed level immediately, and the JSON line format. (The privacy rules are replaced by the stricter ones above.)

## Acceptance tests

1. With tracing on, no file named `trace.log*` is created under `_drspec/`; the log is in `<tempdir>/drspec-trace-<uid>/<hash>.log`; the folder is mode 0700 and the file 0600.
2. Two projects running at the same time (a worker in each, commands in both) write to two files; a command run in one project adds no event to the other project's file, and `trace summary` in each project reports only its own events.
3. `trace list` shows both with hash, size and last event time and no path; `trace clear` empties the current project's log only; `trace clear --all` empties both.
4. A folder that exists and belongs to another user, or is a symbolic link, makes tracing stay off for that process with a visible one-line message, and no command fails.
5. Six processes appending to one project's file with a small limit (the earlier rotation test) still end with all six tracing and no corrupt line.
6. A file older than `trace.keep_days` is removed on worker start; with a total over `trace.max_total_mb` the oldest files go first and the file in use stays.
7. A legacy `_drspec/trace.log` is still readable (marked legacy) and `trace clear` removes it; the worker no longer writes to it.
8. A `DRSPEC_TRACE_DIR` pointing to a writable folder moves the log there; to an unwritable one, tracing stays off without failing a command.
9. **No project data.** In a scratch project, put a marker word in a directory name, a file name, a function name, a function body, a contract text and a search pattern, run `scan`, `search` (literal, regex and semantic), `source get`, `contract save/get`, `deps get` and a re-scan by the worker with `trace.level = all`: the marker appears nowhere in the log folder (neither in the log, its older file nor any sidecar), and neither does the absolute path of the project. A call that passes a non-whitelisted field to `tracing.emit` drops it and counts it.
10. The `file` and `pattern` fields no longer appear in the events of a re-scan and of a search.

## Not decided here

The retention numbers (7 days, 200 MB), whether the temporary directory or the user's state folder (`~/.local/state`, kept across reboots) is the better default (the user asked for the temporary directory), and whether the text setting `trace.dir` is worth adding next to the environment variable.

# 47. Proposal: Drain the Re-scan Queue Promptly and Start With What Matters (Implemented — see Task 46; no `nice`)

**Status: proposal, not yet a task.** Asked for by the user: on a big project the first use should not wait for a full scan; what the user needs should be usable fast, the rest should follow in the background without noise, and only an explicit flag or command should force a full scan. Confirmed by the user ("yes, write it as §47"), then corrected by the user: the CPU cap is of the whole machine, and the queue is drained continuously with a dynamic pause; the fixed 60 s belongs to the detector of new and changed files only.

## What was measured (fresh project of 400 files, 1,200 functions, embedding off, `trace.level = all`)

- `drspec init` returned in **1.2 s**. It does not scan; the worker discovers and indexes everything in the background. So nothing blocks, and an explicit `drspec scan` stays the only synchronous full scan.
- Left alone, the worker ran a pass every **60.4 s** (four passes in a row: 60.4, 60.4, 60.4) and each pass did only **356-431 ms** of work (`detect` 4-10 ms, `discover` 63-102 ms, `rescan` 268-326 ms; in a re-scan publish: parse 10 files 14 ms, checkpoint 31 ms, copy 6 ms, sync 144 ms, commit 9 ms, fsync 86 ms, replace 3 ms; database 9.5 MB). The worker is busy about 0.7% of the time and idle the rest.
- The cause is the loop in `run_worker` (`staleness_worker.py`, around lines 519-526): after a pass that did work, `sleep_for = interval`, the full `worker.poll_interval` (60 s), whether or not more files are queued. With `worker.rescan_budget` = 10, the queue drains at **10 files per minute**: 400 files in about 40 minutes, 5,000 files in about 8 hours, before the whole project is searchable.
- The much faster progress seen when polling by hand (60 files in about 40 s) happened only because every client request wakes the worker (`worker.wake_min_gap` 5 s), i.e. each of my `drspec status` calls triggered a pass.
- Order today: the queue is ordered by reason (search first, then drift, then new files; `_RESCAN_PRIORITY_SQL`), then by when the file was queued, then by path. New files are queued by a walk in sorted (alphabetical) order, so the order inside "new files" is alphabetical: a file edited today and a file nobody has touched for years are treated alike.
- `drspec search` says nothing when part of the project is not indexed yet, so a function in a file that has not been reached looks like "no match" (the same kind of false zero as §41).

## Changes

1. **Two jobs with two rhythms (decided by the user).**
   - **Detector** (the heavy part: the drift sweep that stats every known file, and the walk that looks for new files): stays on `worker.poll_interval` (60 s), and on the dormant interval after 120 s in which nothing was found, as today. That is what the 60 s is for. It only puts files in the queue.
   - **Processor** (parsing and publishing the queued files): runs **continuously** while the queue is not empty, with no fixed wait. The pause between two batches is dynamic and is zero when the machine has room. A pause happens only when a limit says so:
     - **CPU cap, of the whole machine.** New setting `worker.cpu_percent` (proposed default 20): the worker's average CPU use over a short window, process CPU time over (wall time x number of cores), must stay at or below it. 20% on a 12-core machine is 2.4 cores, on a 4-core machine 0.8 core. After a batch of CPU time `c` and wall time `w`, wait `gap = max(0, c / (cores x cpu_percent / 100) - w)`. (Different from `embedding.cpu_percent`, which means a share of one core.)
     - **Load and memory guards**, the same as for embedding (`embedding.max_load_per_core`, `embedding.min_available_memory_mb`): pause while the machine is busy.
     - **A short yield between batches** (`worker.yield_ms`, proposed 50 ms) so a waiting client request gets the worker's lock before the next batch; a request is never starved by the drain.
   A file queued by the detector, by `search`, or by a command that names it wakes the processor at once; it does not wait for the next 60 s. When the queue is empty the processor sleeps until woken.
   **Estimate** from the measured 0.4 s per 10 files on a small database: about 25 files/s, i.e. 400 files in about 16 s and 5,000 files in about 3.5 minutes (slower as the database grows, because each publish copies the whole file; see §50 if that is taken up).
   **Consequence to be aware of:** the worker parses in one thread, so on a large machine the whole-machine cap does not slow it (one busy core is about 8% of a 12-core machine): it runs flat out on one core until the queue is empty. On a 4-core machine one core is 25%, so there the cap does slow it. **Accepted by the user:** using a full core until the queue is empty is fine. A lowest operating-system priority on top of it is proposed (see "Not decided").
2. **Time-boxed passes.** A pass stops after `worker.pass_seconds` of work (default 1 s of parsing plus publishing, at least one file) or `worker.rescan_budget` files (the cap; raise its default from 10 to 200, since the time box now protects the lock), whichever comes first, and publishes once. This keeps the foreground stall at what §36 required (a command waits at most about one pass) while draining about 10 to 25 files per second on a small database. The publish copies the whole database, so the rate falls as the database grows; the time box keeps the stall bounded anyway.
3. **Priority order** (the queue stays persistent, so a restart continues where it stopped):
   1. files named by what the user asked: the existing `search_drift` / `search_unscanned` reasons, extended so that `source get`, `deps get` and `contract` commands on a file that is not indexed yet queue it first;
   2. then the other existing reasons in their current order (drift, changed);
   3. then new files in two tiers: files modified recently (default: within the last 14 days, by modification time, newest first) before older files, then by path.
