   The discovery walk stays alphabetical with its saved cursor (so resuming is unchanged); the tier is stored when the file is queued, as a `priority` column or as two reasons (`new_file_recent`, `new_file`); the dev chooses.
4. **Say when the index is incomplete.** `search` (CLI and MCP, every mode), `deps get` and `source get` add a note when files are still queued or undiscovered: `Index incomplete: N of M files are not scanned yet; results may be missing. Run 'drspec scan <path>' to scan now.` The note disappears when the queue is empty. JSON gets `index: {pending: N, total: M}`. `worker status` and `status` already show the counts.
5. **Explicit full scan unchanged.** `drspec scan [path]` remains the synchronous way to scan everything (or one directory) at once. Nothing in this section makes `init` or any first command scan the whole project in the foreground.
6. **Trace.** The `pass` / batch event gains `queued_after` (files left), `gap_ms` and `pause_reason` (`none`, `cap`, `load`, `memory`, `yield`), so the drain can be checked from the log without any project data (§46).

## Acceptance tests

1. A fresh project of 400 small files after `init`, left alone: fully indexed in under 1 minute (before: about 40 minutes); the trace shows batches back to back (no gap of about 60 s) while `queued_after` > 0, except pauses whose `pause_reason` is `cap`, `load` or `memory`; the worker's average CPU as a share of the whole machine (process CPU time over wall time and cores, from the trace) never exceeds `worker.cpu_percent`; on a machine or a simulated core count where one core is above the cap, the worker is slowed to the cap.
2. Detector and processor are independent: with an empty queue the detector keeps its 60 s cadence and then the dormant interval after 120 s idle (trace), and a file it finds is processed within about 1 s of being found, not at the next 60 s.
3. During the drain `drspec status` and `drspec search` answer within the stall bound (for example under 3 s) on a project of 2,000 files.
4. A file modified today whose name sorts last is indexed before an unmodified file that sorts first (two tiers); within a tier the order is by path.
5. `drspec source get` on a file that is not yet indexed queues it first and it is indexed on the next pass; a `search` that would have matched it shows the incomplete-index note until then.
6. A killed worker restarted mid-drain continues from the persistent queue without re-parsing files already published.
7. While the queue is non-empty `search` prints the note with the right counts and nothing when it is empty; the JSON field matches.
8. Two projects draining at the same time both progress, and the load and memory guards still pause embedding when the machine is busy.

## Not decided here

The default numbers (`worker.cpu_percent` 20, yield 50 ms, pass time box 1 s, cap 200 files, 14-day tier), whether the tier uses modification time or "tracked and changed in git" (modification time needs no git and is proposed), whether the incomplete-index note should also appear in `contract` commands, and **whether the drain should also run at the lowest operating-system priority** (`nice` 19, as the embedding process already does), so that it never competes with the user's own programs even when the whole-machine cap does not bind. Proposed: yes.

# 48. Feedback From Use on Another Project: A C++ File With One Unparseable Construct Is Not Indexed at All (Implemented — see Task 47)

**Status: reported, not yet a task.** Source: a scan of `behavior_nodes/conditions` in another C++ project (83 files, 285 functions, 19 parse errors, all on lines like `template<> ConditionNodeImpl<X>::~ConditionNodeImpl<X>() = default;`). The reporter concluded "valid C++, a tree-sitter-cpp 0.21 limitation; it does not lose the other functions in those files". The first half is right; the second half is **not**, as checked here. (The rest of that report is feedback on the other project's own code and is not about drspec.)

## Reproduced

A file with a generic class, an ordinary function and two explicit-specialisation defaulted destructors (`template<> Node<A>::~Node<A>() = default;`):

- The parser alone (`CppParser().parse`) reports 2 to 3 `Syntax error: ERROR` entries on the specialisation lines and still extracts the other functions (`Node::tick`, `helper`, the generic destructor).
- A real `drspec scan` of a project with that file and a clean file: `Files Scanned: 2`, `Functions Found: 1` (only the clean file's function), `Error Count: 2`. `search helper` returns **0 matches**. `worker status` shows `Files In Retry Wait: 1`. So **every function of the file is missing, including the ones that parsed fine**, and the file waits in backoff and is re-parsed (and fails) again and again.

## Cause

`Scanner.scan_file` sets `parse_status = "partial"` when the tree has any ERROR node (`scanner.py`, around line 347). `_scan_single_file` (`scan.py` line 330) and `_scan_dir` (line 360) then use the functions of a file **only when `parse_status == "successful"`**, and add only such files to `scanned_file_paths`; a partial file is also put in `protected_file_paths`. This is deliberate: a transient syntax error in a half-typed edit must not be read as "every function in this file was deleted". But the rule is applied to all partial files, so valid code that the grammar cannot parse is never indexed, whatever the number of good functions around the bad construct.

Why not simply upgrade the grammar: on Python 3.8 the newest `tree-sitter` available from the package index is 0.21.3 and the project pins `tree-sitter-cpp` to 0.21 (checked: nothing newer installs). So the grammar limit stays for the supported Python versions, and the fix has to be on drspec's side.

## Suggested change

1. **Index what parsed cleanly, protect what did not.** For a partial file, extract every function whose own syntax subtree has no ERROR node (the tree-sitter node has `has_error`) and sync those like a normal scan (new and changed ones are written), but **do not run the removal pass for that file** and keep the stored functions that are not in the fresh parse: that keeps the original safety (a half-typed edit never deletes stored functions) and no longer drops the good ones. A function whose own subtree contains the error is skipped and its stored version, if any, is kept as it is.
2. **Do not report valid C++ as an error.** A node that matches a known harmless construct, starting with an explicit specialisation of a member that is `= default;` or `= delete;` (`template<> ... = default;`), is skipped without counting as an error; such a construct has no body to index. Count what was skipped separately ("skipped constructs: N") so the omission is visible.
3. **A partial file is settled, not retried for ever.** When the clean functions were synced, the file counts as scanned: it leaves the retry wait, and its snapshot is recorded so it is re-scanned only when it changes. Files that could not be read at all keep the current backoff.
4. **Say it.** `scan` output and `status` show "files partially parsed: N (M functions skipped)" with the first few files and line numbers, so a user knows that part of a file is not indexed. `search` and `deps get` do not need to repeat it.

## Acceptance tests

1. The sample above: after `scan`, `search helper` and `Node::tick` find their functions; the specialisation lines are reported as skipped constructs, not as errors; the file is not in retry wait.
2. A file with a real syntax error in the middle of one function (an unclosed brace) and three clean functions: the clean ones are indexed, the broken one is skipped, and a function stored earlier under the broken one's ID is still stored (not deleted).
3. A file that is half-typed (an editor save in the middle of an edit) never causes a stored function to be deleted; the next clean save restores the normal behaviour, including deleting functions that were really removed.
4. The 19-error project of the report (or a scratch copy of its pattern): all ordinary functions of the affected files appear, with the error count reduced to the real errors (zero for the specialisations).
5. Python and TypeScript files with a real syntax error behave the same way (the rule is language-independent).
6. `status` / `scan` show the partial-file count and the skipped functions.

## Not decided here

Whether skipped functions inside a partial file should be listed one by one or only counted; whether other harmless constructs (explicit instantiations, `requires` clauses and other C++20 syntax that the old grammar rejects) should join the skip list now or be added as they are reported.

# 49. Proposal: One Embedding-Model Cache Per User, Not Per Project (Implemented — see Task 48)

**Status: proposal, not yet a task.** Decided by the user: a shared cache ("yes"), and the per-user cache folder in the table below, confirmed ("ok").

## Why

`embedder._default_cache_dir()` returns `Path.cwd() / "_drspec" / "models"` (the only place the path is built; `_get_model` and `model_is_cached` call it). Every project therefore downloads and keeps its own copy: 87 MB in `drspec`, 87 MB in `artos`, 87 MB for each next project, and each first embedding waits for a download. Because it is relative to the current directory and not to the database path, it also depends on where the embedding process happens to run (the worker changes into the project root at start, which is why it works today).

## Where

A per-user **cache** folder, not the temporary folder and not the project:

| System | Folder |
|---|---|
| Linux | `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/drspec/models`, else `~/.cache/drspec/models` |
| macOS | `~/Library/Caches/drspec/models` |
| Windows | `%LOCALAPPDATA%\drspec\Cache\models` |

Override with the environment variable `DRSPEC_MODEL_DIR` (for a shared team folder, a container volume or an offline machine with a pre-copied model). Why not the temporary folder as for the trace log (§46): the log is disposable, the model is 87 MB that would be downloaded again after every restart of the machine. Why not `~/.local/state`: that is for state that must be kept; a re-downloadable model is exactly what the cache folder is for, and tools that clean caches know it.

The layout inside is unchanged (`models--qdrant--all-MiniLM-L6-v2-onnx/snapshots/...`), so `model_is_cached` keeps working with the new path.

## Rules

1. **Several projects at once.** Two workers may need the model at the same moment. Only one may download: take an exclusive lock on a file inside the cache folder (`.download.lock`, `fcntl.flock`) around the download; the others wait and then find it cached. The downloader writes to a temporary name and renames, so a killed download never leaves a half-written model that looks complete. (The dev should check what `fastembed` and the Hugging Face hub library already lock, and add only what is missing.) The state shown to the user stays `downloading` / `loading` as today.
2. **Migration.** If the shared folder has no model and the project's old `_drspec/models` has one, copy it (hard link when on the same disk) into the shared folder once, so nothing is downloaded again. The old project folder is then unused; `drspec status` or `init` prints one line saying it can be deleted (87 MB). Nothing is deleted automatically.
3. **Fallback.** If the shared folder cannot be created or written (read-only home, restricted container), use the project-local folder as today and say so once in `worker status`.
4. **Permissions.** Created with the user's normal umask; the model is public data, so no special mode, but the folder must belong to the current user (the same check as §46).
5. **Version.** The model name and revision stay pinned in `embedder.py`; a new revision is a new folder next to the old one (the library already does this), and a cleanup command is not part of this change.
6. **Not in the project any more.** `_drspec/models` stops being created; the packaging spec and `.gitignore` entries for it can be removed once the migration step has shipped.

## Acceptance tests

1. A new project on a machine that already has the model in the shared folder: `scan --embed` embeds without any download and creates no `_drspec/models`.
2. Two projects whose workers start together on a machine with no model: one download happens (one set of files, one network use), both then embed; killing the downloading process midway leaves no folder that `model_is_cached` reports as cached.
3. A project with the old `_drspec/models` and an empty shared folder: the first embedding copies it across, does not download, and the status line mentions the old folder.
4. `DRSPEC_MODEL_DIR` pointing at a writable folder is used; at an unwritable one falls back to the project folder and reports it.
5. The path does not depend on the current directory: starting the embedding process from another directory finds the same model.
6. `model_is_cached` and the state shown while embedding (`downloading` or `loading`) are right in each of the cases above.

## Not decided here

Whether `drspec` should offer a `models` command (list, delete old revisions, show size), and whether the 87 MB figure should be printed once before the first download.

# 50. Proposal: Stop Deleting and Restoring a Function's Contract Just to Change It — Drop the Extra Indexes, Move the Vectors, Remove the Whole-File Copy (Implemented — see Task 49; the 1-second publish target is not met)

**Status: proposal, not yet a task.** Decided by the user ("write option 1"). This is the "option 1" of the discussion about foreign keys: **keep every foreign key**, remove the cause of DuckDB's foreign-key error instead of working around it.

## The problem, with evidence

DuckDB turns an `UPDATE` of an indexed column, or of a list column, into a delete plus an insert of the whole row (documented as a limitation: https://duckdb.org/docs/stable/sql/indexes). A row that other tables point at by a foreign key cannot survive that, even when the key does not change. `artifacts` is the only referenced table and has three secondary indexes (`status`, `file_path`, `function_name`) and a list column (`embedding`), so a plain change of a function's status, or storing its vector, is refused.

The code works around it in `_fk_references_detached` (`queries.py`): park the function's contract, traces, findings and edges in temporary (memory-only) tables, delete them from the real tables, **commit**, change the function, put the rows back, commit again. `cascade_delete_artifact` has a similar commit before its last delete. The consequences:

1. **Data can be lost on the live database today, not only in scans.** Callers of the workaround: `update_artifact_status` (contract saves, stale marking, verification), `embedding_backlog._store` (the embedding process, for every stored vector), `graph_sync._backfill_embeddings`, and `insert_artifact` (inside a scan). Reproduced with the real code in a scratch database: `update_artifact_status(f, "STALE")` on a function with one contract, one reasoning trace and one edge, process killed right after the first commit: after restart `contracts`, `reasoning_traces` and `dependencies` were all **0**, the status unchanged. The window is milliseconds long and each embedded function opens it once, but contracts are the product and the temporary tables vanish with the process.
2. **The whole-file copy.** To protect a scan from the same window, `staged_scan` copies the whole database, syncs into the copy and renames it over the original: checkpoint, copy, commit, fsync of the new file, replace, reconnect. Measured on a replica with this project's database (about 50 MB): checkpoint 58 ms, copy 24 ms, commit 50 ms, fsync 215 ms, about 0.35 s of overhead per publish whatever the number of files, growing with the database, and 50 MB written each time. The worker holds its lock during all of it.
3. A single transaction does not help: tested on DuckDB 1.3.2, park / update / restore inside one `BEGIN ... COMMIT` still fails (the key check ignores the uncommitted delete). Other mainstream databases update such a row in place and also have deferred constraints; DuckDB has neither here.

## What was tested (DuckDB 1.3.2, small scratch tables shaped like ours; not yet drspec's own code)

| Case | Result |
|---|---|
| update `body` of a referenced function | works |
| update `status` / `function_name` / `file_path` with the secondary indexes present | fails |
| the same updates with the three secondary indexes dropped (primary and foreign keys kept) | **works** |
| update a list column (`embedding`) of a referenced row, indexes dropped | fails |
| vector in its own table (`function_id` primary key, foreign key to `artifacts`): delete then insert | **works** |
| several updates of one edge row, including its vector, in one transaction | works |
| delete a function after deleting its dependents **in the same transaction** | **fails** |
| re-point a contract to a new function and delete the old one in one transaction | **fails** (same reason) |
| rollback of a transaction with updates and deletes | restores everything |

The last three matter for the design: removals cannot be part of the one-transaction step (see "Scan sync").

## The change

1. **Schema.** Drop `idx_artifacts_status`, `idx_artifacts_file_path` and `idx_artifacts_function_name`. Keep the primary key and every foreign key, and the indexes on the child tables (nothing points at those). Move the function vector out of `artifacts` into `artifact_embeddings(function_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES artifacts(function_id), embedding FLOAT[] NOT NULL)`. The edge tables keep their own `embedding` column (nothing points at an edge row). A vector is written as delete plus insert in that table, and a re-scan that changes a function deletes its row there instead of writing NULL. (A table that never holds NULL also avoids the old DuckDB null-validity problem of §39.)
2. **Remove the workaround.** Delete `_fk_references_detached` and the `_ROW_REWRITING_COLUMNS` branch of `insert_artifact`; `update_artifact_status`, `insert_artifact`, `embedding_backlog._store` (`store_embeddings`) and `_backfill_embeddings` become plain statements with no commit in the middle. The semantic search joins `artifact_embeddings`; `find_backlog`, the embedding counts and `check_embedding_length` follow (the dev finds every use by searching for `embedding`).
3. **Scan sync in two steps** (replaces `staged_scan`):
   1. **One transaction on the live connection**: insert new functions, update changed ones, sync edges and references, copy a carried-over function's contract, traces, findings and usage to its new identifier (§43; the old ones are not deleted yet), settle the queue, record the file snapshots, and **write the identifiers to remove into a small table `pending_removals(function_id PRIMARY KEY)`** (no foreign key). Any error rolls the whole step back. No copy of the file, no fsync of a new file, no reconnect.
   2. **Removals**, after the commit: for each row of `pending_removals`: delete its dependents (traces, findings, contract, edges, references, vector, config keys), commit, delete the function (the commit before the last delete is the one DuckDB requires), delete the pending row. Every statement is safe to repeat. Anything left after a crash is finished at worker start and at the start of every pass, and `status` shows the count. A crash can never lose data of a live function: the only things deleted in this step belong to functions that are already gone from the source or already copied to their new identifier.
4. **`drspec scan` and the worker use the same path.** `publish_scan_results` runs step 1 on the live connection; `sweep_orphaned_staging` stays for the rebuilds of `db/migration.py` and `identity_migration.py`, which still build a new file.
5. **Migration** of existing databases (same pattern as §39 and §44): marker `schema:fk_layout = v1`, run at worker start and in `init`; build a new file from a copy: current schema, every table copied in foreign-key order with counts compared, the function vectors **copied** into `artifact_embeddings` (they are derived data, but copying avoids re-embedding), the three indexes not created, sequences moved, backup kept until the next successful start, atomic rename; a failure leaves the old file untouched and the worker refuses to start with the reason.

## Costs

- **Lookups by status, file and name lose their index.** Measured on a scratch table of 100,000 functions: by file path 0.20 ms with the index, 0.83 ms without; by function name 0.17 ms and 0.68 ms; list of 50 stale functions 0.38 ms and 0.27 ms; count of PENDING 1.60 ms and 1.58 ms. A drain that looks up one file per file scanned adds a few seconds in total at 5,000 files; the dev should measure it in the real code.
- **Code and tests**: the embedding paths, the semantic search query, the migration, the scan publish, carry-over (copy first, delete later) and the tests that mention the old workaround.
- **One-time rebuild** of every existing database.
- Safety now rests on a DuckDB transaction for step 1 and on `pending_removals` for step 2, instead of on a file swap.

## What it gives

No window in which a contract, trace, finding or edge is out of the database (reproduced loss above); no whole-file copy and no reconnect per publish (about 0.35 s and 50 MB written per publish on this project's database, growing); a shorter lock; fewer commits; status changes and vector stores that are single statements.

## Acceptance tests

1. **Crash injection.** The scratch script of this review (kill the process after the first commit of `update_artifact_status`) is replaced by: kill the process before, between and after every statement of `update_artifact_status`, `store_embeddings` and `contract save` on a function with a contract, a trace and an edge: after restart all three are still there.
2. 100 status changes and 100 vector stores on one function with a contract, edges and traces: every dependent row intact, and no commit in the middle (count commits on the connection).
3. A scan with additions, updates, edge changes and a carry-over, with an error injected in the middle of step 1: the database is exactly as before (compare a dump), and during a normal scan no `.drspec-scan-*` folder or copy of the file appears.
4. Removal: delete a function that has a contract and edges; kill between step 1 and step 2: after restart the removal is completed, nothing of other functions is lost, nothing is orphaned, and `status` showed the pending count meanwhile.
5. Rename with a contract; kill between the steps: the new function has the contract and the old one is removed after restart; a second rename in a row still works.
6. Foreign keys still enforced: inserting a contract, trace, finding or edge for a missing function fails; deleting a function that still has dependents fails.
7. Performance on a copy of this repository's database: a publish of five files falls from the 2.2 s measured after Task 39 to under 1 s, with no copy of the file in the trace (`rescan publish` has no `copy_ms`, `fsync_ms` or `replace_ms`); lookups by file, status and name at 100,000 functions stay under 5 ms.
8. Migration of a copy of the real database: row counts equal, the vectors present and equal (count and a sample), the three indexes gone, foreign keys kept, marker set, backup identical to the original, an interrupted migration leaves the old file byte-identical, and a semantic search returns the same functions with the same scores as before.

## Not decided here

Whether `drspec scan --force` should keep an option to build in a copy (`--safe-copy`) for debugging; the exact list of statements of step 1; whether the edge-table vectors should also move out later (not needed now).

# 51. Plan: Bring C++ Support Up to the Level of Python (Implemented — see Task 50)

**Status: plan, not yet a task.** Asked for by the user ("if it does not fully support C++, write a plan"). It is based on real scans of two scratch C++ projects during the review of Tasks 41-44 (an overload sample, and a four-file project with a header, namespaces, a class, an operator, a template, a lambda and a test macro). Python is the reference because the call resolver, the identity rule and the tests were built around it.

## What works for C++ today (verified)

- **Identity and overloads** (§44): all eight definitions of a file with three `add` overloads, two `Calc::mul` and two `Calc2::div` are indexed (four before); every overload has its own hash; a plain `file::add` lists the candidates; changing one overload marks only that one.
- **Calls to an overloaded name** (same file, no namespace): one edge per candidate with `AMBIGUOUS` confidence.
- **Carry-over** (§43), tested on C++: changing the parameter types of one `add` overload moved its contract to the new identifier (STALE, confidence 69) and left the other overload's contract alone; renaming `scale` to `multiply` carried the contract (similarity 1.0).
- Templates, constructors, destructors, static members and `operator==` declared in a header are indexed; a lambda is not (expected).

## Gaps found

| # | Gap | Evidence (scratch project) | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| C1 | **A file with any parse error loses all its functions** | `template<> X<A>::~X<A>() = default;` gave 2-3 errors and `Functions Found` dropped the whole file (§48) | whole files missing from the index |
| C2 | **Almost no call edges** | the four-file project has **1 edge in total** (`run` to the static `local_util`, same file). `Calc::mul` calling `helper(a)` in the same file and namespace: 0 callees. `run` calling `math::helper(r)`, `c.mul(2, 3)` and `math::Calc::twice(r)`: unresolved, also with `--deep-scan` | `deps get`, `unused` and callers are close to empty for namespaced or object-oriented C++ |
| C3 | **Declarations in headers are indexed next to their definitions** | `calc.h` yields 7 entries, `calc.cpp` 6; every method exists twice (a declaration with no body in the header, a definition in the source) | duplicate search hits, two places for a contract, `unused` and callers split between them |
| C4 | **Out-of-class operator definitions get the wrong name** | `bool Calc::operator==(...) const {...}` in `calc.cpp` is stored as `math::Calc` (hash `db88ca56`); the header declaration is `math::Calc::operator==` | the operator is indexed under the class name |
| C5 | **Explicit template specialisations with a body are silently skipped** | `template <> int identity<int>(int v) {...}` is missing from the index, with no error | missing functions with no warning |
| C6 | **Test macros are indexed as a function named `TEST`** | `TEST(CalcSuite, Multiplies) {...}` is `TEST#cb689ae2`; every test of a file is called `TEST` and told apart only by a hash | unreadable and unsearchable by test name |

Not yet checked (listed so they are not forgotten, nothing is claimed): function-like macros that wrap a definition or a declaration (`EXPORT int f()`), `#ifdef` branches that define the same function twice, attributes (`[[nodiscard]]`), C++17/20 syntax that the old grammar rejects (concepts, `requires`), `.h` files that are really C, and the identity migration (§44) on a C++ database.

## Plan, in order

1. **C1 first** — §48 (index what parsed cleanly, keep the stored functions, skip harmless constructs such as `= default` specialisations, count the skipped ones). Everything below is wasted on files that are not indexed at all. *Grammar:* the newest `tree-sitter` available for Python 3.8 is 0.21.3, so the grammar cannot be upgraded there; the fix is in drspec.
2. **C4, C5, C6: names.** In the C++ parser: (a) read the name of an out-of-class operator, conversion operator and `operator()` definition (the `operator==` in the sample); (b) index explicit specialisations with a body, with the specialisation arguments in the qualified name (`identity<int>`); (c) treat the common test macros (`TEST`, `TEST_F`, `TEST_P`, `TYPED_TEST`, `BOOST_AUTO_TEST_CASE`, Catch2 `TEST_CASE`) as functions named `Suite.Test` (`Suite::Test` in the style of the language), and let the list of macros be extended in one place. *Acceptance:* the sample project shows `Calc::operator==` once, `identity<int>`, and `CalcSuite.Multiplies`.
3. **C3: declaration or definition.** A function is an index entry only where it has a body (member functions defined in the class body, inline functions and templates in headers count as definitions). A header declaration without a body is not a separate entry; it is recorded as an alias of the definition, so `source get include/calc.h::math::Calc::mul` resolves to the definition in `calc.cpp`, and the definition's entry lists the header where it is declared. When no definition exists in the project (a library header), the declaration stays as an entry marked `declaration`, excluded from `unused` and from "no contract" lists. *Acceptance:* the sample project has one entry per method, and a contract saved on it is found through either path.
4. **C2: call resolution for C++**, in tiers, each with its own confidence (the existing `EXTRACTED`, `INFERRED`, `AMBIGUOUS`):
   1. *Scope-aware names in one file*: a bare call resolves against the enclosing namespace chain and the enclosing class (`helper(a)` inside `math::Calc::mul` finds `math::helper`); a qualified call (`math::helper(r)`, `Calc::twice(r)`) resolves by its qualified name. A name that matches several functions of the same qualified name stays `AMBIGUOUS` per overload as in §44.
   2. *Across files through `#include`*: record each file's includes (the project's own headers, resolved relative to the file and to the common include folders); a call that is not found in the file is looked up in the files it includes, directly and one level further, and resolved to the definition through the declaration alias of step 3.
   3. *Member calls on a variable* (`c.mul(2, 3)`): use the declared type of the variable from its declaration in the same function (`math::Calc c(3);`, `Calc* p`, `const Calc& r`); `auto` and other inferred types stay unresolved. This is the same idea the Python resolver already uses for annotated parameters (`declared_types`), extended from parameters to local declarations and `this->`.
   4. *Not attempted*: overload resolution by argument types, argument-dependent lookup, calls through function pointers, virtual dispatch and templates instantiated elsewhere. These stay `AMBIGUOUS` or unresolved, and the output says so.
   *Acceptance:* on the sample project the edges are `Calc::mul` to `helper`, `run` to `helper`, to `Calc::mul` and to `Calc::twice` (with the right confidence), none to a wrong function, and a project-wide count of edges that rises from 1 to at least 5.
5. **Check the unchecked list** above on a corpus: the other C++ project from which the §48 report came (83 files, 285 functions), plus the sample project. Report the share of files with parse errors, skipped constructs and unresolved calls in `status`, so progress on C++ is measurable and not anecdotal.
6. **C++ in the migrations.** The identity migration reads each stored C++ body "as a header"; test it on a real C++ database (the sample project scanned before and after §44) and require identifiers equal to those of a fresh parse, as was done for the Python database of this repository.

## Acceptance for the plan as a whole

The scratch project of this section (`include/calc.h`, `src/calc.cpp`, `src/main.cpp`, `src/test_calc.cpp`) becomes a test fixture. After the six steps: every non-lambda function appears once; `Calc::operator==`, `identity<int>` and `CalcSuite.Multiplies` have correct names; the header and the source share one entry per method; the call edges listed in step 4 exist with sensible confidence; a file with a harmless unparseable construct is indexed; and the carry-over, identity and lookup behaviour described in §43 and §44 still pass on it.

## Not decided here

Whether declarations without a definition anywhere in the project should be entries at all; the list of macros to treat as functions (a configurable list in the project's `_drspec` folder would let a project name its own); whether the include folders should be read from `compile_commands.json` when it exists (more accurate, but only some projects have it); and whether C (`.c` files and C headers) is in scope.

# 52. Proposal: Documents as Searchable Nodes, Linked to Code Without an LLM, and "Which Docs Need an Update?" (Implemented — see Task 51; no embeddings tier)

**Decision (user, after the review of Tasks 45-53): documents are OFF by default.** `docs.enabled` defaults to `false` until the user decides to keep the feature; `drspec config set docs.enabled true` turns it on. Task 51 as built has it on by default (`core/settings.py`), so the default must change. Stored document rows stay if the setting is switched off, but a scan and the worker neither index nor re-link documents, and `search`, `status` and `deps get` show no document results or counts while it is off. The shared-command design (§56 finding 1) applies once it is on.

**Status: proposal, not yet a task.** Idea by the user: parse Markdown into nodes (headings, keywords, links, important information), link the sections to the code nodes they talk about (there is no direct link, so by names and by fuzzy matching, embeddings optional), and use the links to answer "this code changed; which documents need an update too?". Decided by the user after a short look at other tools: **no LLM** anywhere in this feature.

## What other tools do (short research, not exhaustive)

- **Graphify** (code graph for AI assistants): code is parsed by tree-sitter; Markdown, text, PDFs and images are read by the assistant's model through subagents (instructions in its `skill.md`; `extract.py` has no document handling). The model creates the document nodes (`id`, `label`, `file_type`, `source_file`, `source_location`, plus front-matter fields when present) and judges the links (`references`, `conceptually_related_to`, `semantically_similar_to`, with `EXTRACTED` / `INFERRED` / `AMBIGUOUS` and a score); design reasons become a `rationale` attribute. Without a model the documents are not in the graph. **It has no step that marks a document stale when the code it describes changes**: an unchanged document keeps its cached extraction, and its git hook ignores document changes. Sources: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/safishamsi/graphify/main/graphify/skill.md, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/safishamsi/graphify/main/graphify/detect.py.
- **LlamaIndex** (retrieval library): a deterministic Markdown parser that splits by heading; each chunk carries its heading path (for example `/Install/Linux/`) and PARENT, NEXT, PREVIOUS and SOURCE relationships. No links to code. https://docs.llamaindex.ai/en/stable/api_reference/node_parsers/markdown/
- **Swimm** (documentation tool): documents are coupled to code (snippets, tokens, paths); auto-sync follows renames and moves, and a CI check marks documents "possibly outdated" when a change needs a person. The coupling is made by the author. The claims are the vendor's own. https://docs.swimm.io/features/keep-docs-updated-with-auto-sync

What is taken: the **heading tree with path and neighbours** (LlamaIndex), **deterministic matching of what the text names** (the part of Swimm's coupling that can be found automatically), the confidence tiers drspec already uses, and, as new work, **staleness by the code hash at link time** (Graphify has none, Swimm does it only for explicit couplings).

## Why not the existing `artifacts` table

`artifacts` is built for functions: `signature`, `parent`, `is_property`, a status lifecycle that contracts depend on, a foreign key from contracts, traces, findings and edges, and everything that reads it assumes functions (call resolution, `unused`, `status` counts, `search`). Documents there would pollute all of it. There is also no precedent for other node kinds: `ScannedConstant` exists in `scanner.py`, but nothing calls it and the schema has no constants table. So documents get their own tables, which only point **at** `artifacts` by identifier, not the other way round.

## Tables (additive: `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, no rebuild of existing databases)

- `doc_sections`: `section_id` (primary key: `<file>::<heading path>#<8 hex>`, the hash of the heading path plus the position among equal titles, so equal titles in one file stay distinct), `file_path`, `level`, `title`, `heading_path`, `parent_id`, `prev_id`, `next_id`, `start_line`, `end_line`, `text`, `text_hash`, `keywords` (JSON list), `important` (JSON list), `mentions` (JSON list of the names, paths and identifiers the section contains), `code_blocks` (JSON: language and text, truncated), `front_matter` (JSON, first section only).
- `doc_links`: `(section_id, function_id)` primary key; `kind` (see below), `confidence` (`EXTRACTED` / `INFERRED` / `AMBIGUOUS`), `score`, `state` (`ok`, `drifted`, `renamed`, `removed`), `reason`, `code_hash_at_link`, `signature_at_link` (text, to show "was / now"), `previous_id`, `changed_at`. **No foreign keys** (derived data, rewritten freely, and it avoids the DuckDB foreign-key limits of §50); the code keeps it consistent.
- `doc_file_links`: `(section_id, file_path)`, `kind`, `confidence`, `file_hash_at_link`: a section that names a file or directory without naming a function (coarser, used for staleness at file level).
- Optionally `doc_mentions(token, section_id)` as an inverted index so that a changed function finds the sections that mention its name without scanning every section (the dev decides; a scan of the `mentions` column is fine for small projects).

## Same pipeline, same commands (asked by the user)

**Pipeline.** Documents go through the **same** detector, queue and processor as code files; nothing parallel is built:

| Step | For code | For documents |
|---|---|---|
| Find new files | discovery walk (§47), scanner ignore rules | the same walk, with the document extensions added to the supported set |
| Detect change or deletion | stat sweep against `scanned_files` (size, modification time) | the same table and sweep |
| Queue | `staleness_queue` with the same reasons (`new_file`, `size_changed`, `mtime_changed`, `missing`, search-driven) and the same backoff for files that fail | the same table, reasons and backoff |
| Process | parse, then sync in one transaction (§50) | parse into sections, sync sections and links in the **same** transaction as the code batch |
| Order | first | after the code files, so `init` makes code usable before documents |
| Removal | `pending_removals` (§50), contract and edges deleted | sections and links of a deleted or emptied file are removed; links to a removed function become `removed` findings |
| Budget and pacing | CPU cap, load and memory guards, yield (§47) | the same; documents are cheap to parse |

What is different: a document has no contract, no call edges and (at first) no vector; and a **code** change can make a *document* finding even though the document did not change (the drift of its links, below). That check runs in the code batch, not in the document queue.

**Commands.** No parallel command set either; the existing ones learn about documents:

| Command | Behaviour with documents |
|---|---|
| `drspec scan [path]` | scans documents under the path as well as code (`--code-only` / `--docs-only` select one); the summary adds sections found, new, changed, removed and links made |
| `drspec search` | **one command for both**: returns functions and document sections together, grouped by file as in §45, each result marked with its kind (`Kind: function` / `Kind: section`); `--code-only` / `--docs-only` (the same two flags as `scan`) narrow it; `--status STALE` lists stale contracts **and** sections whose links drifted or were removed, in priority order with the reason and "was / now"; `--undocumented` lists functions that no section links strongly; `--semantic` stays on code until sections have vectors |
| `drspec status` / `worker status` | document counts and the documents still waiting in the queue; the index-incomplete note of §47 counts them |
| `drspec source get <id>` | a section ID returns the section text, its lines and its links; a function ID is unchanged |
| `drspec deps get <id>` | document links are edges of the same graph: a function shows "Documented in" (sections, confidence, state of each link), a section shows "Documents" (the functions it is linked to) |
| `drspec unused` | unchanged (code with no callers) |
| `drspec config` | the new `docs.*` settings |
| `drspec trace` | duration and count events for document parsing and linking (no titles or text, §46) |
| new: `drspec docs ack <section> [<function>]` | the only new command: say "reviewed, the document is still right"; it has no code equivalent |

Everything also exists as MCP tools (the existing `search`, `source_get`, `deps_get`, `scan`, `status` gain the document behaviour; one new tool for `docs ack`), and `--json` output follows the same grouped shape as §45. A search result for a section shows: `ID` (the heading path with its hash), `Kind`, `Title`, `Status` (`CURRENT`, or `STALE` when a link drifted), `Possibly Stale`, `Keywords`, `Linked To` (count) and the line range; the function fields are unchanged.

## Parsing, no LLM

Files: `.md` and `.markdown` to start (`docs.extensions`), with the scanner's ignore rules (vendor, build and hidden folders), a size cap (`docs.max_file_kb`, 1024) and a skip for files that look generated. A small line-based reader or a pure-Python library such as markdown-it-py (the dev checks that it installs on Python 3.8 and decides; a new dependency must be justified):

- headings, both `#` and underline styles, never inside a fenced block; nesting into a tree with `parent`, `prev`, `next`, line ranges and the heading path;
- front matter keys (`title`, `tags`, ...);
- fenced code blocks (language and text), inline code spans, links and images (text and target; a relative target is a file link), tables (header row), list items, HTML comments (ignored);
- **keywords** per section: word frequency (TF-IDF over the whole document set), identifiers split at `snake_case`, `camelCase` and `::`, stop words removed, top 12 (`docs.keywords_per_section`);
- **important information** is rule-based, so it is predictable and the rules live in one list: admonitions and callouts (`> **Note**`, `> [!WARNING]`, `!!! note`), bold phrases, lines with MUST, SHOULD, MUST NOT, DEPRECATED, BREAKING, TODO, FIXME, "Note:", "Warning:", and table header rows. What counts as important is a convention, not a judgement.

## Linking sections to code, in tiers

Each link keeps its `kind`, so the reader can see why it exists.

| Tier | What the text contains | Confidence | Score |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | a full function ID, with or without `#hash` | EXTRACTED | 1.0 |
| `qualified` | a code span or code-fence token equal to a qualified name (`Queue.add_item`, `math::helper`) of exactly one function | EXTRACTED | 0.95 |
| `path+name` | a file path and a bare function name in the same section, and that function lives in that file | EXTRACTED | 0.9 |
| `name` | a code-span bare name that matches exactly one function in the project | INFERRED | 0.7 |
| `fence-call` | a call such as `foo(1)` or `obj.foo()` in a code block, by the rules of `name` | INFERRED | 0.6 |
| `fuzzy` | overlap of the section's title and keywords with a function's name, parent and signature words (identifier words, a BM25-like score above a threshold), at most 3 per section | INFERRED | 0.3 to 0.5 |
| ambiguous | a name or qualified name that matches several functions | AMBIGUOUS | one link per candidate, 1/n |
| `file` | a file path or relative link without a function name | EXTRACTED | file link |
| `near` | the document sits in the same directory as code files (a README beside its modules) | AMBIGUOUS | 0.2, file level only, never listed per function |
| `embedding` (**off by default**, later) | similarity of the section's vector with an embedded function's vector | INFERRED | the similarity |

Rules: names that are too common (`get`, `set`, `run`, `main`, `init`, `test`, and any name carried by more than `docs.common_name_limit` functions, default 8) create no `name` link unless qualified or accompanied by a path; links are capped at `docs.max_links_per_section` (50), keeping the best; a link below `docs.link_min_score` (0.3) is not stored. The embeddings tier uses the existing local model, not a generative one, and reaches only functions that have a vector (the most-used `embedding.top_n`, default 2000), so it is a later, optional addition. Linking uses the in-memory name index that the call resolver already builds; the cost is a dictionary lookup per mention, not a scan of all functions.

Recomputation: a changed document re-links its own sections; a changed set of functions (new, removed, renamed; §43 and §44 events) re-links only the sections whose `mentions` contain the names involved.

## "Which documents need an update?"

- Each link stores the function's `code_hash` and its signature text at link time.
- When a function changes, its links become **drifted** with a reason: `signature_changed` (the signature differs), `body_changed` (only the body). When a function is renamed (a §43 carry-over) the link follows it to the new identifier (`renamed`, `previous_id` kept). When a function is removed the link is kept as **removed**: a document that mentions something that no longer exists is the strongest signal. These updates happen in the same transaction as the code sync (§50).
- **Priority** of a finding: reason weight (removed 3, signature changed or renamed 2, body changed 1) times a tier weight (EXTRACTED 1.0, INFERRED 0.6, AMBIGUOUS 0.3, fuzzy 0.2); a mention inside a code block or table is raised one step. `search --status STALE` lists findings above a threshold, grouped by document and section, with "was: `def f(a, b)` / now: `def f(a, b, c)`" for signature changes (the stored text), and the kind of link.
- **Clearing a finding.** (a) Editing the section's text clears its drifted links automatically and records that it was edited after the code change (`docs.auto_ack_on_edit`, default on): the usual workflow is to change code, then fix the document; (b) `drspec docs ack <section> [<function>]` marks "reviewed, the document is still right" and stores the current hash; (c) a later code change drifts the link again.
- **Entry points** are the shared commands: `drspec search --status STALE [--docs-only] [--path docs/] [--min-score ...]` for the findings, `source get` and `deps get` for one function or section, `drspec docs ack` to clear one, and `drspec scan` ending with "N documentation sections may need an update (drspec search --status STALE --docs-only)". The default `search` returns functions and sections together; `--code-only` gives the old behaviour.
- **Undocumented code.** `drspec search --undocumented` lists public functions (not tests, not names starting with `_`) with no link of tier `qualified` or better.

## Settings, logging, privacy

New settings: `docs.enabled` (true), `docs.extensions` (`md,markdown`), `docs.max_file_kb` (1024), `docs.keywords_per_section` (12), `docs.max_links_per_section` (50), `docs.link_min_score` (0.3), `docs.common_name_limit` (8), `docs.auto_ack_on_edit` (true). The trace (§46) records durations, counts and sizes of document parsing and linking, never a title, text, path or name. Everything works offline and with `embedding.enabled = false`.

## Costs and limits, stated plainly

- Fuzzy links will sometimes be wrong; the tiers and the priority keep them below exact findings, and nothing in the tiers claims certainty for `INFERRED` or `AMBIGUOUS`.
- A document that describes a function without ever naming it (a pure concept description) can only be reached by the fuzzy tier, and by embeddings if they are added later.
- "Important information" is a convention (the list above), not an understanding of the text.
- It is more tables, a parser and a linker to maintain. Documents are small, so the CPU cost is low, but the linking logic is real code.

## Acceptance tests

1. **Parse.** A fixture with front matter, `#` and underline headings, nested levels, a fenced block containing `# comment` lines, a table, a callout, links, and two sections with the same title: the tree has the right levels, parents, neighbours and line ranges; the `#` lines in the fence are not headings; the equal titles get different identifiers; identifiers are unchanged by a re-scan and by editing an unrelated section.
2. **Search.** `search install` returns the function hits and the section hits together, grouped by file, each marked with its kind; `--code-only` returns exactly what the search returned before this feature; `--docs-only` returns only sections; a section is found by title, keyword and text.
3. **Links.** A document that contains a full ID, `` `Queue.add_item` ``, a path with a bare name, `` `helper` `` that matches one function, `` `get` `` (too common), a name that matches three functions, and a code block with `foo(1)`: the stored kinds, confidences and scores equal the table; the three-way name makes three AMBIGUOUS links with score 1/3; `get` makes none.
4. **Drift.** Changing a function's body: its links become `drifted / body_changed`; changing its signature: `signature_changed`, and `search --status STALE` shows the old and the new signature; renaming it (carry-over): `renamed`, the link follows the new identifier; deleting it: `removed`, kept and ranked first; `docs ack` clears; editing the linked section's text clears the drifted links and records the edit; a second code change drifts again.
5. **Ranking.** `search --status STALE --docs-only` orders removed above signature above body, EXTRACTED above INFERRED above AMBIGUOUS, groups by document and section, honours `--path` and `--min-score`, shows the reason and, for signature changes, the old and the new signature, and has JSON output; `--status STALE` without `--docs-only` also lists the stale contracts as before.
6. **Worker.** A new `.md` file is indexed during the drain after the code files; an edit re-parses it; a deletion removes its sections and links; a kill in the middle of a batch leaves a consistent database (§50).
7. **Privacy.** A marker word placed in a heading, a body and a code block does not appear in the trace folder (§46).
8. **Scale.** 500 Markdown files (about 2 MB) next to 5,000 functions: the first document scan costs under 10 s of CPU; renaming one function re-links only the sections that mention its name (count the statements).
9. **No model, no network.** Works with `embedding.enabled = false` and offline.
10. **Existing databases.** After the upgrade the new tables appear without a rebuild, code behaviour is unchanged, and `status` shows the document counts.

## Not decided here

Whether to add `.rst`, `.txt` and `.adoc` after Markdown; whether the embeddings tier is worth adding (needs vectors for all candidate functions, not only the most used); whether docstrings and long code comments should also become sections linked to their function (they are already in the function body); notebooks and HTML; the exact stop-word and common-name lists; whether the "important" markers should be configurable per project.

# 53. Plan: Full Support for JavaScript and TypeScript, and a New Language, Rust (Implemented — see Task 53)

**Status: plan, not yet a task.** Asked for by the user: "write a plan to fully support other popular languages: Rust, JavaScript". Evidence comes from a real scan of a scratch JavaScript and TypeScript project (five files, 25 functions) and from checks of what Rust needs on this project's pins. Same method as §51: what works, what is missing, then an ordered plan with acceptance tests. "Fully supported" is defined at the end.

## Where JavaScript and TypeScript stand today (verified)

Parsing and naming work well. The scratch project had ES modules, CommonJS, classes, arrow functions, TypeScript and TSX:

| Works | Evidence |
|---|---|
| functions, arrow functions assigned to `const`, default exports | `add`, `mul`, `main` |
| classes with constructor, static, getter and setter (two entries), async, generator, class-field arrow functions, private `#priv` | `Calc.constructor`, `Calc.get_value`, `Calc.set_value`, `Calc.handler`; the private method is `Calc.#priv#d9e613a7` and both the plain and the full identifier find it |
| TypeScript: generics, overloads (collapse into the implementation, which is right), classes, `static`, TSX components | `g`, `identity`, `Circle.unit`, `Button`, `Card`, `App` |
| extensions `.js .jsx .mjs .cjs .ts .tsx`; `node_modules`, `dist`, `build` ignored | `scanner.LANGUAGE_MAP`, `DEFAULT_IGNORES` |
| calls inside one file, through `this`, and to a generic function | `main` to `add`, `Calc.load` to `add`, `Calc.handler` to `Calc.twice`, `useShape` to `identity` |

**Gaps:**

| # | Gap | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| J1 | **Calls across files through `import` are not resolved** | `run` in `app.js` calls `add` (named import), `m.mul` (namespace import), `main` (default import), `plus` (re-export alias), `new Calc`, `c.twice` and `Calc.of`: **no callee at all**. `Button` calling `add`: none. The whole project has **5 call edges**, all inside one file |
| J2 | **CommonJS is not resolved** | `const { legacy } = require('./math'); legacy()` in `useLegacy`: no callee |
| J3 | **Re-exports and barrel files** | `export * from './math.js'` and `export { add as plus } from './math.js'` leave `plus(1, 2)` unresolved |
| J4 | **`new X()` and `super()` give no edge** | `Calc.of` containing `new Calc(b)`: no callee |
| J5 | **Methods called on a local or typed variable** | `const c = new Calc(1); c.twice(2)`, and `s.area()` where `s: Shape` (an interface): unresolved |
| J6 | **JSX components are not calls** | `Card` using `<Button label="x" />`: no callee |
| J7 | **Object-literal methods, `export default { ... }` handlers and test callbacks are not indexed** | `obj.method`, `obj.arrow`, `obj.fn`; and the `describe` / `it` callbacks, whose body calls `run`, have no node |
| J8 | **TypeScript namespaces lose their qualifier** | `Util.helper` is indexed as `helper`, so two namespaces in one file with the same function name collapse into one entry |

Not yet checked (listed so they are not forgotten, nothing claimed): Vue and Svelte single-file components, Flow annotations, decorators, class static blocks, `.d.ts` files, monorepos with workspaces, `import type`, dynamic `import()`.

## Rust (new language): is it possible on our pins?

Yes. `tree-sitter-rust` 0.21.2 requires Python ≥ 3.8 and `tree-sitter ~= 0.21` and ships `cp38-abi3` wheels for Linux (x86_64, aarch64, musl), macOS and Windows (checked on PyPI), the same family as the other grammars (`tree-sitter>=0.21,<0.22`). Newer releases (0.23 and up) need Python ≥ 3.9 and a newer `tree-sitter`, so, as with C++, the project stays on the 0.21 line while Python 3.8 is supported.

What a language needs in this code base (the same list applies to any future language): a parser in `parsers/` returning `ExtractedFunction` with parameter tokens (`parsers/parameters.py`); an entry in `scanner.LANGUAGE_MAP` and the ignore list (Rust: add `target`); a normaliser in `hasher.py` (Rust has nested block comments and raw strings such as `r#"..."#` that can contain `//`); the dependency in `pyproject.toml` and the hidden import in `pyinstaller.spec`; the language name in the MCP tool filters; a call resolver; a health block in `status` (as `cpp_health.py`); the identity migration's reader (`identity_migration.py`, which re-parses stored bodies); and tests with a fixture project.

## Plan, in order

### A. A language interface and a shared test fixture (first, short)
Today each language is wired in several places by hand (the list above). Introduce one small registration: language name, extensions, parser, hasher normaliser, resolver, health function, and "how to re-read a stored body" for the migration. Generalise `cpp_health` into a per-language health block in `status` (files, partially parsed, skipped, calls, unresolved share). Add one fixture project per language to the tests, each with an expected list of nodes and of edges; the sample projects of §51 (C++), this section (JavaScript/TypeScript) and the Rust one below become those fixtures. This keeps the work below repeatable and makes "how well is language X supported" a number in `status`.

### B. JavaScript and TypeScript resolution (largest value, already supported by the parser)
1. **Module graph.** Resolve an import path to a file: relative paths with extension and `index.*` inference (`./math` to `math.js`, `math.ts`, `math/index.tsx`); `tsconfig.json` `paths` and `baseUrl`; `package.json` `main`, `module`, `exports` and workspace packages; `.mjs`, `.cjs`, `.jsx`. Unresolvable (bare package names in `node_modules`) stay unresolved and are not guessed.
2. **Import bindings per file** (the same idea as the Python import map, `imported_names` / `imported_modules`): named, default, namespace and aliased imports; CommonJS `require` with destructuring and `module.exports` / `exports.x`; `import()` with a string literal; `import type` (no runtime edge, but kept for documentation links of §52).
3. **Re-exports.** Follow `export * from`, `export { a as b } from` and barrel `index.ts` files transitively, with a depth limit and cycle protection.
4. **Constructors and `super`.** `new X()` is an edge to `X.constructor` (or, when the class has no explicit constructor, to the class entry); `super(...)` and `super.m()` to the parent class members when the parent is resolvable.
5. **Receivers.** `this.m()` already works. Add: a variable initialised with `new X(...)` or annotated `x: X` (TypeScript parameters, variables, fields), resolved to `X`'s members, including inherited ones; a receiver typed as an `interface` or abstract class resolves to the implementers' members with `AMBIGUOUS` confidence; unknown receivers stay unresolved.
6. **JSX / TSX.** `<Button />` is a call to the component `Button` when it resolves through the import bindings; lower-case tags are DOM elements and ignored.
7. **Confidence** as elsewhere: exact import or qualified path `EXTRACTED`, a unique name inferred by type `INFERRED`, several candidates `AMBIGUOUS`.

### C. JavaScript and TypeScript indexing details
1. Object-literal methods and arrow properties, and `export default { ... }`, become nodes named `object.method` where the object is a named `const`.
2. Test blocks (`describe`, `it`, `test`, `beforeEach`, `afterEach` and the Jest / Vitest / Mocha families) become nodes named `describe > it` (the test-macro idea of §51 C6), so test files can be searched by test name; other anonymous callbacks stay unindexed.
3. TypeScript `namespace` and `module` blocks add their name to the qualified name (`Util.helper`); `enum` members and interface members are not functions.
4. Report, as for C++ (§48, §51), functions dropped by the partial-parse rule, and decorator or annotation syntax that the grammar cannot read.

### D. Rust
1. **Parser** (`parsers/rust_parser.py`). Nodes: free `fn`; methods and associated functions in inherent `impl` blocks (`impl<T> Foo<T>`); methods in trait impls; trait declarations with default methods; `async`, `const`, `unsafe` and `extern` functions; nested functions; `macro_rules!` definitions. Not nodes: closures, struct fields.
2. **Identity** (§44 rule). Qualified name = module path + type + function (`net::server::Server::start`); a method of a trait impl is named with the trait (`net::Server::<Display>::fmt`) so an inherent `fmt` and a trait `fmt` never collide; generics are kept as written; `self`, `&self`, `&mut self` are not parameter tokens (like Python's `self`); `#[cfg(...)]` variants with the same name and parameters keep one entry, as the `#ifdef` case does for C++.
3. **Module tree.** Build the module tree from `mod name { ... }`, `mod name;` (file `name.rs` or `name/mod.rs`), `lib.rs` / `main.rs` / `bin/` roots, and `Cargo.toml` (package, workspace members, `[lib]` and `[[bin]]` paths). Respect `#[path = "..."]` when present.
4. **Imports.** `use` declarations, groups `use a::{b, c as d}`, globs, `self`, `super`, `crate`, and `pub use` re-exports, followed like the JavaScript barrels (B.3).
5. **Call resolution** in tiers: a bare call in the same module; a path call (`a::b::f()`, `crate::x::y()`, `Self::helper()`, `super::f()`); associated functions (`Type::new()`) looked up in the impls of the type; methods on `self` inside an impl; methods on a receiver whose type is known from a `let` annotation, a `Type::new(..)` initialiser, or a parameter type (including `&T`, `&mut T`, `Box<T>`, `Option<T>` unwrapped one level); a receiver of a generic type with a trait bound resolves to the trait's method declaration with the implementers as `AMBIGUOUS`; anything else stays unresolved and is counted. **Macro arguments:** the grammar keeps the arguments of `assert_eq!(add(1, 2), 3)`, `vec![f()]` and `println!("{}", x.len())` as opaque token trees, so calls in them need a second, light parse of the token tree as expressions; standard library macros are not edges.
6. **Tests.** A function under `#[test]` or inside `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` is marked as a test (excluded from `unused`, as for other languages' tests).
7. **Packaging.** `tree-sitter-rust>=0.21.2,<0.22.0` in `pyproject.toml`, `tree_sitter_rust` in the PyInstaller hidden imports, `.rs` in the extension map, `target` and `Cargo.lock` in the ignore list, `rust` in the MCP language filters, a Rust health block in `status`.
8. **Hasher.** A Rust comment normaliser that handles nested `/* /* */ */` blocks, `///` and `//!` doc comments, and raw strings.

### E. Order of work
A first (small, makes the rest measurable); then B and C together (JavaScript and TypeScript are already supported and the missing cross-file edges are the biggest gap); then D (Rust, a new language). §51 (C++) and §48 (partial parse) are prerequisites that already exist.

## What "fully supported" means here (acceptance for the whole plan)

1. Every non-anonymous function, method, constructor and test block of the fixture project of the language appears once with a correct qualified name and a stable identifier.
2. On the fixture, the edges listed for it exist with sensible confidence and none points to a wrong function. For the JavaScript and TypeScript fixture above: `run` has callees `add`, `mul`, `main`, `plus` (through the re-export), `Calc` (constructor), `Calc.twice` and `Calc.of`; `useLegacy` to `legacy`; `Button` to `add`; `Card` to `Button`; `useShape` to `Shape.area` / `Circle.area` (`AMBIGUOUS`), `Util.helper` and `identity`; the `describe` / `it` node to `run`; the project goes from **5 call edges to at least 18**.
3. `status` shows the language block, and the unresolved-call share on the fixture is below 25% (JavaScript and TypeScript) and 35% (Rust), with the reasons counted (`dynamic`, `unknown receiver`, `external package`, `macro`).
4. A file with a construct the grammar cannot read keeps all its readable functions, and the dropped ones are counted and listed (§48 and §51 findings).
5. The identity migration, carry-over (§43), lookups with candidates, and the document links (§52) work on the fixture of each language.
6. Packaging: a fresh install on Python 3.8 and the PyInstaller build both include the grammar and scan the fixture.

## Rust fixture (to write when D starts)

A crate with `src/lib.rs`, `src/net/mod.rs`, `src/net/server.rs`, `src/main.rs` and `Cargo.toml`: a struct with an inherent `impl` and a trait `impl` that both define a method of the same name; a generic function with a trait bound; a `use` with a group and an alias; a `pub use` re-export; `crate::`, `super::` and `Self::` paths; an `async fn`; a `macro_rules!` and a call inside `assert_eq!`; a `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` with `#[test]` functions; a method called on a `let`-bound value of a known type and one on an unknown receiver.

## Not decided here

Whether C (`.c`, currently read by the C++ parser) deserves its own entry; Go, Java, C#, Kotlin and Ruby (each would use the interface of step A, so the cost per language is the parser, the identity rules and the resolver); Vue and Svelte single-file components; whether to read `compile_commands.json` for C++ and `tsconfig` / `Cargo.toml` even when they are outside the scanned folder; and whether the unresolved-call targets above are right (they are proposals to be tuned on real projects).

# 54. Feedback: Review of the C++ Support Work (§48 and §51) — Three Gaps That Lose Functions or Edges (Implemented — see Task 52)

**Status: reported, not yet a task.** Written after the dev finished the C++ items of §48 and §51. Method as before: reinstall, full suite, then real scans of scratch projects (the four-file project of §51 rerun unchanged, plus one file per construct). Note: design.md has no task entry for §46 to §52 and their headings still say "(Proposed)", so only behaviour could be reviewed, not the dev's own claims; please record the tasks.

## What was verified and works

On the four-file project of §51 (`include/calc.h`, `src/calc.cpp`, `src/main.cpp`, `src/test_calc.cpp`), compared with the same project before:

- **C3, declarations.** Each method exists once, in `calc.cpp`; the header keeps only `math::identity` (a template with a body). `source get include/calc.h::math::Calc::mul` resolves to the definition in `calc.cpp`, and a contract saved through the header path is found through the source path.
- **C4, C5, C6, names.** `math::Calc::operator==`, `math::identity<int>` and `CalcSuite.Multiplies` are correct.
- **C2, edges.** The project went from 1 call edge to 6: `run` calls `Calc::mul`, `Calc::twice`, `math::helper` and `local_util`; `Calc::mul` calls `helper`. `status` has a C++ block (files, partially parsed, skipped constructs, calls, unresolved share: 8 calls, 2 unresolved).
- **C1, partial parse.** A file with `template<> ... = default;` and ordinary functions after it (`[[nodiscard]]`, `#ifdef` pair, plain functions) keeps all of them; the scan reports `Skipped Constructs: 1`; the file is not in retry wait.
- **Constructs that index correctly**, one per file: `[[nodiscard]]`, an `#ifdef`/`#else` pair of the same function (one entry), `extern "C" { ... }`, `operator()`, a trailing return type, `final`, `constexpr`, `static inline`, default arguments, `__declspec(dllexport)`, nested namespaces (`outer::inner::deep`, and a call to it resolves), `requires`-constrained templates.
- Full suite: 2176 passed, 3 failed (`test_handles_syntax_error`, `test_the_workers_own_rescan_uses_the_same_helper`, `test_a_command_replaces_it_before_sending_its_request`); all three **passed when rerun alone**, so they are flaky under load or while sources are being edited during the run (the build-identifier test is sensitive to that). Worth making them robust.

## Findings

### 1. The "harmless construct" filter hides the error but not the damage

`CppParser.parse` calls `_without_harmless` (`cpp_parser.py` lines 62-66 and 685), which removes the *error entries* that sit on a line matching `template<> ... = default|delete;`. It does not change the tree. When tree-sitter's error recovery folds the following code into the same error region, the functions inside that region are still dropped, now with no error to show for it.

**Reproduced** (parser alone, so no scan effects):

| Source | Functions returned | Errors |
|---|---|---|
| a class `N`, `template<> N<A>::~N<A>() = default;`, then `EXPORT int exported(...)` and `int p1(...)` | `['N::~N']` only: **`exported` and `p1` are lost** | 0 |
| the same, then `[[nodiscard]] int attributed(...)`, an `#ifdef`/`#else` pair `sw`, and `p1` | `['N::~N', 'sw', 'p1']`: `exported` and `attributed` lost | 1 |
| the same specialization followed only by `[[nodiscard]]`, `#ifdef` functions and plain functions (no `EXPORT`) | all six present | 0 |

So the filter works only when the recovery happens to stay on that one line. A scan of the first case says `Skipped Constructs: 1` and `Functions Skipped: 0`, which is false: two functions were lost.

**Suggested fix.** Treat the harmless line before parsing: replace it with spaces of the same length (line and column numbers unchanged) and count it as a skipped construct, so the tree is clean and recovery cannot spread. Keep the error filter only as a safety net. **Acceptance.** The first row above returns `['N::~N', 'exported', 'p1']` with 0 errors, and `Skipped Constructs` is 1.

### 2. Functions with a leading macro are lost without any message

`#define EXPORT __attribute__((visibility("default")))` followed by `EXPORT int exported(int x) { ... }` is parsed with an error, and the partial-parse rule then drops that function; the scan prints no warning for it.

**Reproduced** (real scans of one-construct files):

| Source | Result |
|---|---|
| `EXPORT int exported(int x) {...}` then `int after_a(...)` | only `after_a` is indexed |
| `API int api_fn(int x) {...}` then `int p1(...)` | the parser returns `api_fn` with 1 error, and the scan drops it as above |
| `class EXPORT Widget { public: int size() const { return 1; } };` then `int p1(...)` | **`Widget::size` lost** (2 errors); only `p1` remains |
| `__declspec(dllexport) int dll_fn(...)` | indexed correctly |

Export and API macros before functions and classes are normal in C++ libraries (`Q_DECL_EXPORT`, `*_API`, `DLL_EXPORT`, `EXPORT`). A class decorated this way loses **all its methods**. The loss is silent: `Functions Skipped` stays 0 and the single-file scan output printed no partial-parse line.

**Suggested fix.** Before parsing, blank out decorator macros (replace with spaces, keeping positions): a default pattern such as `(\w+_)?(API|EXPORT|IMPORT)(_\w+)?` and `Q_DECL_*`; every macro the file itself defines as an attribute, a `__declspec` or empty; and a project list (a small file in the project's `_drspec` folder, as §51 suggested for test macros). Separately, make the partial-parse rule report what it drops: count each function skipped because its subtree has an error, list the first few in `scan` and `status` ("3 functions skipped in 2 files"), so a loss is visible. **Acceptance.** The three rows above index `exported`, `api_fn` and `Widget::size`; a function that is really broken (an unclosed brace) is skipped *and* counted and listed.

### 3. Calls into an anonymous namespace are not resolved

`namespace { int anon_helper(int x) { return x; } } int calls_anon(int v) { return anon_helper(v); }`: the helper is indexed as `<anonymous>::anon_helper` and `deps get calls_anon --direction callees` shows **0 callees**. In the same file `outer::calls_deep` calling `inner::deep` resolves correctly, so the named-namespace logic works and only the unnamed scope is missing (`cpp_resolver._scopes`, line 102). Unnamed namespaces are the standard way to write file-local helpers in C++, so many same-file edges are missing (the C++ block showed 3 of 10 calls unresolved in my project).

**Suggested fix.** Treat `<anonymous>` as part of the enclosing scope for lookups inside the same file (and, since it is internal linkage, never from other files). **Acceptance.** `calls_anon` has one callee, `anon_helper`; a call to it from a different file stays unresolved.

## Other observations (not findings)

- A function-like macro that defines a function (`DEFINE_HANDLER(foo) { return 1; }`) is indexed under the macro name (`DEFINE_HANDLER`), like `TEST` was before the test-macro fix. Fine for now; the project macro list of finding 2 would also let a project say how such macros are named.
- The identity migration on a C++ database (step 6 of §51) was not reproduced; it needs a database made before §44.
- `.c` files are read by the C++ parser; C-specific syntax was not tested.

## Why these three first

They are all silent: the index looks healthy and complete while functions or edges are missing, the same class of problem as the false "0 matches" of §41. Findings 1 and 2 also make the "Functions Skipped" figure untrustworthy, which is the figure a user would check.

# 55. Proposal: One Pipeline for Code and Documents — the Worker Does All the Work, `scan` Asks It and Waits (Implemented — see Task 54)

**Status: implemented (Task 54).** Decided with the user in conversation, one question at a time (the answers are listed under "Decisions").

## Why

`drspec scan` and the background worker are two separate pipelines that do the same job:

| Step | Worker | Manual `scan` |
|---|---|---|
| Find what changed | size and mtime against the stored snapshot (`staleness_worker.py`, drift detection), plus a walk for new files | none: every file is taken |
| Parse | queued files only | every code file; every document is read |
| Publish | `_remediate_queued_files`, then `sync_scan_results`, then `ingest_document` | `publish_scan_results`, then `sync_scan_results`, then `ingest_document` (`scan.py:143`) |
| Deleted file | its own handling (`gone_documents`) | its own loop (`scan.py:136-139`) |

Because the worker's fast check never applies to a manual scan, an unchanged scan of this repository (211 code files, 355 documents, 5,952 sections) takes 72-88 s. The trace shows `request sync_scan` 62.1 s, of which 355 `docs ingest` events are 51.1 s: every document is rebuilt on every scan, and `ingest_document` recomputes the word-frequency table over all sections each time (cost about documents × sections). The whole publish runs under the worker's lock, so every other request waits (the problem of §40 finding 2, now larger). Each new kind of file (C++, JavaScript, Rust, documents) has had to be wired into both paths.

## Decisions (each one the user's answer)

1. **The worker is the only process that detects, parses and publishes.** `drspec scan` becomes a request to it. There is one change check (size and mtime, then the hash after parsing) for every kind of file, code and documents alike. A new kind of file is added once.
2. **What a plain `scan` covers.** No argument: every added, changed or removed file in the project (found with the fast check). With an argument: that target and everything reachable from it by call and document links.
3. **What can be named.** A path (a file, or a directory meaning every file under it) or a function ID (`src/a.py::Class.method#hash`; the hash may be left out when unique). A document path or section ID works the same way.
4. **Unchanged targets are skipped, and the output says so** ("up to date, nothing to do").
5. **Neighbours of a target.** The links are followed all the way out (everything reachable). A neighbour whose file did not change on disk is not read again: only its call links and document links are re-resolved against the target's new state, and its staleness flags are updated.
6. **`--full`** re-reads and re-publishes every file and document of the project whether or not it changed, and re-resolves every call link. It replaces `--force` (kept as an alias with the same meaning). One flag, not two.
7. **`--deep-scan` and `--embed`** are carried by the queue items this scan creates, so only those files get deep resolution or vectors. The worker's own background work keeps its defaults.
8. **Waiting is the default.** The scan queues its items and does not return until they are done. Its items go in a new top tier of the queue, above what a search or lookup asked for, and they run flat out: the CPU cap (`worker.cpu_percent`) and the "machine busy" pause do not apply while a scan waits. The scan waits only for its own items; other waiting items continue afterwards. A file already queued at a lower tier is moved up, not queued twice.
9. **`--no-wait`**: the items are queued at normal background priority, with the cap, and the command returns at once with the counts.
10. **Ctrl-C cancels.** The items this scan queued and that have not run are removed from the queue and the scan stops. Batches already published stay (see 14). *(The first answer recorded was "detach"; the user corrected it to "cancel".)*
11. **No worker, no scan.** If the worker cannot start (a sandbox that blocks sockets, for example), `scan` fails with a clear message. There is no foreground path. The test suite must start a worker for scan tests.
12. **One write queue with tiers is enough.** A single ordered queue lets the 1000 items of a scan pass the 100 already waiting; the batch in progress (at most about 1 s of parsing) finishes first. Tiers, top to bottom: **a waiting scan**, what a search or lookup asked for, drift, new files changed in the last 14 days, other new files, documents; within a tier by path. Reads are not queued work (see 13).
13. **Reads are answered while a publish runs.** `search`, `status`, `source get`, `deps get` and the like are served from the last published state while the worker builds the next one, and never wait for the publish. This is the read/write split that Task 39 postponed (§40 suggested fix 3), now in scope.
14. **Publishing is by batch**, as the worker does it (up to `worker.rescan_budget` files or `worker.pass_seconds` of parsing, at least one file), each batch all-or-nothing. A scan is no longer one transaction; the summary says how many files were done. Calls into code of a later batch are re-resolved when that batch publishes (the symbol invalidation that already exists).
15. **Documents are part of the pipeline from the start**, as a second kind of file, and stay switchable (`docs.enabled`). They are **off by default** (`docs.enabled` is `false`, the user's decision; see §52), and when the user turns the setting on they are handled by this pipeline. The shared commands are decided in §56 finding 1; nothing in this design depends on the default.
16. **MCP `scan`** waits up to a limit (proposed 60 s), then returns "N files still queued, the worker continues" with the progress so far; the items are not cancelled.
17. **Terminal output.** While waiting, one updating progress line on a terminal ("Scanning 120/340 files (code 80, documents 40)"), nothing extra when the output is piped; then the summary. **Every existing summary and JSON field is kept** (names and meanings), and these are added: files queued, files done, files cancelled, neighbours re-resolved.
18. **Delivery in three stages**, each one working and each checked by the full suite and by the fixtures (Python, C++, JavaScript/TypeScript, Rust, Markdown) giving the same results as before.
19. **Parsing is parallel** (the user's decision). Today there is no process or thread pool anywhere in `src/drspec`: parsing and publishing run in one process, so even "flat out" uses about one core. Parsing is independent per file; the publish into the database stays with the single owner. A waiting scan uses **all cores minus one** parser processes (on 12 cores, 11), with a setting (proposed `scan.workers`) to lower or raise it. The background drain (the worker's own work and `--no-wait` scans) also parses in parallel, with as many parsers as the cap allows: `worker.cpu_percent` is a share of the whole machine, so 20% of 12 cores is about 2 parsers, with the same pauses when the machine is busy or short of memory. Processes are planned because Python threads share one interpreter lock and it is not known whether the tree-sitter parser releases it; the dev measures threads against processes first and records which one is used.

## Stages

**Stage 1: the fast check and one ingest (fixes the 72 s scan).**
- Documents take the same size/mtime check as code in a manual scan; an unchanged document is not read or rebuilt.
