- `ingest_document` computes the document-frequency table once per publish, not once per document.
- One function does the document ingest and the removal of deleted documents, called from both paths.
- Acceptance: on this repository an unchanged `scan` takes under 5 s on an idle machine, and `docs ingest` events number 0; edit one document and exactly one event appears; the stored sections and links after a changed scan equal those of the old full re-ingest.

**Stage 2: `scan` asks the worker and waits.**
- The detector takes a root and returns added, changed and removed files for every kind of file; the worker's periodic sweep and `scan` call the same function.
- The new top tier; the request that queues a scan's items and reports their progress; waiting, `--no-wait`, Ctrl-C cancel, `--full` (`--force` alias), the per-item `deep` and `embed` flags, targets by path or function ID, link-following with link-only re-resolution of unchanged neighbours.
- The CLI no longer parses files or sends `sync_scan` with documents; the old CLI-side parse path is removed.
- Parallel parsing (decision 19): a pool of parser processes feeds the single publisher; the number of parsers follows decision 19; results are published in batches as before, in a stable order so that the same input gives the same output.
- Acceptance: (a) a 1000-item scan passes 100 queued items; (b) an unchanged scan reports "up to date"; (c) `scan util.py::helper` after editing `helper` re-resolves the caller and flags the linked document section without reading their files; (d) Ctrl-C leaves the published batches and removes the rest from the queue; (e) `--no-wait` returns at once and the worker finishes at normal priority; (f) no worker: a clear error; (g) every old summary field is present and agrees with the old command on the fixtures; (h) the MCP tool returns a status after its limit; (i) on a project of several hundred files the first waiting scan is at least 3 times faster than one parser on a machine with at least 8 idle cores, with identical stored results; the background drain never averages above `worker.cpu_percent` of the machine; a worker killed during a parallel parse leaves no half-published batch.

**Stage 3: reads during a publish.**
- Reads are served from the last published state while a publish is built; writes still go through the single owner.
- Acceptance: during a publish of 300 rewritten files, `search`, `status` and `deps get` answer in under 1.5 s; the answers are the pre-publish state until the batch commits and the new state after; a kill during a publish leaves a complete old or complete new state (the crash tests of §50 still pass).

## Risks

- This touches the most delicate path (staged publish, the foreign-key layout of §50, the single owner of the database). The fixtures must give the same results before and after each stage.
- Resolution of calls needs all the changed code of the project in one view; with batches this relies on the existing symbol invalidation, which must be checked on a multi-batch scan.
- A waiting scan that runs flat out can load the machine; that is accepted because the user is waiting at the terminal. With all cores minus one parsing it will load the machine more than before.
- Parser processes use memory (each loads the grammars, about the size of the current worker), so the number of parsers is also bounded by the memory guard (`min_available_memory_mb`).
- A scan is slower than today's all-out parse when the queue is long and other items are ahead of it. They are not: a scan's items are first.

## Not decided here

- (Decided afterwards: `docs.enabled` is off by default, see §52.) Whether the document feature stays at all is still the user's decision.
- The other six findings of the review of Tasks 45-53 are written down in §56.

# 56. Feedback: Review of Tasks 44-53 (Sections 45 to 54) — Documents Do Not Use the Shared Commands, and Five Smaller Findings (Implemented — see Task 55)

**Status: implemented (Task 55).** Written after the dev finished the items of §46 to §54 (Tasks 45 to 53; the task numbers are one below the section numbers). Method as before: reinstall, full suite (**2312 passed, 20 deselected, 1 xfailed**), then real scans of scratch projects: a Python, C++, TypeScript, Rust and Markdown project, and a second one with harder constructs (CommonJS, re-exports, `super`, JSX, object-literal methods, `describe`/`it`, a Rust trait and macro, a C++ specialisation). Each finding was confirmed with the user one at a time. The seventh problem found, the 72 s unchanged scan, is in §55.

## What was verified and works

- **Data safety (§50).** After a body edit the contract is kept and marked STALE, with its traces and edges (checked on a copy of the database including its write-ahead log). Removing a function that has a contract and a caller works. `artifact_embeddings` and `pending_removals` exist; the extra indexes are gone.
- **Drain (§47).** 300 new files after `init` were indexed in about 10 s; the "Index incomplete: 114 of 300 files" note shows during the drain.
- **Trace (§46).** The log is in `/tmp/drspec-trace-<uid>/<hash>.log`, folder mode 0700, files 0600, and contains no project paths or names.
- **Model cache (§49).** `~/.cache/drspec/models` is used; `worker status` says the project copy can be deleted.
- **C++ (§51, §54).** Export-macro functions and classes and functions in an anonymous namespace are found, and calls into an anonymous namespace resolve; overloads, `override`, `Base::f()` and out-of-line methods resolve.
- **JavaScript/TypeScript (§53).** `require`, an aliased re-export (`export { fmt as format } from`), `super`, JSX components and `describe`/`it` callbacks give EXTRACTED edges across files.
- **Rust (§53).** `mod`/`use` paths, trait methods (INFERRED), macro definitions and test modules.
- **Documents (§52).** A changed function body flags its linked section as `body_changed`; `docs ack` clears it.
- **Packaging.** `tree-sitter-rust` is pinned and in the PyInstaller spec; the suite runs on Python 3.8.10.

## Findings

### 1. Documents do not use the shared commands (high)

**Decided (§52):** one set of commands for code and documents. **Built:** a separate `drspec docs` group (`stale`, `for`, `search`, `links`, `ack`, `undocumented`) and three separate MCP tools (`docs_search`, `docs_for`, `docs_stale`).

**Seen:** `drspec search Widget` finds nothing while `drspec docs search Widget` finds the section; `search --docs-only` fails ("No such option … did you mean --docs?"); `scan` has no document flags; `source get 'docs/guide.md::/Guide/Widget/#…'` and `deps get docs/guide.md` return `FUNCTION_NOT_FOUND`. The Task 51 entry does not say why.

**Decision (user):** no `drspec docs` group at all, only the shared commands:

| Action | Command |
|---|---|
| Index documents | `drspec scan` (`--code-only` / `--docs-only`) |
| Find sections | `drspec search` (results marked `Kind: function` / `Kind: section`; `--docs-only`, `--code-only`) |
| Stale sections | `drspec search --status STALE --docs-only` |
| Sections that describe a function | `drspec deps get <function>` ("Documented in") |
| Functions a section describes | `drspec deps get <section>` |
| Read a section | `drspec source get <section>` |
| Functions no document names | `drspec search --undocumented` |
| Mark as reviewed | **`drspec ack <id>`** (a section ID now; a function ID for a stale contract can follow, since contracts have no "still right" command either) |

The MCP tools follow: `docs_search`, `docs_for` and `docs_stale` go away; `search`, `scan`, `source_get` and `deps_get` gain the document behaviour; one `ack` tool is added. The default `search` returns both kinds; `--code-only` gives the old result. This replaces "only `docs ack` is new" in §52 with "only `ack` is new".

**Acceptance.** `drspec --help` has no `docs` entry; each row of the table works as described, on the CLI and on MCP; `search --code-only` returns exactly what `search` returned before documents existed.

### 2. A signature change shows as "removed" (medium)

The ID holds a hash of the parameter types (§44), so adding a parameter gives the function a new ID and the document link to the old one is reported `removed`.

**Seen.** `docs/guide.md` names `helper`; change `def helper(x)` to `def helper(x, y: int = 0)` and scan. The finding reads State: removed, Reason: removed, Priority 3.0, for a function that still exists, with no old and new signature. `signature_changed` (weight 2.0, `core/docs.py:44`) is reached only when the ID stays the same.

**Fix.** Pair the removed and the new function in the same file with the pairing carry-over uses (§43), and report the link as `signature_changed` with the old and the new signature, priority 2.0; a real removal stays at 3.0.

**Acceptance.** The example above gives Reason: signature_changed, Was `def helper(x)`, Now `def helper(x, y: int = 0)`, Priority 2.0; deleting `helper` still gives removed, 3.0; a body-only change still gives body_changed.

### 3. The partial-parse report names the wrong line and does not count a lost function (low)

**Seen.** A C++ file with `int ok1(){…}` on line 1, `int broken( { return ;` on line 3 and `int ok2(){…}` on line 4: the scan reports `First Error Line: 1` (the wrapper error node that covers the file) and `Functions Skipped: 0`, although `broken` is not indexed. `ok1` and `ok2` are kept, which is right. In a second file, an export macro on line 5 was reported as line 4.

**Fix.** Report the first real error node inside the file, and count the functions that could not be read (§48 asked for the count).

**Acceptance.** The file above reports line 3 and 1 function skipped; a clean file reports no partial parse.

### 4. The note right after `init` tells a new user to give up (low)

**Seen.** In a copy of a 300-file project, `drspec init` and at once `drspec search f7_3`: "Files Searched: 0 … Nothing was searched: no file drspec has indexed matches this project … use grep". Half a second later the note is the correct "Index incomplete: 114 of 300 files are not scanned yet"; after about 10 s the result is complete.

**Fix.** When files are waiting for their first scan and none is indexed yet, say "Index being built: 0 of 300 files scanned; try again in a moment". The "use grep" note is for the case where nothing is waiting.

**Acceptance.** The first call after `init` shows the new note, and the "use grep" note appears only on a project with no indexed file kind and nothing pending.

### 5. `new X().m()` is not resolved in JavaScript and TypeScript (low)

**Seen.** `load(id) { return new Repo().find(id); }` with `class Repo { find(id) {…} }` gives no edge from `load` to `Repo.find`; `status` counts the call as unresolved ("no constructor: 1") because `Repo` has no explicit `constructor`. A call through a typed variable or parameter does resolve.

**Fix.** Type the result of `new X(…)` as `X` and resolve the method against it; a class without a constructor has the implicit one.

**Acceptance.** The example gives an EXTRACTED edge `load → Repo.find`; the unresolved share of the fixture falls accordingly; `new X()` on an unknown or imported-from-a-package class stays unresolved with its reason.

### 6. Documents (and code) in git-ignored folders are indexed (low)

**Seen.** `_bmad/` (about 215 Markdown files) and `_bmad-output/` (about 116) are git-ignored, yet `status` shows 352 documents and 5,952 sections where git sees about 20 document files; a search for `worker` lists `_bmad/…/auth-session.md` first. Discovery uses only the built-in ignore list. Code scanning has the same rule, so it is consistent, but the effect is larger with Markdown, which is common in ignored folders. These files also make every scan longer (see §55).

**Decision (user).** Respect `.gitignore` for code and documents.
- The list of files comes from `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard` (nested and global ignores included); without git, the built-in list stays.
- Settings: `scan.ignore` (folders to skip although git tracks them, for example `docs/archive`) and `scan.include` (folders to index although git ignores them, for generated code people read).
- The scan summary and `status` say how many files were skipped because of `.gitignore`, so a missing file is not silent.
- Documents and functions already indexed from an ignored folder are removed at the next scan.

**Acceptance.** In this repository the document count falls to about 20; a file in an ignored folder named in `scan.include` is indexed; a tracked folder named in `scan.ignore` is not; a project without git behaves as today; the count of skipped files appears in the summary.

## Related

The slow unchanged scan (72-88 s, 51 s of it re-ingesting every document) is §55, stage 1. The item "reads during a publish" of Task 39 is §55, stage 3.

# 57. Feedback: Review of Tasks 54-55 (§55, §56) — Where the Publish Time Goes, and a 16-Minute Test Suite (Implemented — see Task 56)

**Status: implemented (Task 56; xdist not tried).** Written after the dev finished §55 and §56 (Tasks 54 and 55). Method as before: reinstall, full suite, real scans of scratch projects and of this repository, then a profile of one publish. The review itself found §55 and §56 working (see "What was verified"); this section holds the two things the user asked to have measured.

## What was verified and works

- **Unchanged scan:** 1.7 s on this repository (3,822 functions; before §55 it was 72-88 s). A full rescan (`scan --full`, 220 files, all unchanged) took 23.4 s at load average 6.
- **Shared commands:** the `docs` group is gone; `search` marks `Kind: function` / `Kind: section` and has `--code-only`, `--docs-only`, `--status STALE --docs-only`, `--undocumented`; `deps get` and `source get` take a section ID; `drspec ack <section>` clears a finding; the combined search is grouped by file again.
- **The six findings of §56:** a changed signature reads `signature_changed` with the old and new signature; the partial-parse report says line 3 and 1 function skipped; `new X().m()` gives an EXTRACTED edge; `.gitignore` is respected (333 documents left the index of this repository); `scan.include` works; documents are off by default and, while off, `search --docs-only` says so.
- **Scan behaviour:** Ctrl-C leaves 905 queued, 0 done, 905 cancelled and an empty queue; `--no-wait` returns after 2 s; reads during a full publish took 2-4.8 s against 2 s idle on a loaded machine.
- **Suite:** 2451 passed, 20 deselected, 1 xfailed in 994 s, with nothing else running. A first run with scratch scans running at the same time showed 28 failures; they are not in the clean run.

## Finding 1: the publish is slow because of many small queries and repeated lookups, not because of DuckDB (medium)

**Where the time goes in a full rescan.** On this repository: parsing 1.9 s, publishing about 21 s. On a 900-file scratch project: parsing 0.35 s of 12.3 s. So the parser processes of Task 54.5 help little (`scan.workers` 1, 4 and the default all took 11-12 s); the publish is the cost.

**Profile of one forced re-publish** (`cProfile`, a scratch copy of this repository, 121 Python files, worker stopped, the same `_remediate_queued_files` call the worker makes with `force=True`): **10.0 s**, of which `sync_scan_results` 7.8 s (`_sync_edges` 4.4 s, `_sync_nodes` 1.0 s, `_sync_references` 0.9 s) and parsing 1.9 s.

| Cost | Calls | Time | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| `get_dependency_edge` | 1,749 | 0.78 s | one SELECT per edge |
| `insert_artifact` | 1,252 | 0.73 s | one statement per function, run even when the hash did not change |
| `get_artifacts_by_file` | 749 | 0.46 s | one SELECT per file |
| `get_dependencies_by_callee`, `get_dependencies_by_caller` | 1,252 each | 0.38 s each | one SELECT per function |
| `get_references_by_callee`, `get_references_by_caller` | 1,252 each | 0.31 s each | one SELECT per function |
| `get_artifact_code_hash` | 1,252 | 0.30 s | one SELECT per function |
| `_guess_module_file_path` | 1,752 | 1.15 s | the same module names resolved again and again, no cache per publish |
| `_functions_by_name_in_package` | 848 | 0.74 s | same |
| `dataclasses.asdict` | 1,494 | 0.7 s | 175,000 nested objects walked to turn facts into dictionaries |

The one-query-per-row calls add up to about 3.7 s of the 10 s; the repeated import resolution about 2 s. Each call costs 0.25-0.6 ms, small alone and large at this count.

**Fix, in order of effort.**
1. Read the stored rows for the whole batch in a few queries (all artifacts, edges and references of the batch's files), compare in memory, and write only what differs. A `--full` run with no real change then becomes mostly reads.
2. Cache the module-to-file and package-name lookups for the length of one publish.
3. Skip `insert_artifact` when the hash and the metadata are unchanged.
4. Hold facts as tuples or a plain structure, not `asdict`.
5. Not now: moving the write-heavy part to another store. The profile does not point to DuckDB itself.

**Acceptance.** On this repository `scan --full` takes under 10 s at the same load (23.4 s now); a forced re-publish of the 121-file copy spends under 3 s in `sync_scan_results` (7.8 s now); the number of SELECTs in a publish no longer grows with the number of functions (a trace counter or a test that counts statements); the stored result is identical before and after (the fixtures of §55).

**Not measured.** One run, on a loaded machine; the real project has 4,865 edges where the copy has fewer. The size of the database file (142 MB here, against 13 MB for the 121-file copy; the `config` table holds 4,327 rows and about 10 MB of per-file JSON facts, and DuckDB does not give freed space back) is a separate question that this profile does not answer.

## Finding 2: the test suite takes 16-19 minutes, most of it in a few real-process tests (low)

**Measured** (clean run, nothing else running): 2,451 tests, 994 s. The 40 slowest take 351 s, 35% of the total, each 4-25 s. They are almost all tests that start a real worker or several processes:

| File | Slowest tests (seconds) | Together |
|---|---|---|
| `test_worker_ipc.py` | 17.5, 17.0, 15.7, 15.2, 11.3, 8.7, 7.2, 6.3 | about 99 s |
| `test_scan_with_worker.py` | 18.3, 15.4, 11.3, 7.6 | about 53 s |
| `test_drain.py` | 10.8, 8.2, 7.6, 5.9 | about 33 s |
| `test_parse_pool.py` | 24.8 (one parser against several, the same stored result) | 25 s |
| `test_mcp_server.py` | 16.3 (`learn_analyze` dry run), setups 6.8 and 4.8 | about 28 s |
| `test_docs.py` | 14.3 (scale), 5.0 | about 19 s |
| `test_trace_location.py`, `test_staleness_worker.py`, `test_identity_migration.py`, `test_api.py` | 12.1, 12.8, 6.3 + 6.2, 6.5 | about 44 s |

The other 2,400 tests share about 640 s, about 0.27 s each; they were not timed one by one (only the 40 slowest are listed), so which of them are redundant is **not known**. Counts per file: `test_prompt_commands.py` 169, `test_mcp_server.py` 97, `test_tracing.py` 81, `test_prefer_drspec_hook.py` 81, `test_db_queries.py` 73.

**There is no parallel run:** `pytest-xdist` is not installed, so a 12-core machine runs the suite on one core. The measured slowness is mostly waiting for real processes, which parallelises well.

**Options, from safest.**
1. Add `pytest-xdist` to the development dependencies and run `-n auto`. No test is removed; the real-process tests need their own temporary directories (they already use them), and the tests that name a fixed socket or port must be checked. Expected: several times faster; not measured.
2. Add a `slow` marker (as `model` and `socket` already exist) for the real-worker tests and the scale tests, and run the quick lane by default and the slow lane in CI and before a release. The default `addopts` then excludes `slow`.
3. Merge or trim only after a full-suite `--durations=0` run and a coverage comparison: no test is deleted unless a measurement shows that another test covers the same lines and behaviour. Candidates to look at first: the three embedding end-to-end tests of `test_worker_ipc.py` (17.5 + 17.0 + 15.2 s), the several tests that each start a worker to check one scan option in `test_scan_with_worker.py`, and `test_parse_pool.py::...same_with_one_parser_and_with_several` (25 s, one test).

**Acceptance.** The default `pytest` run (quick lane) finishes in under 5 minutes on this machine; the slow lane and the quick lane together give the same number of passing tests as today (2,451 + 20 deselected + 1 xfailed, less any test removed with the evidence above); CI runs both lanes.

# 58. Feedback: Review of the Search Fixes (Task 57), and `drspec init` Does Not Register the MCP Tools (Implemented — see Task 58)

**Status: implemented (Task 58).** Part 1 is the review of Task 57. Part 2 is a gap found when drspec was installed in another project: the native tools (`mcp__drspec__search` and the others) were not there, and the assistant could only call drspec through Bash.

## Part 1: What was verified (Task 57, and part of Task 56)

Method: reinstall, then real `search` runs on this repository, on the command line and through the MCP tool.

- **A cut-off is no longer silent.** `search insert_artifact --limit 1` prints `Total Matches: 134`, `Truncated: Yes` and "Showing 1 of 134 matching functions, best match first; raise --limit to see more." With `drspec --json search …` the JSON has `total_matches` 134, `truncated: true` and the same note. A search that is not cut off says `Truncated: No`.
- **Best match first.** `insert_artifact --limit 1` returns the definition; `--limit 4` returns the definition, then names that contain the pattern. The order also holds for `-i` and `-E`. `-c` still counts 134; `-v` and the status listing keep the file order, as the dev said.
- **An unknown language is named.** `--include nosuchlang` gives `INVALID_INPUT` with "Known: cpp, javascript, python, rust, tsx, typescript"; a known language with no indexed file keeps the "nothing was searched" note.
- **Exit code of a search with no matches stays 0.** Accepted: the dev's reason (callers read the JSON result) is sound.
- **Publish and lanes (Task 56, measured earlier):** full rescan of this repository 23.4 s to 6.6 s on an idle machine; a forced re-publish costs about 16-24 ms per file against 64 ms; 21 tests are marked `slow`; the quick lane takes about 10 minutes, so the "under 5 minutes" acceptance of §57 is not met without a parallel run. `pytest-xdist` is in the development dependencies but was never installed here (the install was refused), and a hand-made parallel run (three groups at once) gave 2,447 passed and 1 failure that passes alone (`test_api.py::TestRoutesThroughTheWorker::test_api_calls_succeed_against_a_real_running_worker`, a test that is sensitive to load).

**Not a finding but worth knowing.** A running `drspec mcp-serve` keeps the code it started with. After this reinstall the MCP `search` tool still returned the old result (file-path order, no `truncated`), because the servers had started at 09:05 and the source changed at 16:46-17:58. The command line was correct. The worker detects an outdated build and replaces itself; `mcp-serve` does not, so an assistant must be restarted after an upgrade.

## Part 2: Finding. `drspec init` does not register the MCP tools (medium)

**Seen.** In another project ("artos") the assistant had no `mcp__drspec__*` tools after drspec was installed and initialised. The tools come from the MCP server `drspec mcp-serve` (24 tools), which a client starts only when it is told to. Claude Code reads a `.mcp.json` in the project; this repository has one, written by hand and untracked in git (`{"mcpServers": {"drspec": {"type": "stdio", "command": "drspec", "args": ["mcp-serve"]}}}`). `init` created none, `init --help` has no option for it, and the README does not explain it. The assistant's only way in was Bash, and the fix was to write the same `.mcp.json` by hand and restart the session.

**Decision (user).** The tools are installed when `drspec init` configures a provider: the choice that `init` already asks for (`--ide`: `claude-code`, `codex`, `cursor`, `github-copilot`) also writes the MCP server entry for that provider. No extra question.

**What each provider needs** (the dev checks the file name and format against each client's current documentation before relying on this list):

| Provider | Where the server entry goes | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| `claude-code` | `.mcp.json` in the project, key `mcpServers`, type `stdio` | project |
| `cursor` | `.cursor/mcp.json`, key `mcpServers` | project |
| `github-copilot` | `.vscode/mcp.json`, key `servers`, type `stdio` | project |
| `codex` | the `[mcp_servers.drspec]` table of `~/.codex/config.toml` | user (so it follows `--codex-global`: without it, print the lines to add instead of editing a file outside the project) |

**Rules.**
1. The entry is `drspec mcp-serve`, with the command found as `drspec` on the path (or the absolute path of the running executable when `drspec` is not on the path, so a virtual environment works).
2. An existing file is merged, never overwritten: other servers stay; an existing `drspec` entry is left as it is and `init` says so; an invalid file is left alone with a clear message.
3. `init` ends with one line per provider: "Restart <client> and approve the drspec server to get the tools."
4. `--no-ide` writes nothing. `--non-interactive --ide claude-code` writes the entry. A project without a chosen provider gets nothing.
5. The README gets a short section: what the MCP server is, what `init` writes for each provider, and how to register it by hand for any other client.
6. `drspec status` (and `worker status`) says "drspec MCP server: not registered for <providers>" when a configured provider has no entry, so missing tools are not silent. It also says when the entry points to a command that cannot be found.
7. Optional: `drspec mcp-serve` records the build it started with and, when the installed build is newer, answers with a one-line notice in its first tool result ("drspec was upgraded; restart this server"). This addresses the stale server noted in Part 1.

**Acceptance.** In an empty directory, `drspec init --ide claude-code --non-interactive` creates `.mcp.json` with the entry and `drspec status` reports it as registered; running `init` again changes nothing; a `.mcp.json` that already holds another server keeps it and gains the `drspec` entry; an invalid JSON file is not touched; the same checks for each provider's file, and for `codex` without `--codex-global` the lines are printed and no file outside the project is written; `--no-ide` creates no file; with the entry removed, `status` says it is not registered.

# 59. Feedback From Use on Another Project: The C++ Grammar Cannot Read `x.member < value`, and the Function Around It Is Lost (Implemented — see Task 59)

**Status: implemented (Task 59).** Reported by a session that scanned a C++ project (1,317 files under `src`) with drspec. The grammar part was reproduced here; the numbers of that project were not (the project is not available).

## What was reported

drspec uses tree-sitter-cpp 0.21.0 (with tree-sitter 0.21.3, the newest that runs on Python 3.8). That grammar fails on a comparison whose left side is a member access. The reporter counted, on their project, 342 of 8,919 functions dropped outright, about 325 more inside error regions, 105 files affected; the worst file lost about 285. The missing functions are the ones with the most comparison logic, the ones a contract is most wanted for.

## What was verified here

Small snippets parsed with the installed grammar (error means the parse tree contains an error node):

| Expression | Result |
|---|---|
| `a < B`, `(x.m) < B` | parses |
| `x.m < B`, `x->m < B`, `x.m < 3`, `x.m < y.n`, `a.b.c < 1`, `if (x.m < 3)`, `x.m < B ? 1 : 2` | **error** |
| `x.m <= B`, `x.m > B`, `x.m() < 3` | parses |

The grammar reads `x.m <` as the start of a template argument list and waits for a `>`.

In a scratch project with the current build (Tasks 47 and 52, partial parse): a function that contains such a comparison is **not indexed**, the file counts as partially parsed, the scan reports "Functions Skipped" with the right number (2 for two such functions) and the first error line. **The functions after the broken ones were all indexed**, including ones that contain `>`, `std::vector<int>`, `if (a > b)` and a template function. **Correction (found later, on Dear ImGui, see §60):** the spill **is** reproduced on this build. The small tests above had only one `<` per expression. With two member comparisons in one expression, for example `return (pos.x < c->x) || (pos.y < c->y);`, the grammar swallows every function after it: a file with six functions keeps only the first one (the scan reports "Functions Skipped: 5" and indexes none of them). In `imgui.cpp` this takes out about 500 functions after line 4,052.

## Findings

### 1. A function with a member comparison is dropped (high for C++ projects)

**Seen.** `bool compare(S x, int B) { return x.m < B ? true : false; }` and `bool reached(S x, S y) { if (x.m < y.n) {…} }` are missing from the index; `before`, `afterOne`, `afterTwo`, `afterThree` and `clean` (which uses `(x.m) < B`) are present.

**Fix, in order of preference.**
1. **Rewrite the comparison for parsing only, keeping every position.** The text handed to the parser has the `<` of a comparison replaced by a token of the same length that the grammar accepts as an operator (for example `-`), so line and column of everything stay the same. drspec needs the functions, their signatures, bodies and calls, none of which depends on the operator; the stored body is the original text. The rewrite applies only to a `<` that follows an identifier reached by `.` or `->` (or a closing parenthesis or bracket) and is **not** the start of a template argument list: the rule must leave `x.get<int>()`, `obj.template f<T>()`, `std::vector<int>` and `#include <x>` alone, for example by checking that no matching `>` closes before the next `;`, `{` or `)` at the same nesting. The rewrite is tried only on a function (or a stretch) whose first parse has an error, so correct code is never touched.
2. **Try tree-sitter-cpp 0.22.x.** Releases 0.22.0 to 0.22.3 still support Python 3.8 (0.23 and later need 3.9), but whether they fix this comparison is **not known**; the dev measures it on the snippets above before relying on it. If it fixes them, the pin changes (`pyproject.toml`, `pyinstaller.spec`) and the rewrite stays as a fallback only if some snippet still fails.
3. Do not change line or column positions, whatever is chosen: contract locations, `source get` and the trace depend on them.

**Acceptance.** All twelve snippets of the table give a function with its body, signature and call edges; a file made of the reporter's two examples (`headingResolution_deg < EPSILON ? …`, `expansionOptions.historyIdx < START_EXPANSION_IDX`) indexes every function in it; `x.get<int>()`, `std::vector<int>` and `template <typename T>` functions are unchanged; start and end lines and columns of every function equal those of the original text; a file with a real syntax error still reports it (§48 and §54).

### 2. A function with an error should be kept and marked partial, not dropped (medium)

**Seen.** Today a function whose text contains an error node is not indexed at all; it is counted ("Functions Skipped: 2") but not searchable, has no contract, and its callers show no edge to it. A missing function looks like it never existed.

**Fix.** Keep the function (name, signature, location, body, hash) when its head parsed, mark it `partial` (a flag on the function, shown in `search`, `source get` and `status`), index the calls that were read, and count it under "partial functions", separately from "skipped" (a stretch that could not even be recognised as a function). A contract for a partial function is allowed but the output says the body was read in part.

**Acceptance.** In the scratch file of finding 1 without the rewrite, `compare` and `reached` are found by `search`, carry `Partial: Yes`, and have the calls the parser could see; `status` gives separate counts for partial and skipped functions; after the rewrite of finding 1 they are no longer partial.

### 3. The scan summary does not say what the errors cost, per file (low)

**Seen.** The current summary has the number of skipped functions and a few examples with the first error line, but no list of the files that lose the most; the reporter's version printed only an error count ("3,019 errors").

**Fix.** The summary and `status` list the five files with the most skipped or partial functions, each with its count and first error line, and the totals ("342 functions skipped, 325 partial; 105 files"). The JSON has the full list, capped at a number that the output states.

**Acceptance.** A scan of a project with three affected files lists them in order of loss with their counts; the totals equal the sum of the per-file counts.

## Order

Finding 1 first (it removes most of the loss), then finding 2 (it protects against every other kind of parse error), then finding 3. Ask the reporter which drspec version produced the spill, so that it is not looked for in a build that has already changed.

# 60. Feedback: A Full Scan of Dear ImGui (C++) — Most of the Core Is Not Indexed, Several Losses Are Silent (Implemented — see Task 59)

**Status: implemented (Task 59).** The user asked for the C++ support to be checked on a whole real project. Project: Dear ImGui (`/media/thanh/data/Projects/imgui`: 56 `.cpp`, 39 `.h`, 11 `.mm`, 1 `.c`, 9 MB). Method: the project was copied (without `.git`) to a scratch folder, so the original got no `_drspec` folder; `drspec init` and `scan --full` ran there. **Ground truth** for the five core sources (`imgui.cpp`, `imgui_draw.cpp`, `imgui_tables.cpp`, `imgui_widgets.cpp`, `imgui_demo.cpp`) comes from the compiler: each was compiled with `g++ -g` (all five compiled without error, so every parse error below is a limit of drspec or of the grammar, not of the code), and the functions it defines were listed with `nm -C -l`. Names were compared by last component, which is lenient, so the true recall is not higher than the figures. Each cause below was reproduced on a small file, with sentinel functions before and after.

## Result

| | |
|---|---|
| Time | `init` 1.3 s; the whole index complete after 55 s (machine load about 15); database 26 MB |
| Files | 97 scanned; **71 are "partially parsed"** (10 of them lost nothing); **1,408 functions skipped**; 8,485 parse errors |
| Functions | 2,030 found by the scanner (207 are header declarations, kept in `function_declarations`, so 1,823 artifacts; by design) |
| Edges | 12,370 call sites, 2,141 edges (1,628 EXTRACTED, 513 AMBIGUOUS); status says **78.6% unresolved** |
| Not scanned | 11 `.mm` files (Objective-C++) are not scanned at all |

**Recall against the compiler (functions defined in the file):**

| File | Defined | Indexed (lenient match) |
|---|---|---|
| `imgui.cpp` | 653 | 114 (**17%**) |
| `imgui_draw.cpp` | 276 | 239 (86%) |
| `imgui_tables.cpp` | 113 | 97 (85%) |
| `imgui_widgets.cpp` | 320 | 290 (90%) |
| `imgui_demo.cpp` | 127 | 90 (70%) |
| all five | 1,489 | 830 (**56%**) |
| inline and template functions of `imgui_internal.h` used by those files | 290 | 38 (**13%**) |
| the same for `imgui.h` | 122 | 93 (76%) |

In `imgui.cpp` the compiler defines about 500 functions between lines 4,500 and 16,500; **drspec indexed none of them** (last indexed function before the gap: `RenderTextWrapped`, line 4,018; next one: line 17,185). These are the main API (`ImGui::Begin`, `ImGui::Button` and so on live in `imgui.cpp` and `imgui_widgets.cpp`).

## Causes, each reproduced alone (four sentinel functions around the construct)

| # | Construct | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Two member comparisons in one expression, `return (pos.x < c->x) \|\| (pos.y < c->y);` | **Every function after it in the file is lost** (six functions, one kept). The cascade that empties `imgui.cpp`: the first such line is 4,052. The scan counts "Functions Skipped: 5", but they are not indexed. |
| 2 | One member comparison, `p.x < c->x`, `a->x < b->x`, also in `if` and `for` | That function is lost (the known bug of §59); the rest of the file survives. 191 error nodes in `imgui_widgets.cpp` alone. |
| 3 | `va_arg(args, const char*)` inside a function | The function is lost (a type as a macro argument). |
| 4 | `"%" PRId64` (string literal next to a macro) | The function is lost. |
| 5 | `#if __has_warning("-Wx")` | The functions are kept, but the file is flagged "partially parsed". In `imgui.cpp`, `imgui_widgets.cpp`, `imgui_draw.cpp` and others it is the first error, so the "partial" count (71 of 97) is inflated by a harmless line. |
| 6 | An attribute macro after the parameters of a declaration in a struct, `void AppendF(const char* fmt, ...) IM_FMTARGS(2);` followed by `void Other() { }` in the same struct | **`Other` is silently lost and the file is reported as `successful`.** Dozens of declarations in `imgui.h` carry `IM_FMTARGS`. |
| 7 | Conversion operators, `operator int() const {…}`, `operator float*() {…}` | **Silently lost**; the constructor and destructor of the same struct are found, the file is `successful`. (`ImColor::operator ImVec4`, `operator unsigned int` are missing from `imgui.h`.) |
| 8 | A macro before a constructor in a struct, `NODEBUG constexpr inline W() : x(0), y(0) { }` | The constructor is lost (skipped 1). `ImVec2()`, `ImVec4()` and the other basic constructors of `imgui.h` carry `IM_NODEBUGSTEP`; 619 `ImVec2(…)`/`ImVec4(…)` call sites have no target. |
| 9 | `.mm` files | Not in the extension map; the 11 Objective-C++ backends are not scanned, and the summary says nothing. |

Not a problem: export macros on a class (`struct API Cls`) and on a declaration, `extern API V* g;`, designated initialisers and `[[nodiscard]]`, lambdas, templates, const and non-const `operator[]` overloads (both found, with different IDs).

## Findings

### 1. The `x.m < y` cascade: one such line removes the rest of the file (high)

Cause 1 and 2. This is the same grammar fault as §59, but with two `<` in a statement it destroys the parse of everything after it, not only the function. In `imgui.cpp` it removes about 500 functions, 83% of the file, including the core API.

**Fix.** As in §59 finding 1: parse a same-length rewrite of the comparison (`<` replaced by a token the grammar accepts, positions unchanged), applied only where the first parse has an error and never to a real template argument list. Because the damage spreads, the rewrite must run on the whole file when a file-level error appears, not on one function. Try tree-sitter-cpp 0.22.x (still Python 3.8) on the repro of cause 1 first.

**Acceptance.** The six-function repro keeps all six; `imgui.cpp` indexes at least 95% of the functions the compiler defines (653); `imgui_widgets.cpp`, `imgui_tables.cpp`, `imgui_draw.cpp` at least 95%; start and end positions equal those of the original text.

### 2. Losses that are silent: the file says `successful` while functions are missing (high)

Causes 6 and 7 (and part of 3 and 4): the method after an attribute macro and every conversion operator vanish, and nothing is reported (`parse_status` is `successful`, skipped is 0). This breaks the promise of §48 and §54 that a loss is counted.

**Fix.** (a) Accept a macro invocation after a declarator's parameter list (`) IM_FMTARGS(2);`), like the existing handling of leading macros. (b) Read conversion-operator definitions (`operator T()`, `operator T*()`, `operator unsigned int()`), named `operator int` and so on. (c) A cross-check that does not depend on the grammar: compare the number of function-like definitions found by a plain token scan (an identifier, a balanced `(...)`, optional qualifiers, then `{`) with the number the parser returned, and report the difference as "possibly missed" for the file, even when the parse has no error.

**Acceptance.** Cases 6 and 7 index every method and operator; the check of (c) reports a non-zero "possibly missed" count for a file where a function is dropped without an error node.

### 3. Constructors with a leading macro, and the basic types of the library (medium)

Cause 8. `ImVec2` and `ImVec4` constructors, and many others in `imgui.h`, start with `IM_NODEBUGSTEP`, so they are lost, and each call `ImVec2(…)` stays unresolved. Treat a leading all-caps identifier, or one defined by `#define` in the project, that is not followed by a declarator, as a macro (§54 did this for `EXPORT`; the same rule must cover constructors and `constexpr inline` prefixes).

**Acceptance.** `ImVec2::ImVec2` (both overloads), `ImVec4::ImVec4` and the conversion constructors are indexed; the `ImVec2(…)` call sites resolve to them.

### 4. The "unresolved share" counts macros, constructors and libc as calls (medium)

Of 12,370 call sites, 44% name a function that is in the index and 56% do not. The names most often unresolved are `IM_ASSERT` (531), `ImVec2` (415), `SameLine` (252, lost with `imgui_widgets.cpp`'s neighbours), `ImVec4` (204), `GetIO` (144), `IM_COUNTOF` (138), `IMGUI_DEMO_MARKER` (120), `memset`, `memcpy`, `printf`, `fprintf`, `IM_UNUSED`, `IM_COL32`, `GL_CALL`. Macros and libc are not calls to project functions; counting them gives 78.6% "unresolved" and hides the real problem (lost targets). In a file that parses well (`imgui_widgets.cpp`) 82% of the call sites to indexed names became edges (631 of 767), so edge building itself is fine.

**Fix.** (a) Do not count an invocation of a macro defined in the project (`#define NAME(`), or an all-caps name, as a call; list macro invocations separately ("macro: 1,420"). (b) Report libc and standard-library calls as `external` (as the JS and Rust blocks do). (c) The C++ block of `status` gives the same reasons as the JS and Rust blocks (`unresolved_reasons`); today it has none, so 9,720 unresolved calls come with no explanation.

**Acceptance.** On this project the unresolved share excludes macros and external calls and says why for the rest; the reasons add up to the unresolved count.

### 5. A harmless line makes a file "partially parsed" (low)

Cause 5, and `0 skipped` for 10 of the 71 flagged files. `#if __has_warning(…)`, `__has_include`, `__has_feature` and `__has_builtin` conditions should be treated like the other harmless preprocessor lines of `_prepare` (§48): replaced by `#if 1` for parsing, positions unchanged. The "partial" count then means files that really lost something.

**Acceptance.** `imgui_widgets.cpp` with only that line as an error is `successful`; the partial count drops from 71 to the files that lose functions.

### 6. `.mm` files are not scanned, and nothing says so (low)

11 Objective-C++ backends (`imgui_impl_metal.mm`, `imgui_impl_osx.mm` and others). Either map `.mm` to the C++ parser (the C++ parts parse; Objective-C message sends will be partial) or have the scan summary list "files not scanned because of their type: 11 (.mm)".

### 7. The scan summary counts declarations as functions (low)

"Functions Found: 2,030" while 1,823 artifacts exist; 207 are header declarations that are kept in `function_declarations` (by design, §51). The summary should say "2,030 found (1,823 definitions, 207 declarations)".

## Order

Finding 1 first (it recovers about 500 functions of the main source and probably most of the rest), finding 2 (silent loss; without the check of 2(c) the next loss will be silent too), then 3, 4, 5, 6, 7. After finding 1 and 2, the dev repeats the comparison of this section: compile the five core files, list the defined functions with `nm -C -l`, and compare; the target is 95% or more for each file and for the headers' inline functions.

## What works

Parse and scan speed (97 files in about 55 s under load), const and non-const overloads (different IDs), templates, lambdas, designated initialisers, `[[nodiscard]]`, export macros on classes and declarations, constructors and destructors without a macro, namespaces and nested types in `imgui_draw.cpp` (86%) and `imgui_widgets.cpp` (90%), edge building where the targets exist (82%), and no file of the user's project was written to.

# 61. Feedback: Review of Task 59 (C++ Recovery, §59 and §60) — Recall Is 98% on Dear ImGui, but a Defined Macro Brings the Losses Back and a Scan Got Ten Times Slower (Implemented — see Task 60)

**Status: implemented (Task 60).** Written after the dev finished Task 59. Method: reinstall; the same Dear ImGui check as §60 on a scratch copy (the original was not written to): `g++ -g` on the five core sources, the functions the compiler defines listed with `nm -C -l`, names compared by their last component; thirteen isolated constructs with four sentinel functions around each; then a timed `scan --full` and a `cProfile` of one forced publish. The machine was heavily loaded by other jobs during the whole review (load average 19-22 from a Jupyter job of another project, and for part of it the dev's own test run), so all **times are inflated and noisy**; the recall figures are not affected. The quick lane of the test suite was not run in this review (see the end).

## What was verified and works

| | §60 baseline | Now |
|---|---|---|
| `imgui.cpp` (compiler: 653 functions) | 114 (17%) | **653 (100%)** |
| `imgui_draw.cpp` (276) | 239 (86%) | **276 (100%)** |
| `imgui_tables.cpp` (113) | 97 (85%) | **113 (100%)** |
| `imgui_widgets.cpp` (320) | 290 (90%) | 317 (99%) |
| `imgui_demo.cpp` (128) | 90 (70%) | 116 (**90%**) |
| all five (1,490) | 830 (56%) | **1,475 (98%)** |
| inline and template functions of `imgui_internal.h` used by the core files (290) | 38 (13%) | 230 (79%) |
| the same for `imgui.h` (122) | 93 (76%) | 104 (85%) |
| functions found by the scan | 2,030 | 4,495 (2,953 definitions, 1,542 declarations; the summary says so) |
| functions skipped | 1,408 | 82 |
| unresolved share of calls | 78.6%, no reasons | 23.9%, reasons given (`not found`), macro invocations and library calls counted apart |
| `.mm` files scanned | 0 | 11 |

The dev's own figures (100% on `imgui.cpp`, `imgui_draw.cpp`, `imgui_tables.cpp`; 99% on `imgui_widgets.cpp`) were reproduced. Isolated constructs: `__has_warning` (file `successful`), `va_arg(args, const char*)`, one and two member comparisons (the cascade is gone: all four sentinels survive), `"%" PRId64`, conversion operators (`operator int`, `operator float*`, constructor and destructor), a leading macro on a constructor, const and non-const `operator[]`, designated initialisers, `[[nodiscard]]` and a `for` with a member comparison all pass.

## Findings

### 1. A macro that is defined in the file brings the losses back; the heuristic looks at the name, not at the definition (high)

**Seen** (current build; each case has `after()` and the method as the things to find):

| Case | Result |
|---|---|
| `#define IM_FMTARGS(x) __attribute__((format(printf, x, x+1)))` then `struct T { void AppendF(const char* fmt, ...) IM_FMTARGS(2); void Other(){ } };` | **`Other` lost**, file partial |
| the same with `FMT` as the macro name | `Other` lost |
| `#define API` then `class API Cls { public: int m(){…} };` (also `struct`) | **`m` lost**, partial |
| `#define EXPORT __attribute__((visibility("default")))` then `class EXPORT Widget { public: int size() const {…} };` | **`size` lost** |
| `#define MY_API` then `class MY_API Cls3 {…}` | lost |
| the same constructs **without** the `#define` line (`class DLLEXPORT Cls4`, `struct IMGUI_API ImGuiStorage`) | found, file `successful` |

So the rule works when the macro is unknown and fails when the file defines it. The real `imgui.h` defines `IM_FMTARGS` and `IMGUI_API` itself, which fits the 79% (`imgui_internal.h`) and 85% (`imgui.h`) above and the 12 of 128 functions missing from `imgui_demo.cpp`. **This is also a regression:** `class EXPORT Widget` with a defined `EXPORT` worked in the review of Task 52 (§54) and fails now.

**Fix.** Decide by the definition, not by the shape of the name. A name that the project defines with `#define` (in the same file or in a header of the project) and that is used where a type, a qualifier or an attribute would be is a macro to blank, whatever it looks like (`FMT`, `API`, `EXPORT`); an unknown all-caps name keeps today's handling. Run the check with the defined-macro case in the test file, not only with unknown macros.

**Acceptance.** All rows of the table above find the method and leave the file `successful`; `imgui.h` and `imgui_internal.h` reach 95% of the inline functions the compiler used; `imgui_demo.cpp` reaches 95%.

### 2. A scan of Dear ImGui takes minutes: the publish cost grows with the project and is paid again for every small batch (high)

**Seen** (under load 19-22, so read the ratios, not the seconds).

| | Before Task 59 (§60) | Now |
|---|---|---|
| index complete after `init` (97 files) | 55 s at load 15 | 154 s at load 20 |
| `scan --full` of 108 files | not timed | **472 s and 568 s** (two runs) |
| the same files published in **one** batch, in-process | not measured | 55 s |
| `scan --full imstb_rectpack.h` (one small header) | not measured | **25.8 s** |
| `scan --full imgui_tables.cpp` | not measured | 14.9 s |
| unchanged `scan` | not measured | 2.2 s |

One file costs 15-26 s and 108 files in one batch cost 55 s, so the time is a fixed cost per publish, not per file. A file now takes more than the `pass_seconds` budget (1 s of parsing) to read, so a batch ends after one file and the project is published about a hundred times (472 s ≈ 100 × 4.7 s). The Task 54 rule "a batch ends after `worker.pass_seconds` of parsing" fits fast parsing and now cuts the work into pieces that each pay the whole fixed cost.

**Profile of one publish of all 107 files** (`cProfile`, in-process, 55.3 s): parsing 27.1 s (49%), `sync_scan_results` 26.3 s (48%).

| Where | Calls | Self time | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| `graph_sync._has_overloads` | 3,040 | 6.9 s | scans the project for overloads once per function (about 2.3 ms each, grows with the project) |
| `tree_sitter.Parser.parse` | 172 | 6.7 s | 107 files parsed 172 times: the rewrite attempts of 59.1 |
| `declarations.find_definition` | 1,746 | 5.0 s | a linear search for each declaration (2.9 ms each) |
| `json encoder.iterencode` | 7,909 | 3.8 s | facts serialised again |
| `str.split` | 35,139 | 3.7 s | name handling |
| `declarations._store_declaration` | 1,542 | 3.1 s | one statement per declaration |
| `provenance.compute_contexts` / `refresh_contexts` | 1 | 6.6 s cumulative | contexts recomputed for the whole project in every publish |
| `cpp_parser._collect_calls`, `_extract_imports` (1.0 million node visits for 107 files), `_collect_errors` | | 2.6 s, 2.0 s, 1.5 s | tree walks |

The first, third, fourth and sixth lines are the same kind of problem that §57 removed from the Python path: work for each function or declaration that scans the whole project, so the cost grows with the square of its size.

**Fix, in order.**
1. A waiting scan must not cut its batches by parsing seconds. Cut by a number of files (or by size), so a project of 108 files is one or a few publishes. (Estimate: 472 s → about 60 s on this machine under the same load; the 55 s in-process figure.)
2. Build `name → overloads` and `name → definitions` once per publish (a dictionary), instead of scanning the project for each function and each declaration. (Estimate: `_has_overloads` and `find_definition` together 12 s → under 1 s.)
3. Insert declarations in one statement per batch; compute the provenance contexts only for the callers of changed functions; serialise facts once. (Estimate: a further 8-10 s of the 26 s.)
4. Reduce the parse work of a file that needs the rewrite: one pass over the tree for imports, calls and errors instead of three, and do not parse again a file whose text did not change after the rewrite. (Estimate: a quarter of the 27 s; not measured.)

**Acceptance.** `scan --full` of the Dear ImGui copy takes at most twice the time of the in-process 55 s under the same load; a one-file `scan --full` takes under 5 s; the number of publishes of a waiting scan of a 108-file project is at most 3; a trace shows no function with more than 1,000 calls whose self time grows with the project.

### 3. Files flagged "partially parsed" that lost nothing, and the numbers behind it (low)

64 of 108 files are partial; **50 of them have nothing skipped**, and 82 functions are skipped in total. A flag that does not mean a loss is noise (mostly C headers: `glfw3.h`, `imstb_*.h`, the OpenGL loader). Report such a file as "read with warnings" and keep "partial" for a file that lost or only partly read a function; the status line "Files Partially Parsed" then counts losses. Also: the new "possibly missed" check is a brace heuristic (the dev says so); its false reports and misses are not known. **Acceptance:** on Dear ImGui the partial count equals the number of files with a skipped or partial function.

### 4. Still open from Task 59 (known, low)

tree-sitter-cpp 0.22.x was not tried because installs are refused, so it is unknown whether the grammar itself reads `x.m < y` (the rewrite stays either way); a contract on a partial function does not say the body was read in part; the real project of the §59 report was not available. These are not new findings; they should become tasks when the environment allows.

## Not done in this review

The quick lane of the test suite was not run, because the machine was loaded by other jobs and by the dev's own runs; timing-sensitive tests fail under such load (28 failed in an earlier overloaded run, none in a clean one).

# 62. Feedback: A Full Scan of Django (Python) and Bevy (Rust) — Recall Is 99% on Both, but the Fixed Cost of a Publish Makes Large Projects Slow, and Rust Call Resolution Takes 80% of the Time (Implemented — see Task 61)

**Status: implemented (Task 61; parts not done are listed there).** The user asked for the same check as for Dear ImGui on a Python and a Rust project. Method: each project was copied (without `.git`, `target`, `node_modules`) to a scratch folder, so the originals got no `_drspec` folder; `drspec init`, the background index, then `scan --full`, timed. **Ground truth:** for Python the standard library `ast` module (every `FunctionDef` and `AsyncFunctionDef`, nested ones included, Python 3.8); for Rust the compiler's own parser (`rustc` 1.74, `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP=1 -Z unpretty=ast-tree`, edition 2021, every `Fn` item with a body flag). Names were compared per file as a multiset. Each cause below was reproduced on a small file. The machine was loaded by other jobs during the whole review (load average 18-22), so **times are inflated and noisy**; recall and call counts are not.

## Result

| | Django (Python) | Bevy (Rust) |
|---|---|---|
| Source | 2,943 `.py` + 111 `.js`, 74 MB | 2,014 `.rs`, 97 MB |
| Functions (artifacts) | 33,205 | 7,371 in the 477 files indexed before the run was stopped |
| **Recall** against the reference parser | **99.1%** (30,119 of 30,379); 118 of 2,899 files miss at least one function, 29 files below 90% | **99.0%** (7,321 of 7,395) in the 473 indexed files; 21 files miss a function |
| Files the reference parser could not read | 44 (syntax newer than Python 3.8); drspec indexed 2,601 functions in them, which I could not check | 0 |
| `init` + background index | **1,163 s (19 min)** | **not finished after 67 minutes: 477 of 2,014 files indexed**, then stopped by me |
| `scan --full` | 428 s | not run |
| One-file `scan --full` | **11-16 s** (the 220-file drspec project: about 1.7 s) | not measured |
| Unchanged `scan` | 1.5 s | not measured |
| Database | **327 MB** for 74 MB of source | 58 MB for 477 files |
| Call edges | 9,645 (9,605 EXTRACTED, 40 AMBIGUOUS) | 28,963 call sites, 77.5% unresolved (`unknown receiver` 9,132, `not found` 4,962, `external package` 4,245, `macro` 467) |

The extra names drspec has in Rust (`create_entities!` 8 and eight others) are `macro_rules` macros, indexed on purpose; they are not defects.

## Profiles (`cProfile` of one forced publish, in-process, the same call the worker makes)

**Django, 2,943 files: 127 s** — parsing 77 s (61%), `sync_scan_results` 48 s (38%).

| Where | Calls | Self time |
|---|---|---|
| `python_parser._extract_imports` | 4.4 million node visits for 2,943 files | 13.5 s |
| `json encoder.iterencode` (facts) | 71,814 | 12.1 s |
| `python_parser._collect_calls` | 3.8 million | 10.5 s |
| `python_parser._collect_symbol_reads` | 3.8 million | 9.1 s |
| `tree_sitter.Parser.parse` | 2,943 | 7.3 s (6%) |
| `queries.get_artifacts_by_file` | 3,154 | 5.3 s |
| `json decoder.raw_decode` | 5,999 | 5.3 s |
| `_collect_property_reads`, `set_config_many`, `str.split` | | 4.8 s, 4.6 s, 5.9 s |
| `provenance.refresh_contexts` / `compute_contexts` | 1 | **17.6 s cumulative, for the whole project, in every publish** |

**Bevy, 473 files: 198 s** — `sync_scan_results` 189 s (96%), of which `_sync_edges` 172 s and **`rust_resolver.resolve_rust_calls` 159.5 s (80% of everything)**: `lookup` 8.65 million recursive calls for 28,491 top-level lookups (88 s self-time), `resolve_path` 5.5 million calls, `types_in`/`functions_in` 4.8 million calls each (14 s and 10 s), `type_entity` 44 s. The tree-sitter parse of all 473 files takes 1.8 s.

## Findings

### 1. Rust: call resolution costs 80% of the time and grows faster than the project (high)

**Seen.** About 300 recursive steps for each lookup; no memoisation of a path in a module. This is why bevy was indexed at 8.5 s per file (477 files in 67 minutes), 20 times slower per file than django (0.4 s per file), and why the run could not finish.

**Fix.** (a) Memoise `resolve_path` and `lookup` per (module, path) for the length of one publish. (b) Build the module and re-export graph once per publish and look names up in dictionaries, not by walking and rebuilding set comprehensions at each step (`types_in`, `functions_in`). (c) Resolve a caller only when it, or a name it uses, changed (the incremental resolution that already exists for Python and JavaScript). Estimate (not measured): the resolver falls to a few percent of the time; the index of bevy to well under an hour on the same loaded machine.

**Acceptance.** The index of bevy completes; the resolver is under 30% of a forced publish; the time per file does not grow with the number of files (500 files and 2,000 files within a factor of 1.5 per file); the stored edges are the same as before (a test compares them on a fixture); the unresolved share is reported with the same reasons.

### 2. Rust: several impls of one trait method for one type become one function (high)

**Seen** (scanner output, no database involved):

| Source | Functions returned |
|---|---|
| `impl IntoIterator for &A`, `for &mut A`, `for A` (each `fn into_iter(self)`) | **1** (`A::<IntoIterator>::into_iter`) |
| `impl AsRef<[u32]> for A` and `impl AsRef<Vec<u32>> for A` | **1** |
| `impl<T> Tr for W<Vec<T>>` and `impl Tr for W<u32>` | **1** |
| `impl PartialEq<B> for A`, `PartialEq<A>`, `PartialEq<u32>` (different parameter types) | 3 (the hash tells them apart) |
| `impl From<u32>`, `From<u8>`, `From<bool>` for `A` | 3 |

In bevy these cases are the missing names: `into_iter` 13, `eq` 10, `extend` 5, `as_ref` 5, `from` 4, `default` 4, in `entity/unique_slice.rs` (16 of 162 functions), `query/fetch.rs` (13 of 363), `entity/unique_vec.rs` (12 of 125) and others. Only the first of several identical-looking functions is kept, so two contracts cannot exist and a call may resolve to the wrong body.

**Cause.** The ID is `Type::<Trait>::name#hash` with the hash over the parameter types, and `self` is excluded (§44). The impl header (the trait's generic arguments, the self type with its reference and generic arguments) is not in the ID.

**Fix.** Put the impl header into the identity: the trait with its generic arguments and the normalised self type (`&A`, `&mut A`, `A`, `W<u32>`) in the hash input, and in the readable part when it is short (`A::<IntoIterator for &mut A>::into_iter`). Existing IDs change, so it needs the migration of §44.

**Acceptance.** The first three rows give 3, 2 and 2 functions with distinct IDs; recall on the 473 indexed bevy files reaches 100% of the reference (7,395); the identity migration keeps contracts of functions whose header did not change.

### 3. Python: a function nested in a decorated function is not indexed (medium)

**Seen.** 260 functions are missing in django; **236 (91%) are nested functions**, 59 of them also decorated. Reproduced alone:

| Source | Indexed |
|---|---|
| `class T:` with `@mock.patch("time.time") def t1(self, m):` containing `def a_view(request): ...` | `T.t1` only |
| the same with the nested function decorated too | `T.t2` only |
| the same method **without** the outer decorator | `T.t4` and `T.t4.a_view` |
| a nested function in a module function, in `for`, `with`, `if`, `try`, two levels deep | all found |

So the loss starts when the *outer* function has a decorator, which is the common shape of tests (`@mock.patch`, `@override_settings`) in `tests/decorators`, `tests/validators`, `tests/auth_tests`.

**Fix.** Extract nested definitions also when the outer definition is a `decorated_definition`.

**Acceptance.** The two failing rows index the nested function; recall on django reaches at least 99.8% of the reference.

### 4. Python: the same name and parameters twice in one scope collapse to one function (low)

12 misses in django are `def` statements in the two branches of an `if` or `try` (for example `django/db/backends/postgresql/operations.py`: `last_executed_query` and `adapt_integerfield_value` defined once for each branch). Same name, same parameter types, so the same ID. Only one runs, but both are code. Fix: when a scope already has that ID, add an ordinal to the hash input (`#hash` plus the position among duplicates), so both exist and keep their IDs when unrelated code changes. Acceptance: both branches are indexed with different IDs.

### 5. The cost of a publish grows with the project and is paid for every batch (high for large projects)

**Seen.** Django: a forced publish of all files in one piece takes 127 s, but `scan --full` took 428 s and the background index 1,163 s. A one-file scan takes 11-16 s in a 33,000-function project and 1.7 s in a 3,800-function one. The same pattern as ImGui (§61 finding 2), now on Python: the whole project's contexts (`refresh_contexts`, 17.6 s) are recomputed in every publish and a project is published in batches of about 200 files (15 batches × 17 s ≈ 260 s, which is the gap).

**Fix, in order.** (1) Compute provenance contexts only for the callers and callees of the functions that changed (the dependents), not for the project. (2) Do not reload and re-serialise facts of unchanged files: 71,814 `json.dumps` (12 s) and 5,999 `json.loads` (5 s) per publish; store facts in a table keyed by file and write only changed rows; this also bears on the database size (327 MB for 74 MB of source, the `config` table holds one JSON entry per file). (3) One tree walk per function that collects imports, calls, symbol reads and property reads together; today four walks visit 15 million nodes in Python. Estimate (not measured): parse 77 s to about 35 s; the fixed cost of a publish from about 18 s to a second or two. (4) Read the stored rows of a batch in one query (`get_artifacts_by_file` is still one query per file: 3,154 calls, 5.3 s).

**Database size (measured afterwards on this repository's own database, 3,935 functions; the django copy was already deleted, so its breakdown is an estimate).** The file is 133.5 MiB: **236 of 534 blocks are free (59 MiB, 44%)**, which DuckDB does not give back after the rewrites of a re-index; the used part is 74 MiB. Per table: `doc_sections` 29.9 MB for 573 rows (documents are off, the rows stay), `artifacts` only 3.4 MB (the function bodies are 2.4 MB of text), `dependencies` 1.0 MB. The per-file facts in `config` (`scan:*` keys) are **10.1 MB of JSON in 237 files (about 43 KB per file)**, three times the size of the whole `artifacts` table. For django's 3,054 files the same ratio would be roughly 130 MB of facts and, with the free space of three full writes, explains much of the 327 MB (an estimate, not measured). So the functions are small; the weight is in the facts, the unreclaimed free space and kept document rows.

**Measured afterwards on a slice of django** (the four packages `django/db`, `django/utils`, `django/forms`, `django/core`: 292 files, 4.4 MB of source, 5,331 functions; a scratch copy; the full 327 MB database was not available):

| Database | Size |
|---|---|
| as written after the first index | **40.9 MB** (9 times the source) |
| the same, compacted (export and import, nothing removed): the **free blocks are 38% of the file** (60 of 156) | 24.4 MB |
| compacted and without the per-file facts (`config` `scan:*`, 11.1 MB of JSON text in 296 rows, 38 KB per file) | **11.8 MB**: the facts are **12.6 MB, 52% of the compact database** |
| compacted, without facts and without function bodies (3.0 MB of text) | 7.1 MB: the bodies cost 4.7 MB, the rest (ids, signatures, edges, indexes) is 7.1 MB |
| after two more `scan --full` | 40.9 MB again (35% free): the free space is reused, it does not keep growing |

So for a Python project the per-file facts are about half of the stored data and the free blocks about a third of the file; the functions themselves (bodies) are about a fifth. Scaled to django's 74 MB of source this fits the 327 MB (about 130 MB of facts and about 100 MB free; an estimate).

**What reads the per-file record, checked in the code (`core/graph_sync.py`, `provenance.py`, `language_health.py`).** The record is needed in part: when a symbol changes anywhere, callers in unchanged files are re-resolved from their stored calls, symbol reads and imports, so the file need not be parsed again. Three things in it are not needed:
1. **The body text is kept only to be hashed.** `graph_sync.py:287` rebuilds each stored function and compares `compute_hash(f.body, ...)` with `artifacts.code_hash` to check that the record still matches the database. A stored hash of the body does the same; the body, signature and id copies in the record duplicate `artifacts`.
2. **Every record is rewritten when symbols change.** `graph_sync.py:274-301` reads all `scan:file:` records, sets `symbols_hash` in each and writes them all back (`json.dumps(snapshot)` per file, `set_config_many`), although the same value is stored once as `scan:symbols` (line 302). One added function rewrites all the records (10 MB in this repository; about 130 MB estimated for django), which also leaves the free blocks.
3. **Whole records are read for one field.** `provenance.py:16-17` decodes every record and keeps only `imports`; `language_health.py:75` reads every record for a `status` call.

**Keep the body in `artifacts`, remove it from the record.** `artifacts.body` is read by search (`queries.py:611,623`: `regexp_matches(body, ...)` and `body LIKE`), by `source get`, `get_artifact`, `list_artifacts` and `get_artifacts_by_file` (`queries.py:269,365,573`), by the embeddings backfill (`graph_sync.py:476`), by carry-over (`carry_over.py:201`) and by the identity migration (`identity_migration.py:300`). Nothing reads the copy in the per-file record except the hash check of point 1. The body in the record is about a fifth of a Python project's stored data (4.7 MB of 24.4 MB compacted in the django slice).

**Decision (user): remove the second copy first, before the other items of this finding.** It is the smallest change with a measured gain, and it changes nothing that the user sees. Scope of this first step:
- the per-file record (`scan:file:<path>`) stops holding the body, signature, id and name of each function; it keeps the two hashes, the imports, and for each function the **body hash** and the calls and symbol reads;
- the check at `graph_sync.py:287` compares the stored body hash with `artifacts.code_hash` (no body is rebuilt);
- `symbols_hash` is kept only in the global `scan:symbols` entry and no longer written into every record (`graph_sync.py:274-301` stops rewriting all records when a symbol changes);
- `provenance.py:16-17` and `language_health.py:75` read only what they use;
- **existing databases:** the old records still hold the bodies; they are rewritten in the new form once (at the first scan after the upgrade, or on the layout check the worker already does at start), and the layout key records which form a database has, so a downgraded or half-converted state is detected, not trusted;
- `artifacts.body` is not touched.

**What the record is made of (measured on this repository: 10.35 MB, 4,024 functions).** `body` 2.59 MB (25.1%), `calls` 2.40 MB (23.2%), `symbol_reads` 1.91 MB (18.5%), `property_reads` 0.44 MB (4.3%), `function_id` 0.35 MB (3.4%), `signature` 0.27 MB (2.6%), `qualified_name` 0.18 MB (1.8%), `file_path` 0.13 MB (1.3%), hashes and imports 0.29 MB (2.8%), the rest (name, parent, language, lines and so on) about a fifth. So the body and the repeated id, signature, name and path are about **a third (about 3.5 MB)** of the record; the calls, symbol reads and property reads, which the re-resolution really needs, are **46% (about 4.8 MB)** and stay in this step. (An earlier version of this section said the step would bring the records under 3 MB and save about 12 MB on the django slice; that counted all of the facts and was too high.)

**Acceptance for this step.** On this repository the `scan:file:*` records are under 7 MB in total (10.3 MB now); on the django slice (292 files) the compacted database is about 4 MB smaller (24.4 MB now; the facts were 12.6 MB, of which about a third goes); the edges, the resolution reasons and the `neighbours_re_resolved` counts of a scan after a rename are equal before and after (a test on a fixture with an unchanged caller whose callee is renamed); a database from the previous layout is converted on the first scan and a second scan changes nothing; adding one function no longer rewrites the records of the other files (a trace or statement count shows writes only for changed files).

**Fix (size).** (0) Per file keep only the hashes, the imports and the calls and symbol reads, in a table keyed by file or function, with the body hash instead of the body; keep `symbols_hash` only in the one global entry; let `provenance` and `language_health` read only the columns they use. (a) Store the per-file facts in a table in a compact form (or compressed), not as JSON text in `config`. (b) Compact the file when more than about 30% of its blocks are free (copy to a new database in the staged publish, which exists already). (c) Delete the document rows when `docs.enabled` is turned off for good, or keep them but report their size in `status`. (d) Report the database size, free space and the three largest tables in `status`.

**Acceptance.** `scan --full` of django at most 1.5 times the one-piece publish (about 190 s here); a one-file scan under 3 s in the django copy; the database under 150 MB for the same index (to be confirmed by the dev; this is a target, not a measurement); on this repository the file has under 10% free blocks after a `scan --full`, and the facts take under 7 MB after the first step (the second copy removed) and under 3 MB only after a second step that also stores the calls, symbol reads and property reads compactly (a table keyed by function, with each name stored once and referred to by number), which is 46% of the record today.

### 6. `status` has no Python block, so the unresolved share of Python calls is not visible (low)

The language blocks of `status` exist for `cpp`, `rust`, `javascript` but not for `python`. Django has 33,205 functions and 9,645 call edges; whether that is a good graph or a poor one cannot be told. Add the same block (calls, unresolved, reasons, partial files) for Python. Acceptance: `status` on django shows `unresolved_calls` with reasons that add up.

### 7. Not measured

The whole of bevy (the run was stopped), the one-file cost on bevy, the quick lane of the test suite (the machine was loaded by other jobs; timing-sensitive tests fail under such load), JavaScript in django beyond its status block (111 files; 77.8% of calls unresolved, mostly external packages and unknown receivers, two files partial).

## Order

**First (user's decision): the second copy of the body in the per-file record, finding 5 item 0**, a small change with a measured gain. Then finding 1 (without it a Rust project of the size of bevy cannot be indexed), the rest of finding 5 (the same cost on every large project), finding 2 and 3 (identity and nested functions), then 4 and 6. After the changes the dev repeats the comparison of this section: copy, index, `scan --full`, compare with `rustc` and `ast`; the targets are in each finding.

# 63. Feedback: Review of Task 60 (§61) — A Scan of Dear ImGui Went From 8 Minutes to 15 Seconds; 55 Functions Are Still Skipped, 21 of Them Avoidable (Implemented — see Task 62)

**Status: implemented (Task 62).** Written after the dev finished Task 60 (the §61 items; the §62 items are not done yet and are not reviewed here). Method: reinstall; the check of §60 and §61 run **in place** in the user's clone `/media/thanh/data/Projects/imgui` (the user allowed it; `_drspec` was removed afterwards and `.gitignore` restored byte for byte); `scan --full`, a one-file `scan --full` and a `cProfile` of one forced publish; the thirteen isolated constructs; then the quick lane of the test suite. The machine was loaded by other jobs (load average about 20) during all timings, so **seconds are inflated**; the ratios are what counts.

## What was verified and works

| | §61 | Now |
|---|---|---|
| index complete after `init` (97-108 files) | 154 s | **58 s** |
| `scan --full` of 108 files | 472 s and 568 s | **15.3 s** |
| `scan --full` of one small header (`imstb_rectpack.h`) | 25.8 s | **3.3 s** |
| `scan --full` of `imgui_tables.cpp` | 14.9 s | 3.9 s |
| unchanged `scan` | 2.2 s | 2.8 s |
| recall of the five core files against the compiler | 98% | 98%: `imgui.cpp` 100%, `imgui_draw.cpp` 100%, `imgui_tables.cpp` 100%, `imgui_widgets.cpp` 99%, `imgui_demo.cpp` **90%** |
| inline and template functions of the headers used by the core files | `imgui_internal.h` 79%, `imgui.h` 85% | 79% and 85% (no change) |
| isolated constructs (13) | 11 pass; a defined trailing macro and a defined export macro fail | **13 of 13 pass** |
| files "partially parsed" | 64 | 31, and 4 "read with warnings" |
| functions skipped | 82 | 55 |
| unresolved share of calls | 23.9% | 23.9% |
| `.mm` files scanned | 11 | 11 |

The big change is the cost of a publish: a waiting scan is cut by file count, and the work that grew with the project (declaration lookup, overload grouping, contexts, one statement per row) is gone. The same files in one forced publish: sync 26.3 s in §61, 4.6 s now.

## Profile of one forced publish of the 107 files (`cProfile`, in-process, 24.1 s)

| Phase | Time |
|---|---|
| parsing (`cpp_parser.parse`) | 18.5 s (**77%**) |
| `sync_scan_results` | 4.6 s (19%); `_sync_edges` 2.5 s |

Inside parsing: `tree_sitter.Parser.parse` 5.3 s for **169 parses of 107 files** (the rewrite attempts parse 55 files again); `_extract_imports` 2.2 s (1.03 million node visits: it walks the whole tree); `_collect_calls` 2.0 s; regular expressions 1.4 s (234,535 searches); `_better_tree` 3.2 s cumulative (55 calls). In the real scan the parsing runs in the pool of processes, which is why the scan takes 15 s and not 24 s. There is no single hotspot above a quarter of the time left, unlike the Python project (publish 3.2 s of 5.2 s) and unlike §61.

**Ideas, with estimates (not measured).** (1) `_extract_imports` only needs the `#include` lines, which are top-level nodes: look at the top level, not the whole tree (saves most of its 2.2 s). (2) After a rewrite re-parse only the damaged region with the tree editing API of tree-sitter, instead of parsing the file again (2-3 s of the 5.3 s). (3) Collect calls, symbol reads and property reads in one walk (the django profile shows the same four walks). (4) Skip a rewrite attempt for a file whose first parse has a handful of errors that no attempt of the same kind fixed before in a similar file (a cache by the shape of the error). None of these is needed before the §62 items.

## Findings

### 1. Of the 55 skipped functions, 33 are unavoidable, 1 is a false alarm and 21 are avoidable losses (medium)

I listed the skipped entries and read their source lines (scratch copy, 12 files):

| Count | Where | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 33 | eight Objective-C++ files (`imgui_impl_metal.mm` 14, `imgui_impl_metal4.mm` 7, `imgui_impl_osx.mm` 4, four `example_apple_*/main.mm` 2 each) | **Unavoidable with a C++ grammar**: `@interface` and `- (void)m:` are Objective-C. |
| 1 | `backends/imgui_impl_wgpu.cpp:162`, `fn main(in: VertexInput) -> VertexOutput {` | **False alarm**: WGSL shader text inside a C++ raw string literal; the brace scan counts it as a function. |
| 14 | `imgui.h` (lines 383, 2814, 3551-3555, 3864-3866, 3964-3966, 4066): valid one-line inline functions such as `inline void PushTextureID(ImTextureRef tex_ref) { PushTexture(tex_ref); }`, `inline bool Draw(const char* label, float width) { ... }` | **Avoidable.** They sit in `#if/#else` regions (obsolete-function blocks); Task 59.1 reads only the first branch of each `#if/#else` and lists "the stripped-down variants of `#if` branches are not indexed" as not done. Hypothesis, not proved for every line. |
| 3 | `imgui_internal.h:512,513,578` (`ImRsqrt`, `ImRsqrtPrecise`, `ImIsTruncated4`) | Avoidable; same cause (the non-SSE `#else` branch). |
| 2 | `imgui_tables.cpp:4488-4489` (`void ImGui::DebugNodeTable(ImGuiTable*) {}` and its pair) | Avoidable; the stub branch of `#ifndef IMGUI_DISABLE_DEBUG_TOOLS ... #else`. |
| 2 | `imstb_truetype.h:383,2794` (`int main(...)` in an example block; `STBTT_DEF stbtt_uint8 *stbtt_FindSVGDoc(...)`, where `STBTT_DEF` stands for a storage class) | Avoidable for the second; the first is code in a test block. |

**Fix.** (a) For a file whose conditional branches matter, parse each branch of an `#if/#else/#elif` on its own (the other branches blanked, same length) and keep the **union** of the functions; a function defined in two branches needs the ordinal of §62 finding 4. (b) Blank string literals, raw strings included, before the brace scan, so shader or SQL text is not read as code. (c) A macro that is defined as a storage class or a linkage keyword (`#define STBTT_DEF extern`, `static`) is blanked like the attribute macros.

**Acceptance.** On Dear ImGui the skipped functions in C++ files (not `.mm`) fall from 22 to 0 and the false alarm disappears; `imgui_internal.h` and `imgui.h` inline functions reach 95% and `imgui_demo.cpp` 95%; a function that exists in both branches of an `#if/#else` gets two IDs; the `.mm` files remain the only source of skipped functions, and `status` says so ("Objective-C: 33 skipped").

### 2. The headers and `imgui_demo.cpp` did not move (medium)

`imgui_internal.h` 79%, `imgui.h` 85%, `imgui_demo.cpp` 90% are the same as in §61, below the 95% target. Finding 1 explains 21 of the skipped functions but not all the misses (the headers lost 60 of 290 and 18 of 122 inline functions that the compiler used; `imgui_demo.cpp` lost 12 of 128).
