The dev should list the misses per file as in this review (compile with `g++ -g`, `nm -C -l`, compare by name) and attribute each to a cause before the next round. **Acceptance:** each of the three files at 95% or more, and the remaining misses each with a stated cause.

### 3. The stored C++ text is not what Task 60.1 says (low)

Task 60.1 says the stored signature and body keep the macro. For a trailing macro they do not (current build, small file with `#define FMT(x) ...`):

| Source | Stored |
|---|---|
| `void A(const char* fmt, ...) FMT(2);` | `void A(const char* fmt, ...)       ;` (the macro is spaces) |
| `API int B(int a) FMT(1) { ... }` | signature `API int B(int a)        { ... }`; body `int B(int a)        { ... }` (the leading macro is also missing from the body) |

Python keeps comments, blank lines, tabs and inner spacing, but drops the first line's indentation, trailing spaces of the last line, and turns CRLF into LF. The hash is over the stored text, so this is consistent, but search for `FMT(` in bodies finds nothing and `source get` shows a gap. **Fix:** store the original text of the function's range (from the first token the user wrote, including a leading macro, to the end), and compute the hash and the call text from the same original. **Acceptance:** the stored signature and body of the rows above equal the source lines character for character (except the Python cases listed, which are documented).

### 4. Two tests are fragile in a full run (low)

In the quick lane (2,565 passed, 47 deselected, 1 xfailed, 877 s) two failed and both pass alone: `test_worker_build.py::TestAnOutdatedWorker::test_a_command_replaces_it_before_sending_its_request` (also failed in an earlier run of mine, 6.9 s alone) and `test_followup_regressions.py::test_process_termination_before_publish_keeps_original` (8.1 s alone). The source was being edited during my runs, and the worker build id includes a source fingerprint, so an edit mid-run changes it; the load was about 20. **Fix:** give these tests a fixed fake build id and no dependence on the clock or on the state of the tree; mark them `slow`. **Acceptance:** 5 full runs in a row without these two failing, with a source file touched during one of them.

### 5. The quick lane is still 14.6 minutes (low, known)

The target of §57 was under 5 minutes; `pytest-xdist` is in the development dependencies but could not be installed here, and a hand-made parallel run gave only a 20% gain. Unchanged from §57.

## Not reviewed here

The §62 items (Rust resolver and impl identity, Python nested functions in decorated functions, the Python status block, the database size and the first step of removing the second copy of the body) are not done yet; django and bevy will be run again when they are.

# 64. Plan: Full Support for C on the Existing C/C++ Parser (Implemented — see Task 63)

**Status: implemented (Task 63; decision 4 not followed to the letter, see 63.1).** Decided with the user one question at a time (the answers are under "Decisions"). Evidence comes from a check of raylib (`/media/thanh/data/Projects/raylib`, a C project: 291 `.c`, 78 `.h`, 5 `.m`; run in place, `_drspec` removed afterwards) and from a test of 18 C-specific constructs on small files.

## What exists and what was measured

- C is read today by the C++ parser: `scanner.py:44` maps `.c` to `cpp` ("C uses C++ parser (subset)"), and every C function is stored with `language = 'cpp'` (raylib: `{'cpp': 9449}`). `status` shows only a `cpp` block; `search --include c` cannot select C.
- **raylib against `gcc -g` + `nm -l`** (233 of 245 C files in `src/` and `examples/` compile): recall **100%**, 1,443 of 1,443 functions (`src/` 621/621, `examples/` 822/822). 369 files scanned, 9,274 definitions and 2,214 declarations, unresolved calls 24.7%, 51 files partially parsed, 9 read with warnings. `init` + index 304 s, `scan --full` 102 s, at load about 20.
- **8,892 functions are skipped**, but 7,088 of them are in one vendored file, `src/external/miniaudio.h`; then `RGFW.h` 892, `jar_xm.h` 218, `minigamepad.h` 168. raylib's own code is read; the large single-header libraries are not.
- **Profile of one forced publish of 369 files: 143.9 s.** Parsing 70.6 s (49%; tree-sitter 12.7 s for 519 parses, `_collect_calls` 9.4 s, `_extract_imports` 8.3 s with 3.9 million node visits). Publish 72.5 s (50%) and almost all of it is one quadratic spot in `graph_sync.py`: `_build_same_file_functions` (23.5 s self, 9,274 calls) and the dict and set comprehensions at lines 769-770 (30 s, 9.4 million set builds) rebuild, for each function, a structure over **all functions of the same file**; for `miniaudio.h` (about 7,000 functions) that is about 49 million operations.
- **18 C-specific constructs, each with sentinel functions around it:** 16 pass (compound literals `(Col){...}`, designated initialisers, `static inline`, `restrict`, an API macro before the function, statement expressions, bit-fields, variadic functions, a member comparison, `goto`, `_Generic`, `__asm__`, a function-pointer table, and C++ keywords used as names, which pass but leave the file "partial"). **2 fail:** a K&R definition (`int kr(a, b) int a; int b; {...}`) and a function that returns a function pointer (`int (*pick2(void))(int)`).
- A pure-C snippet parsed with the C++ grammar produces no C++-only node types (43 node types, all shared), so the extraction code written for C++ already applies to C.

## Decisions (each one the user's answer)

1. **One C/C++ parser.** Keep the C++ parser and the C++ grammar for C; add only what C needs. No `tree-sitter-c`, no second copy of the extraction, macro, `#if` or resolver code. (If the list of C-only rewrites grows long, a separate grammar can be added later behind the same label.)
2. **A stored label `c`.** `.c` files are stored as `c`; a `.h` is labelled **by its neighbours**: `c` when the `.c` files in its folder (or the files that include it) are C and no C++ file is near, otherwise `cpp`; the label of a file is decided at its scan and changes only when the neighbours change. `.mm` stays `cpp` as it is today and `.m` is **not** added (decision 6).
3. **Filters and status.** `search --include cpp` returns C and C++ together; `search --include c` returns only C. `status` keeps one `cpp` block and adds the C count inside it (`c_files`, `c_functions`); the block's other numbers cover both.
4. **No migration.** Existing databases are not converted: a layout key is bumped and an old database is rebuilt by a clean scan (the existing layout check, as for the identity and embedding layouts). Nothing is carried over.
5. **Vendored code is read properly, with no special rule.** The causes of the 7,000 skipped functions are fixed, so vendored libraries are indexed like any other code; `scan.ignore` still skips folders.
6. **Objective-C is out of scope for this plan** (the user changed this answer after first choosing to read `.m` like `.mm`). `.mm` is left as it is (read as C++, a message send makes the file partial), `.m` is not added, and `status` keeps saying how many functions are skipped there. Objective-C needs its own grammar and is a separate language.

## Work

**C1. The label and the family lookup.** `LANGUAGE_MAP` maps `.c` to `c` (`.m` is not added; `.mm` stays `cpp`); a `.h` goes through the neighbour rule (a function of `core/languages.py`, not a branch in the scanner). `core/languages.py` gets one notion, "C family" (`c` and `cpp`: the same parser, the same resolver kind, the same comment remover and hints), and the 26 places that test `== "cpp"` (`cpp_resolver.py:90`, `graph_sync.py:801,820`, `hasher.py:93`, `hints.py:113`, `language_health.py`, `identity_migration.py`, `scanner.py`) ask it instead. `search --include cpp` expands to the family; `--include c` is exact. **Acceptance:** raylib stores `c` for the `.c` files and the C headers (`raymath.h`, `rlgl.h`) and `cpp` for none; a mixed folder (a `.cpp` next to a `.h`) labels the header `cpp`; `search --include c` and `--include cpp` give the counts of decision 3; `status` shows the C count inside the `cpp` block; no test mentions a language name that the family lookup could have supplied.

**C2. The C-only constructs.** Through the same-length rewrite of Task 59: K&R definitions (parameter declarations between the declarator and the body), a function that returns a function pointer, and the C++ keywords used as names (`new`, `class`, `this`, `template`, `delete`, `namespace`, `typename`, `operator`) so the file is `successful`, not partial. A conformance file `tests/fixtures/c/constructs.c` holds the 18 constructs of the review plus `_Generic`, `typeof`, variable-length arrays, flexible array members, `_Bool`, X-macros and a function defined by a macro. **Acceptance:** 18 of 18 on the review's cases and all of the conformance file; none of them makes a file partial; start and end positions equal those of the source.

**C3. Single-header and implementation-guarded libraries (decision 5).** Vendored libraries such as `miniaudio.h`, `stb_*.h`, `RGFW.h` put their code inside `#ifdef NAME_IMPLEMENTATION` or large `#if/#else` regions. Read the guarded implementation section (treat a macro named `*_IMPLEMENTATION` as defined for the parse) and, as in §63 finding 1, parse each branch of an `#if/#else` and keep the union of the functions. **Acceptance:** the functions skipped in `src/external` fall from 8,468 to under 5% of what `gcc -DMINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION` lists for `miniaudio.h`; raylib's skipped functions fall from 8,892 to under 400 in total; a function defined in two branches gets two IDs (§62 finding 4).

**C4. The same-file map.** Build the `name → functions` map of a file once per publish, not once per function (`graph_sync.py:769-770,878`). Estimate (not measured): 50-60 s of the 144 s of the raylib publish. **Acceptance:** a forced publish of raylib's 369 files takes under 60 s at the same load; no function with more than 1,000 calls whose self time grows with the size of one file.

**C6. Tests and the external check.** The conformance file above; a fixture project of 4-6 small C files with a header and a mixed folder for the label rule; the raylib check as an optional slow test that is skipped without the project or `gcc` (as the ImGui check is): compile every C file with `gcc -g -c`, list the definitions with `nm -C -l`, compare by name and file. **Acceptance:** the optional test reports recall of at least 99% for `src/` and `examples/`.

## Order and risks

Order: C1 (label and family), C2 (C-only constructs), C4 (the quadratic spot, which also helps every large C++ file), C3 (vendored libraries), then C6 alongside each step. (There is no C5: Objective-C was taken out of the plan.) Risks: (1) the 26 `"cpp"` checks: a missed one makes C behave like another language in a corner (the family lookup and a grep-free test that every language name goes through it reduce this); (2) the neighbour rule for `.h` can flip a label when a folder changes, which changes the stored language of its functions; the label must not be part of a function's ID (it is not today); (3) treating `*_IMPLEMENTATION` as defined can index code that a real build excludes; it is a parse choice, and the status line should say it.

## Not decided here

Whether to add a separate C grammar later (decision 1 leaves it open), whether `search --include cpp` including C should be reversed when scripts depend on it, and the `status` JSON field names (`c_files`, `c_functions` are proposals).

# 65. Feedback: Review of Task 61 (§62) — Python Recall 100% and Rust 99.9%, the Rust Resolver Fell From 80% to 11%, but Half of Every Database Is Free Space, a One-File Scan Is Not Under 3 s, and Rust Parsing Re-Parses Every Macro (Implemented — see Task 64)

**Status: implemented (Task 64; parts not done are listed there).** Written after the dev finished Task 61 (§62). Method: reinstall; the check of §62 repeated **in place** in the user's clones (`/media/thanh/data/Projects/{django,bevy,imgui}`, allowed by the user; `_drspec` removed afterwards and `.gitignore` restored byte for byte; raylib was not rerun because no C work exists yet): index, `scan --full`, a one-file `scan --full`, the database size and free blocks, the recall against the reference parsers (Python `ast`, `rustc` 1.74, `g++ -g` with `nm`), the trace of one scan and a `cProfile` of one forced publish (the first 150 and 450 Rust files of bevy, one Python file of django). The machine was loaded by other jobs (load average 17-26) during all timings, and my runs were at low priority, so **seconds are inflated**; the ratios are what counts. A reboot of the machine had cleared the earlier scratch work; the figures of the earlier reviews are quoted from this document.

## What was verified

| | §62 review | Now | Dev's report (Task 61) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **django** recall vs `ast` | 99.1% (260 missing) | **100.0%** (30,379 of 30,379; no file misses a function) | 100.00% |
| django `init` + background index | 1,163 s | **683 s** | 250 s |
| django `scan --full` | 428 s | **117 s** | 124 s |
| django one-file `scan --full` | 11-16 s | **5.2 s** warm; 17.4 s right after a worker start (see finding 1) | 6-7 s |
| django unchanged scan | 1.5 s | 4.0 s | 2.3 s |
| django database file | 327 MB | **249.6 MB, 49% free** (490 of 952 blocks used: 127 MB) | 237 MB, 45.5% free |
| django `status` | no Python block | `python` block: 122,646 calls, 90.2% unresolved (`unknown receiver` 82,913, `not found` 27,753), 16,657 library calls counted apart | same |
| **bevy** recall vs `rustc` | 99.0% (473 files) | **99.9%**: 25,510 of 25,525 in **1,741 files** (14 files miss 1-2 functions, e.g. `bevy_ptr/src/lib.rs` 2) | 99.94% (all files) |
| bevy files indexed | 477 in 67 min | **1,741 of 2,014 in 12.7 min** (my cap) | all |
| bevy publish of 450 Rust files | 198 s | **31.7 s** (150 files: 3.8 s) | 26 s |
| Rust resolver share of a publish | 80% (159 of 198 s) | **about 11%** (`lookup` + `build_rust_index` 3.4 s of 31.7 s; `lookup` is called 130,000 times, it was 8.65 million) | 5% |
| bevy database after 12.7 min | n/a | 171.5 MB, 46% free; 25,658 functions | n/a |
| **imgui** (C++, unchanged by Task 61) | index 79-154 s; `scan --full` 15-17 s after Task 60 | index 79 s; `scan --full` 17 s; one-file 2.8 s; unchanged 1.9 s; recall 98% (`imgui.cpp` 100%, `imgui_demo.cpp` 90%); database 38.3 MB, 44% free | n/a |

**Quick lane of the test suite (default lane, nothing else running): 2,614 passed, 1 xfailed, 47 deselected, 0 failed, in 11 min 25 s**, the same count as the dev reports; the two tests that failed in my earlier runs (`test_worker_build...replaces_it_before_sending_its_request` and one in `test_followup_regressions`) did not fail. The lane still takes about 11 minutes (the target of §57 was under 5). The small reproductions of §62, run on the working tree before Task 61 was marked done, passed: three impl blocks of `IntoIterator` give 3 functions with different IDs, `AsRef<[u32]>` and `AsRef<Vec<u32>>` give 2, `impl<T> Tr for W<Vec<T>>` and `impl Tr for W<u32>` give 2; a function nested in a decorated method (plain or decorated) is found; the same name in both branches of an `if/else` gives 2 functions with different IDs; a defined trailing macro and a defined export macro in C++ no longer lose methods. **Not verified here:** the 13 isolated C++ constructs of §61 (not rerun), the size of this repository's per-file records (the dev reports 6.53 MB, was 10.35 MB), the dev's "all of bevy publishes in 92.6 s", and the compaction on a real large database in the sense of its final size. The quick lane result is at the end.

## Findings

### 1. Free space is given back only when the worker starts, and the start waits for it (medium)

**Seen.** After a scan every database is 44-49% free blocks (ImGui 44%, raylib 47%, bevy 46%, django 49%): django's 249.6 MB file holds 127 MB of data and 122 MB of free blocks. DuckDB does not return the blocks a rewrite frees; the index writes everything, `scan --full` writes every record again, and the old copies stay as free blocks. `ensure_compact` (`db/compaction.py`) is called from one place, `run_worker` (`staleness_worker.py:718`), when the worker starts, and rebuilds the file if more than 30% of it is free and it is at least 32 MB. Nothing compacts while the worker runs, so a worker that runs for days keeps its free space (the file stops growing but stays at its peak).

**Cost, measured.** On django's 250 MB file the first command after a worker start waited about **11 s** (`drspec config set` took 11.2 s in the trace: the rebuild). That is also why my first one-file scan measured 17.4 s, against 5.2 s with a warm worker.

**Fix.** Compact in the background when the worker has been idle for a while (for example 10 minutes) and more than 30% is free, not only at start: only when no scan or publish is running and no request is waiting; build the new file from a consistent copy while reads continue (the queued writes wait or are replayed), swap it with one rename, and leave the old file in place on any failure (the machinery of the layout migrations already does this). At start keep the check, but do it after the first request is answered, not before. **Acceptance.** After `scan --full` and 10 idle minutes the free share of every database is under 10% without a restart; a search issued during the rebuild is answered in the time of an idle search; the first command after a worker start on a 250 MB file waits under 2 s; a forced failure of the rebuild (a full disk, a killed process) leaves the old file readable and the next start tries again.

### 2. A one-file scan still takes 5 s on a large project (medium)

**Seen** (django, warm worker, trace of one `scan --full django/utils/functional.py`): 5.2 s of wall time (1.65 s of CPU in the client). `scan_start` took about 1.7 s (a scan of a named file should not have to look at the project), the publish 1.7 s, the rest is the start of the command and the requests. In-process, one publish of one file takes 2.7 s of which `refresh_contexts` 1.4 s: **`_read_inputs` decodes every `scan:file:` record (47.8 MB of JSON) to read only the imports (0.65 s)**, and `read_all_settings` costs 0.5 s in every publish. The dev's target (under 3 s) is not met.

**Fix.** (a) A scan of a named file or function looks at that file (and the files it names), not at the whole project, in `scan_start`. (b) Keep the imports of each file where the contexts can read them without decoding the records (a small table, or the imports as their own config entry per file). (c) Read the settings once per worker and refresh them on a change, not on every publish. **Acceptance.** A one-file `scan --full` on the django clone takes under 3 s warm at the same load; the trace shows no `scan_start` over 0.2 s for a named file and no read of all records in a publish of one file.

### 3. Rust parsing is now 56% of a publish: every macro is parsed again (medium)

**Seen** (bevy, first 450 files, 31.7 s): parsing 17.6 s (56%), `sync_scan_results` 13.5 s (43%). In `rust_parser._macro_calls` (line 479) the token tree of every macro (`assert_eq!`, `format!`, `vec!`, `println!`, ...) is wrapped in a fake function and **parsed again** with tree-sitter, up to twice (as arguments, then as statements), recursively for nested macros: **4,965 parses for 450 files** (about 11 per file; Python and C++ parse about once per file), 5.0 s in tree-sitter plus about 2.6 s in `_macro_calls`. On top of that each function's subtree is walked in Python several times: `_collect_calls` 692,072 nodes (5.7 s), `_local_types` 655,063 (2.9 s), `_nested` 654,321 (1.9 s). The publish has its own costs: `_store_unresolved` 2.7 s (two calls), `get_config` 1,491 calls (1.1 s), `set_config_many` 0.9 s.

**Fix, with estimates (not measured).** (a) **Parse fewer macros, two rules, counted on the first 300 Rust files of bevy (3,024 macro calls with arguments):** (i) a macro whose text has no `(` and no `name!` followed by `(`, `[` or `{` cannot contain a call (a call needs a parenthesis, a nested macro needs `name!(`), so its text is not parsed at all; a bare `!` is not enough to keep it, because `!` is also the logical not and `!=` (of the 218 macro texts with a `!` in the 300 files only 94 hold a real nested macro, 124 hold only `!x` or `!=`); the looser test `no ( and no !` skips 1,265 of 3,002 (42%), the tighter one 1,279 (43%): **1,281 of 3,024 (42%)**; (ii) cache the result of the others for the length of a publish, with a size limit, **keyed by the shape of the text, not the exact text** (the user's idea: a macro is like a template, its invocations are instances). The shape replaces string literals, numbers and plain names (an identifier that is not called, not followed by `(`, `!`, `::`, and not a receiver before `.`) by a placeholder, and keeps every called name and every receiver, because those decide the edges; `assert_eq!(compute_total(&items), 10)` and `assert_eq!(compute_total(&other), 10)` share the shape `assert_eq!(compute_total(&_), _)`. Measured on the same 300 files (1,737 macros that have a `(` or `!`): an exact-text cache skips 341 (20%), **a shape cache skips 757 (44%)**; with rule (i) that is roughly 67% of all macro parses, against 54% for exact text (an estimate from counts, not timed). A shape cache is a normalisation, so the dev compares its edges with today's on a fixture and on a few hundred bevy files before switching it on. Exact-text version, for reference: (ii) cache the result of the others by (depth, text) for the length of a publish, with a size limit: the stored calls (`RawCall`: receiver, name, text) carry no position, so the same text gives the same calls anywhere: **772 of 3,024 repeat (26%)**, 347 (20%) of those that remain after rule (i). **Both rules together (exact-text cache) skip 1,628 of 3,024 parses (54%)**, which is the lower bound of the saving because each macro is parsed once or twice (the macro share of the publish is about 7.6 s of 31.7 s at 450 files; the estimate is about 3.5 s, not timed). (a') **Preferred, the user's idea (a macro is like a class and an invocation like creating an object): treat a known macro as an ordinary call by a same-length rewrite.** For macros whose arguments are ordinary expressions, replace the `!` of `name!(` by a space (`assert_eq!(a, b)` becomes `assert_eq (a, b)`, the same length, so every line, column and byte position stays the same). The grammar then reads the macro as a call and its arguments as real expressions, **in the one normal pass over the file, with no second parse**; the call to the macro name itself is filtered out (the original text has the `!`). Same mechanism as the same-length rewrite of C++ in Task 59, and, as there, the rewritten text is kept only if it does not add parse errors. Names whose arguments are expressions: `assert`, `assert_eq`, `assert_ne`, the `debug_assert` variants, `println`, `print`, `eprintln`, `eprint`, `format`, `write`, `writeln`, `panic`, `unreachable`, `todo`, `unimplemented`, `dbg`, `warn`, `info`, `error`, `debug`, `trace`, written with `(`. Macros whose arguments are not expressions keep the old re-parse: `quote!`, `matches!` with a pattern or a guard, `vec![x; n]`, `macro_rules!` and the project's own macros (about 15 to 20% of the invocations in the sample). **Measured on the first 120 Rust files of bevy (1,820 functions): tree-sitter parses fall from 876 to 231 (74% fewer); 1,819 of 1,820 functions have the same calls; parse errors 2 and 2.** The one function that differed (`parse_bsn`) differs by two calls of a project macro that was not in the list and was not examined. This removes more of the re-parses than rules (i) and (ii) (54%, or about 67% with the shape cache), is simpler, and gives real positions. The rules combine: skip macros with no call, rewrite the known ones, re-parse the rest (with the shape cache, if still needed). The dev checks the edges against today's on a fixture (`println!("{x}", x = 3)`, `assert!(cond, "msg {}", a)`, `format!` with named arguments, `vec![...]` inside `assert_eq!(...)`, a macro written with `[` or `{`) and on a few hundred bevy files. (b) **One walk per function** for calls, local types and nested items (`_collect_calls` 692,072 node visits, `_local_types` 655,063, `_nested` 654,321 at 450 files): an estimate of about half of the 17.6 s of parsing, not measured.  (c) **The second template of a macro is tried only when the first one does not parse cleanly.** `_macro_calls` already stops on a clean parse, so the cost is in the macros that need both attempts; with rule (a)(ii) a macro text is parsed at most once per publish in each form.  (d) **Rejected (the user's decision, after the measurement below): do not skip the arguments of standard-library macros** (`println!`, `format!`, `assert!`, `assert_eq!`, `vec!`, the 46 names of `STANDARD_MACROS` in `rust_parser.py`). On the first 300 Rust files of bevy, **1,503 standard macros have a `(` or `!` in their text, and 86% of them (1,293) produce at least one call**; the largest are `assert_eq!` (684 invocations, 618 with calls) and `assert!` (574, 515 with calls). Those calls are mostly tests that call project functions (`assert_eq!(compute_total(&items), 10)`). Skipping them would remove those edges, so `deps get` would not list the tests of a function and the unused-code report would call a function that is only exercised in `assert!` or `println!` unused. The macros the project defines itself behave the same way (all 180 produced calls). Rules (a) to (c) save more without this loss.  (f) **Batch the macros of one function into one parse (an untested idea).** Today each macro invocation is parsed on its own, even when its text equals the one before; a function with 20 macro calls costs 20 to 40 small parses (about 11 per file on bevy). Instead build one fake function that holds the argument text of every macro of the function as separate statements (`__f(args1); __f(args2); ...`), parse it once and read the calls statement by statement; this is one parse per function. The risk is errors: today the choice between the two templates is made from `root_node.has_error` of the whole tree, so one macro that does not parse cleanly would affect the others. Locate the error node, find the statement it belongs to and parse only that macro again on its own. The dev measures first: parses per file, and equality of the edges with today's (a fixture with `println!`, `assert_eq!`, nested macros and macros whose text is not valid Rust).  (e) **Write the unresolved calls of a publish in one statement** (`_store_unresolved` 2.7 s). **Not proposed:** expanding macros (it needs the compiler's macro engine and often the build of the crate), and ignoring macro arguments (calls in `assert!`, `format!` and test macros are real calls). If (a)-(c) are not enough, a possible next step is to find the calls from the macro's own token tree without parsing again (a call is an identifier or path followed by a group, a method call is `.` identifier group, a nested macro is identifier `!` group); its accuracy is not measured and it needs a fixture that compares its edges with the parsed ones first. Bevy has about 15 functions and 70 calls per file against django's 11 and 41, so Rust will stay somewhat slower per file; the target is a smaller gap. **Acceptance.** A forced publish of the first 450 files of bevy takes under 15 s at the same load; tree-sitter parse calls per file are under 3; the stored edges are equal before and after for steps (a) to (c) and (e) (a fixture test); a macro whose text has no `(` and no `!` produces no calls (a test over `assert!`, `format!` and `vec!` shapes); a test over a fixture shows that `assert_eq!(project_fn(x), 3)` still produces the edge to `project_fn`.

### 4. The per-file records are still 48 MB of JSON on django (low, known)

Task 61 removed the second copy of the text, so the per-file facts on django are 47.8 MB (3,054 records) in `config` and `provenance:inputs` 3.5 MB; the dev lists the compact table as not done. Unchanged from §62 finding 5; the next step there (calls, symbol reads and property reads stored once per name in a table) is what brings the file under 150 MB. **Acceptance** as in §62.

### 5. bevy: the last 0.1% is `#[cfg]` variants (low)

The dev says the misses they looked at are functions that exist in several `#[cfg(...)]` variants and are kept as one entry on purpose. In my run 14 of 1,741 files miss 1-2 functions (15 of 25,525). That is consistent, but the dev did not check all of them, and I did not either. **Acceptance:** list the 15 misses with their cause (cfg variant, macro-generated, other) in the notes; any that is neither is a finding.

### 6. Python: 90% of the calls are unresolved, and the reasons are mostly not a defect (low)

`unknown receiver` is 82,913 of 110,666 unresolved calls (a method of an object whose type is not known, for example `self.assertEqual`, `queryset.filter`); `not found` 27,753. It is accurate and now visible. Two cheap improvements: a call on `self` or `cls` to a name that no class in the project defines is a call into an inherited external class, and should be counted as `external`, not `unknown receiver`; and a call on a variable assigned from a project class (`x = Model(); x.save()`) can be typed from the assignment. **Acceptance:** the `unknown receiver` share of django falls (the dev states a target) and the reasons still add up to the unresolved total.

## What works

Django recall 100% against `ast`; bevy 99.9% against `rustc`; the Rust resolver no longer grows with the square of the modules (about 130,000 lookups at 450 files, not 8.65 million); time per file in the publish of bevy does not explode between 150 and 450 files (3.8 s and 31.7 s; the larger set holds larger files, so I do not read it as a trend); definitions inside decorated methods, repeated definitions in one scope and impl blocks are indexed as separate functions; `status` has a Python block and a database block; the worker rebuilds a file with more than 30% free space at start and keeps the old file when the rebuild fails.

## Order

Finding 1 first (the delay and the wasted half of every database), finding 2 (one-file scans are the common case), finding 3 (Rust parsing), then 4, 5 and 6. The C plan (§64) is independent of these.


# 66. Feedback: Review of Tasks 62–64 (§63, §64, §65) — C Is Its Own Label and Raylib Publishes in 38 s, ImGui Skipped Functions Are All Objective-C, but an Idle Worker Does Not See Its Free Space, and the Rust and Per-File Targets Are Not Met (Implemented — see Task 65)

**Status: implemented (Task 65; parts not done are listed there).**

## Method

The dev's Tasks 62, 63 and 64 are marked [x], no dev test run was active and nothing in `src/` or `tests/` changed after 12:37. `pip install -e . --no-deps -q`, then, one after another at `nice -n 10`, in place in the clones the user allowed (the project's `.gitignore` saved outside the project first; afterwards only `_drspec` removed and `.gitignore` restored; checked with `ps` and `ls`: no worker and no `_drspec` left in imgui, raylib, django, bevy): Dear ImGui, django, bevy (12 minute cap, then a profile of 450 files before cleanup), raylib (against `gcc`), then the quick lane. Extra checks on file copies or with drspec's `Scanner` on small files: the 15 C++ constructs of §61/§63, five C constructs, the Rust identity and macro-edge cases, the Python nested and duplicate cases (all pass), ImGui against `g++`/`nm` per function, miniaudio against `gcc -DMINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION`, and a copy of the ImGui database before and after `CHECKPOINT`. The machine was at load 17–21 from other jobs (a Jupyter job of another project) for most of the review, so times are compared as ratios.

## What was verified and works

| | Before | Now (this review) | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|
| ImGui `scan --full` | 17 s | **9 s** (108 files, 4,550 functions) | — |
| ImGui one-file / unchanged scan | 2.8 s / 1.9 s | **1.3 s / 0.9 s** | — |
| ImGui skipped functions | 55 (22 in C++ files) | **27** in the project scan, 31 when each file is scanned alone: all Objective-C (27 in `.mm`, 4 in the two Metal `.h` files) | C++ files 0: **met** |
| django recall against `ast` | 100% | **100%** (30,379 of 30,379) | met |
| django one-file `scan --full` | 5.2 s | **1.9 s** by the clock | under 3 s: **met** |
| django unresolved calls | 90.2% | **86.5%** (76,782; "unknown receiver" 82,913 → 49,029; library calls 50,541) | the numbers equal the dev's |
| bevy files in the 12 minute cap | 1,741 | 1,752 (about 2.3 files/s, the same) | — |
| bevy recall against `rustc` | 99.9% | **99.9%** (25,537 of 25,552, 14 files) | — |
| bevy publish of 450 files | 31.7 s | **9.5 s** (the dev: about 17 s at load 20) | 15 s: met on my run, **not** on the dev's |
| bevy tree-sitter parses, 450 files | 4,965 | **2,588** (5.8 a file; equals the dev's number) | under 3 a file: **not met** |
| raylib label | `cpp` | **`c`**: 9,718 functions, `.c` and `.h` both `c`; `search --include c -c` 9,718, `--include cpp -c` 9,718 | met |
| raylib recall against `gcc` | 100% (1,443) | **100%** (1,443 of 1,443; 233 of 245 files compile) | met |
| raylib skipped functions | 8,892 | **135** (`miniaudio.h` 60, `m3d.h` 54, `jar_xm.h` 9) | met |
| raylib `scan --full` | 102 s (publish 143.9 s) | **38 s** for 369 files and 11,757 functions | under 60 s: **met** |
| raylib `init` + first index | 304 s | 174 s at load 20 (the dev: 64.5 s) | — |
| quick lane | 2,614 passed | **2,679 passed, 55 deselected, 1 xfailed, 11 min 18 s**; no failure | — |
| miniaudio against `gcc` with the implementation on | — | **1,977 of 1,990 = 99.3%** (the dev's number) | skipped under 5%: **met** |

**A correction of my own review.** §63 and §65 said `imgui_demo.cpp` 90%, `imgui_internal.h` inline 79% and `imgui.h` 85%, and asked the dev to fix them. I rechecked the misses one by one. They are **not lost functions**: they are constructors and destructors that the compiler *generates* for a class that has none written (`ExampleTreeNode::~ExampleTreeNode`, `ImGuiStorage::ImGuiStorage`, …; `nm -l` attributes them to the class line), and template functions whose symbol name carries a return type (`bool DataTypeClampT`, `char const* ImAtoi`). Real source-level recall of the five core files is **100%** (`imgui_widgets.cpp` 99%, the three templates above). **The dev was right in Task 62.2 and my figures counted compiler artifacts; finding 2 of §63 (header recall) is withdrawn.** The comparison script now cannot count a class that has no constructor written.

**Where the time goes** (by phase, from this review):

| Run | Longest phase | Can it improve? |
|---|---|---|
| ImGui | the first index, 125 s (about 2 parser processes by design; `scan --full` is 9 s) | not worth it; the index is a background job |
| django | the first index, 1,143 s; `scan --full` 143 s | the index is throttled by design (finding 5); a waiting scan is already parallel |
| bevy | publish: `sync_scan_results` 5.8 s of 9.5 s, `_sync_edges` 3.4 s, parsing 3.2 s, `set_config_many` 0.64 s | finish item (a') of §65 (estimate: 74% fewer macro parses, parsing 3.2 s to about 1.5 s) and do one walk per function (estimate 0.5–1 s); the sync is now the larger part |
| raylib | `init` + first index 174 s; `scan --full` 38 s | the sync was 21 s in the dev's measure; acceptable |

## Findings

### 1. The idle worker decides before it checkpoints, so it does not see its free space (medium)

`WorkerIPCServer.compact_if_needed` (`worker_ipc.py:872`) calls `needs_compaction` first and runs `CHECKPOINT` only after that. DuckDB keeps new data in the write-ahead log until a checkpoint (it checkpoints by itself when the log passes about 16 MB), and the file does not show the free blocks until then.

Measured: Dear ImGui in place, default settings, after `init` and `scan --full`, the worker left idle for **15 minutes**: the file stayed **18.4 MB, 4.3% free** (`status`), with a 9.6 MB log. A copy of the same files, checkpointed: **39.6 MB, 42% free**, which is above both `MIN_BYTES` (32 MB) and `FREE_SHARE` (0.30). So the call answered `compact` (nothing to do) and the file was not rebuilt. After a worker stop the file is 39.6 MB, 42% free, and since Task 64.1 **a start no longer compacts**, so it takes a second run and 10 more idle minutes. The dev's django test (237.8 MB to 127.9 MB with the idle time at 5 s) passed because that log had already been checkpointed by its size. This is the "free space stays there" problem the user asked about, left in the small and middle projects.

**Suggested fix:** run `CHECKPOINT` before the question in `compact_if_needed` once the idle time has passed (it is cheap when nothing is waiting), or read the size of the file plus the log. **Acceptance:** an idle worker on ImGui (default 600 s) goes from about 40 MB to under 22 MB, 5% free or less, without a restart; a test with a log below the auto-checkpoint size.

### 2. Rust macro parsing: 5.8 parses a file against under 3 (medium)

`RustParser._macro_calls` (`rust_parser.py:482`) skips text that has neither `(` nor `!` and keeps an exact-text cache; the shape cache and the same-length rewrite `name!(` to `name (` for expression-like macros (§65 item (a')) were not done. 450 bevy files: **2,588 parses (5.8 a file; the dev's number, reproduced)**, 4,965 before. In my profile of the forced publish (9.5 s): tree-sitter itself is 0.8 s, `parse_file` 3.0 s, `sync_scan_results` **5.8 s**. `_local_types`, `_collect_calls` and `_nested` are called 650,000 times for 6,363 functions (item (b), one walk per function, not done). The dev's 17 s for the same publish was measured at load 20; mine, 9.5 s, at a lower load later in the run, so the real gain is between 1.9x and 3.3x. Edges are unchanged (the small cases keep the edge to `project_fn` through `assert_eq!`, `println!` and `vec!`; a method call inside a macro is found).

**Acceptance:** under 3 parses a file on the same 450 files with the stored edges equal; publish under 15 s on the dev's load.

### 3. 13 functions of `miniaudio.h` are lost, and the 60 skipped entries have no name (low)

With `MINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION` defined `gcc` defines 1,990 functions in the file, drspec finds 1,977. The 13: `ma_default_vfs_*__stdio` (8), `ma_*__nix` (2), `ma_dr_flac__full_read_and_close_*` (3). Not the double underscore (the same names alone between two sentinel functions are read, also in both branches of an `#if`). The three `ma_dr_flac__full_read_and_close_*` are produced by a macro and cannot be found by text; the others are real definitions in a region the tree reads as an error. The 60 skipped entries are all named "(a definition the parser could not read)" with only a line (`scanner.py:496`), so I could not match the 13 to them. **Acceptance:** each skipped entry carries the name the text before its brace gives, when there is one (a user can then tell what is missing).

### 4. Per-file records: 46.1 MB on django, target under 7 MB (low; the dev says it was not done)

The imports are out of them (Task 64.2), the rest is unchanged: 46.1 MB (dev's figure; I did not re-measure on django). On ImGui the "per-file facts" are 4.1 MB of 17.6 MB used (23%). **Acceptance** as in §65 finding 4: under 7 MB on django, with the same incremental re-resolution (same edges after a one-file change).

### 5. The background index runs on one parser when the machine is busy, and nothing says so (low)

`parser_count` (`core/parse_pool.py:141`) takes `worker.cpu_percent` (20%: 2 parsers on 12 cores) and **one** while `pause_reason` says "machine busy", that is when the 1-minute load per core is above `embedding.max_load_per_core` (1.0, a load of 12 here). My django and bevy runs were at load 17–21, so they most likely ran on one parser, and django's index took 1,143 s (683 s earlier at a lower load) and its `scan --full` 143 s (117 s; a waiting scan has no load check). **This is an inference:** the parser count was not logged, so I do not claim a regression. Rates measured: ImGui 12 files/s and 505 functions/s in `scan --full`, django 21 and 234, raylib 10 and 309, the background index of django 2.7 files/s, bevy 2.3 files/s (34 functions/s). **Suggested:** `status` shows `parsers: N (machine busy)` while the backlog drains. **Acceptance:** the line appears, and the test checks it for a busy and an idle machine.

## Decisions to look at (not defects)

- **Task 63.1 does not follow decision 4 of §64 to the letter.** The user said no migration was needed ("we can clean and scan again"); the dev relabelled in place (`db/c_label.py`, one statement at the first worker start, hash of `c` equal to the hash of `cpp`), so no identifier or contract changes. That is safer, costs one statement, and I saw it work on raylib; the dev says so openly. I accept it, but the user should know that the answer was not followed literally.
- **A C header far from its sources is labelled `cpp`** (Task 63 "Not done"). Accepted by decision 1.
- Objective-C stays out of scope: 31 ImGui functions (the two Metal headers included, which the dev's text does not mention) and the `.mm` files remain skipped, by the user's decision.
- The dev's compaction tests inject the failure and count the answers; the latency of a read during a rebuild is not measured (the dev says so).

## Order

Finding 1 first (a fix of a few lines, and it is the user's open question about free space), then 2 (the one acceptance of §65 that the dev marked not met), then 5 (cheap), then 3 and 4.

## Decision (the user, after this review): remove the automatic relabel of Task 63.1

The user decided that **no migration code is wanted for the `c` label**; an older database is rescanned when needed.

**For the dev:**
- Remove `src/drspec/db/c_label.py` (`label_c_files`, the marker `schema:c_label`), its import and its call in `staleness_worker.py`, and the tests of it (`TestARelabelledDatabase` in `tests/unit/test_c_support.py`).
- **Keep** the part of Task 63.1 that makes a rescan work: the sync compares the stored language with the new one, so a `scan --full` of an older database relabels its `.c` and `.h` functions by itself. Without it the user would have to delete `_drspec`.
- Say in the task text how an older database is brought up to date (`drspec scan --full`, or delete `_drspec` and scan again), and put one line in the release notes or `README`.
- **Acceptance:** an older database (language `cpp` for `.c` files) scanned with `scan --full` ends with those functions as `c` and the same identifiers, hashes and contracts as before; no code path reads `schema:c_label`; the quick lane passes.


# 67. Feedback: Review of Task 65 (§66) — The Idle Worker Gives Back Half of Django's File, Per-File Records Are 6.6 MB, a Scan on a Quiet Machine Is 3–7 Times Faster; 13 miniaudio Functions Are Still Lost Without a Report (Implemented — see Task 66)

**Status: implemented (Task 66; parts not done are listed there).**

## Method

Task 65 is marked [x]; the dev's test runs were finished (the newest file change in `src/` or `tests/` was 15:29). `pip install -e . --no-deps -q`, then in place in the clones the user allowed, one after another at `nice -n 10` (`.gitignore` saved outside the project first and restored, only `_drspec` removed, checked with `ps` and `ls`: no worker and no `_drspec` left in imgui, raylib, django, bevy): Dear ImGui, **django with the worker left idle for 14 minutes at the default `worker.compact_idle_seconds` (600)**, bevy (no cap needed: the whole project indexed) with a profile of 450 files, raylib against `gcc`, then the quick lane. Extra checks without a project: the 29 small cases of §61–§65 with `Scanner` (all pass), the Rust fast path against the parse path on 2,204 files, the miniaudio functions against `gcc -DMINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION`, and a throwaway project for the relabel removal. **The machine was at load 3–4 this time (17–21 in the last review)**, so these times are not comparable with §66 as they are; the ratio between the two is itself a result (finding 5 of §66).

## Where the time goes

| Run | Longest phase | Can it improve? |
|---|---|---|
| ImGui, raylib | the first index (24 s, 120 s) | no; it is a background job |
| django | the first index 159 s (`scan --full` 56 s: parse in parallel, then one serial publish) | the publish; not profiled in this review |
| bevy publish (450 files) | sync 4.3 s of 6.3 s: `_sync_edges` 2.7 s, 1,568 `get_config` calls 0.3 s, `asdict` of the import records 0.3 s | batch the `get_config` reads and cache the import dictionary (estimate 0.5 s of 6.3 s); `_sync_edges` and the Rust resolver (`lookup`, `_solve`, `_evaluate` about 0.8 s) are the next target (estimate: half of it by one walk per function) |

## Findings

### 1. 13 functions of `miniaudio.h` are lost, and not one of the 17 skipped entries is any of them (low–medium)

With `MINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION` defined, `gcc` defines 1,990 functions in the file; drspec finds 1,977 (99.3%, status `partial`). The 17 skipped entries now carry names (`ma_IActivateAudioInterfaceAsyncOperation_*` ×4, `*__coreaudio` ×3, `ma_context_init__webaudio`, `ma_job_process__resource_manager__*` ×9) — a good change. **The 13 that are missing are in none of them**: `ma_default_vfs_*__stdio` ×8 (lines 61373–61534), `ma_context_init_backend_apis__nix` and its `uninit` (42976–42983), and `ma_dr_flac__full_read_and_close_*` ×3 (92452–92454, produced by a macro). So they are a silent loss, which is what the user objected to in §60. The first ten sit in the `#else` branch of an outer `#if` (line 61372) whose first branch has a nested `#if/#else` inside a function; a small file of that shape with sentinels is read correctly, so I did not find the cause.

**Acceptance:** every function that the compiler defines and drspec lacks is either found or in the skipped list by name; on raylib `miniaudio.h` the 13 are found or reported (the 17 may stay).

### 2. The token fast path does what the acceptance asked, and saves almost no time (low; a note on my own estimate)

Parses fell as planned (macro texts 2,013 to 662, **2.7 parses a file**) with calls equal on 2,204 files, so it is safe. But on the same 450 files in one process, four runs each in alternating order, the scan takes **2.22 s with it and 2.34 s without** (about 0.1 s). §65 and §66 estimated "parsing 3.2 s to about 1.5 s"; that **assumed the time follows the parse count, and it does not**: the exact-text cache of Task 64.3 had already removed the repeats, and a macro text is small. The cost left in a publish is the sync (4.3 s of 6.3 s), not the parsing (1.6 s with the pool). I would keep the code (57 tests, no differences), but **the next Rust work should be the sync and the resolver, not the macros**.

### 3. `m3d.h`: 54 of the 81 skipped functions are C++ wrapper methods in a C project (low)

`m3d.h` has its C++ wrapper under `#ifdef __cplusplus` (lines 6323–6572); a C compile never sees it, yet it counts as 54 lost functions in `status` and in the scan line (`Functions Skipped: 81`). I checked the position of the block, not each of the 54 names. **Acceptance:** functions in a block that the label's language does not compile (a `__cplusplus` block of a `c` file) are not counted as lost.

## What the dev's text says and what I measured

- 65.1: the dev's ImGui rebuild "did not occur" because the file stayed under 32 MB; true here too (ImGui 18.9 MB before stop). **The case is shown on django instead** (164.9 MB to 90.7 MB at default settings): finding 1 of §66 is closed.
- 65.4: "I did not run the full django index": done here; the figure holds, 6.6 MB in the database.
- 65.5: `parsers: 11` shows after a scan; the "machine busy" line is covered by the unit tests only.
- Not done by the dev and still open: one walk per function; a read's latency during a rebuild (not measured by me either).

## Order

Finding 1 first (a silent loss), then 3 (a number that misleads), then the sync and resolver as the next performance work (finding 2).


# 68. Feedback: Review of Task 66 (§67) — The Lost miniaudio Functions Are Found and the C++ Blocks Are Gone From the Count, but One Function Now Has a 5,135-Line Body and the Other-Branch Reading Costs 28–39% More Parse Time (Implemented — see Task 67)

## Method

Extra checks with `Scanner` on files and no project: miniaudio against `gcc -DMINIAUDIO_IMPLEMENTATION`; the functions that the new reading adds, found by switching `CppParser._other_branch_texts` off in the test process and comparing (every added function checked against the text of its own lines); the same on all raylib and ImGui files; the time of the reading on and off (two runs each, alternating); the header label with a fresh `Scanner` for each case; 29 small cases of §61–§65 (all pass). Two stray folders of my own review (`/tmp/rvDU…`, `/tmp/rvNF…`, and `/tmp/rvIM_obj`) that the dev found had broken a test (Task 66.4) were removed by me; my scripts now clean up after themselves. Load was 3–4 (a quiet machine), as in §67; some of my own checks ran while the raylib step was timed, so its times are not a clean comparison.

## What was verified and works

| | §67 | Now | Acceptance |

**66.2 (the two sync savings).** The dev measured 5.1 s to 4.5 s of sync in one process. On the same 450 files my profile shows `sync_scan_results` **4.29 s to 4.24 s** and the wall time 6.29 s to 6.17 s (about 1–2%), `set_config_many` 0.29 s, `refresh_contexts` 0.45 s. The edges and every count in the status are unchanged, so it is safe; **the saving I see is much smaller than the dev's**, which may be load or the profiler, and I would not count on more than a few percent. The cost that is left is `_sync_edges` (2.9 s of the 6.2 s) and the pool wait for the parse (1.6 s).

## Findings

### 1. A function that the other branches add can get the wrong extent: one now holds 116 other functions (medium)

`ma_context_init__coreaudio` in `miniaudio.h` was a skipped entry in §67 and is now one function with the lines **36518–41652 (5,135 lines, 223,532 characters)** that contain **116 other functions** and end in an unrelated function (`return MA_SUCCESS; }`). `source get` returns 223 KB, a search for any of the 116 functions also finds it, its hash changes whenever any of them changes, and the calls of all of them are credited to it. The dev's text says the `coreaudio`/`webaudio` functions are "reported but not read": this one is read, wrongly. A second case: `ma_atomic_compare_and_swap_8` (line 16317) has a body that starts inside the macro before it (`_asm__ __volatile__(...`). On raylib **2 of the 138** added functions are wrong, on ImGui **0 of 58**; the other 136 bodies equal the file text at their own lines (two `RGFW.h` declarations that my first-line test flagged are false alarms: `__declspec(dllimport)` stands in front).

**Acceptance:** no function of `miniaudio.h`, raylib or ImGui contains more than 3 other functions' lines unless it really does (a test over the three files); `ma_context_init__coreaudio` is either read with its real extent (about 1,400 lines, my estimate from its neighbours' sizes) or **skipped by name**, as the dev's text says; the body of every added function starts at its own first line (the same test).

### 2. The other-branch reading costs 28–39% more parse time on a project with many `#if` (low–medium)

The dev timed only `miniaudio.h` (11.7 s to 18.7 s). **Serial parse of all 369 raylib files in one process, the reading off and on, two runs each in alternating order: 40.3 s and 55.9 s (+39%)**; the texts were made for **58 files**; `miniaudio.h` is only 6.4 s of the 15.6 s extra, then `RGFW.h` 1.3 s, `dr_flac.h` 0.7 s, `glad.h` 0.7 s. **ImGui (96 files): 6.6 s and 8.45 s (+28%)**, 20 files (`imgui.cpp` 0.6 s, `imgui_draw.cpp` 0.5 s). In return: 138 functions on raylib (+1.2%) and 58 on ImGui (+1.3%), all real. Parsing runs in parallel inside a real scan, so a scan is slower by less than that; I did not measure the scan itself (my other checks ran at the same time as the raylib step). Whether the price is right is the user's decision; it is paid on every scan of a file that has an `#if` crossing a brace.

**Suggestion (estimate, not tested):** reuse the tree of the first reading for the branches that differ only after the first conditional (one parse per *different* branch, not per level), or read the other branches only for the stretches where the first reading found an error (the check that reports a loss already knows the line). **Acceptance:** the same functions found on raylib and ImGui (138 and 58), with the serial parse of raylib under +20% (about 48 s).

## What the dev says and what I measured

- 66.1: "17 of the 30 are functions a macro defines": I did not count them separately; the 30 and the named 6 of `miniaudio.h` I did see.
- 66.2: see above (my saving is 1–2%, theirs 12%).
- 66.3, 66.4: reproduced.
- Not done by the dev and still open: one walk per function; the `gcc` comparison after the change (done here: 99.85%); the time on other files (done here: finding 2).

## Order

Finding 1 first (a wrong function in a tool whose job is to give the right text), then finding 2 (the user decides whether the price is right).


# 69. Proposal: `drspec init` Starts the Indexing, and `init --scan` Runs It at Full Speed (the User's Decisions) (Implemented — see Task 67)

**Status: implemented (Task 67; the 45-60 s estimate is not met, see 67.3).**

## What exists, measured and read

- **`drspec init` does the setup only** (`cli/commands/init.py::init_command`): the `_drspec` folder, the database and its schema, the agent templates, `.gitignore`, the IDE files. It starts **no worker and no scan** (a throwaway project: 0 workers 3 s after `init`; 1 worker 3 s after the first `drspec status`). The worker starts at the next command that needs it, and then its own periodic pass (`run_one_pass` → `discover_new_files`) queues every file with the background reasons (`new_file`, `new_file_recent`). A user who runs `init` and leaves gets **no index until some later command or MCP call**.
- **That index is throttled by design**: `parser_count` takes `worker.cpu_percent` (20% of the whole machine: about 2 parsers on 12 cores, 1 when the machine is busy). A waiting scan (`drspec scan`, reason `scan`) runs flat out: all cores but one (`scan.workers`), no load check, first in the queue (`_RESCAN_PRIORITY_SQL`).
- **Measured (bevy, 2,014 files, quiet machine):** the throttled first index 127 s; `scan --full` of the same files 43 s. django 149–159 s and 50–56 s. On a busy machine the throttled index is about 7 times slower (django 1,143 s).
- **`scan` already has the wait switch**: it waits by default; `--no-wait` queues the files in the background lane and returns at once.

## Decisions (the user)

1. **`drspec init` starts the worker**, so the throttled background index begins right away, with no second command ("the default is throttled").
2. **`drspec init --scan`** runs a **waiting scan** after the setup: flat out, the live progress line of §55, Ctrl-C cancels the scan (the setup stays), and the command ends with the result line, as `drspec scan` does.
3. **`--no-wait` works as in `scan`**: `init --scan --no-wait` queues the files in the background lane and returns at once. Without `--scan`, `--no-wait` is not needed (the plain `init` already does not wait).
4. `scan` is unchanged.

## Work

- **W1.** After the setup (fresh `init` and re-`init`), start the worker through the same call the other commands use. A failure to start is a **warning in the result, not a failed `init`**. Add a way to skip it for tests and CI (suggestion: `--no-index`, and the environment variable the tests set). The worker does not exit when idle (`IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` is infinite; I did not check how a worker is stopped otherwise), so every `init` in a test folder would leave one: the test fixtures must stop them.
- **W2.** `--scan` calls the same scan code as `drspec scan` for the whole project (no new worker code). Options of `scan` (`--deep-scan`, `--embed`, `--code-only`, `--docs-only`) stay on `scan`; the success message says so.
- **W3.** The MCP `init` tool gets the same two options; as the MCP `scan` it waits up to 60 s and then leaves the scan running.
- **W4.** The result of `init` says what happens: "indexing started in the background (about 20% of the machine); `drspec scan` finishes it at full speed" (or "scan finished: N files, M functions").
- **W5.** `init --help`, `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`, and the sentence in §55 that names the throttled index.

## Acceptance

- After `drspec init` alone, on a throwaway project, a worker runs within 5 s and the artifact count rises **with no other command** (a test with `ps` and `status`).
- `init --scan` returns after all files are indexed; `status` during the run shows `parsers: N-1`; the total is close to `scan --full` (bevy: about 45–60 s, an estimate not yet measured, against 127 s).
- `init --scan --no-wait` returns in a few seconds and the files are queued as background.
- Ctrl-C during `init --scan` cancels the scan and leaves the project initialised.
- `init` on an already initialised project scans only when `--scan` is given.
- The quick lane passes and no worker is left by the tests.
