## The profile of repeated work

**No file is parsed twice** (`scan_file` calls equal the number of files in every forced publish, 1 and 450, on both projects), and no function is parsed twice. What repeats is what is built from the whole project on every publish, and a few queries that run once per item.

| | bevy (2,014 files, 27,356 functions) | django (2,943 files, 33,512 functions) |
|---|---|---|
| Forced publish of **one file**, in process | **0.73 s**: `refresh_contexts` 0.53 s (`_read_inputs` 0.24 s: 2,020 `loads`, 2,023 `dumps` of the per-file records, 27,356 `repr`), `symbols_hash` 0.06 s | **0.63–0.71 s** (1.9 s on the busy machine): `refresh_contexts` 0.35 s (`_read_inputs` 0.12 s: 3,056 `loads`, 3,059 `dumps`), `build_classes_by_name` 0.10 s, `symbols_hash` 0.07 s (+ `_symbol_facts` 0.05 s) |
| Share that scales with the project, not with the change | about **75%** (0.55 of 0.73 s) | about **80%** (0.55 of 0.7 s) |
| Forced publish of **450 files** | 7.14 s (6.2 s in the last review: other load) | **3.72 s** |
| Repeated SQL in the 450-file publish | `SELECT value FROM config WHERE key = ?` **1,568 times, 0.34 s**; `SELECT code_hash ... WHERE function_id = ?` **684 times, 0.16 s** | `SELECT DISTINCT file_path FROM artifacts WHERE file_path LIKE ... OR ...` **320 times, 0.65 s** (`project_facts.py:246`); `SELECT function_name, function_id, file_path FROM artifacts WHERE parent IS NULL AND function_id NOT IN (...)` **216 times, 0.40 s** (`project_facts.py:283`) |
| One idle pass in process (what the worker does every `worker.poll_interval`) | 0.17 s (0.45 s the first time: imports and settings) | **0.43 s**, of which `discover_new_files` 0.38 s: `list_project_files` walks the whole tree (3,055 `_walk_all` calls) each pass |
| Worker CPU while idle | 3.0 CPU s in 181 s (about 1 s a minute, 1.7% of a core) | flat at 60 s after the index finished, then about +1 s in 100 s (the sampling step is 1 s) |

My two django runs both ended their wait early (the backlog read zero for a moment), so the first part of their "idle" samples is indexing; the figures above are from after it stopped growing. I did not run the django one-file publish for the second time with the new §69 code.

## Findings (all in `ReportFindings`, with estimates marked)

1. **A one-file publish recomputes the project (medium).** `refresh_contexts` (`provenance.py:131`), `symbols_hash` and `build_classes_by_name` read every file's record and every function each time: about 0.55 s of 0.7 s. Estimate, not tested: store the context input and the symbol hash per file and recompute the changed file and its neighbours; a one-file publish near 0.2–0.3 s. This is the next step after the sync savings of 66.2 (which gave 1–2%). **Acceptance:** a one-file forced publish on django under 0.3 s in process with the same contexts, symbol hash and edges (bytes equal) as the full recompute, tested on a change of one file and of one signature.
2. **The same project-wide query runs once per file (medium).** 320 + 216 queries cost 1.05 s of 3.72 s (28%) in a 450-file publish on django. **Acceptance:** each runs once per publish (the module path index and the functions-by-name map built once and reused), the same edges, and the 450-file publish under 2.8 s (estimate).
3. **One read per item (low).** 1,568 config reads and 684 hash reads on bevy: 0.5 s of 7.1 s. **Acceptance:** `IN` queries, the same results.
4. **Every idle pass walks the tree (low).** 0.38 s of a 0.43 s pass on django; at a 60 s poll about 0.7% of a core. A directory-mtime check or a file watch would remove it (estimate). **Acceptance:** an idle pass on django under 0.1 s with a new file still found within one poll.
5. **Prototypes (low–medium).** See above. **Acceptance:** a macro-prefixed prototype (`STBIDEF void f(int);`) in a header is indexed as a declaration like any other, and the three lost ones return.

## Order

Findings 1 and 2 (most of the time of the common case, one changed file), then 3 and 4, then 5.


# 71. Feedback: Review of Task 67 (§69, `init` Starts the Indexing) — It Works as Decided and Ctrl-C Behaves, but `init --scan` Is 1.3–2 Times Faster Than the Throttled Index, Not 3 Times (Implemented — see Task 68)

**Status: implemented (Task 68).**

## Method

Task 67 is marked [x]; no dev test run was active and `src/` and `tests/` had not changed in the last 30 minutes. `pip install -e . --no-deps -q`. **`init` was run on throwaway projects only** (never on the clones): 40 small Python files; 283 files of django (`db`, `utils`, `core`); and two full copies of django (3,054 files, 33,512 functions, no `.git`); each project was stopped (`worker stop`, then `ps`) and deleted afterwards. Quiet machine (load about 2). The branch checks of §68 were repeated on raylib and ImGui for 67.1. The quick lane and the slow tests of `test_init_indexing.py`.

## What was verified

| Claim | Measured |
|---|---|
| `init` starts the worker, and the index fills with no other command | **yes**: it returned in **1.9 s**, one worker running, 80 functions of the 40 files in the index 3 s later, the result says "indexing started in the background (about 20% of the machine); 'drspec scan' finishes it at full speed" |
| A second `init` on an initialised project | no changes, the worker is not started twice (`Already Running: Yes`); the message still says "indexing started" (finding 1) |
| `init --scan` runs the scan at full speed and waits | **yes**: `parsers: 11` in `status` all the time, ends with "Scanned 283 file(s), found 4,942 function(s)", exit 0; 113 s on the full django copy |
| `init --scan --no-wait` | **returned in 2.3 s** with "283 file(s) queued; the worker reads them in the background"; all indexed a few seconds later at `parsers: 2` |
| `--scan --no-index` refused before anything is set up | **yes**: exit 1, `INVALID_INPUT`, no `_drspec` created |
| `--no-index` and `DRSPEC_DISABLE_AUTO_WORKER=1` start nothing | **yes**: exit 0, 0 workers |
| Ctrl-C during `init --scan` | **exit 130**, the project stays initialised; 12 s into the scan of the full copy the queue held 2,254 `scan` items and 5,781 functions; after Ctrl-C the `scan` items were gone and the **background index finished the project** (new files queued as `new_file_recent`, `parsers: 2`; 33,512 functions about 125 s later, in two discovery passes). So Ctrl-C ends the flat-out scan, and indexing carries on at the throttled speed |
| MCP `init` has `scan`, `no_wait`, `no_index` | **yes** in the code (`mcp_server.py:477`, `_begin_indexing`); not run (the MCP server of this session runs older code) |
| 67.1 (the declarations in `#else` branches of a C file) | raylib **11,893 functions** (the dev's number), 139 added by the other branches, **0 holding another function, 0 not in the file text**; parse time **+5%** (the dev +6%; acceptance +20%); ImGui +58 functions, **+18%** (the dev +11%); miniaudio 1,987 of 1,990 as before. The three `stb_image.h` prototypes of §70 finding 5 are **still lost** (not part of this task) |
| Tests | quick lane **2,802 passed, 62 deselected, 1 xfailed, 8 min 51 s**, no failure; the 4 slow tests of the init file **pass (26 s)**; no worker left by them |

## About my own estimate

§69 said `init --scan` on bevy would take "about 45–60 s (an estimate not yet measured)". **That was wrong.** The 43 s of `scan --full` in §67 is a re-publish of an indexed project. A first scan is slower because the publish is serial: on the django copy `init --scan` took **113 s** against about 150 s for the throttled index (measured earlier) and 46 s for a later `scan --full`; the dev's bevy numbers are 130 s against 263 s. So `init --scan` is **1.3 to 2 times faster** than waiting for the background index, and it is not faster than `drspec scan`, which it uses.

## Findings (in `ReportFindings`)

1. **Wording (low).** `indexing.py:15`: the message is the same on every `init`, also when the worker was already running. **Acceptance:** when `Already Running` is true the message says so ("indexing is already running"), the test checks both.
2. **The Ctrl-C test is weak (low, the dev says so in 67.4).** `test_ctrl_c_during_init_scan_...` (`test_init_indexing.py:287`) accepts exit 130, 0, 1 or a signal exit and checks only that `_drspec` exists. **Acceptance:** on a project large enough that the scan is still running when the signal arrives (a few hundred larger files, or a hook that stops after N files) the test checks exit 130, that no `scan` item stays in the queue, and that the files not yet read are indexed by the background lane afterwards (what I saw by hand).
3. **The first scan is dominated by the serial publish (idea).** 113 s for 3,054 files against 46 s for the same files re-published. I did not profile the first publish: it is the next place to look for repeated work, as in §70 (the one-file publish spends about 75% on work that scales with the project). **Acceptance:** a profile of the first publish with the same table as §70 and the biggest repeated cost removed.

## Order

Finding 3 is where time is left for the user (a first scan is the first thing a new project does); 1 and 2 are small.


# 72. Feedback: Profile of the First Scan and of Real Edits — The First Scan Is 4 Times Slower Than It Needs to Be, and Any Change to the Set of Functions Republishes the Whole Project (20 s on bevy) (Implemented in part — see Task 68)

**Status: implemented in part (Task 68): finding 1 (the batch size) and finding 3 (for the names without `_`) are done; findings 2 and 4 (an edit republishes the project, the contexts) are done for every language but Rust and JavaScript; see 68.1 to 68.3 for what is not met.** Requested by the user ("the first scan is the next place to profile").

## Method

The first scan was profiled **in one process with the worker's own code**: `scan_service.start_scan` queues every file with the reason `scan` (as `drspec scan` does), then `run_one_pass(detect=False)` is repeated until the queue is empty (what the worker loop does), under `cProfile`, with every SQL statement counted by its normalised text, the `sync_scan_results` and `refresh_contexts` calls timed per pass, and the real parser pool (parsing runs in separate processes, so the profile of the main process shows the publish and the wait). In place on django and bevy for the first scan (`init --no-index`, nothing else; `_drspec` removed and `.gitignore` restored afterwards), and on **copies** (the clones are never written) for the edits. Quiet machine (load 2–3). The first run of the script parsed serially, because `DRSPEC_DISABLE_AUTO_WORKER` also turns the pool off; its totals (124.8 s on django) are used only for the shares, the rest are runs with the pool.

## Where the first scan goes

| django, 3,054 files, 33,512 functions (batches of 200, the default) | seconds | share |
|---|---|---|
| Whole first scan, pool on | **106.8** (the real `init --scan`: 113 s) | |
| Publish (`sync_scan_results`) | 94.4 | **88%** |
| of which `refresh_contexts` | 13.5 | 13% |
| Parsing (serial in the first profile: 26.5 s; with the pool a few seconds) | — | the rest |

**The cost of a pass grows with the index**, for the same 200 files: pass 1 0.8 s, pass 3 2.0 s, pass 9 7.7 s, pass 15 11.6 s; the publish alone is 0.3 s in pass 1 and 10.5 s in pass 15. The total is quadratic in the number of files. The last pass, only 54 files, still costs 10.3 s on django: **a fixed cost of about 10 s per publish at full size** (bevy: 18 files, 21 s).

| Batch size (`worker.rescan_budget`) | django (first scan) | bevy (first scan, 2,018 files, 27,356 functions) |
|---|---|---|
| 200 (default) | **106.8 s**, 16 passes | **133.4 s** (the dev: 130 s), 11 passes; contexts 68.3 s (**51%**) |
| 1,000 (the allowed maximum) | 48.0 s, 4 passes | 62.5 s, 3 passes |
| One batch (my script ignores the limit) | **25.5 s** (publish 14.9 s) | **31.6 s** (publish 26.4 s, contexts 12.3 s) |

The numbers of functions are the same in every run (33,512 and 27,356). **Not checked:** edges, references, hashes and contexts between the batch sizes, and the peak memory of one batch.

Inside the publish on django (first profile, 124.8 s): the **name and import queries of `project_facts.py`** (`_guess_module_file_path`, `_functions_by_name_in_package`, `_guess_package_reexport_class_file`) ran **19,259 times and cost 31.8 s (25%)**, each one a `LIKE` / `NOT IN` scan of the artifacts table (10,203 × 1.8 ms, 6,606 × 1.4 ms, 2,450 × 1.5 ms), so they grow with the index; `_may_resolve_differently` + `_restore_function` 249,872 calls 6.0 s (a walk over every function already indexed, each batch); `refresh_contexts` 13.5 s; `_sync_references` 7.6 s; `file_records.unpack` 24,000 calls 6.9 s; `insert_rows` and the transactions about 6 s (the writes that are really needed).

## The cost of real edits (copies, first scan with batches of 1,000, then one change each)

| Edit | django | bevy |
|---|---|---|
| Nothing changed (`start_scan` only) | 0.43 s (0.37 s of it listing the tree) | 0.50 s |
| A comment in a function body (no new symbol) | 1.27 s | **32.2 s** (contexts 18.0 s, `build_rust_index` 9.2 s, 2,020 single config reads 7.6 s) |
| One new function in a file | **6.33 s** | **43.6 s** (`resolve_rust_calls` 27,352 calls 14.4 s, contexts 15.6 s) |
| One new file with one function | 6.58 s | 21.3 s |
| That file deleted | 5.42 s | 10.7 s |
| Ten files, a new function in each | 6.95 s | 20.3 s |
| A rename of a function used in many files (`__init__`, 285 files) / (`default`, 1,263 files) | 9.15 s | 21.4 s |

So the cost of a publish does not follow the size of the change: **ten files cost the same as one**, and a comment costs 25 times more on bevy than the 0.73 s I measured in §70 (that was a forced re-publish of an unchanged file, which takes a short cut). Every change to the set of functions makes the publish walk the whole project: django 33,460 `_may_resolve_differently` and `_restore_function` calls plus `refresh_contexts` 1.3 s; bevy `resolve_rust_calls` for all 27,352 functions plus `refresh_contexts` 11 s. A worker that publishes once per pass while a user edits spends about 20 s of one core on each pass on bevy.

## Findings (all in `ReportFindings`, estimates marked)

1. **The first scan publishes in batches of 200 (high).** One batch is 4.2 times faster on both projects; batches of 1,000 are 2.2 times faster with no code change but are the allowed maximum. **Suggestion:** a waiting scan publishes once (parse all files in the pool, then one publish), or takes batches as large as memory allows; `worker.rescan_budget` stays for the background drain. **Acceptance:** `init --scan` of django under 35 s and of bevy under 45 s (quiet machine), with the same functions, edges, references, hashes and contexts as the batches of 200 (compared byte for byte), and a note of the peak memory.
2. **A change to the set of functions republishes the whole project (high).** **Suggestion (estimate, not tested):** keep a map from a called name to the calls that mention it; when a function is added, removed or renamed, re-resolve only those calls (and the neighbours); recompute contexts for the changed files and what reads them (§70 finding 1). Expected one new function: about 1–2 s on django, 2–5 s on bevy. **Acceptance:** one new function, one new file and one delete publish in under 2 s on django and under 6 s on bevy, with edges, references, hashes and contexts equal to a full recompute (tests for add, remove, rename and a body-only change), and ten files no more than twice the time of one.
3. **The name and import queries scan the artifacts table 19,000 times (medium).** **Acceptance:** each query family runs once per batch against a map built in memory; the first scan of django at batches of 200 loses at least 20 s (estimate 25 s).
4. **Contexts are rebuilt per publish (medium).** 51% of bevy's first scan at batches of 200. Solved with finding 2 and §70 finding 1. **Acceptance** as in §70.

## Order

Finding 1 first (the smallest change, 4 times on the first scan, which is also what `init --scan` does), then 2 and 4 together (they share the per-file context input), then 3.

# 73. Feedback: Review of Task 68 (§70, §71, §72) — The First Scan Is 26 s on Django and 32 s on Bevy, and the Filter on Re-resolution Gives the Same Graph as a Fresh Index in 42 Edit Steps; Ctrl-C Now Waits for the Whole Batch, Deleted Files Leave Rows Behind, and a Rust Edit Is Still 13 s (Implemented in part — see Task 69)

**Status: implemented in part (Task 69): findings 1, 2 and 4 done; finding 3 (Rust) not done, see the note under 69.3.** Requested by the user ("dev finished, review").

## What was checked and how

**The quick lane** (`python3 -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider -rf tests`): **2,827 passed, 62 deselected, 1 xfailed, 15 min 47 s** (the dev's count). The slow lane was not run.

**The first scan, through the real CLI and a real worker**, in place on the django and bevy clones (`.gitignore` saved and restored, only `_drspec` removed; `drspec init --no-index`, then the time of `drspec scan`; machine load 5): **django 26.3 s** (acceptance under 35 s; the dev measured 28.3 s), **bevy 32.2 s** (under 45 s; the dev 31.0 s). The counts are those of before: **33,512 functions, 9,674 calls, 443 references** (django), **27,356 functions, 42,596 calls** (bevy). The memory I sampled (the worker and its parser processes, matching by project path) peaked at 624 MB and 577 MB; the dev's figures are 1,150 and 1,032 MB, so my sampling is likely to miss part of the parser processes and **I do not confirm the memory figure**.

**The filter on re-resolution (68.2b) against a fresh index** — the main risk of Task 68, because a filter that wrongly says "this file does not mention the changed name" loses an edge and nothing complains. I built small throwaway projects, scanned them, applied an edit, scanned again (the incremental result), copied the tree and indexed the copy from nothing (the fresh result), and compared **functions, calls, references and every config row** (contexts, per-file records, imports, mentions). **42 edit steps** in five projects:

| Project | Steps | What the steps were |
|---|---|---|
| Python, 16 steps | module imported before it existed; a base class added later; a star-import target; a relative import target; a re-export added to `__init__.py` (no new symbol); an attribute-call target; a decorator; a name that was a global call; a dotted import; delete the callee; rename; a second function with the same name (ambiguity); move a method to a parent class; remove the star module's function; a body that gains a call; delete the base class |
| Python, 6 steps | delete a caller, change its callee's callee while it is absent, re-add it **byte-identical** (the case where a stale signature could keep a stale context); the same one level further |
| C++, 9 steps | a declaration gets its definition later; a call to a function defined in a file added later; a method body in a file added later; overloads; delete the definition; change the declaration's parameters; delete and re-add with another body |
| JavaScript, 6 steps | a module imported before it existed; a function behind a re-export; a method added; a renamed export; a deletion; a body-only edit |
| Rust, 5 steps | a module added later; a function added; a body that gains a call; delete; re-add with another name |

**Result: functions, calls, references and contexts are the same as a fresh index in all 42 steps.** The filter does not lose an edge in anything I tried. (Only three kinds of config row differed, below.)

**The edit costs** (the copies of §72, in process, the same `edit_scan.py`, one worker pass loop under `cProfile`; load 5 to 7):

| Edit | django (§72 → now) | bevy (§72 → now) |
|---|---|---|
| nothing changed | 0.43 → 0.53 s | 0.20 s |
| a body (my "comment") | 1.27 → 1.42 s | 32.2 → 8.8 s (see the note) |
| one new function | 6.33 → **1.76 s** | 43.6 → **13.2 s** |
| one new file | 6.58 → **1.92 s** | 21.3 → **12.6 s** |
| the file deleted | 5.42 → **1.69 s** | 10.7 → **11.9 s** |
| ten files, one new function in each | 6.95 → **2.20 s** (1.25 times one function) | 20.3 → **13.2 s** |
| a function renamed that 285 (django) or 1,263 (bevy) files name | 9.15 → 3.77 s | 21.4 → 21.3 s |

Django meets the acceptance of §70 and §72 (a new function, a new file and a delete under 2 s; ten files within twice one). **Bevy does not** (a new function 13.2 s against 6 s): `_sync_edges` is 8.6 s and `resolve_rust_calls` 5.9 s of it (all 27,352 functions, every time), as the dev said in 68.2. Contexts, which were 15.6 s, are 0.7 s.

*Note on the bevy "comment" row:* in my script the edit inserts `  # edited` after a line of the function; in Rust that is not a comment (it is the start of an attribute), so it is a real body change and a broken one, in a function that many others reach, which is why the contexts cost 6.6 s. The dev's 0.84 s for a comment edit is a different edit. Neither figure is a measurement of a comment in Rust.

## Findings

1. **Ctrl-C on a first scan now waits for the whole scan, and then reports "cancelled" with everything indexed (medium; it is the trade the dev disclosed in 68.1, here measured).** On django, `drspec scan`, Ctrl-C after 8 s: the command exited **130 after 18.6 s**, and a minute later the project had **all 33,512 functions** and nothing pending. With one batch, the batch in flight is the whole project. The progress line also does not move while the batch runs. **Suggestion:** (a) on Ctrl-C the client returns at once and the worker finishes the batch in the background (the work is not lost either way), or (b) the batch is cut so that Ctrl-C has something to cut (every 1,000 files is 48 s against 28 s on django, so this costs 70%), or (c) the parse phase reports its progress, which is most of a first scan and needs nothing from the publish. **Acceptance:** Ctrl-C returns in under 3 s on django's first scan.
2. **Deleting a file leaves its rows behind (low).** After a delete, the incremental database has `scan:file:<path>`, `scan:imports:<path>` and `scan:mentions:<path>` for the deleted file, and `provenance:inputs` keeps the signature of every removed or renamed function until the next change that touches a context (a fresh index has none of these). It happened in all five projects and in every delete and rename step. **No result differed** (the functions, edges and contexts were equal, including the delete, change, re-add cases), and `_read_inputs` reads all `scan:imports:*`, so a deleted file's imports are still read. Each deleted or renamed file leaves about 3 rows for the life of the database. I did not check whether Task 62 to 65 introduced it (the whole tree is uncommitted). **Acceptance:** a deletion removes the file's three rows, and `provenance:inputs` has no function that is not in `artifacts`.
3. **A Rust edit is still 13 s on bevy (medium; the dev lists it as not met in 68.2).** Measured: a new function 13.2 s, a new file 12.6 s, a delete 11.9 s, a rename 21.3 s (the contexts of the rename are 9.2 s of it). Every Rust change re-resolves all 27,352 functions (`resolve_rust_calls` 5.9 s) and rebuilds the edges (`_sync_edges` 8.6 s). **Acceptance** as in §72: a new function under 6 s on bevy. A start: re-resolve only the functions whose file mentions a changed name, as for Python, and treat a `pub use` or a `mod` change as the case that needs everything.
4. **Two smaller things (low).** `provenance:inputs` is written with its keys in another order after an edit than after a fresh index (the same entries by value, 0 differing entries): harmless, but it makes two equal databases look different to a digest. And my memory sampling was not enough to confirm the dev's peak-memory figures; if the setting `worker.scan_budget` is to be trusted on a larger project, a measurement by the dev's method on a project with several times django's functions is what is missing (the dev lists it too).

## Order

Finding 1 (the user sees it on the first scan), then 3 (the last open acceptance of §70/§72), then 2.

## What I did not do

I did not run the slow lane. The differential check is for what I wrote down above (42 steps); a rename of a directory, a symbolic link, an edit while the worker is publishing, and a project with several languages mixed were not tried. Rust and JavaScript always re-resolve everything, so the equality for them shows the contexts and the edges of the first part (68.2a), not the filter. The scripts are in the scratchpad (`diff_check.py`, `scen_*.py`, `real_scan.py`, `ctrlc_big.py`).
